Annexes

171 articles for reading the essay: the theories of its authors, the history of Christianity and of Europe, the disciplines it draws on, the Greek myths it assumes the reader knows. They are reading notes, written without quotations; they do not replace the sources. In the text, terms underlined with dots link to them.

Theories of the authors

Plato's philosophy: Forms, soul, city, the GoodTheories of the authors

Plato (428/427-348/347 BCE), a disciple of Socrates and the founder of the Academy, wrote some thirty dialogues in which almost all later philosophy found its questions. The essay is concerned above all with the Laws, the Republic and the Euthyphro; here is the framework within which these texts take their place.

The Forms and the intelligible world

Socrates asked what courage, piety and justice are, without being content with examples. Plato answers that these definitions aim at realities: the Ideas or Forms, the Beautiful itself, the Just itself, the Equal itself, unchanging, intelligible, distinct from the sensible things that participate in them and bear their name. The sensible world, changing and imperfect, is the image of an intelligible world that the soul contemplated before birth and that it recollects: to learn is to remember. At the summit of the Forms stands the Good, which the Republic compares to the sun: it gives the Forms their being and the soul its capacity to know them, and it is itself beyond essence. The allegory of the cave describes education as the liberation of a prisoner who turns round from the shadow towards the light. The Parmenides and the Sophist, late dialogues, subject the theory of Forms to objections that Plato does not entirely resolve, and the Neoplatonists will draw their system from them.

The soul

The soul is immortal, and Plato gives several proofs of this: in the Phaedo through the kinship of the soul with the Forms, in the Phaedrus and the Laws through the fact that it moves itself and that a principle of motion cannot perish. The Republic divides it into three parts, reason, spirit and appetite, whose harmony under the government of reason is justice. The Phaedrus compares it to a team of horses; the Timaeus situates it within the cosmos, the work of a demiurge who shaped the sensible world by looking at the intelligible model and endowed it with a world soul. The salvation of the soul passes through philosophy, which is an exercise in dying, a separation of soul from body, and through contemplation.

The city

The Republic builds the just city on the model of the soul: three classes, producers, guardians, philosophers, and the rule of philosopher-kings, the only ones who know the Good. Education is controlled there, the poets expelled for having attributed vices to the gods, and a purified theology taught. The Laws, the last dialogue, describe a second-best city, more achievable, governed by laws each of which is preceded by a persuasive preamble; Book X deals with impiety, refutes three forms of it, atheism, the denial of providence and the belief that the gods can be swayed, punishes them with imprisonment and, for repeat offenders, with death, and entrusts the guarding of the doctrine to the nocturnal council. In the Euthyphro Plato raises the question whether the pious is loved by the gods because it is pious, or pious because it is loved by the gods.

What the essay assumes

The essay reads Book X of the Laws as the pure case of its criterion and the ancestor of the Grand Inquisitor, and the Republic as the first name of spirituality, the conversion of the soul towards the Good; it sees between these two Platos a tension that is its own. It takes as known the theory of Forms, the hierarchy of the Good beyond essence, which Murdoch will take up, the proof from the self-moving soul and the place of the Euthyphro in the debate on morality without God. It sets Plato against Lucretius and Rousseau in constructed confrontations, and recalls Popper's reading.

In the essay: Plato · Rousseau · Dostoevsky · Iris Murdoch

Further reading: Plato, République (c. 375 BCE) ; Timée (c. 360 BCE) ; Lois (c. 348 BCE) ; Luc Brisson and Jean-François Pradeau, Le Vocabulaire de Platon (1998) ; Monique Dixsaut, Plato (2003) ; André Laks, Médiation et coercition. Pour une lecture des Lois de Platon (2005)

Aristotle's philosophy: act and potency, causes, the unmoved mover, virtue, contemplationTheories of the authors

Aristotle (384-322 BCE), a pupil of Plato for twenty years and then founder of the Lyceum, left lecture treatises covering logic, physics, biology, metaphysics, ethics, politics and poetics. The essay reads above all books Λ, E and A of the Metaphysics and the tenth book of the Nicomachean Ethics; here is their context.

The concepts of physics and metaphysics

Against Plato's separate Ideas, Aristotle holds that forms are in things: each individual substance, this horse, this man, is a compound of matter and form. To conceive change, he distinguishes act and potency: the acorn is an oak in potency, the oak is the act of the acorn; motion is the passage from potency to act. Every complete explanation draws on four causes, material, formal, efficient and final, and nature acts for the sake of an end. Logic, set out in the Organon, invents the syllogism and the theory of demonstration. The Metaphysics, a title given by the editors to the books placed after the Physics, seeks the science of being qua being and of first principles; being is said in several senses, the first of which is substance. Book Λ crowns the whole with theology: the eternal motion of the heavens requires an eternal, immobile substance, pure act without matter, which moves as an object of love and whose life is thought thinking itself.

The soul and knowledge

The treatise On the Soul defines the soul as the form of a natural body having life in potency, and distinguishes the nutritive, sensitive and intellective functions. The intellect that thinks all things must be separate and impassible; the famous passage on the agent intellect, which brings intelligibles into act as light brings colours, will feed the debates of Alexander of Aphrodisias, Avicenna, Averroes and Thomas on the immortality and unity of the intellect. Knowledge begins with sensation, passes through experience and is completed in the grasp of principles.

Ethics and politics

The Nicomachean Ethics asks what the highest good for man is: happiness, eudaimonia, an activity of the soul in accordance with virtue in a complete life. The moral virtues, courage, temperance, justice, liberality, are dispositions acquired by habit and consist in a mean determined by practical wisdom; the intellectual virtues are science, art, practical wisdom, intellect and wisdom. The tenth book places at the summit the contemplative life, theôria, the most continuous, the most self-sufficient and the most divine, and invites man, as far as possible, to immortalize himself, athanatizein, by living according to what is highest in him. The Politics makes man a political animal, examines regimes and their corruptions, and prescribes a common education. The Poetics defines tragedy by the purgation of the passions, catharsis.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the doctrine of act and potency, the theory of causes, the definition of the first mover and of thought thinking itself, which it makes the God of the philosophers of the three monotheisms, and the place of contemplation at the summit of ethics, which it reads as a spirituality of exercise without worship. It also retains the passage of Λ 8 on the myths added to persuade the multitude, the ancestor of the elitist reading, and the definition of first philosophy as theological, the source of Heidegger's onto-theology.

In the essay: Aristotle · Heidegger · Transition (part one) · Why is there something rather than nothing? A constructed debate

Further reading: Aristotle, Métaphysique (4th century BCE) ; Éthique à Nicomaque ; Physique ; De l'âme ; Pierre Aubenque, Le Problème de l'être chez Aristote (1962) ; Jonathan Barnes, Aristotle (1982) ; Pierre Pellegrin, Le Vocabulaire d'Aristote (2001)

The philosophy of Epicurus: atoms, canonic, pleasure and the remedy for fearsTheories of the authors

Epicurus (341-270 BCE), born on Samos, founded the Garden in Athens in 306, a school open to women and slaves, where people lived as friends according to a doctrine that makes philosophy a therapy of the soul. Of his three hundred scrolls only three letters, two collections of maxims and some fragments remain, and Lucretius's poem is the fullest exposition of his doctrine.

Canonic and physics

The canonic is the theory of the criterion of truth: sensations are always true, errors come from judgement; to them are added the preconceptions, concepts formed by the repetition of sensations, and the feelings of pleasure and pain. The physics takes up the atomism of Democritus: the universe is made of atoms, indivisible, eternal bodies, infinite in number, and of the void, through which they fall; everything else, worlds, souls, gods, is composed of atoms and dissolves. Epicurus adds to Democritus the swerve, Lucretius's clinamen: a tiny, uncaused deviation of the atoms in their fall, which explains their encounters and grounds freedom against fate. Celestial and meteorological phenomena admit several explanations, all acceptable provided they exclude divine intervention. The soul is bodily and mortal; death is nothing to us, for while we are, it is not, and when it is, we are no longer.

The gods and religion

Epicurus is not an atheist. The gods exist, as common evidence attests; they live in the intermundia, blessed and incorruptible, indifferent to human affairs, concern for which would disturb their bliss. The impious man is not the one who denies the gods of the crowd, but the one who attributes to the gods the opinions of the crowd: anger, favour, providence. Popular religion, with its fears of punishment and its hopes of reward, is the main source of disturbance, and knowledge of nature frees us from it. The gods nonetheless remain models of the happy life, whom the sage contemplates and honours.

The ethics of pleasure

Pleasure is the beginning and the end of the happy life, but it is defined negatively: the absence of pain in the body, aponia, and of disturbance in the soul, ataraxia. Epicurus classifies desires as natural and necessary, natural and unnecessary, neither natural nor necessary, and prescribes satisfying the first, moderating the second and fleeing the third. Prudence is worth more than philosophy itself, friendship is the greatest of goods, and the sage lives hidden, away from politics. The Letter to Menoeceus sums up the whole, and later Epicureans condensed it into the fourfold remedy, tetrapharmakos: the gods are not to be feared, death is not to be dreaded, the good is easy to obtain, evil easy to endure. The school lasted until the fourth century CE, opposed by the Stoics and the Christians, and was reborn in the seventeenth century with Gassendi.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with atomism, the mortality of the soul, the doctrine of the gods of the intermundia and the therapeutic meaning of the knowledge of nature. It places Epicurus in the regime of error while stressing his piety and his spiritual dimension, ataraxia as an exercise, and judges that Onfray inherits only half of him. Hume discusses Lucretius, and the trilemma of evil attributed to Epicurus returns in the Dialogues.

In the essay: Epicurus and Lucretius · Hume

Further reading: Epicurus, Lettres et maximes, trans. Marcel Conche (1987) ; Diogenes Laertius, Vies et doctrines des philosophes illustres, book X ; Jean-Marie Guyau, La Morale d'Épicure (1878) ; Jean Salem, Tel un dieu parmi les hommes. L'éthique d'Épicure (1989) ; Pierre-Marie Morel, Épicure. La nature et la raison (2009)

Lucretius and the De rerum natura: nature explained against religionTheories of the authors

Titus Lucretius Carus (c. 98-55 BCE) is the author of a single book, the De rerum natura, On the Nature of Things, a poem in six books and more than seven thousand hexameters that sets out the physics and ethics of Epicurus to a Roman, Memmius. Almost nothing is known of his life; the legend of a love potion and a suicide comes from Jerome, four centuries later. Cicero is said to have revised the poem after his death.

The poem

Book I invokes Venus, the generative power of nature, then sets out the principles: nothing is born from nothing, nothing returns to nothingness, everything is atoms and void. Book II deals with the motion of atoms, their swerve, the clinamen, and the plurality of worlds; Book III with the soul, material and mortal, and with death, which is nothing to us; Book IV with sensation, simulacra and love, which Lucretius satirizes; Book V with the origin of the world, of living beings and of civilization, in a history of humanity without providence, from the first humans to cities, language and the arts; Book VI with celestial and terrestrial phenomena, lightning, volcanoes, the magnet, and it ends with the plague of Athens. The poem is a lesson in physics with a moral aim: knowing the causes frees us from the fear of the gods and of death, which is the source of the passions and of crimes.

The critique of religion

The De rerum natura is one of the most violent ancient texts against religion. From Book I onwards, Lucretius celebrates Epicurus as the Greek who first dared to raise his eyes against the religion that crushed humankind, trampled it underfoot and, by his victory, made us equal to heaven; he then recounts the sacrifice of Iphigenia by Agamemnon at Aulis, to conclude that religion could prompt so many evils. Book V explains the birth of belief in the gods by the visions of dreams, by ignorance of the causes of celestial phenomena and by fear, and shows men prostrating themselves before altars without finding peace there. Lucretius does not deny the gods, blessed and distant; he denies that they govern the world and that they are to be feared. True piety is to contemplate all things with a mind at peace.

Legacy

Little read in the Middle Ages, the poem is rediscovered in 1417 by Poggio Bracciolini in a German monastery, printed in 1473, and read by Montaigne, who quotes it abundantly, by Gassendi, who rehabilitates Epicurus, by the libertines, by Diderot and d'Holbach, by Hume, by Marx, whose dissertation deals with Democritus and Epicurus, and by Bergson, who published extracts from it. It has become the emblem of a materialist critique of religion, and the line on the evils of religion one of the most quoted formulas of ancient literature.

What the essay assumes

The essay quotes the lines of Book I on religion trampled underfoot, on the victory that makes us equal to heaven, in which it sees a divinization of man that announces Feuerbach, and on Iphigenia. It assumes familiarity with the plan of the poem, with the explanation of belief by fear and ignorance, which Hume, Freud, and Norris and Inglehart will rediscover, and with the fact that Lucretius, like Epicurus, leaves religion without leaving the divine, which the essay calls the first form of its paradox.

In the essay: Epicurus and Lucretius · Hume · Norris and Inglehart · Montaigne

Further reading: Lucretius, De rerum natura (c. 55 BCE), trans. Alfred Ernout (1920) and José Kany-Turpin (1993) ; Pierre Boyancé, Lucrèce et l'épicurisme (1963) ; Stephen Greenblatt, Quattrocento (The Swerve, 2011) ; Alain Gigandet, Lucrèce. Atomes, mouvement (2001)

The philosophy of Plotinus: the hypostases, procession and conversionTheories of the authors

Plotinus (205-270), born in Egypt, a pupil of Ammonius Saccas in Alexandria, taught in Rome from 244 before a circle of disciples and senators. His fifty-four treatises, written late in life and with no concern for style, were arranged by his disciple Porphyry into six groups of nine, the Enneads. He sees himself as a mere interpreter of Plato; the moderns call him the founder of Neoplatonism.

The three hypostases

Reality is ordered into three principles, or hypostases. At the summit is the One, or the Good, absolutely simple, beyond being and thought, of which nothing can be said except what it is not; Plotinus derives it from the Good beyond essence of the Republic and from the first hypothesis of the Parmenides. From the One proceeds, without the One losing anything or willing it, like light from the sun, Intellect, Nous, which thinks itself and contains all the Forms, true being: it is Aristotle's god, placed in the second rank. From Intellect proceeds Soul, which contemplates Intellect and produces the sensible world, in which it unfolds as World Soul and as individual souls. Matter, the lowest degree, is privation and darkness, what Plotinus calls evil, without making it a principle. This system, procession, descends by degrees from unity to multiplicity; conversion, epistrophê, climbs back along the reverse path.

The ascent of the soul

The human soul, having descended into a body, keeps a part of itself in the intelligible. Philosophy is the itinerary of its return: purification through the virtues, detachment from the sensible, dialectic, contemplation of the Forms in Intellect, and finally union with the One, in which the soul ceases to think and to distinguish itself from what it reaches. Porphyry reports that Plotinus knew this union four times during the years he spent with him. This experience, which the moderns call ecstasy, is not a gift coming from elsewhere: it is obtained through an exercise, cutting away everything that is not the One, and the last sentence of the Enneads describes it as a flight of the alone to the alone. Plotinus has neither revelation, nor Church, nor cult; he refuses to sacrifice to the gods and writes against the Gnostics, who despise the sensible world, a treatise in which he defends the beauty of the cosmos.

Posterity

Porphyry, then Iamblichus and Proclus, who multiplies the intermediaries and reintegrates theurgic rites, hand the system down to the last pagans; Augustine reads it in the Latin translations of Marius Victorinus and finds in it the Word without the Incarnation; Pseudo-Dionysius Christianizes it and passes it on to the whole of medieval mysticism, Eriugena, Eckhart; Ficino translates it in the Renaissance; Hegel, Bergson and Bréhier reread it; Hadot, in 1963, makes it the model of a spirituality of the simplicity of vision.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the doctrine of the three hypostases, procession and conversion, the negative theology of the One, which it places at the origin of the regime of experience, and Porphyry's account. It makes Plotinus the consummate example of a spirituality that might not have become religion, religious in James's sense, the ancestor of the spiritualities without God that Hadot describes, and notes that the sacrifice criterion does not apply to him. Levinas, in A Religion for Adults, targets him by name, an attested response: to the Plotinian soul that goes towards nothing other than itself, he opposes a real transcendence.

In the essay: Plotinus · Meister Eckhart · Pierre Hadot

Further reading: Plotinus, Ennéades, trans. Émile Bréhier (1924-1938) and Luc Brisson and Jean-François Pradeau (2002-2010) ; Porphyry, Vie de Plotin (c. 301) ; Pierre Hadot, Plotin ou la simplicité du regard (1963) ; Jean Trouillard, La Procession plotinienne (1955) ; Dominic O'Meara, Plotin. Une introduction aux Ennéades (1992)

The philosophy of Averroes: the Commentator, the intellect, the reply to al-Ghazali, Latin AverroismTheories of the authors

Abû l-Walîd Muhammad ibn Rushd (1126-1198), Averroes to the Latins, born in Cordoba into a family of jurists, was qadi of Seville and of Cordoba, physician to the Almohad caliph and commentator on Aristotle at the prince's request. Disgraced in 1195 and exiled for a time, he dies in Marrakesh. His work has had two destinies: almost forgotten in Islam, it shaped Latin philosophy.

The Commentator

Averroes commented on almost the whole of Aristotle, in three formats: the epitomes, the middle commentaries and the long commentaries, which follow the text sentence by sentence. Dante calls him the one who made the great commentary, and scholasticism names him simply the Commentator. His project is to restore Aristotle freed from the Neoplatonic additions of Avicenna: he rejects emanation and the Avicennian distinction between essence and existence, and defends the eternity of the world, natural causality and God's knowledge of himself as the principle of all things. In the Long Commentary on the De anima, he holds that the material intellect, the receptacle of intelligibles, is one and eternal for the whole of humanity, like the agent intellect: human beings think by joining it, and personal immortality cannot be demonstrated by reason. This is the thesis of the unity of the intellect, or monopsychism, which Thomas Aquinas will combat.

The defence of philosophy

Against al-Ghazali, who in The Incoherence of the Philosophers had condemned twenty theses of the falsafa, Averroes writes The Incoherence of the Incoherence, a point-by-point refutation that defends causality against occasionalism and the necessity of the laws of nature. The Decisive Treatise, a short juridical treatise, establishes that the revealed Law commands philosophical study for those capable of it, that truth does not contradict truth, that the passages of the Qur'an whose apparent meaning clashes with demonstration must be interpreted, and that this interpretation must not be divulged to the common people, whom it would disturb: human beings fall into demonstrative, dialectical and rhetorical classes, and each must receive the truth in the form that suits it. The Exposition of the Methods of Proof criticizes the theologians of the kalam. Averroes also commented on Plato's Republic, since he did not have the Politics.

Latin Averroism

Translated into Latin from the 1220s, in Toledo and then in Sicily, Averroes enters the University of Paris with Aristotle. Masters of arts, Siger of Brabant and Boethius of Dacia, teach the eternity of the world and the unity of the intellect as necessary conclusions of reason while professing the faith; Thomas Aquinas writes On the Unity of the Intellect (1270) against them, and in 1277 Bishop Étienne Tempier condemns two hundred and nineteen propositions, accusing these masters of holding that a thing can be true in philosophy and false in theology, the double truth, which none of them formulated in those terms. Averroism continues in Padua until the sixteenth century, with Pomponazzi, and in 1852 Renan devotes his thesis to it, Averroès et l'averroïsme, which makes it an ancestor of free inquiry. The Guide for the Perplexed of Maimonides, his contemporary and compatriot, answers neighbouring questions.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the role of the Commentator, the quarrel with al-Ghazali, an attested response, the doctrine of the three classes of people and of reserved interpretation, and the legend of the double truth born of the condemnations of 1277. It concludes that Averroes neither leaves nor founds anything, but divides, and finds this solution again in Spinoza, Comte, Renan and Leo Strauss; the essay constructs a confrontation with Spinoza, who did not read The Decisive Treatise but targets the same arrangement when he attacks Maimonides in the Theological-Political Treatise, and Voegelin grants Averroes a point.

In the essay: Averroes · Maimonides · Leo Strauss · Islam before the exit from religion

Further reading: Averroès, Discours décisif, trans. Marc Geoffroy (1996) ; L'Incohérence de l'incohérence, trans. Simon Van den Bergh (1954, in English) ; Grand Commentaire du De anima, trans. Alain de Libera (1998) ; Alain de Libera, Averroès et l'averroïsme (1991) ; Ali Benmakhlouf, Averroes (2000) ; Dominique Urvoy, Averroès. Les ambitions d'un intellectuel musulman (1998)

The thought of Maimonides: the Guide for the Perplexed, the Mishneh Torah, prophecyTheories of the authors

Moses ben Maimon (1138-1204), Rambam for the Jewish tradition, Maimonides for the Latins, is born in Cordoba, flees the Almohad persecution, passes through Fez and Palestine and settles in Cairo, where he is physician to Saladin's vizier and head of the Jewish community. Jurist, philosopher and physician, he writes his philosophical works in Arabic and his code of the law in Hebrew.

The code and the law

The Commentary on the Mishnah (1168) contains the thirteen principles of faith, the first creed of Judaism, which affirm the existence, unity and incorporeality of God, prophecy, the superiority of Moses, the Torah as coming from heaven and immutable, retribution, the Messiah and the resurrection. The Mishneh Torah (c. 1180), in fourteen books, is the first complete code of Jewish law, written in clear Hebrew, without discussion or citation of sources: it is meant to make the law knowable without going through the Talmud, an ambition that earns it criticism. Its first book, the Book of Knowledge, places at the beginning of the law the knowledge of God through physics and metaphysics.

The Guide for the Perplexed

The Guide (c. 1190), written in Arabic for a disciple trained in philosophy and troubled by the letter of Scripture, the perplexed, aims to show that the Torah rightly read agrees with the science of Aristotle. The first part explains the terms of the Bible that seem to attribute a body or passions to God: they are homonyms, words with several meanings, whose bodily sense must be set aside. It develops the doctrine of negative attributes: one cannot say of God what he is, but only what he is not, and silence is the most fitting praise; the positive attributes of Scripture describe his actions, not his essence. The second part demonstrates the existence, unity and incorporeality of God from the principles of Aristotle, discusses the eternity of the world, which Maimonides judges indemonstrable either way and decides in favour of creation on the strength of revelation, and presents prophecy as a natural perfection of the intellect and the imagination, which God may withhold, Moses being the exception. The third deals with evil, with providence, proportioned to the intellect, with the book of Job, and with the reasons for the commandments: the sacrifices, in particular, are a pedagogical concession to people accustomed to pagan worship, intended to turn them away from it little by little. The introduction warns that the book contains deliberate contradictions, meant to hide from the vulgar what does not suit them.

Legacy

The Guide, translated into Hebrew as early as 1204 and into Latin in the thirteenth century, is read by Thomas Aquinas and by Eckhart, who cites Rabbi Moyses, and provokes within Judaism a century-long controversy over philosophy. Spinoza criticizes it in the Theological-Political Treatise, Leo Strauss makes it the model of esoteric writing, and Levinas finds in it a theology of attributes in the imperative.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the addressee of the Guide, the method of homonyms, the negative theology of attributes, which it treats as an anticipation of the regime of experience, the historical explanation of the sacrifices, which it says is separated by a narrow step from an explanation without God, and the seventh cause of contradiction, Strauss's source. It confronts Maimonides, in a constructed confrontation, with Feuerbach, for whom negative theology is a subtle atheism, and holds that each is right for a different audience.

In the essay: Maimonides · Averroes · Leo Strauss · Levinas

Further reading: Maimonides, Le Guide des égarés, trans. Salomon Munk (1856-1866) ; Mishné Torah (c. 1180) ; Leo Strauss, Persecution and the Art of Writing (1952) ; Maurice-Ruben Hayoun, Maimonides (1987) ; Herbert A. Davidson, Moses Maimonides. The Man and His Works (2005) ; Pierre Bouretz, Lumières du Moyen Âge. Maïmonide philosophe (2015)

The thought of Meister Eckhart: detachment, the birth of God in the soul, the trialTheories of the authors

Eckhart of Hochheim (c. 1260-1328), a Thuringian Dominican, twice master of theology in Paris, provincial of his order in Saxony, preacher in Strasbourg and Cologne, left a Latin body of work, scholastic in form, and a German one, sermons and treatises addressed to nuns and to lay people, which made him the father of Rhineland mysticism and of German philosophical prose.

God, the Godhead, being

In the Latin works, Eckhart comments on Scripture with the tools of scholasticism and holds that God is being itself, esse est Deus, and that creatures, in themselves, are pure nothingness, having only received being. In the German sermons he goes further: beyond God as he reveals himself in three persons and relates to creatures, there is the Godhead, Gottheit, a groundless ground, a desert, where there is neither Father nor Son nor Spirit, and where God and the soul are one. He takes up the negative theology of Dionysius and of Maimonides, whom he cites as Rabbi Moyses, and pushes it so far as to pray God to rid him of God, that is, of every representation of God that would still be a creature.

Detachment and the birth of God in the soul

The practical teaching comes down to one word, Abgeschiedenheit, detachment, to which Eckhart devotes a treatise: to empty oneself of every image, every will of one's own, every attachment, even to God as an object, so that God may be born in the soul. For there is in the soul a ground, a spark of the soul, Seelenfünklein, uncreated and uncreatable, where the Father begets his Son eternally, and what he begets is the soul itself become Son. Sermon 52, on the poor in spirit, defines true poverty: to will nothing, to know nothing, to have nothing, not even a place within oneself where God could act. The treatise On the Noble Man, Gelassenheit, the letting-be that follows from detachment, and the without-why of life, which lives because it lives, an image Silesius would take up with the rose, express the same doctrine, which does not despise action: Eckhart prefers Martha to Mary.

The trial

In 1326 the archbishop of Cologne opened inquisitorial proceedings against Eckhart, who defended himself, protested his orthodoxy and appealed to the pope. He died before the end of the trial, probably early in 1328. The bull In agro dominico of John XXII, dated 27 March 1329, condemned twenty-eight propositions drawn from his works, seventeen as heretical and eleven as suspect, noting that Eckhart had retracted before his death anything that might give rise to error. His work, read by Tauler and Suso, then by Nicholas of Cusa, was rediscovered in the nineteenth century by the Romantics and Franz Pfeiffer, and edited critically from 1936 onwards.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with sermon 52, the prayer to be rid of God, the bull of 1329 and the word Gelassenheit. It distinguishes two readings of Eckhart, negative theology pushed to its limit and the ancestor of spiritualities without God, and follows the second line in Schopenhauer, who hails him as a brother of the sages of India, in Heidegger, who displaces Gelassenheit, and in Simone Weil, whose decreation resembles his teaching. It notes that Feuerbach has the same form and the opposite conclusion.

In the essay: Meister Eckhart · Arthur Schopenhauer · Simone Weil

Further reading: Meister Eckhart, Sermons, trans. Jeanne Ancelet-Hustache (1974-1979) ; Traités et sermons, trans. Alain de Libera (1993) ; Josef Quint (ed.), Die deutschen Werke (1936-) ; Alain de Libera, Eckhart, Suso, Tauler et la divinisation de l'homme (1996) ; Kurt Flasch, Maître Eckhart. Philosophe du christianisme (2010)

The thought of Philo of Alexandria: Genesis read with PlatoTheories of the authors

Philo (c. 20 BCE-c. 45 CE), a Jew of Alexandria from a rich and influential family, is the principal witness to a Greek-speaking Judaism that read the Bible in the Septuagint translation and thought with Plato and the Stoics. In 39-40 he led the embassy of the Jews of Alexandria to Caligula, after the pogrom of 38, and left an account of it.

The work and the method

Some fifty treatises survive, almost all commentaries on the Pentateuch: the Allegorical Commentary, including the Legum allegoriae, which follows Genesis verse by verse; the Exposition of the Law, which runs from the De opificio mundi, on the creation, to the lives of the patriarchs and the particular laws; the Questions on Genesis and Exodus; and philosophical or historical treatises, including the De vita contemplativa, which describes the Therapeutae, a community of Jewish ascetics near Alexandria devoted to study and prayer. The method is allegorical exegesis, inherited from the Stoic interpreters of Homer and already practised by Alexandrian Jews: the text has a literal sense, which Philo does not reject and whose observance he maintains, and a deeper sense, in which the characters and events represent the powers of the soul, the virtues and the journey towards God. Abraham is the soul that learns, Isaac the soul that possesses virtue by nature, Jacob the soul that acquires it through exercise.

God, the Logos, the world

God is Being, he who is, according to Exodus read in Greek, transcendent, without name or quality, knowable only in his existence; his relation to the world passes through his powers, the creative and the royal, and above all through the Logos, the divine Reason, at once God's thought, the place of the Ideas, the model of the world, the instrument of creation and the mediator between God and humankind, which Philo calls firstborn, image of God and, once, second God. The De opificio reads the Genesis account with the Timaeus: God first conceived the intelligible world, then fashioned the sensible world in its image. Man created in the image of God is so through his intellect, the image of the Logos. Philo's ethics is that of an ascent of the soul, through virtue, contemplation and sober ecstasy, towards the vision of God.

Legacy

Rabbinic Judaism did not preserve Philo; it was the Christians, Clement of Alexandria, Origen, Eusebius, Ambrose, who copied, quoted and transmitted him, to the point that legend made him a Christian. His doctrine of the Logos supplied the prologue of John and the apologists with a vocabulary, and his allegory supplied the method of Alexandrian exegesis. Modern scholars have seen in him the first to attempt the synthesis of biblical revelation and Greek philosophy that Justin, Augustine, Maimonides and Averroes would each take up for their own tradition.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the reading of Genesis with the Timaeus, the doctrine of the Logos as intelligible world and second God, the link with the prologue of John, the two-level allegory that foreshadows Averroes, the Therapeutae, and the fact that Philo was transmitted by Christians and not by rabbinic Judaism. It places him in a regime of allegory prior to any critique: Philo does not criticize religion, he translates it into philosophy, and refounds nothing.

In the essay: Philo of Alexandria · Justin Martyr

Further reading: Philon d'Alexandrie, Œuvres, ed. Roger Arnaldez, Jean Pouilloux and Claude Mondésert (1961-1992) ; Émile Bréhier, Les Idées philosophiques et religieuses de Philon d'Alexandrie (1908) ; Harry A. Wolfson, Philo (1947) ; Mireille Hadas-Lebel, Philon d'Alexandrie. Un penseur en diaspora (2003) ; Carlos Lévy (ed.), Philon d'Alexandrie et le langage de la philosophie (1998)

Paul of Tarsus: the letters, justification, the Law and the new perspectiveTheories of the authors

Paul (c. 5-c. 64), a Jew from Tarsus in Cilicia, a Pharisee trained in the Law, a Roman citizen according to Acts, a persecutor of the first disciples of Jesus, is converted around 33 on the road to Damascus and becomes the apostle to the nations. His letters, written roughly between 50 and 60, are the oldest Christian texts, earlier than the Gospels, and his theology has shaped Christianity.

The letters

The New Testament contains thirteen letters under his name. Critical scholarship holds seven to be authentic: the first to the Thessalonians, the two to the Corinthians, and those to the Galatians, the Philippians, Philemon and the Romans. The others, Ephesians, Colossians, the second to the Thessalonians and the pastoral letters to Timothy and Titus, are attributed to disciples. The authentic letters respond to concrete situations in communities founded by Paul around the Aegean Sea; they contain the oldest testimonies to the death and resurrection of Jesus, including the received formula of 1 Corinthians 15, and to the Last Supper. The Acts of the Apostles, written by Luke a generation later, recount his missionary journeys, his speech before the Areopagus in Athens and his arrest; his death in Rome under Nero is attested only by tradition.

The theology

At the centre stands Christ crucified and risen, foolishness to the Greeks and a stumbling block to the Jews, whose death has reconciled humankind with God and whose return is near: Paul expects the end of time within his own lifetime. Man is justified, that is, made or declared righteous before God, by faith in Christ and not by the works of the Law; the Law of Moses, holy in itself, reveals sin without delivering from it, and converted Gentiles need not submit to it, nor to circumcision, a question that sets him against Peter and James and that the assembly of Jerusalem settles around 49. The baptized person dies and rises with Christ, lives in the Spirit, and the Church is the body of Christ, in which there is neither Jew nor Greek, neither slave nor free, neither male nor female. The letters also contain moral instructions, on marriage, meat sacrificed to idols, obedience to the authorities, and the hymn to charity.

The readings

Augustine reads Paul against Pelagius and finds in him grace and original sin; Luther finds in him justification by faith alone and makes the opposition of faith and works the heart of the Reformation; Nietzsche makes him the inventor of Christianity, who betrayed the Gospel of Jesus. Since E. P. Sanders in 1977, the new perspective on Paul has contested the Lutheran reading: first-century Judaism was not a religion of merit, and Paul's problem is not the justification of the anxious individual but the entry of the Gentiles into the people of God. Contemporary philosophers, Taubes, Badiou, Agamben, have reread Paul as a thinker of the universal and of the event.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the list of the seven authentic letters, the testimony of Galatians 1, 19 on James the brother of the Lord, Paul's eschatology as Schweitzer established it, Sanders's new perspective, and the readings of Engels and Nietzsche. It retains from Romans 12, 1 that Paul spiritualizes sacrifice instead of abolishing it, and sees in this the birth certificate of a religion that will be able to demand the whole offering.

In the essay: Paul of Tarsus · Nietzsche

Further reading: E. P. Sanders, Paul and Palestinian Judaism (1977) ; Albert Schweitzer, Die Mystik des Apostels Paulus (1930) ; Simon Légasse, Paul apôtre (1991) ; James D. G. Dunn, The Theology of Paul the Apostle (1998) ; Daniel Marguerat, Paul de Tarse. L'enfant terrible du christianisme (2023)

Justin and the apologists of the 2nd century: Christianity as philosophyTheories of the authors

The name apologists is given to the Christian writers of the second century who, in treatises addressed to the emperors or to the educated public, defend Christianity against the accusations aimed at it and present it as the true philosophy. Justin, who died a martyr in Rome around 165, is the most important of them, and the first to think through the relation of Christianity to Greek reason.

Justin

Born at Flavia Neapolis in Samaria, into a pagan family, Justin relates at the beginning of the Dialogue with Trypho his journey through the schools, Stoic, Peripatetic, Pythagorean, Platonist, up to his meeting with an old man who reveals to him the prophets and Christ. He keeps the philosopher's cloak and opens a school in Rome. His First Apology, addressed around 153 to Antoninus Pius, refutes the accusations of atheism, immorality and subversion, describes the baptism and eucharist of the Christians, and maintains that Christ is the Logos, the divine Reason, in which all men have a share: those who lived according to the Logos, Socrates, Heraclitus, were Christians before Christ. The Second Apology develops the doctrine of the seminal Logos, and asserts that everything that has been well said belongs to the Christians. The Dialogue with Trypho, a two-day discussion with a learned Jew, argues from the prophecies that Jesus is the Messiah and that the Law is abolished, and contains the first formulations of Christian anti-Jewish polemic. Justin also takes over from the Alexandrian Jews, Aristobulus and Philo, the idea that Plato had read Moses, the theft of the Greeks. Denounced, he is tried by the prefect of Rome and beheaded with six companions.

The other apologists

Aristides of Athens, around 125, is the earliest. Tatian, a disciple of Justin, writes an Address to the Greeks of radical hostility to pagan culture. Athenagoras of Athens, around 177, addresses to Marcus Aurelius a Plea that refutes with philosophical method the three accusations of atheism, Thyestean banquets and incest. Theophilus of Antioch, the Epistle to Diognetus and Melito of Sardis complete the Greek series. In Carthage, Tertullian (c. 155-c. 220), jurist and rhetorician, writes around 197 an Apology in the tone of a courtroom plea and, in the De praescriptione, opposes Athens to Jerusalem and rejects philosophy as the mother of heresies, while reasoning as a Stoic. Clement of Alexandria (c. 150-c. 215), at the opposite pole, makes Greek philosophy a preparation for the Gospel given by God to the Greeks as the Law was given to the Jews, and describes the true gnostic, the perfect Christian, in Platonic terms; his pupil Origen will continue along this path.

What is at stake

The apologists respond to a legal situation, the condemnation of Christians for the name itself, and to an intellectual situation, the contempt of the educated. Their response commits Christianity to the path of a rational theology: the Logos of John becomes the Logos of the philosophers, the God of the Bible the transcendent God of Middle Platonism, and faith a philosophy. Celsus, around 178, will answer them that this philosophy is a plagiarism.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the account of Justin's conversion, the doctrine of the seminal Logos, with its double face of openness and annexation, the Dialogue with Trypho, the theft of the Greeks, Tertullian's saying on Athens and Jerusalem, and Justin's martyrdom, of which it says that it is Rome that demands the sacrifice.

In the essay: Justin Martyr · Celsus and Origen

Further reading: Justin, Apologies, ed. Charles Munier (2006) ; Dialogue avec Tryphon, ed. Philippe Bobichon (2003) ; Henry Chadwick, Early Christian Thought and the Classical Tradition (1966) ; Bernard Pouderon, Les Apologistes grecs du IIe siècle (2005) ; Éric Osborn, Justin Martyr (1973)

Celsus and the True Doctrine: the first philosophical critique of ChristianityTheories of the authors

Celsus is a pagan philosopher of the second half of the 2nd century, author c. 178 of a treatise against the Christians, the True Doctrine, Alèthès logos, entirely lost but three-quarters reconstructed thanks to the quotations Origen makes from it in his refutation, seventy years later. It is the oldest comprehensive critique of Christianity, and almost all its arguments have had a long afterlife.

The man and the book

Nothing is known of Celsus apart from his book. Origen takes him for an Epicurean, but the text shows a Middle Platonist, steeped in Plato's Timaeus and Letters, believing in a supreme, transcendent God, who governs the world through subordinate gods and daemons, to whom the cities owe their worship. He knows Judaism, the Gospels, which he has read, and the divisions among Christians, including the Gnostics. The True Doctrine is built in two stages: Celsus first gives the floor to a Jew who attacks Jesus and the Christians from the point of view of the Law; then he speaks in his own name, from the point of view of philosophy and the Empire.

The arguments

Jesus, according to Celsus's Jew, was born of an adulteress and a soldier named Panthera, learned magic in Egypt, gathered disciples of low condition, was abandoned and executed as a criminal; his resurrection rests on the testimony of a hysterical woman. The Christians, according to Celsus, ask people to believe without examining and recruit among the ignorant, slaves, women and children, whom they forbid to listen to the wise; what is good in them, the idea of a single and good God, the immortality of the soul, judgement, they stole from Plato and the Greeks, distorting it; what is their own, the incarnation of a God in a mortal body, the resurrection of the flesh, the idea that God takes a particular interest in human beings, is absurd and unworthy of God; finally, they refuse the gods of the city, the service of the emperor and of the army, and thereby undermine the order of an Empire that tolerates them. Celsus invites them to join the common worship and to help the emperor, for if everyone did as they do, the Empire would fall into the hands of the barbarians. He never denies that Jesus existed.

Afterlife

The book mattered enough for Origen to agree, c. 248, to refute it book by book. Porphyry, a century after Celsus, and the emperor Julian would take up its arguments; the libertines, Voltaire, Renan and Nietzsche would rediscover them without always knowing it, and the True Doctrine, translated in 1925 by Louis Rougier, has become a classic of anti-Christian critique. Modern historians read it above all as the testimony of a religious conservative anxious for the unity of the Empire.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the date and the reconstruction of the True Doctrine, the four families of accusations, Jesus as magician, faith without reason, an audience of the ignorant, plagiarism of Plato, and the fact that Celsus does not doubt the existence of Jesus. It makes him a religious conservative, ancestor of Voltaire, Maurras and the late Onfray, who defends the gods of the city against the religion of slaves, and judges that neither of the two adversaries, Celsus or Origen, leaves religion.

In the essay: Celsus and Origen

Further reading: Celsus, Discours vrai contre les chrétiens, trans. Louis Rougier (1925, 1965) ; Origen, Contre Celse, ed. Marcel Borret (1967-1976) ; Pierre de Labriolle, La Réaction païenne (1934) ; Robert L. Wilken, The Christians as the Romans Saw Them (1984) ; Michael Frede, Celsus philosophus Platonicus, article (1994)

The thought of Origen: allegory, the pre-existence of souls, apocatastasisTheories of the authors

Origen (c. 185-c. 253), born in Alexandria into a Christian family, the son of a martyr, heads the catechetical school of his city while still young, studies philosophy under Ammonius Saccas, the teacher of Plotinus, then settles in Caesarea in Palestine around 232 after a conflict with his bishop. Tortured during the persecution of Decius, he dies from the effects of his wounds. He is one of the first great theologians and the first scholarly exegete of Christianity.

The exegete

His immense body of work is largely lost, because of posthumous condemnations. The Hexapla set out in six columns the Hebrew text of the Bible, its transliteration and four Greek translations, in order to establish the text of the Septuagint: it is the first work of biblical textual criticism. His commentaries and homilies cover almost the whole of Scripture. His method distinguishes three senses, corresponding to the body, the soul and the spirit: the literal or historical sense, the moral sense, and the spiritual or mystical sense, which only allegory reaches and which concerns Christ and the Church; certain passages, impossible or unworthy if taken literally, have no literal sense and oblige the reader to look higher. He inherits this method from Philo and Clement and bequeaths it to all ancient and medieval exegesis, which will develop it into four senses.

On First Principles

The Peri Archon, On First Principles, written around 230 and preserved mainly in Rufinus's softened Latin translation, is the first theological summa. It sets out the Trinity, with a Son subordinate to the Father, eternally begotten; the first creation of free and equal intelligences, whose fall, through the cooling of their love, explains the diversity of angels, humans and demons, and the creation of the material world as a place of education; free will, defended against the Gnostics and the astrologers; and the end of all things, when God will be all in all: the apocatastasis, a universal restoration in which all creatures, perhaps even the devil, will return to God, the punishments of the afterlife being medicinal and not eternal. Origen puts forward these theses as hypotheses for inquiry where the rule of faith is silent. Against Celsus, around 248, refutes the True Doctrine point by point and defends the rationality of faith, the moral superiority of Christians and their civic loyalty, while acknowledging that most of them believe without being able to demonstrate.

Origenism and its condemnation

Origen's disciples and continuators, Gregory Thaumaturgus, Pamphilus, Eusebius, then the Cappadocians, who compile an anthology of his texts, the Philocalia, make him the chief authority of the Greek East. But the pre-existence of souls, the apocatastasis and subordinationism provoke, from the end of the fourth century, an Origenist controversy, Epiphanius and Jerome against Rufinus, which leads in 543, under Justinian, to the condemnation of propositions attributed to Origen, confirmed by the Council of Constantinople of 553. His influence survives in Evagrius, Maximus the Confessor and Eriugena, and his rehabilitation by twentieth-century theologians, de Lubac, Daniélou, Balthasar, was one of the driving forces of the return to the Fathers.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with Against Celsus as the oldest attested response, with Origen's position on the faith of the simple and the demonstration of the learned, with the doctrine of the two cities that he sketches before Augustine, and with his Platonism. It retains that Origen opens up spiritual equality and closes off freedom of thought, and that he does not leave religion.

In the essay: Celsus and Origen · Philo of Alexandria

Further reading: Origène, Traité des principes, ed. Henri Crouzel and Manlio Simonetti (1978-1984) ; Contre Celse, ed. Marcel Borret (1967-1976) ; Henri Crouzel, Origen (1985) ; Henri de Lubac, Histoire et Esprit. L'intelligence de l'Écriture d'après Origène (1950) ; Pierre Nautin, Origène. Sa vie et son œuvre (1977)

Porphyry: the editor of Plotinus and the critic of the ChristiansTheories of the authors

Porphyry (c. 234-c. 305), born in Tyre in Phoenicia, a pupil of Longinus in Athens and then of Plotinus in Rome from 263 to 268, is at once the editor of his master, a philosopher and an encyclopaedic scholar, and the author of the most feared anti-Christian treatise of Antiquity. His very extensive work survives only in part.

The editor and the logician

Porphyry arranged the treatises of Plotinus into six Enneads and prefaced them with a Life of Plotinus, an almost unique source on the philosopher, which recounts his ecstasies, his refusal to let himself be painted, his remark about the gods who must come to him, and the crisis that Porphyry himself went through, when Plotinus turned him away from suicide by sending him to Sicily. His Isagoge, an introduction to Aristotle's Categories, translated into Latin by Boethius, was the logic textbook of the whole Middle Ages and raised, concerning genera and species, the question that would become the dispute over universals. He commented on Aristotle and Plato, wrote on abstinence from meat and on the cave of the nymphs in the Odyssey, interpreted allegorically, and composed for his wife a Letter to Marcella that sums up a philosophical piety: honouring God through silence and virtue rather than through sacrifices.

The religious thinker

The Philosophy from Oracles, a youthful work, collects and comments on oracles of Apollo and Hecate in order to find in them a way of salvation through rites; it is there that, according to Augustine, the gods declare Christ pious and immortal and the Christians defiled. Later, Porphyry shows himself reserved towards theurgy, which Iamblichus would defend against him in the De mysteriis, and places the salvation of the soul in philosophy. He is thus, within Neoplatonism, the representative of an inward and rational religion, and the last before the school's ritual turn.

Against the Christians

The treatise in fifteen books, written around 270 according to the most common dating, was banned and burned by Constantine, and again in 448; only fragments remain, quoted by those who refuted it, Eusebius, Methodius, Apollinaris, Jerome, Augustine, Macarius Magnes, and their attribution is often disputed. In it Porphyry applies philological and historical criticism to the Bible: he points out the contradictions between the gospels, the quarrel between Peter and Paul, the ignorance of the apostles, and above all he demonstrates, in Book XII, that the book of Daniel was written not in the 6th century by the prophet but in the 2nd, under Antiochus Epiphanes, by an author who told the past in the form of prophecy; modern exegesis has proved him right. He denies neither the existence nor the wisdom of Jesus, but accuses his disciples of having divinized him. Julian, a century later, would take up his arguments.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known Porphyry's connection with Plotinus and with Origen, whom he had met when young, the fragment on Daniel, in which it sees an anticipation of Spinoza and of David Friedrich Strauss, the passage of the Philosophy from Oracles quoted by Augustine, and the thesis of a wise Jesus betrayed by his disciples, which Renan and Nietzsche would take up. Augustine answers him in the City of God, and it is from this answer that the essay dates the form of Western theology.

In the essay: Porphyry and Augustine · Plotinus

Further reading: Porphyry, Vie de Plotin, ed. Luc Brisson et al. (1982-1992) ; Contre les chrétiens, fragments, ed. Adolf von Harnack (1916) ; Lettre à Marcella, ed. Édouard des Places (1982) ; Pierre Hadot, Porphyre et Victorinus (1968) ; Sébastien Morlet (ed.), Le Traité de Porphyre contre les chrétiens (2011)

The thought of Augustine: Confessions, City of God, grace, original sin, controversiesTheories of the authors

Aurelius Augustinus (354-430), born at Thagaste in Numidia, a rhetorician at Carthage, Rome and Milan, converted in 386, baptized by Ambrose, priest and then bishop of Hippo from 395 until his death, during the siege of the city by the Vandals, left a body of work of more than a hundred titles which shaped Latin theology, and through it Western thought, for more than a thousand years.

The itinerary and the Confessions

The Confessions (397-401), in thirteen books, recount to God, in the second person, Augustine's life up to his conversion: childhood, the theft of the pears, his studies, the reading of Cicero's Hortensius, which fired him with a love of wisdom, nine years among the Manichaeans, who set two principles against each other, good and evil, the scepticism of the New Academy, the discovery of the books of the Platonists, which led him to conceive an immaterial God and evil as privation, and finally the scene in the garden at Milan and the child's voice, take up and read. The last books meditate on memory, on time, which Augustine makes a distension of the soul, and on creation. The book is the first account of an inner life, and its formula, God more inward than my inmost self, founds Western interiority.

The City of God and the controversies

The De civitate Dei (413-426), written after the sack of Rome by Alaric in 410, answers the pagans who blamed Christianity for it: the first ten books refute Roman religion and philosophy, discussing above all the Platonists, the closest to the Christians; the twelve that follow tell the history of the two cities, the city of God and the earthly city, founded on two loves, the love of God to the point of contempt of self and the love of self to the point of contempt of God, intermingled until the judgement. Three controversies also structure the work. Against Manichaeism, Augustine defends the goodness of creation and free will. Against the Donatists, a schismatic Church of Africa that demanded the purity of its ministers, he holds that the validity of the sacraments does not depend on the holiness of the one who administers them, and ends by accepting imperial coercion to bring the schismatics back, invoking the Gospel's compel them to come in. Against Pelagius, a monk from Britain for whom man can avoid sin by his own strength, he elaborates the doctrine of grace and original sin: since Adam, humanity is a mass of perdition, no one can will the good without grace, and God predestines those whom he saves; Pelagianism is condemned in 418 and 431.

Knowledge and the Trinity

Against the sceptics, Augustine holds that whoever doubts exists, si fallor sum; seeks truth within the soul, where the inner teacher illuminates it; defines the method of believing in order to understand; and, in the De Trinitate, finds in memory, understanding and will the image of the Trinity. His theology of history, his doctrine of grace and his psychology nourished scholasticism, Luther, an Augustinian monk, Calvin, Jansenism and Pascal, Descartes and Heidegger.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the narrative of the Confessions, in particular the reading of the Platonists, the plan of the City of God and its discussion of the Platonists in book VIII, the formula of interiority, the method of believing in order to understand, and the Donatist episode, which it cites without having reread it. It makes the Augustinian synthesis of the God of the philosophers and the God of the Bible the major fact of the intellectual history of the West, which Pascal and Heidegger undo, and sees in rational theology armed by the Christian emperors, for which Augustine supplied the justification against the Donatists, a strict refoundation.

In the essay: Porphyry and Augustine · Pascal · Descartes

Further reading: Augustine, Confessions (397-401) ; De civitate Dei (413-426) ; De Trinitate (c. 400-426) ; Peter Brown, La Vie de saint Augustin (1967) ; Étienne Gilson, Introduction à l'étude de saint Augustin (1929) ; Serge Lancel, Saint Augustin (1999) ; Goulven Madec, Introduction aux Révisions et à la lecture des œuvres de saint Augustin (1996)

The thought of Montaigne: the Essays, scepticism, custom, experienceTheories of the authors

Michel Eyquem de Montaigne (1533-1592), a magistrate in Bordeaux, then withdrawn into his library from 1571, mayor of Bordeaux from 1581 to 1585, a mediator between Henry of Navarre and Henry III during the Wars of Religion, wrote a single book, the Essays, published in 1580, enlarged in 1588 and after his death in 1595, and endlessly rewritten in the margins. A practising Catholic, he is the first to make himself the matter of his book.

The book

The Essays are not a treatise: a hundred and seven chapters of unequal length, on subjects drawn from his reading of Plutarch, Seneca and the historians, Of Cannibals, Of Friendship, devoted to La Boétie, Of Coaches, Of Vanity, Of Repentance, Of Experience, in which thought advances by digressions, quotations and returns upon itself. To essay is to weigh, to put one's judgement to the test on everything, and first on oneself, who changes at every instant: Montaigne does not portray being but passing. The book forms a single body with its author, consubstantial, and its sincerity, which does not exclude art, has made it the model of writing about the self.

Scepticism and faith

The Apology for Raymond Sebond, the longest chapter (II, 12), starts from the defence of a fifteenth-century Catalan theologian who claimed to demonstrate the faith by reason, and turns it into a demolition of reason itself: man is not superior to the animals, the senses deceive, the philosophers contradict one another, science is opinion, and we hold our religion only by birth and country, Christians in the same way as we are Perigordians or Germans. Montaigne takes up the tropes of the Pyrrhonism of Sextus Empiricus, which he has engraved in his library, and his motto, Que sçay-je ?, in the form of a question. This scepticism is not unbelief: it serves to humble reason so that faith may be received from God alone, a position called fideism and held by several Catholics of the time. But its effect is to make custom the sole explanation of belief, and Pascal, Bayle and Hume will each draw different consequences from it.

Custom, experience, wisdom

Custom is for Montaigne the queen of the world: it makes laws, morals and religions, and the wise man conforms to it outwardly while keeping his judgement free inwardly, in a back shop all our own. Faced with the civil wars, he condemns cruelty, torture, witch trials, and doubts that a man should be burned for a conjecture. The last chapter, Of Experience, proposes a wisdom of acceptance of the body, of pleasure and of the human condition: nothing is so fine as to play the man well and properly, and the greatest thing in the world is to know how to belong to oneself. This wisdom does without salvation and the beyond, without denying them.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the structure of the Apology, the motto, the formulas on custom and on faith received by birth, the friendship with La Boétie, and the chapter Of Experience, which it reads as a spirituality without salvation. It holds Montaigne to be a sincere fideist and not a masked atheist, notes the unintended effect of his scepticism, Hume before Hume, and reports Pascal's attested response in the Entretien avec Sacy and the Pensées.

In the essay: Montaigne · Pascal · Hume

Further reading: Montaigne, Essais (1580, 1588, 1595), ed. Pierre Villey (1924) ; Hugo Friedrich, Montaigne (1949) ; Jean Starobinski, Montaigne en mouvement (1982) ; Richard Popkin, The History of Scepticism from Erasmus to Descartes (1960, expanded ed. to Spinoza 1979) ; Bernard Sève, Montaigne. Des règles pour l'esprit (2007)

The philosophy of Descartes: doubt, the cogito, God as guarantor of scienceTheories of the authors

René Descartes (1596-1650), educated at the Jesuit college of La Flèche, soldier, traveller, settled in the United Provinces from 1628 to 1649, who died in Stockholm at the court of Queen Christina, is regarded as the founder of modern philosophy. A mathematician, the inventor of analytic geometry, and a physicist, he sought to refound the whole of knowledge on a certain foundation.

Method and doubt

The Discourse on the Method (1637), a preface to three scientific essays, recounts in the first person the formation of a mind which, disappointed by letters and by the schools, resolves to accept as true only what it knows evidently, and sets down four rules: evidence, division, order, enumeration. The Meditations on First Philosophy (1641, in Latin), followed by the objections of Hobbes, Arnauld and Gassendi and by Descartes's replies, put the method to work. The first Meditation pushes doubt to the end: the senses deceive, dreaming cannot be told apart from waking, and an evil genius might deceive the subject even in mathematics. The second discovers the fixed point: whoever doubts thinks, and whoever thinks is, he is a thing that thinks; the cogito, formulated in the Discourse as I think, therefore I am, is the first certainty, grasped by the mind alone and not by the senses. The analysis of the piece of wax shows that bodies are known by the understanding and not by the imagination.

God, the world, man

The third Meditation proves God from the idea of the infinite: the subject finds in himself the idea of a perfect and infinite being, which cannot come from himself, a finite and doubting being, and whose cause must have as much reality as the idea contains; God therefore exists, and he is no deceiver. The fifth adds the ontological proof, existence belonging to the essence of the perfect being as the sum of its angles belongs to the triangle. God then guarantees the truth of what the mind conceives clearly and distinctly, and science becomes possible; error comes from the misuse of the will, which is infinite and judges beyond what the understanding conceives. The sixth Meditation establishes the existence of bodies and the real distinction between the soul, whose essence is thought, and the body, whose essence is extension: dualism, with the problem of their union, which Descartes locates in the pineal gland and treats in The Passions of the Soul (1649). The letters to Mersenne of 1630 add a singular thesis: the eternal truths, mathematics included, were freely created by God, who could have made them otherwise. Cartesian physics is mechanistic: matter is extension, animals are machines, and the world is explained by figures and motions.

Reception

The Meditations were placed on the Index in 1663; Cartesianism prevailed in the universities from the late seventeenth century, and the dispute over the cogito, circle or intuition, over the proof from the idea of the infinite and over the creation of the eternal truths has never ceased. Pascal reproaches Descartes for a useless and uncertain God, reduced to a flick of the finger; Spinoza starts from him; Leibniz objects that the possibility of God must first be proved; Kant dismantles his proofs; Husserl claims him as a forerunner; Heidegger sees in the Cartesian subject the beginning of modern metaphysics; Foucault places there the Cartesian moment, when truth ceases to require a transformation of the subject.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes the reader knows the order of the Meditations, the cogito, the two proofs of God, the function of God as guarantor of science, the creation of the eternal truths and dualism. It retains the dedication to the Sorbonne, which argues from the social usefulness of belief, Pascal's response, Leibniz's attested response, and the difficulty Descartes poses for Foucault's Cartesian moment, since his contemplation of God remains an exercise. Descartes makes God a component of reason, which Kant will dismantle.

In the essay: Descartes · Pascal · Michel Foucault · Leibniz

Further reading: René Descartes, Discours de la méthode (1637) ; Meditationes de prima philosophia (1641) ; Principia philosophiae (1644) ; Martial Gueroult, Descartes selon l'ordre des raisons (1953) ; Ferdinand Alquié, La Découverte métaphysique de l'homme chez Descartes (1950) ; Jean-Luc Marion, Sur la théologie blanche de Descartes (1981)

The thought of Pascal: the three orders, the wager, JansenismTheories of the authors

Blaise Pascal (1623-1662), a precocious mathematician and physicist, inventor of the calculating machine, theorist of the vacuum and of probability, turns to religion after a first conversion in 1646 and the night of 23 November 1654, the account of which, the Memorial, he keeps on his person. Linked to Port-Royal, he defends the Jansenists in the Provincial Letters and dies at thirty-nine, leaving the fragments of an apology for Christianity, the Pensées.

Jansenism and the Provincial Letters

Jansenism takes its name from the Augustinus (1640) of Cornelius Jansen, bishop of Ypres, which takes up the Augustinian doctrine of grace in its strictest form: human nature is corrupt, efficacious grace is given to whom God wills, and man can do nothing without it. The abbey of Port-Royal, led by Mother Angélique Arnauld, and the solitaries attached to it, Antoine Arnauld, Nicole, Sacy, are its centre in France; the Jesuits, supporters of a sufficient grace offered to all and of an accommodating morality, its adversaries. Five propositions drawn from the Augustinus are condemned by Rome in 1653. When Arnauld is threatened with exclusion from the Sorbonne, Pascal publishes in 1656-1657, under a pseudonym, eighteen Provincial Letters, a masterpiece of irony that ridicules the casuistry of the Jesuits, the direction of intention, the sufficient grace that does not suffice, and turns public opinion. Placed on the Index, they do not prevent the persecution of Port-Royal, which will be destroyed in 1710.

The Pensées

The fragments found at Pascal's death, some of them sorted by him into bundles, were published from 1670 and re-edited in different orders, by Brunschvicg in 1897 by theme, by Lafuma and Sellier according to the copies. The apology was to start from the wretchedness of man without God, the disproportion between the two infinities, the impotence of reason, the diversion that turns us away from thinking about our condition, then show his greatness, the thinking reed, and explain this contradiction by the Fall, which only the Christian religion makes intelligible. Proofs of God from nature are set aside: God is hidden, and gives himself through the heart, which has its reasons that reason does not know, through the prophecies, the miracles and the figure of Christ. The fragment on the wager addresses whoever can neither believe nor decide: the infinite stake makes the choice of God reasonable, and practice, taking holy water, will give rise to belief. The doctrine of the three orders, of bodies, of minds, of charity, separated by an infinite distance, sums up the Pascalian hierarchy. In the Pensées the spirit of finesse is distinguished from the spirit of geometry, and Montaigne is at once their model and their adversary.

Legacy

Voltaire attacks the Pensées in 1734 as the work of a sublime misanthrope; the Romantics, Chateaubriand, Sainte-Beuve, author of Port-Royal, rehabilitate them; Nietzsche sees in Pascal the most instructive victim of Christianity; Kierkegaard, Unamuno, Shestov, Simone Weil, then the existentialists make him the father of a philosophy of existence set against the system.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the Memorial, the Jansenist controversy, the hidden God, the wager in its practical reading, the heart and the opposition between the God of Abraham and the God of the philosophers, which it reads as a critique of religion in the name of faith, foreshadowing Heidegger and Marion. It places Pascal's attested response to Montaigne, the fragment on Descartes's flick of the finger, and the line of the exit from above, Kierkegaard, Barth, Weil.

In the essay: Pascal · Montaigne · Søren Kierkegaard · Do we have the right to believe? Clifford, James and analytic philosophy

Further reading: Blaise Pascal, Pensées, ed. Léon Brunschvicg (1897), ed. Louis Lafuma (1951), ed. Philippe Sellier (1976) ; Les Provinciales (1656-1657) ; Jean Mesnard, Pascal (1951) ; Henri Gouhier, Blaise Pascal. Commentaires (1966) ; Philippe Sellier, Pascal et saint Augustin (1970) ; Vincent Carraud, Pascal et la philosophie (1992)

Spinoza's philosophy: substance, attributes, modes, conatus, kinds of knowledge, the Theological-Political TreatiseTheories of the authors

Baruch Spinoza (1632-1677), born in Amsterdam into a family of Portuguese Jewish merchants, expelled from his community by the herem of 1656, a lens grinder, lives in Rijnsburg, Voorburg and The Hague, declines a chair at Heidelberg and dies at forty-four. In his lifetime he published under his own name only the Principles of Cartesian Philosophy (1663), and anonymously the Theological-Political Treatise (1670); the Ethics appears in his posthumous works.

The Ethics: God, the mind, the affects

The Ethics, demonstrated in geometrical order, by definitions, axioms, propositions and scholia, has five parts. The first establishes that there exists only one substance, infinite, cause of itself, God, of which everything that exists is a mode; thought and extension are two of its infinite attributes, among an infinity; God does not act by will or with ends in view, but by the sole necessity of his nature, and the appendix denounces the finalist prejudice and the asylum of ignorance. The second deals with the mind, the idea of the body, and with the parallelism of the two attributes, and distinguishes three kinds of knowledge: imagination, the source of error; reason, through common notions; intuitive science, or the third kind, which grasps singular things under the aspect of eternity. The third defines the conatus, the striving by which each thing perseveres in its being, and deduces from it the geometry of the affects, joy, sadness, desire and their compounds. The fourth, Of Human Bondage, shows the power of the affects and defines the good as one's own advantage, virtue as the power to act according to reason. The fifth, Of Human Freedom, leads to the intellectual love of God, to blessedness, which is virtue itself, and to the eternity of a part of the mind.

The Theological-Political Treatise

Written to defend the freedom to philosophize against the theologians, the Treatise founds the historical exegesis of the Bible: Scripture must be interpreted by itself, like nature, by studying its language and the history of each book and of its authors; Moses did not write the Pentateuch; the prophets had a vivid imagination, not a superior knowledge; miracles are natural events poorly understood; faith aims only at obedience and justice, and leaves philosophy free. The second part grounds the state in the pact, defends democracy as the most natural regime and concludes that in a free state everyone may think what he wishes and say what he thinks. The Political Treatise, unfinished, extends this theory. The book was banned in 1674 and Spinoza held to be an atheist for a century.

Posterity

Bayle judges him a systematic but virtuous atheist; Leibniz visits him and distances himself from him; the pantheism controversy (1785) revives him in Germany, where Lessing, Goethe, Herder, Schleiermacher and Hegel celebrate him; Nietzsche recognizes him as a precursor; Freud, Einstein, Deleuze, Comte-Sponville and Hadot claim him as their own; Leo Strauss sees in him the founder of the modern critique of religion.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the structure of the Ethics, the doctrine of substance and attributes, the kinds of knowledge, the conatus, the Treatise's thesis on faith and obedience, and the critique of Maimonides in chapter VII. It makes Spinoza the major exception, the position of immanence, who keeps the name of God out of conviction, and discusses the objections of Hegel, of Leo Strauss in the eleventh part, and the one it formulates itself: Spinoza does not explain the need for error.

In the essay: Spinoza · Leo Strauss · André Comte-Sponville · Pierre Hadot

Further reading: Spinoza, Ethica (1677), trans. Bernard Pautrat (1988) ; Tractatus theologico-politicus (1670), trans. Jacqueline Lagrée and Pierre-François Moreau (1999) ; Martial Gueroult, Spinoza (1968-1974) ; Gilles Deleuze, Spinoza. Philosophie pratique (1981) ; Pierre-François Moreau, Spinoza et le spinozisme (2003) ; Steven Nadler, Spinoza. Une vie (1999)

The thought of Pierre Bayle: the comet, the Dictionary, tolerationTheories of the authors

Pierre Bayle (1647-1706), son of a pastor of the county of Foix, converted to Catholicism and then returned to Calvinism, professor at Sedan, a refugee in Rotterdam in 1681 after the closure of the Protestant academy, teaches there, edits the Nouvelles de la République des Lettres and composes a body of scholarly and polemical work that nourished the Enlightenment. His personal faith is the subject of a debate that still continues.

The Various Thoughts on the Comet

The comet of 1680 gives Bayle the pretext for a long anonymous treatise (1682, enlarged in 1683) against the belief that comets portend misfortune. The argument widens: the authority of tradition proves nothing, for an error handed down remains an error; human beings act not according to their principles but according to their passions, and convinced Christians commit every crime; idolatry, which attributes vices to the gods, is worse than atheism; and a society of atheists could subsist as well as a society of pagans, provided the laws punish crimes, for human justice makes the virtue of most people. These theses, which separate morality from religion, cause a scandal; the pastor Jurieu, Bayle's colleague in Rotterdam and long his protector, becomes his relentless adversary and in 1693 obtains his dismissal.

Toleration

The revocation of the Edict of Nantes in 1685 and his brother's death in prison lead Bayle to write the Philosophical Commentary on compel them to come in (1686-1688), a refutation of the Augustinian interpretation of the parable that justified religious coercion. Bayle argues in it that conscience, even when erring, has rights: whoever errs in good faith must follow his conscience, and no one can be compelled to believe, since coercion produces only hypocrisy; toleration must extend to all confessions, and even to atheists. He goes further than Locke, whose Letter Concerning Toleration (1689) excludes atheists and Catholics, and opposes Jurieu, who demands toleration for persecuted Protestants while refusing it to others.

The Dictionary

The Historical and Critical Dictionary (1697, 1702), an enormous work in which the text of the articles is submerged by remarks in small type, is at once an undertaking to correct the errors of earlier dictionaries and a laboratory of scepticism. The articles David, Manichaeans, Paulicians, Pyrrho and Spinoza, the longest, raise the problem of evil, conclude that reason cannot solve it and that one must rely on faith, a fideist position that his adversaries take for a ruse and that Leibniz sets out to refute in the Theodicy. The Dictionary was the bedside book of Voltaire, Diderot and Hume; the Enlightenment drew method and arguments from it, and the debate over Bayle, believing sceptic or concealed atheist, still sets Labrousse against Mori.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with paragraphs CXIV and CLXI of the Various Thoughts, the quarrel with Jurieu, an attested response, Bayle's position on toleration and the problem of evil, and Leibniz's response. It places Bayle among the five answers of the constructed debate on morality without God, morality holds through society, and confronts him with Ivan Karamazov: order against guilt.

In the essay: Bayle · Leibniz · A constructed debate · Dostoevsky

Further reading: Pierre Bayle, Pensées diverses sur la comète (1682-1683) ; Commentaire philosophique sur ces paroles de Jésus-Christ : Contrains-les d'entrer (1686-1688) ; Dictionnaire historique et critique (1697, 1702) ; Élisabeth Labrousse, Pierre Bayle (1963-1964) ; Gianluca Mori, Bayle philosophe (1999) ; Hubert Bost, Pierre Bayle (2006)

Leibniz's philosophy: monads, pre-established harmony, sufficient reason, theodicyTheories of the authors
