Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646-1716), born in Leipzig, jurist, diplomat, librarian and historian of the dukes of Hanover, inventor of the infinitesimal calculus at the same time as Newton, founder of the Berlin Academy, pursued mathematics, logic, physics, theology, law and projects for the reunion of the Churches all at once. His philosophical work is scattered across short pieces, letters and two books, the New Essays, published posthumously, and the Theodicy.

The principles

Leibniz reasons from two great principles: the principle of contradiction, which governs truths of reason, which are necessary, and the principle of sufficient reason, which governs truths of fact, which are contingent, and according to which nothing happens without there being a reason why it is so rather than otherwise. To these are added the principle of indiscernibles, there are no two perfectly similar beings, and that of continuity, nature makes no leaps. Applied to the existence of the world, the principle of reason leads to God: the ultimate reason of contingent things must lie outside their series, in a necessary being, and the question why there is something rather than nothing, raised in the Principles of Nature and Grace, finds its answer there. God, who has in his understanding all possible worlds, chose to create the best of them, according to the principle of the best.

The monads

Against Descartes, who reduced matter to extension, Leibniz reintroduces force and substantial form. True substances are monads, set out in the Discourse on Metaphysics (1686) and the Monadology (1714): simple unities, without parts or windows, endowed with perception and appetition, each of which expresses the whole universe from its own point of view, with more or less distinctness, from bare monads to souls and minds. Bodies are well-founded phenomena resting on aggregates of monads. Since monads do not act on one another, their agreement, and that of soul and body, is explained by pre-established harmony: God created them in such a way that they correspond, like two clocks set together. The complete notion of each substance contains everything that will happen to it, which raises the problem of freedom, which Leibniz resolves by distinguishing inclination without necessity.

The Theodicy

The Essays on Theodicy (1710), the only book published in his lifetime, reply to Bayle, who held the problem of evil to be insoluble. Leibniz distinguishes metaphysical evil, the imperfection of every creature, physical evil, suffering, and moral evil, sin; God permits evil without willing it, because the best possible world contains some, a world without evil being either impossible or less good as a whole. Optimism, the name later given to this doctrine, was ridiculed by Voltaire in Candide after the Lisbon earthquake, and Kant judged that every philosophical theodicy fails. Russell saw in Leibniz's logic the key to his metaphysics; possible worlds have become a tool of modal logic.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the principle of sufficient reason, the question of § 7 of the Principles of Nature and Grace, the doctrine of the best of worlds and of the three evils, the attested response to Bayle, Voltaire's response, and the visit to Spinoza in 1676. It judges that theodicy asks the individual to sacrifice his complaint, that Ivan Karamazov is its moral answer, and that Leibniz's question survives his answer.

In the essay: Leibniz · Why is there something rather than nothing? A constructed debate · Voltaire · Bayle

Further reading: G. W. Leibniz, Discours de métaphysique (1686) ; Essais de théodicée (1710) ; Monadologie and Principes de la nature et de la grâce (1714) ; Bertrand Russell, A Critical Exposition of the Philosophy of Leibniz (1900) ; Michel Fichant, Science et métaphysique dans Descartes et Leibniz (1998) ; Paul Rateau, La Question du mal chez Leibniz (2008)

The philosophy of Hume: empiricism, causation, miracles, the natural history of religionTheories of the authors

David Hume (1711-1776), a Scot from Edinburgh, a successful historian, diplomat and librarian, a friend of Adam Smith and of the Encyclopedists, never obtained a chair because of his reputation as an unbeliever. He is the most consistent of the empiricists and the Enlightenment philosopher who most lastingly shook natural religion, without professing atheism.

Empiricism and causation

A Treatise of Human Nature (1739-1740), which Hume said fell dead-born from the press, and then An Enquiry concerning Human Understanding (1748) ground knowledge in experience: all our ideas derive from impressions, and an idea without a corresponding impression is empty. The relation of cause and effect, on which all reasoning about matters of fact rests, comes neither from reason nor from the experience of a necessary connection: we observe only constant succession, and it is habit that makes us expect the effect and project a necessity onto things. Belief is a feeling, not a demonstration, and induction has no rational foundation. The self itself is only a bundle of perceptions. This scepticism is not a paralysis: nature makes us believe, act and play backgammon, and philosophy must take its rule from common life. The Enquiry contains the chapter on miracles: no testimony can establish a miracle, for it is always more probable that the witness is mistaken or lying than that the law of nature has been violated.

Morals and politics

Books II and III of the Treatise and An Enquiry concerning the Principles of Morals (1751) make reason the slave of the passions and ground morality in sentiment: we approve what is useful or agreeable, to ourselves or to others, through sympathy. Justice is an artificial virtue born of conventions. The Essays and the History of England (1754-1762) develop a moderate political thought, hostile to religious enthusiasm as well as to superstition.

Religion

The Natural History of Religion (1757) does not ask whether religion is true but where it comes from: not from the contemplation of the order of the world, but from fear and hope in the face of life's events; polytheism is the primitive religion, monotheism emerges from it through the progressive flattery of one god, and the two alternate in a flux and reflux; monotheism, more rational, is more intolerant. The Dialogues concerning Natural Religion, completed before his death and published in 1779, have three characters debate: Cleanthes, who defends the argument from design, Demea, who defends the a priori proof and an incomprehensible God, and Philo, the sceptic, who ruins both, poses the problem of evil in the form of Epicurus's trilemma and concludes, in an ambiguous twelfth part, that the cause of the order of the world probably bears some remote analogy to human intelligence, which grounds neither worship nor morality. Hume died serene, without religion, as Boswell observed with dismay.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with empiricism, the critique of causation, the chapter on miracles, the plan of the Natural History and of the Dialogues, and the three characters. It makes Hume the only one of the five Enlightenment authors to put nothing in the place of God, the sceptic of the series who replaces God with no capital letter but offers no way of life, and notes that Boyer, in a constructed confrontation, finds in him the faces in the moon, and Norris and Inglehart fear.

In the essay: Hume · A constructed debate · Norris and Inglehart · Tylor and Frazer

Further reading: David Hume, A Treatise of Human Nature (1739-1740) ; An Enquiry concerning Human Understanding (1748) ; The Natural History of Religion (1757) ; Dialogues concerning Natural Religion (1779) ; Michel Malherbe, La Philosophie empiriste de David Hume (1976) ; J. C. A. Gaskin, Hume's Philosophy of Religion (1978)

Voltaire: deism, the Infamous, tolerance, CandideTheories of the authors

François-Marie Arouet (1694-1778), known as Voltaire, poet, playwright, historian, storyteller and polemicist, imprisoned in the Bastille, exiled to England, courtier at Versailles and at Potsdam, then patriarch of Ferney on the Swiss border, was for half a century the most widely read writer in Europe and the symbol of the French Enlightenment. His religious thought is a combative deism.

Deism

Voltaire believes in a God who creates and orders, proved by the order of the world, the clock presupposing a clockmaker, and he holds atheism to be a philosophical error and a social danger: he writes against Spinoza, against d'Holbach and his System of Nature, and against the atheists of the coterie, to whom he objects in the Épître à l'auteur des Trois imposteurs (1769) that if God did not exist, it would be necessary to invent him. This God does not reveal himself, does not become incarnate and has no Church; he guarantees morality, common to all human beings, and justice, which kings must fear. The Philosophical Letters (1734), brought back from England, praise the tolerance of the English sects, Locke and Newton, are condemned and burned by the Parlement, and earn their author a lettre de cachet that makes him flee to Cirey. The Poem on the Lisbon Disaster (1756) and Candide (1759) break with the optimism of Leibniz and Pope: evil is real, Providence incomprehensible, and we must cultivate our garden.

The Infamous

From the 1760s onwards, Voltaire campaigns against what he calls the Infâme, the Infamous, fanaticism, superstition and the Church that upholds them, under the motto Écrasez l'Infâme with which he signs his letters. The Pocket Philosophical Dictionary (1764), the Treatise on Tolerance (1763), La Bible enfin expliquée, dozens of anonymous pamphlets attack dogmas, miracles, the contradictions of the Bible, persecutions. The affair of Calas, a Toulouse Protestant broken on the wheel in 1762 for a murder he had not committed, whose rehabilitation Voltaire obtains in 1765, then the Sirven and La Barre affairs, make him the defender of the victims of intolerance and the model of the writer who takes up causes. He nevertheless remains hostile to the education of the people, wishes servants and the tailor to keep a religion that restrains them, and practises Catholic worship at Ferney, building a church dedicated to God.

History and posterity

The Essai sur les mœurs (1756) writes the first universal history that does not start from the Bible and grants China, India and Islam their place; The Age of Louis XIV founds the history of civilization. Placed in the Panthéon in 1791, Voltaire became for the Republic the symbol of the struggle against clericalism, and for its adversaries the father of the Revolution, of which he saw nothing. Historians distinguish the sincere deist from the tactician, and debate how much social fear there is in his religion for the people.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes that deism, the Infamous, the Calas affair, Candide and the Épître of 1769 are known; it makes the last the programme of the Grand Inquisitor and the example of an avowed refoundation, God invented as the sacred bond of society. It situates Voltaire's attested response to d'Holbach, Candide against Leibniz, and the attested exchange with Rousseau over the Lisbon disaster, and notes that Ivan Karamazov quotes the line from the Épître.

In the essay: Voltaire · Leibniz · A constructed debate

Further reading: Voltaire, Lettres philosophiques (1734) ; Candide ou l'Optimisme (1759) ; Traité sur la tolérance (1763) ; Dictionnaire philosophique (1764) ; René Pomeau, La Religion de Voltaire (1956) ; René Pomeau (ed.), Voltaire en son temps (1985-1994)

Rousseau's thought: state of nature, social contract, general will, EmileTheories of the authors

Jean-Jacques Rousseau (1712-1778), citizen of Geneva, self-taught, musician, contributor to the Encyclopédie and then adversary of the philosophes, author of the most widely read novel of the century, Julie, or the New Heloise, persecuted after 1762 and wandering until his death, thought about man, society, education and religion as a single whole, and his books nourished the Revolution as well as Romanticism.

Nature and history

The Discourse on the Sciences and Arts (1750) maintains against its century that the progress of enlightenment has corrupted morals. The Discourse on the Origin of Inequality (1755) goes back to a hypothetical state of nature in which man, solitary, healthy and limited, is neither good nor wicked, moved by self-love and pity; his perfectibility, property and the division of labour give rise to amour-propre, comparison, inequality and finally an iniquitous contract that legalizes the domination of the rich. Man is born good, it is society that depraves him: the thesis replaces original sin with a history, and Rousseau defends it against the Archbishop of Paris in the Letter to Christophe de Beaumont.

The contract and the general will

The Social Contract (1762) seeks a form of association in which each, uniting with all, obeys only himself. The solution is the total alienation of each to the community, which creates a sovereign body politic whose general will, distinct from the will of all, always aims at the common good; law is the expression of this will, the people alone are sovereign and the government is merely an executor. Whoever refuses to obey the general will shall be compelled to do so, which means, according to the famous formula, that he will be forced to be free. The last chapter, On Civil Religion, distinguishes the religion of man, Christian and pure, but which detaches one from the world, the religion of the citizen, national, and that of the priest, which divides; it prescribes a civil profession of faith whose dogmas, God, the life to come, the happiness of the just, the punishment of the wicked, the sanctity of the contract, and a single negative dogma, intolerance, are required of every citizen on pain of banishment, and of death for anyone who, having acknowledged them, behaves as if he did not believe them.

Education and the religion of the heart

Emile (1762) follows the education of a child according to nature, far from books and prejudices, up to manhood. Book IV contains the Profession of Faith of the Savoyard Vicar: a priest sets out a natural religion founded not on reasoning but on inner feeling, conscience, a divine instinct, the order of the world felt rather than demonstrated, and rejects both the materialism of the philosophes and the authority of revelations, which place so many men between God and the believer. The book is condemned in Paris and in Geneva. The Confessions and the Reveries of the Solitary Walker invent modern autobiography and the sentiment of existence.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes for granted the state of nature, perfectibility, the general will, the chapter on civil religion, which it regards as the text that most exactly illustrates its criterion, the Profession of Faith, taken up by Kant, and the letter to Voltaire of 1756 on the citizen's catechism. It ranks Rousseau among the strict refoundations, as one who left the Church through the heart and re-entered religion through the city, and sets him against Plato in a constructed confrontation.

In the essay: Rousseau · Plato · Durkheim · Assessment of the refoundations

Further reading: Jean-Jacques Rousseau, Discours sur l'origine et les fondements de l'inégalité (1755) ; Du contrat social (1762) ; Émile ou De l'éducation (1762) ; Les Confessions (posth., 1782-1789) ; Jean Starobinski, Jean-Jacques Rousseau. La transparence et l'obstacle (1957) ; Robert Derathé, Jean-Jacques Rousseau et la science politique de son temps (1950) ; Pierre-Maurice Masson, La Religion de Jean-Jacques Rousseau (1916)

D'Holbach: materialism, the Système de la nature, the militant atheism of the EnlightenmentTheories of the authors

Paul-Henri Thiry, Baron d'Holbach (1723-1789), a German from the Palatinate naturalized French, wealthy, a chemist and mineralogist, contributor of several hundred articles to the Encyclopédie, kept in Paris for thirty years the salon where Diderot, Grimm, Helvétius, Hume and foreign visitors gathered, the Holbachian coterie. Under borrowed names and false imprints, he published the first systematic atheist work of modern Europe.

The Système de la nature

The Système de la nature ou Des lois du monde physique et du monde moral (1770), which appeared under the name of an academician who had died ten years earlier, sets out in two volumes a complete materialism. Nothing exists but matter and motion, which is inherent in it; nature is a whole from which everything derives by necessary laws; man is a physical being, his soul a function of the body, his freedom an illusion, and his morality must be founded on enlightened self-interest and the common happiness. The second part attacks religion: ignorance of natural causes and fear engendered the gods, priests exploited that fear, and theology, by lending God contradictory attributes, describes an impossible being; Voltaire's deism is as fragile as Christianity. The atheist, who knows the laws of nature, is more virtuous than the devout, and a society has no need of God. The book is condemned to be burned by the Parlement, refuted by Voltaire and by Frederick II, and causes a scandal even among the philosophes, Diderot excepted, who may have collaborated on it.

The other writings

Le Christianisme dévoilé (1766), La Contagion sacrée (1768), the Histoire critique de Jésus-Christ (1770), Le Bon Sens (1772), a popular summary of the Système, and a Théologie portative attack revelation, miracles, the Bible, the clergy and the alliance of throne and altar; La Politique naturelle and the Système social (1773) develop a utilitarian and reformist politics, enlightened monarchy rather than revolution. D'Holbach takes from Lucretius the explanation of religion by fear, from Spinoza and Bayle certain arguments, and gives the whole a pedagogical and militant form that will make its fortune in the nineteenth century, among freethinkers and in the labour movement, and in the twenty-first among the new atheists.

The religion of Nature

The Système de la nature nonetheless ends with a prayer: d'Holbach addresses Nature, sovereign of all beings, and her adorable daughters, Virtue, Reason, Truth, asking them to be for ever our only divinities. Atheist materialism thus gives itself a cult, on the model of what it fights, and it is this reversal that Voltaire points out, and that the essay sets, by construction, beside Stirner's formula.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the salon, the Système de la nature, the thesis that ignorance engenders the gods, the figure of the virtuous atheist and the final prayer to Nature. It places d'Holbach in the regime of error, sees in him a projection before Feuerbach, and classes him among the transfers of the sacred: a pious atheist before Stirner, who made Nature a divinity. Voltaire answers him in the article Dieu, dieux, an attested response.

In the essay: d'Holbach · Epicurus and Lucretius · Contemporary militant atheists

Further reading: Paul-Henri Thiry d'Holbach, Le Christianisme dévoilé (1766) ; Système de la nature (1770) ; Le Bon Sens (1772) ; Pierre Naville, D'Holbach et la philosophie scientifique au XVIIIe siècle (1943) ; Alan Charles Kors, D'Holbach's Coterie (1976) ; Jonathan Israel, Les Lumières radicales (2001)

Kant's philosophy: the three Critiques, the imperative, the postulates, religionTheories of the authors

Immanuel Kant (1724-1804), who was born and died in Königsberg, which he never left, professor at its university, brought up in Lutheran Pietism, published after the age of fifty-seven the three Critiques that displaced the whole of philosophy: the question is no longer what the world is, but what the mind can know, must do and may hope.

Theoretical reason

The Critique of Pure Reason (1781, revised in 1787) asks how synthetic a priori judgements are possible. The answer is the Copernican revolution: it is not knowledge that conforms to objects, but objects that conform to the forms of our knowledge. Space and time are the a priori forms of sensibility, the categories, among them causality, the a priori concepts of the understanding; knowledge results from their application to the data of the senses, and bears only on phenomena, never on things in themselves. Reason, which wants to go beyond, goes astray: the Transcendental Dialectic shows that the soul, the world and God, the objects of traditional metaphysics, cannot be known, and refutes the three proofs of the existence of God. God remains a regulative idea, and knowledge is limited in order to make room for faith.

Practical reason

The Groundwork of the Metaphysics of Morals (1785) and the Critique of Practical Reason (1788) ground morality on reason alone: an action is moral only if it is performed from duty, according to a maxim one can will to be raised into a universal law, the categorical imperative, another formula of which commands us to treat humanity always also as an end and never merely as a means. The moral law, a fact of reason, reveals freedom, and the will that gives itself its own law is autonomous; every morality grounded on God, happiness or feeling is heteronomous. Practical reason nevertheless postulates what theoretical reason could not know: freedom, the immortality of the soul and the existence of God, necessary so that virtue and happiness may be brought into accord in the highest good. Morality thus leads to religion. The Critique of the Power of Judgement (1790) deals with the beautiful, the sublime and the purposiveness of nature.

Religion and the Enlightenment

Religion within the Boundaries of Mere Reason (1793) defines religion as the recognition of our duties as divine commands, examines radical evil, the propensity of the will to subordinate the law to self-love, makes Christ the ideal of a humanity pleasing to God, whose historicity matters little, and contrasts the invisible Church, an ethical community, with the visible Churches; its fourth part denounces false worship, the fetishism of rites and Pfaffenthum, priestcraft, which earns Kant a reprimand from the King of Prussia. The short essay What is Enlightenment? (1784) defines Aufklärung as man's emergence from his immaturity, under the motto Sapere aude, and Toward Perpetual Peace (1795) proposes a federation of republics.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the distinction between phenomenon and thing in itself, the refutation of the proofs, the categorical imperative, autonomy, the postulates and the content of the Religion of 1793. It holds that Kant dismantles Descartes's God and reinstalls him through morality, and reports the objections of Schleiermacher, of Hegel and of Nietzsche, for whom Kant sacralized morality, before noting that Habermas makes him the first example of a secularizing translation.

In the essay: Kant · Descartes · Friedrich Schleiermacher · Jürgen Habermas

Further reading: Immanuel Kant, Kritik der reinen Vernunft (1781, 1787) ; Grundlegung zur Metaphysik der Sitten (1785) ; Kritik der praktischen Vernunft (1788) ; Kritik der Urteilskraft (1790) ; Die Religion innerhalb der Grenzen der bloßen Vernunft (1793) ; Alexis Philonenko, L'Œuvre de Kant (1969-1972) ; Jean-Louis Bruch, La Philosophie religieuse de Kant (1968)

Schleiermacher's thought: the Speeches, the Glaubenslehre, hermeneuticsTheories of the authors

Friedrich Daniel Ernst Schleiermacher (1768-1834), son of a Reformed chaplain, educated among the Moravian Brethren, a pastor in Berlin, a friend of the Romantics, a professor at Halle and then at the University of Berlin, which he helped to found, and a translator of Plato, is regarded as the father of modern Protestant theology and one of the founders of hermeneutics. He sought to save religion by shifting it from doctrine to experience.

The Speeches on Religion

The Reden, On Religion: Speeches to Its Cultured Despisers (1799), were written in the circle of the Berlin Romantics, Friedrich Schlegel, Novalis, Henriette Herz, for whom the religion of the Churches was dead. Schleiermacher answers them that what they despise is not religion: its essence is neither metaphysics, which thinks, nor morality, which acts, but the intuition and feeling of the universe, the sense and taste for the infinite; each positive religion is an individual form of this intuition, and Christianity the highest, because its central intuition is mediation. Scripture is only the mausoleum of living religion, Spinoza is one of its saints, and a religion without God can be worth more than a badly conceived religion. The book marks the birth of religious Romanticism and of the regime of experience.

The Glaubenslehre

Der christliche Glaube (1821-1822, recast in 1830-1831), The Christian Faith, is the dogmatics of his maturity. Piety is defined there as the feeling of absolute dependence, schlechthinniges Abhängigkeitsgefühl, and God as the whence of this feeling; dogmas are the conceptual expression of the states of Christian religious consciousness, and theology a science of faith as it is lived in a community. Christ is the man in whom God-consciousness was perfectly powerful, and redemption the communication of this consciousness. The Trinity is relegated to an appendix, and miracles and prophecies lose their role as proofs. Hegel, in the preface to Hinrichs (1822), mocks the dog, which would be the best Christian if religion were dependence; Feuerbach and Barth will say that this theology of human consciousness leads to anthropology.

Hermeneutics, Plato, ethics

Schleiermacher founds general hermeneutics, the art of understanding any discourse from its two sides, grammatical and psychological, and sets out the circle of the whole and the parts. His translation of Plato, unfinished, remains in use in German. His Soliloquies and his ethics develop a philosophy of individuality in which each person expresses humanity in his own way, and his Dialectic a theory of knowledge. He also played a part in the Union of the Lutheran and Reformed Churches in Prussia and an educationalist.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the Reden, the definition of the feeling of absolute dependence, the Romantic circle and Schleiermacher's Plato. It makes him the first name of the regime of experience and the first attempt to leave instituted religion without leaving lived religion, and formulates against him the weakness of this whole regime: experience proves that one feels, not what one feels. Hegel, Feuerbach and Otto answer him in attested responses, and Barth draws the conclusion.

In the essay: Friedrich Schleiermacher · Rudolf Otto

Further reading: Friedrich Schleiermacher, Über die Religion. Reden an die Gebildeten unter ihren Verächtern (1799) ; Monologen (1800) ; Der christliche Glaube (1821-1822, 1830-1831) ; Hermeneutik und Kritik (posth., 1838) ; Wilhelm Dilthey, Leben Schleiermachers (1870) ; Friedrich Schleiermacher, Discours sur la religion, trans. Bernard Reymond (2004) ; Christian Berner, La Philosophie de Schleiermacher (1995)

The philosophy of Hegel: dialectic, the Phenomenology, Absolute Spirit, religion and conceptTheories of the authors

Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel (1770-1831), a fellow student of Hölderlin and Schelling at the Tübingen seminary, private tutor, journalist, headmaster of a grammar school, professor at Heidelberg and then at Berlin, where he reigned over German philosophy, built the last great system: a thought of totality in which the real is rational, history has a meaning and religion finds its truth in philosophy.

The dialectic and the Phenomenology

Hegel's method is the dialectic: every finite determination contains its negation, and the movement by which a position passes into its opposite and is then resolved into a higher unity, which both cancels and preserves it, Aufhebung, is at once the law of thought and the law of being. The Phenomenology of Spirit (1807) follows this movement through the history of consciousness, from sense-certainty to absolute knowledge, by way of famous figures: the dialectic of master and slave, in which the master depends on the slave who works; the unhappy consciousness, divided between itself and an unchanging beyond, a figure of medieval Christianity; the Enlightenment against faith; the beautiful soul. The Science of Logic sets out the categories of pure thought, from being to the concept, and the Encyclopaedia of the Philosophical Sciences (1817) the whole system: logic, philosophy of nature, philosophy of spirit, subjective, objective, absolute.

Objective Spirit and history

The Elements of the Philosophy of Right (1821) set out Objective Spirit, abstract right, subjective morality and ethical life, family, civil society, State, the modern State being the realization of freedom; the preface states that what is rational is actual and that the owl of Minerva takes its flight at dusk. The lectures on the philosophy of history make world history the progress of the consciousness of freedom, accomplished by the cunning of reason through the passions of great men.

Religion and the concept

Absolute Spirit manifests itself in art, religion and philosophy, which have the same content, the absolute, in three forms: intuition, representation, concept. Religion tells the truth in the form of images and narratives; philosophy sublates it into concept, and thus preserves Christianity by going beyond it. The young Hegel had written critical texts on the positivity of the Christian religion; Faith and Knowledge (1802) ends on the feeling that God himself is dead and on the speculative Good Friday; the Lectures on the Philosophy of Religion, delivered four times in Berlin, present Christianity as the absolute religion, in which God reveals himself as Spirit, and read the Trinity and the Incarnation in concepts. After his death, the school splits between a right, which holds philosophy to be the ally of faith, and a left, Strauss, Bauer, Feuerbach, Marx, which draws from it the critique of religion.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the dialectic, Aufhebung, the distinction between representation and concept, Faith and Knowledge, the Lectures and the quarrel with Schleiermacher, an attested response. It holds Hegel's argument to be sound and his conclusion false, philosophy becoming the higher religion of the wise, and follows Kierkegaard's attested response and the critique of the Hegelian left, as well as Löwith's genealogy, which places Hegel within secularized eschatology.

In the essay: Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel · Søren Kierkegaard · Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels · Karl Löwith

Further reading: G. W. F. Hegel, Phänomenologie des Geistes (1807) ; Wissenschaft der Logik (1812-1816) ; Enzyklopädie der philosophischen Wissenschaften (1817, 1830) ; Grundlinien der Philosophie des Rechts (1821) ; Vorlesungen über die Philosophie der Religion (posth., 1832) ; Jean Hyppolite, Genèse et structure de la Phénoménologie de l'esprit (1946) ; Bernard Bourgeois, Hegel (1998)

David Friedrich Strauss: The Life of Jesus, myth, The Old Faith and the NewTheories of the authors

David Friedrich Strauss (1808-1874), a pupil of the Tübingen seminary and of Ferdinand Christian Baur, and a tutor there, published at twenty-seven a Life of Jesus that deprived him for ever of any chair and made him, in Germany, the symbol of learned unbelief. A journalist, a biographer, a deputy in 1848, he ended as a bourgeois freethinker, mocked by Nietzsche.

The Life of Jesus

Das Leben Jesu, kritisch bearbeitet (1835-1836), in two volumes, examines the gospel narratives one by one and applies to them a new alternative. The rationalists, like Paulus, explained the miracles by misunderstood natural causes; the supernaturalists held them to be true; Strauss maintains that neither is right, because these narratives are myths: not impostures, but unintentional creations, absichtslos, of the early Christian community, which clothed Jesus in the features expected of the Messiah according to the Old Testament, virgin birth, miracles, transfiguration, resurrection. The concept of myth, borrowed from the study of antiquity and already applied to the Old Testament, is thus extended to the New, and the Gospel of John loses all historical value. There remain a historical Jesus, a Galilean preacher of whom little is known, and a philosophical conclusion of Hegelian inspiration: the predicates the Church attributes to Christ, the union of the divine and the human, death, resurrection, do not belong to an individual but to the idea of the human species; humanity is God made man. The French translation is by Littré, the English by George Eliot.

What followed

After the controversies, Strauss published Die christliche Glaubenslehre (1840-1841), a Christian dogmatics that shows the dogmas dissolving through their own history, lost a chair at Zurich before he had even taken it up, wrote biographies, Ulrich von Hutten, Voltaire, and a Life of Jesus for the German People (1864) in reply to Renan. His last book, Der alte und der neue Glaube, The Old Faith and the New (1872), answers four questions: are we still Christians? no; do we still have a religion? yes, a feeling of dependence on the universe; how do we understand the world? through Darwin and science; how do we order our lives? through common morality, the nation and the classics, Lessing, Goethe, Beethoven. Nietzsche, in 1873, made him the type of the Bildungsphilister, the cultivated philistine, who has given up God without changing anything in his life.

Legacy

Strauss opened the way to the whole criticism of the gospels, Baur, Renan, Bultmann, and his idea of myth without an author distinguishes his position from that of Bauer, for whom the evangelists invented consciously, and from that of the mythicists of the twentieth century, who deny the existence of Jesus. His conclusion about the human species is Feuerbach's starting point.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the thesis of unintentional myth, the conclusion that humanity is God made man, in which it sees Feuerbach's starting point, the role of George Eliot, The Old Faith and the New and Nietzsche's attested response. It ranks Strauss among the transfers of the divine onto humanity, and places his position between that of Bauer and that of the mythicists.

In the essay: David Friedrich Strauss and Bruno Bauer · Did Jesus exist? The Jesus of history and the mythicist thesis · Ludwig Feuerbach

Further reading: David Friedrich Strauss, Das Leben Jesu, kritisch bearbeitet (1835-1836), trans. Émile Littré (1839-1840) ; Die christliche Glaubenslehre (1840-1841) ; Der alte und der neue Glaube (1872) ; Albert Schweitzer, Geschichte der Leben-Jesu-Forschung (1906) ; Horton Harris, David Friedrich Strauss and His Theology (1973) ; Jean-Marie Paul, D. F. Strauss et son époque (1982)

Bruno Bauer: the critique of the Gospels, the Berlin Free Ones, the Jewish questionTheories of the authors

Bruno Bauer (1809-1882), a Hegelian theologian in Berlin, first placed on the right of the school, moves in a few years to the most radical critique of the Gospels and of the Christian state, loses his chair in Bonn in 1842, leads the Berlin circle of the Free Ones and ends as a conservative publicist. He is the teacher, then the adversary, of Marx, and the ancestor of the mythicist thesis.

From the Hegelian right to the Hegelian left

Bauer makes his name in 1835-1836 with a hostile review of Strauss's Life of Jesus, in the name of speculative orthodoxy. His own critique of the Gospel of John (1840), then of the Synoptics, the Critique of the Gospel History of the Synoptics (1841-1842), tips him over: against Strauss, for whom myth is the unconscious work of the community, Bauer maintains that the Gospels are conscious literary compositions, Mark having invented the narrative that Matthew and Luke developed; the self-consciousness of the writer, not tradition, is the source. He comes to doubt that there is any historical Jesus beneath these texts, and his last books, Christus und die Caesaren (1877), have Christianity born in the second century, under the emperors and without a founder, from the meeting of Seneca's Stoicism and Philo's Alexandrian Judaism. In 1841 he publishes anonymously The Trumpet of the Last Judgement against Hegel the Atheist and Antichrist, the Posaune, a pamphlet in which, feigning the voice of an indignant pietist, he shows that Hegel's philosophy, properly understood, dissolves religion and the state into self-consciousness; Marx was to collaborate on its sequel. In 1842 the Prussian ministry withdraws his right to teach.

The Free Ones and the Jewish question

With his brother Edgar, Stirner, Köppen and others, Bauer forms in Berlin the circle of the Free Ones, die Freien, where critique becomes an end in itself: pure critique, standing above the mass and its illusions. In The Jewish Question (1843), he argues that Jews cannot be emancipated as Jews and that political emancipation requires the renunciation of all religion; Marx replies in On the Jewish Question that political emancipation leaves religion in place within civil society and that human emancipation is needed. The Holy Family (1845) and The German Ideology, by Marx and Engels, take as their target Saint Bruno and his critical criticism. After 1848, Bauer draws closer to the conservatives and writes antisemitic works.

Legacy

Engels, in 1882, pays tribute to him for having raised the question of the origins of Christianity as a historian, while judging that he went too far; Albert Kalthoff, Arthur Drews and the mythicists claim him as their forerunner; Albert Schweitzer devotes an admiring chapter to him; Marx, in 1844, starts from the observation that the critique of religion is complete, which he owes to Bauer's circle.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the difference between Strauss and Bauer over the author of the myth, Bauer's doubt about the existence of Jesus, Engels's text of 1882, the circle of the Free Ones in which Stirner writes The Ego and Its Own, and Marx's polemic against Saint Bruno. It places Bauer at the origin of the mythicist path, which Drews, Couchoud and, according to the essay, the late Onfray take up.

In the essay: David Friedrich Strauss and Bruno Bauer · Did Jesus exist? The Jesus of history and the mythicist thesis · Max Stirner

Further reading: Bruno Bauer, Die Posaune des jüngsten Gerichts über Hegel den Atheisten und Antichristen (1841) ; Kritik der evangelischen Geschichte der Synoptiker (1841-1842) ; Die Judenfrage (1843) ; Christus und die Caesaren (1877) ; Friedrich Engels, Bruno Bauer und das Urchristentum (1882) ; Douglas Moggach, The Philosophy and Politics of Bruno Bauer (2003)

Ernest Renan: the Life of Jesus, science, the nationTheories of the authors

Ernest Renan (1823-1892), a Breton from Tréguier and a seminarian at Saint-Sulpice, leaves the Church in 1845 for philology, becomes one of the first orientalists of France, is suspended from the Collège de France in 1862 for having called Jesus an incomparable man, then dismissed in 1864, returns in 1870 and becomes its administrator. Historian of religions, essayist, moralist, he embodied for two generations faith in science and elegant scepticism.

The break and science

The Recollections of My Youth (1883) recount the crisis of 1845: it is the historical criticism of the Bible, learned together with Hebrew, and not philosophy, that takes away his faith; he leaves the seminary keeping the morality and the sensibility of the priest. The Future of Science, written in 1848 and published in 1890, professes that science, and first of all philology, the science of the products of the human mind, will replace religion and organize humanity; the preface of 1890 admits to illusions. Renan remains to the end an idealist for whom the universe tends towards God, who is not but becomes, and a smiling sceptic in the Philosophical Dialogues and the Philosophical Dramas.

The Life of Jesus

The first volume of the History of the Origins of Christianity, the Life of Jesus (1863) is written after a journey to Galilee, whose landscapes seem to him a fifth gospel. It applies the German methods, Strauss, Baur, while softening them, and makes Jesus a man, a young Galilean prophet of exquisite gentleness, preacher of a religion of the heart, who lets himself be drawn little by little towards messianism, wonder-working and death, and whose cult, says the last page, will be endlessly renewed. The book, written like a historical novel, sells hundreds of thousands of copies, is translated everywhere, condemned from the pulpit, and provokes a war of pamphlets. The six following volumes, The Apostles, Saint Paul, The Antichrist, The Gospels, The Christian Church, Marcus Aurelius, follow Christianity to the end of the 2nd century, then the History of the People of Israel goes back to the sources.

The nation, races, the moralist

The lecture What Is a Nation? (1882) defines the nation by consent, a daily plebiscite, against the criteria of race and language of the Germany victorious in 1870. Renan nevertheless wrote, in his works on the Semitic languages, pages on the inferiority of the Semitic mind that postcolonial criticism, Edward Said, has pointed out, and on the superiority of the European races. The Prayer on the Acropolis, in the Recollections, expresses his religion of Greek beauty. He was one of the masters of the Third Republic, and his statue in Tréguier was unveiled in 1903 despite Catholic protests.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes for granted the crisis of 1845, the positivist method of the Life of Jesus, the fifth gospel, the last page on the cult that will be renewed, and Renan's place in the history of the quests for the historical Jesus. It sees in him an exit that preserves religion as a cult of the great man, reports the attested responses of Nietzsche, for whom Renan remained a priest in his entrails, and of Dostoevsky, and notes his contemporary heirs.

In the essay: Ernest Renan · Did Jesus exist? The Jesus of history and the mythicist thesis · Nietzsche

Further reading: Ernest Renan, Vie de Jésus (1863) ; Histoire des origines du christianisme (1863-1883) ; Souvenirs d'enfance et de jeunesse (1883) ; Qu'est-ce qu'une nation ? (1882) ; L'Avenir de la science (1890) ; Jean-Pierre van Deth, Renan (2012) ; Laudyce Rétat, Religion et imagination religieuse. Leurs formes et leurs rapports dans l'œuvre d'Ernest Renan (1977)

Feuerbach's thought: The Essence of Christianity, the species, love, natureTheories of the authors

Ludwig Feuerbach (1804-1872), son of an eminent Bavarian jurist and a student of Hegel in Berlin, was denied a university career after his first book. He lived in retirement at Bruckberg, in Franconia, on the income of his wife's porcelain factory, and there wrote the books that made him, for the whole Hegelian left, the liberator. He died poor, a member of the Social Democratic Party.

From death to religion

The Thoughts on Death and Immortality (1830), published anonymously, deny personal immortality: only the human species is eternal, the individual dies entirely, and belief in the hereafter turns people away from life. Once the author was identified, the book closed the university to him. There followed a history of modern philosophy, a Bayle, a Leibniz, and a critique of Hegel (1839) charging the system with being nothing but a disguised theology.

The Essence of Christianity

Das Wesen des Christentums (1841) applies to religion a method he calls genetic or critical: showing what the religious object is made of. Religion is man's relation to his own essence, taken for another being: God is the human essence, reason, will, love, infinite in the species, projected outside man and worshipped; the consciousness of God is man's self-consciousness, and theology is anthropology. The first part reads each dogma as a human truth: the Trinity is the life of the species, the Incarnation the love of God for man, that is, of man for man, the miracle the fulfilment of a wish, prayer the dialogue of the heart with itself. The second part denounces the contradictions of theology, which separates God from man and sacrifices morality to faith, essentially intolerant. The remedy is not to abolish religion but to turn it around: man must take back what he gave to God, and the love of man for man must become religion.

The philosophy of the future and nature

The Provisional Theses for the Reform of Philosophy and the Principles of the Philosophy of the Future (1843) extend the critique to speculative philosophy, whose secret is theology: subject and predicate must be reversed, the starting point must be sensuous man, the body, the I and the Thou, whose unity is God, and man must be made the supreme being for man. The Essence of Religion (1846) and the Heidelberg Lectures, given to students in 1848-1849 during the revolution and published in 1851, shift the emphasis: before being the projection of the human essence, religion is a feeling of dependence on nature, and desire is its origin. In 1845 Feuerbach replies to Stirner, who accuses him of having deified Man, and turns the epithet of pious atheist back against Stirner. Marx, who owes him his break with Hegel, criticizes him in the Theses of 1845 for an abstract man and a contemplation without practice; Engels, in 1886, for a cult of love.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the method of projection, the Gattung, the formulas of 1841 and 1843, the reply to Stirner and the naturalist turn of 1846 and 1851. It devotes its pivotal section to Feuerbach, sees in him the textbook case of soft refoundation and of the need to consecrate, and follows his readers: Stirner, Marx, Engels, Barth, who grants him that he is right all along the line, and Freud and Durkheim, whom the essay, by construction, reads as his transpositions.

In the essay: Ludwig Feuerbach · Max Stirner · Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels · Barth · Freud

Further reading: Ludwig Feuerbach, Gedanken über Tod und Unsterblichkeit (1830) ; Das Wesen des Christentums (1841), trans. Jean-Pierre Osier (1968) ; Vorläufige Thesen zur Reform der Philosophie and Grundsätze der Philosophie der Zukunft (1843) ; Das Wesen der Religion (1846) ; Vorlesungen über das Wesen der Religion (1851) ; Henri Arvon, Ludwig Feuerbach ou la transformation du sacré (1957) ; Marx W. Wartofsky, Feuerbach (1977)

Max Stirner and The Ego and Its Own: the Ego against the spectresTheories of the authors

Johann Caspar Schmidt (1806-1856), known as Max Stirner because of his high forehead, a teacher at a girls' boarding school in Berlin and a member of the circle of the Free Ones around the Bauer brothers, wrote a single book, Der Einzige und sein Eigentum, The Ego and Its Own (dated 1845, published at the end of 1844), then translated Adam Smith and Say, ran a dairy, knew debtors' prison and oblivion, before being rediscovered by the anarchists and by Nietzsche, who may have read him.

The book

The Ego and Its Own opens and closes with the line from Goethe that sets its cause on nothing. The first part, Man, tells human history as that of the individual: the Ancients, realists, who live in the world; the Moderns, idealists, who since Christianity have lived in the spirit; and the Free Ones, political, social and humane liberals, who believe they have overcome Christianity and have merely replaced God with the State, Society and Man. Every idea placed above the individual, to which he must sacrifice himself, is a spectre, a Spuk, a fixed idea, and whoever is possessed by it has a screw loose; the sacred is what is alien to the individual and what he respects. The second part, I, sets against all these spectres the Unique One: not a concept, but the one who speaks, who acknowledges no vocation, no duty, no essence, and who holds everything, others included, as his property to the extent of his power. The Ego is a creative nothing, the nothing from which he draws everything. Revolution, which changes institutions, is opposed to insurrection, which refuses to let itself be arranged; society, to the union of egoists, a revocable association of individuals who make use of it. Stirner's egoism is not greed but self-enjoyment, and his style, piling up wordplay, parodies the Bible and Hegel.

The receptions

Feuerbach, Szeliga and Hess replied as early as 1845, and Stirner answered them in Stirner's Critics. Marx and Engels devoted to him the greater part of The German Ideology (1845-1846), unpublished until 1932: Saint Max has merely substituted for the domination of ideas that of one idea, the Unique One, and ignores that the individual is produced by real social relations. The book disappeared until its rediscovery by Mackay in 1898; it then became a reference for individualist anarchism, a supposed precursor of Nietzsche, a case for psychoanalysis, and for Camus, in The Rebel, the point where metaphysical rebellion turns into nihilism.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with Goethe's line, the theory of spectres, the sentence on our atheists as pious people, the fear of Man as a modified form of the fear of God, theological insurrections, and the replies of Feuerbach, Marx and Camus. It makes Stirner the author of the law of the whole series, the law of refoundations or Stirner's law, which Stirner himself does not escape, since the Unique One installs itself in the place of God, and it ranks the Ego among the transfers of the sacred.

In the essay: Max Stirner · Ludwig Feuerbach · Camus · Assessment of the refoundations

Further reading: Max Stirner, Der Einzige und sein Eigentum (1844), trans. Robert L. Reclaire (1900), Henri Lasvignes (1900), Pierre Gallissaires and André Sauge (1972) ; Recensenten Stirners (1845) ; John Henry Mackay, Max Stirner. Sein Leben und sein Werk (1898) ; Henri Arvon, Aux sources de l'existentialisme. Max Stirner (1954)

The thought of Marx: historical materialism, alienation, ideology, fetishismTheories of the authors

Karl Marx (1818-1883), born in Trier to a Jewish lawyer converted to Protestantism, doctor of philosophy with a dissertation on Democritus and Epicurus, journalist on the Rheinische Zeitung, exiled to Paris, Brussels and then, from 1849, London, where he lives on Engels's subsidies and works in the British Museum, founded with Engels the socialism that bears his name and dominated the political thought of the following century.

The critique of religion and of Hegel

The early texts start from Feuerbach and the Hegelian left. The Introduction to the Critique of Hegel's Philosophy of Right (1844) regards the critique of religion as completed in Germany and as the presupposition of all critique: religion is the consciousness of an inverted world, the expression of real distress and the protest against it, the sigh of the oppressed creature, the opium of the people; one must therefore criticize the world that needs illusions, and the critique of heaven turns into the critique of earth. The Manuscripts of 1844 analyse the alienation of labour: the worker is dispossessed of his product, of his activity, of his species-being and of other human beings, and communism is the reappropriation of the human essence. The Theses on Feuerbach (1845) break with contemplation: the human essence is the ensemble of social relations, and the philosophers have only interpreted the world, the point is to change it. The German Ideology (1845-1846), unpublished until 1932, founds historical materialism: the production of material life determines the forms of consciousness, and ideology, religion, morality, philosophy, is the inverted reflection of real relations, in which the ruling ideas are those of the ruling class.

History and Capital

The Communist Manifesto (1848), written with Engels, tells history as the history of class struggles, hails the revolutionary work of the bourgeoisie, which has dissolved religious fervour in commercial calculation, and announces the victory of the proletariat. Capital, of which only the first volume appears in Marx's lifetime (1867), analyses the commodity, value, the surplus value extorted from wage labour, accumulation and the laws of development of capitalism, which produce their own negation. The chapter on the fetishism of commodities shows relations between people taking the form of relations between things, and compares this inversion with the religious world, of which Christianity, the cult of abstract man, is the form suited to commodity society. Communist society, in which the associated producers regulate their exchanges in full consciousness, will no longer need this religious reflection.

Readings

Marxism has been read as a science of history by Engels and the Second International, as a humanism by the readers of the Manuscripts, and as an epistemological break by Althusser; Löwith saw in it a secularized eschatology, Ricoeur one of the three masters of suspicion, Aron and Voegelin a secular religion, Michel Henry a philosophy of life. Marx wrote little on religion after 1846; it is Engels who developed the historical study of Christianity.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the complete text of the opium passage, including the Protestation, the Theses on Feuerbach, fetishism and the cult of abstract man, the transparent society, and the readings of Löwith and Blumenberg. It judges that Marx dismissed the sacred without dismissing salvation, but that he remains right against the sages through the Protestation, and in its conclusion seeks to hold Spinoza and Marx together.

In the essay: Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels · Ricoeur · Karl Löwith · The sigh without a heaven

Further reading: Karl Marx, Zur Kritik der Hegelschen Rechtsphilosophie. Einleitung (1844) ; Ökonomisch-philosophische Manuskripte (1844) ; Marx and Engels, Die deutsche Ideologie (1845-1846) ; Manifest der Kommunistischen Partei (1848) ; Das Kapital, Book I (1867) ; Michel Henry, Marx (1976) ; Gareth Stedman Jones, Karl Marx. Greatness and Illusion (2016)

Engels: dialectical materialism, the history of early Christianity, the reckoning with FeuerbachTheories of the authors

Friedrich Engels (1820-1895), the son of a Pietist industrialist from Barmen, in the Rhineland, self-taught, a partner in the family firm in Manchester, was Marx's companion for forty years, his financial support, co-author of the Manifesto and of The German Ideology, editor of volumes II and III of Capital and, after 1883, the intellectual guide of European social democracy. On religion, he wrote more than Marx.

The young Engels and lost faith

Brought up in the Pietist revival of the Wupper valley, Engels lost his faith around 1839 through reading Strauss, then Hegel and Feuerbach, and recounted this crisis in his letters to the Graeber brothers. His Letters from Wuppertal describe the poverty of the workers and the hypocrisy of the pious manufacturers. The Condition of the Working Class in England (1845), the fruit of his stay in Manchester from 1842 to 1844, followed shortly after his decisive meeting with Marx in Paris, in August 1844.

Materialism and history

Anti-Dühring (1878), a refutation of a Berlin professor, became the manual of Marxism: it sets out the dialectics of nature, the three laws, the passage from quantity to quality, the interpenetration of opposites, the negation of the negation, and the materialist conception of history, then socialism become a science; its extract, Socialism: Utopian and Scientific, was the most widely circulated Marxist book. The Origin of the Family, Private Property and the State (1884), following Morgan, recounts the passage from the society of the gens to the State. Ludwig Feuerbach and the End of Classical German Philosophy (1886) takes stock of the Hegelian left: the fundamental question of philosophy is the relation of thought to being; Feuerbach restored materialism but remained an idealist in morality and religion, seeking to complete religion with a cult of love instead of abolishing it; the 1888 pamphlet edition publishes Marx's Theses on Feuerbach as an appendix.

The history of early Christianity

Engels wrote three studies of Christianity: The Peasant War in Germany (1850), which reads Thomas Müntzer's revolt of 1525 as a class struggle in religious garb; Bruno Bauer and Early Christianity (1882), which salutes the radical critic while placing the birth of Christianity in the crisis of the Roman Empire; and On the History of Early Christianity (1894-1895), which compares the first Christians to the modern workers' movement: a religion of slaves, freedmen, the poor and subject peoples, promising a salvation which, failing to be earthly, was placed in the beyond, and of which the Apocalypse of John, dated to 68-69, is the oldest document. Engels does not doubt that Jesus existed, and he sees in Christianity a revolutionary movement led astray.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the 1886 critique of Feuerbach, which supplies it with the distinction between soft refoundation and strict refoundation, with the 1894 text on Christianity as a movement of the oppressed, which it cites in connection with Paul and emancipation, and with the 1882 article on Bauer. It takes note that revolutionary emancipation inherits, according to Löwith and Blumenberg, the function of religion, and that Engels, like Nietzsche, took the existence of Jesus for granted.

In the essay: Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels · Paul of Tarsus · Ludwig Feuerbach

Further reading: Friedrich Engels, Der deutsche Bauernkrieg (1850) ; Anti-Dühring (1878) ; Der Ursprung der Familie, des Privateigentums und des Staats (1884) ; Ludwig Feuerbach und der Ausgang der klassischen deutschen Philosophie (1886) ; Zur Geschichte des Urchristentums (1894-1895) ; Tristram Hunt, The Frock-Coated Communist (2009) ; Georges Labica, Le Marxisme-léninisme (1984)

Kierkegaard's thought: the stages, anxiety, despair, Abraham, the attack on ChristendomTheories of the authors

Søren Aabye Kierkegaard (1813-1855), the son of a melancholy and pious Copenhagen merchant, a student of theology and the author of a dissertation on irony, broke off his engagement to Regine Olsen in 1841 and devoted the fourteen years left to him to a torrential body of work, published partly under pseudonyms and partly under his own name, which attacks Hegel, established Christendom and the forgetting of what it means to exist.

The pseudonyms and the stages

Either/Or (1843), by Victor Eremita, sets the aesthetic stage, that of the seducer who lives in the instant and in the possible, against the ethical stage, that of the husband and of Judge Wilhelm, who chooses and commits himself. Fear and Trembling (1843), by Johannes de Silentio, meditates on the sacrifice of Abraham: the knight of faith suspends the ethical by a leap that no one can understand or justify, and gets Isaac back by virtue of the absurd; this is the religious stage, higher than the ethical and incommensurable with it. The Concept of Anxiety (1844), by Vigilius Haufniensis, describes anxiety as the dizziness of freedom before the possible, bound up with sin. The Philosophical Fragments (1844) and the Concluding Unscientific Postscript (1846), by Johannes Climacus, contrast Socrates, for whom the truth lies within man, with Christ, for whom it comes from outside in the instant, and posit that subjectivity is truth: faith is the infinite passion of inwardness holding fast to an objective uncertainty, and Hegel's system, which claims to complete existence in the concept, forgets that the existing individual is in becoming. Stages on Life's Way (1845) takes up the triad again. The Sickness unto Death (1849), by Anti-Climacus, analyses despair as the sickness of the self that does not will to be itself, or wills to be itself without God, and in which sin is the contrary not of virtue but of faith.

The discourses and the attack

In parallel, Kierkegaard published under his own name Upbuilding Discourses and religious works, Works of Love (1847), Practice in Christianity (1850). In 1854-1855, after the death of Bishop Mynster, who had been praised as a witness to the truth, he launched in the press, then in his own sheet, The Moment, a frontal attack on the Danish state Church: Christendom, where everyone is Christian by birth, has abolished Christianity by making it flourish; the New Testament demands suffering and a break with the world, and the civil-servant pastors make a comfortable living from it. He died in the midst of the polemic, refusing the sacraments from a priest.

Legacy

Widely translated into German at the beginning of the twentieth century, Kierkegaard nourished the dialectical theology of Barth, who borrowed from him the infinite qualitative difference, the existentialism of Jaspers, Heidegger and Sartre, and the critique of Camus, who sees in his leap a philosophical suicide. He is also, with Pascal, the source of the critique of religion in the name of faith.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the stages, the leap, the thesis of subjectivity, the Postscript and The Moment. It sees in Kierkegaard a reversal of Stirner, established religion as a humanism in disguise, an exit from above after Pascal and before Barth, Bonhoeffer and Weil, and objects that he provides no internal criterion between God and the idol: a limit case.

In the essay: Søren Kierkegaard · Barth · Camus

Further reading: Søren Kierkegaard, Enten-Eller (1843) ; Frygt og Bæven (1843) ; Begrebet Angest (1844) ; Philosophiske Smuler (1844) ; Afsluttende uvidenskabelig Efterskrift (1846) ; Sygdommen til Døden (1849) ; Øieblikket (1855) ; Jean Wahl, Études kierkegaardiennes (1938) ; Joakim Garff, Søren Kierkegaard. Biographie (2000)

The thought of Comte: law of three stages, sociology, religion of HumanityTheories of the authors

Auguste Comte (1798-1857), a student of the École polytechnique expelled in 1816, secretary to Saint-Simon, from whom he parted in 1824, examiner and then tutor at the École polytechnique, removed from the first post in 1844 and from the second in 1851, then lives on subscriptions from his disciples. He invented the word sociology, the word altruism, positivism itself, and founded a religion of which he was the high priest.

The Cours and the law of three stages

The Cours de philosophie positive, sixty lectures published from 1830 to 1842, starts from the law of three stages: each branch of knowledge passes through a theological stage, fetishist, polytheist and then monotheist, in which things are explained by supernatural wills; a metaphysical stage, in which they are explained by abstract entities; and a positive stage, in which causes are given up in favour of seeking the laws of phenomena through observation and reasoning. The sciences have followed one another in this order according to their increasing complexity, mathematics, astronomy, physics, chemistry, biology, and the last, social physics or sociology, which Comte founds in the final volumes, must in its turn become positive. Sociology comprises a statics, order, and a dynamics, progress. Positivism rejects metaphysics as it rejects theology, but it is not a materialism: it ranks the sciences, defends the unity of knowledge and subordinates science to its social end, to foresee in order to be able to act.

The subjective turn and the religion of Humanity

In 1844 Comte meets Clotilde de Vaux, who dies in 1846; from this year of unrequited passion dates the second career, in which feeling takes precedence over intelligence. The System of Positive Polity (1851-1854) and the Catechism of Positive Religion (1852), conversations between a woman and a priest of Humanity, found the positive religion: its object is the Great Being, Humanity, made up of the dead, the living and those yet to be born; its motto, Love as principle, Order as basis, Progress as end; its worship, private, domestic and public, includes sacraments, nine of them from presentation to incorporation, a calendar of thirteen months dedicated to great men, festivals and temples; its clergy, positivist philosophers with Comte as high priest; its morality, altruism, living for others. Religion is defined as what regulates the individual and rallies human beings together. Littré and the scientific positivists then part from Comte, while the religious disciples, Laffitte and later the Brazilian positivists, who inscribe Ordem e Progresso on their flag, carry on the cult.

Posterity

Positivism shaped Taine, Renan, Durkheim, who keeps its sociology and rejects its religion, the Third Republic and its schoolteachers, John Stuart Mill, who admired and then fought it, and logical positivism, which kept only its name. Löwith sees in it a history of salvation in three ages; Hayek, a source of scientism.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes the reader knows the law of three stages, the classification of the sciences, the subjective turn, the content of the Catechism and the formula about destroying only what one replaces. It takes Comte to be the avowed strict refoundation, the proof by fact of Stirner's law, the believing version of the Grand Inquisitor, the example of an exit from religion that refounds one with all its parts, and it reports Durkheim's attested criticism of artificial cults.

In the essay: Auguste Comte · Karl Löwith

Further reading: Auguste Comte, Cours de philosophie positive (1830-1842) ; Discours sur l'esprit positif (1844) ; Système de politique positive (1851-1854) ; Catéchisme positiviste (1852) ; Henri Gouhier, La Jeunesse d'Auguste Comte et la formation du positivisme (1933-1941) ; Mary Pickering, Auguste Comte. An Intellectual Biography (1993-2009) ; Michel Bourdeau, Les Trois États. Science, théologie et métaphysique chez Auguste Comte (2006)

Schopenhauer's philosophy: the Will, suffering, compassion, the denial of the willTheories of the authors

Arthur Schopenhauer (1788-1860), son of a Danzig merchant and of a novelist, who took his doctorate at Jena, living on private means in Frankfurt for nearly thirty years after the failure of his lectures in Berlin, where he had scheduled them at the same hours as Hegel's, knew fame only after 1851. He built a unique system, pessimistic and atheist, nourished by Kant, Plato and the Upanishads, which shaped Wagner, Nietzsche, Freud, Thomas Mann and Wittgenstein.

The world as representation and as will

The World as Will and Representation (1819, enlarged in 1844) starts from Kant: the world is my representation, subject to the forms of the subject, space, time, causality, which Schopenhauer reduces to the principle of sufficient reason, studied in his dissertation. But the thing in itself, which Kant declared unknowable, is accessible to us by a path Kant did not see: our own body, which we know from within as will. The thing in itself is the Will, a blind willing, one, without reason and without end, which objectifies itself at every level of nature, in gravity as in the animal and in man, where it becomes the conscious will to live; the world of individuals is the veil of Maya, the illusion of multiplicity. Since all willing arises from a lack and every satisfaction opens another, life swings between suffering and boredom: this is pessimism, which Schopenhauer supports with history, biology and the observation of human beings. Art, and first of all music, the direct expression of the Will, temporarily suspends willing in the contemplation of the Ideas.

Morality and the denial of the will

On the Basis of Morality (1840) rejects Kant's imperative as a disguised theology and grounds morality in compassion, Mitleid, through which the individual recognizes in the suffering of another his own essence, through the veil of individuation. Final deliverance is the denial of the will to live, which Schopenhauer describes in the fourth book in the words of the Christian mystics, Eckhart, Madame Guyon, and of the sages of India: asceticism, chastity, poverty, resignation, up to nirvana, where this very real world, with all its suns, is nothing. He holds authentic Christianity, pessimistic and ascetic, to be superior to optimistic Judaism and Protestantism, and Buddhism to be the truest religion, because it is without God.

The Parerga and religion

The Parerga and Paralipomena (1851), the collection of essays that made his fame, contain the Aphorisms on the Wisdom of Life and the dialogue On Religion, between Demopheles, who defends religion as the metaphysics of the people, the allegorical garment of a truth the crowd cannot receive naked, and Philalethes, who demands the unveiled truth and denounces the crimes of religions; the dialogue reaches no conclusion. Schopenhauer professes a serene atheism, without a substitute cult, and a misogyny his readers have not forgotten.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the doctrine of the Will, pessimism, compassion, the denial of the will to live and the dialogue of Demopheles and Philalethes, a Grand Inquisitor without the tragedy. It judges that Schopenhauer left God without leaving the religion of salvation, credits him with being the first to read India and the mystics outside theology, and follows the attested break of Nietzsche, for whom pity is the practice of nihilism.

In the essay: Arthur Schopenhauer · Nietzsche · The Buddhism of the philosophers · Meister Eckhart

Further reading: Arthur Schopenhauer, Die Welt als Wille und Vorstellung (1819, 1844), trans. Auguste Burdeau (1888) and Christian Sommer (2009) ; Über die Grundlage der Moral (1840) ; Parerga und Paralipomena (1851) ; Clément Rosset, Schopenhauer, philosophe de l'absurde (1967) ; Rüdiger Safranski, Schopenhauer et les années folles de la philosophie (1987) ; Christopher Janaway, Schopenhauer (1994)

Dostoevsky's work: the Karamazovs, Demons, polyphonyTheories of the authors

Fyodor Mikhailovich Dostoevsky (1821-1881), the son of a Moscow army doctor, trained as an engineer and famous as a novelist from Poor Folk (1846) onwards, was sentenced to death in 1849 for his part in the Petrashevsky circle, reprieved before the firing squad and sent to four years of hard labour in Siberia. He came back converted to Orthodox Christianity and to the Russian people, and in twenty years wrote the novels that made him, with Tolstoy, one of the two greatest Russian novelists and, for philosophers, a thinker.

The great novels

Notes from Underground (1864) invents the voice of the underground man, who out of caprice rejects reason and the crystal palace of progress. Crime and Punishment (1866) follows Raskolnikov, the student who kills a moneylender to prove that he belongs among the extraordinary men to whom everything is permitted, and whom conscience brings back to confession. The Idiot (1868-1869) brings into Petersburg society Prince Myshkin, a perfectly good, Christlike man, who fails. Demons, long translated as The Possessed (1871-1872), a political novel inspired by the Nechayev affair, stages Stavrogin, the empty seducer, Pyotr Verkhovensky, the nihilist conspirator, Shatov, who believes in Russia without believing in God, and Kirillov, the engineer who wants to kill himself to prove that man is god. The Brothers Karamazov (1879-1880), completed a few months before his death, is the novel of a parricide: Fyodor Pavlovich, the ignoble father, and his sons, Dmitri the passionate, Ivan the atheist intellectual, Alyosha the novice, and the bastard Smerdyakov, who kills the father by taking Ivan at his word. Book V contains Ivan's rebellion, in which he returns his ticket to God in the name of tortured children, and the legend of the Grand Inquisitor; Book VI, the life and conversations of the elder Zosima, Alyosha's master, an answer to Ivan by example, not by argument.

Polyphony and ideas

In 1929 Mikhail Bakhtin described the Dostoevskian novel as polyphonic: its characters are full consciousnesses whose voices are not subordinated to the author's, and ideas live in it like characters, tested to the very end. This is why the atheism of Ivan and Kirillov is as strong in it as Zosima's faith, and why the novel could be read by believers and unbelievers alike. Dostoevsky knew Feuerbach and Strauss through Belinsky, read Renan, and in A Writer's Diary (1873-1881) waged the polemics of a Slavophile publicist, nationalist, at times antisemitic, hostile to the Catholic and socialist West, which seemed to him to promise the anthill. His Pushkin speech of 1880 was his triumph.

The readers

Nietzsche discovered him in 1887, in French translations, and regarded him as the only psychologist from whom he had learned anything; Freud devoted Dostoevsky and Parricide to him in 1928; Shestov, Berdyaev, Gide, Camus, who adapted The Possessed for the stage and discussed Kirillov in The Myth of Sisyphus and Ivan in The Rebel, Bakhtin, and René Girard, whose second book is Dostoïevski, du double à l'unité, made him a philosopher in spite of himself.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the structure of the Karamazovs, the characters of Ivan, Alyosha, Zosima and Smerdyakov, the figure of Kirillov in Demons, polyphony as Bakhtin understands it, the indirect reception of Feuerbach and Strauss, and the Diary. It makes the Grand Inquisitor the centre of gravity of its question and its touchstone, points out that the formula everything is permitted never appears as such, and follows the readings of Nietzsche, Camus and Freud.

In the essay: Dostoevsky · Camus · Sartre · A constructed debate

Further reading: Fyodor Dostoevsky, Les Frères Karamazov (1879-1880), trans. Henri Mongault (1952) and André Markowicz (2002) ; Les Démons (1871-1872) ; Journal d'un écrivain (1873-1881) ; Mikhail Bakhtin, Problèmes de la poétique de Dostoïevski (1929, 1963) ; Joseph Frank, Dostoevsky (1976-2002) ; Jacques Catteau, La Création littéraire chez Dostoïevski (1978)

The philosophy of Nietzsche: tragedy, genealogy, will to power, eternal return, overmanTheories of the authors

Friedrich Nietzsche (1844-1900), son of a Lutheran pastor from Röcken, in Prussian Saxony, a prodigiously gifted philologist appointed professor at Basel at twenty-four, the friend and then the adversary of Wagner, ill, pensioned off from 1879, wandering between the Engadine, Nice and Turin, sinks into madness in January 1889 and survives eleven years in the care of his mother and then of his sister, who will fabricate The Will to Power. His work, aphoristic and polemical, called morality, truth and religion into question at their root.

From tragedy to genealogy

The Birth of Tragedy (1872) opposes in Greek art the Apollonian, form and measure, to the Dionysian, intoxication and dissolution, and accuses Socrates and rationalism of having killed tragedy; the book ruins his career as a philologist. The Untimely Meditations (1873-1876) attack Strauss and history, and celebrate Schopenhauer and Wagner. The so-called positivist period, Human, All Too Human (1878), Daybreak (1881), The Gay Science (1882), practises a demystifying psychology of ideals; The Gay Science announces the death of God through the mouth of the madman and introduces amor fati. Thus Spoke Zarathustra (1883-1885), a prose poem, preaches the overman, the meaning of the earth, the death of God, the will to power and the eternal return of the same, an abysmal thought which demands that one will each instant as if it were to return eternally. Beyond Good and Evil (1886) and On the Genealogy of Morality (1887) analyse the origin of values: the morality of masters, which says good and bad, was overturned by the morality of slaves, which says good and evil out of ressentiment; bad conscience is born of instinct turned against oneself; the ascetic ideal gives a meaning to suffering, and science is its last form. The writings of 1888, Twilight of the Idols, The Antichrist, Ecce Homo, radicalize the attack on Christianity, a religion of pity and ressentiment, invented by Paul, and oppose Dionysus to the Crucified.

The concepts

The will to power is not the thirst for domination but the ground of every living thing, which wants to grow, to interpret, to impose forms on itself; truth is an interpretation useful to a type of life, and science still rests on a faith. Nihilism is the state in which the highest values devalue themselves, a consequence of the death of God, which Nietzsche wants to pass through by a revaluation of all values. The overman is the man who creates his values without a world behind the world; the eternal return, the ordeal that selects him; amor fati, his attitude towards fate. Nietzsche refuses to be a founder of religion and wants no believers.

The readings

Falsified by his sister and annexed by Nazism, Nietzsche was restored by the Colli-Montinari edition. Heidegger makes him the last metaphysician, who completes Platonism by overturning it; Löwith, Jaspers, Bataille, Deleuze, Foucault and Girard have proposed opposing readings of him; Camus sees in amor fati the moment when revolt turns into total consent.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with § 108, 125, 276 and 344 of The Gay Science, the third essay of the Genealogy, The Antichrist on Jesus and Paul, the overman, the eternal return and Heidegger's readings. It makes Nietzsche an exception of attitude, unstable, who escapes Stirner's law because he knows where his fire comes from, and reports his attested responses to Schopenhauer, Strauss, Kant and Renan, as well as his constructed confrontations with Dostoevsky.

In the essay: Nietzsche · Heidegger · Marcel Gauchet · Weber

Further reading: Friedrich Nietzsche, Die Geburt der Tragödie (1872) ; Die fröhliche Wissenschaft (1882, 1887) ; Also sprach Zarathustra (1883-1885) ; Jenseits von Gut und Böse (1886) ; Zur Genealogie der Moral (1887) ; Der Antichrist and Ecce homo (1888, publ. 1895 and 1908) ; Karl Löwith, Nietzsche. Philosophie de l'éternel retour du même (1935) ; Gilles Deleuze, Nietzsche et la philosophie (1962) ; Patrick Wotling, Nietzsche et le problème de la civilisation (1995)

Durkheim's sociology: the social fact, anomie, the Elementary FormsTheories of the authors

Émile Durkheim (1858-1917), the son of a rabbi from Épinal, a graduate of the École normale supérieure who passed the agrégation in philosophy, professor at Bordeaux and then at the Sorbonne, founder of L'Année sociologique (1898) and of the French school of sociology, sought to make sociology an autonomous science, with its own object and method, and to give the Third Republic a secular morality. His last book is devoted to religion.

The method and the first books

The Rules of Sociological Method (1895) defines the social fact: ways of acting, thinking and feeling that are external to the individual and endowed with a power of coercion; they must be treated as things, and the social must be explained by the social, not by psychology. The Division of Labour in Society (1893) distinguishes the mechanical solidarity of traditional societies, founded on likeness and a strong collective consciousness, from the organic solidarity of modern societies, founded on interdependence; the passage from one to the other produces pathological forms, among them anomie, the absence of rules.
