Suicide (1897), the first great statistical study, shows that the suicide rate varies with the integration and regulation of groups, and distinguishes egoistic, altruistic, anomic and fatalistic suicide: Protestants kill themselves more than Catholics, the unmarried more than the married. Durkheim took part in the Dreyfus affair, theorized secular morality and education, and conceived collective representations as a reality in their own right.

The Elementary Forms

The Elementary Forms of Religious Life (1912) studies the totemism of the Australian Aborigines, taken to be the simplest religion, in order to reach religion in general. Religion is defined there as a system of beliefs and practices relating to sacred things, set apart and forbidden, which unite into one Church all those who adhere to them; the sacred, opposed to the profane, is the fundamental category. The totem is the emblem of the clan, and the force it represents is society itself, experienced in the effervescence of gatherings and projected onto a symbol; rites, negative and positive, periodically remake the group. The categories of thought, time, space, genus, cause, have a social and religious origin. There are therefore no false religions, and religion will not die, since society will always need to think and celebrate itself; new ideals will be born from hours of creative effervescence. The French Revolution, which made the Fatherland and Reason sacred, is the example.

Legacy

The Durkheimian school, Mauss, Hubert, Halbwachs, Granet, dominated the French social sciences; Lévi-Strauss, Bourdieu, Parsons, Goffman, Mary Douglas and Hervieu-Léger are its heirs. Criticism has targeted the choice of totemism, the reduction of religion to the social, contested by Bergson, Malinowski and Evans-Pritchard, and the supposed conservatism of a sociology of order.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the definition of religion by the sacred and the Church, the thesis of the god as hypostasized society, effervescence, the critique of Comte's artificial cults and the pages on the Revolution. It regards Durkheim as one of the two strongest objections to its thesis, one does not leave the sacred, one changes it, and reports his attested dialogues with James and Bergson.

In the essay: Durkheim · Bergson · William James · Why do people believe? A constructed debate

Further reading: Émile Durkheim, De la division du travail social (1893) ; Les Règles de la méthode sociologique (1895) ; Le Suicide (1897) ; Les Formes élémentaires de la vie religieuse (1912) ; Steven Lukes, Émile Durkheim. His Life and Work (1973) ; Marcel Fournier, Émile Durkheim (2007) ; Raymond Aron, Les Étapes de la pensée sociologique (1967)

Weber's sociology: ideal types, rationalization, charisma, value neutralityTheories of the authors

Max Weber (1864-1920), a jurist and economist from Erfurt, professor at Freiburg and Heidelberg, his career interrupted by a depression from 1898 to 1903, then a private scholar, editor of the Archiv für Sozialwissenschaft, a participant in German political life and in the Weimar Constitution, is, with Durkheim, the founder of modern sociology. His work, unfinished and scattered, deals with Western rationality and its religious origins.

The method

Weber founds an interpretive sociology: the object of the social sciences is meaningful human action, which must be understood in order to be explained. Its instrument is the ideal type, a construction that accentuates certain features of reality, capitalism, bureaucracy, charisma, to serve as a yardstick for concrete cases, without claiming to describe them. The scholar must observe value neutrality, Wertfreiheit: he may study values, not prescribe them from his lectern, for no science can decide between the gods. Economy and Society, a posthumous work, systematizes these concepts: the types of action, instrumentally rational, value-rational, affective, traditional; the types of legitimate domination, legal, traditional, charismatic; the forms of community, of law, of the city, of religion.

Religion and rationalization

The Protestant Ethic and the Spirit of Capitalism (1904-1905) shows how the inner-worldly asceticism of the Puritans, fed by the anxiety about salvation that Calvinist predestination created, produced a methodical conduct of life, work as a calling, Beruf, and accumulation without enjoyment, an elective affinity with the spirit of capitalism, which then lost its religious mainspring to become a casing hard as steel. The studies of Confucianism, Hinduism, Buddhism and ancient Judaism ask why economic and scientific rationalization took place only in the West, and build a comparative sociology of religions: ethical and exemplary prophecy, religions of virtuosos and of the masses, theodicies of suffering, ascetic and mystical paths of salvation, flight from the world or mastery of the world. The guiding thread is rationalization, a long-term process that disenchants the world, Entzauberung, by eliminating magic, begun by the prophets of Israel and completed by science. Charisma, the extraordinary gift of the prophet or the leader, is the revolutionary force that breaks routine, before becoming routinized in its turn.

The two lectures

Science as a Vocation, delivered in 1917 and published in 1919, and Politics as a Vocation (1919) draw the consequences: the disenchanted world no longer knows prophets, science gives no meaning, ultimate values are at war, polytheism, and each must choose his god; the politician must hold together the ethic of conviction and the ethic of responsibility. Weber dies in June 1920 of pneumonia following the Spanish flu.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes that the ideal type, value neutrality, the thesis of the Protestant Ethic, the casing hard as steel, rationalization and disenchantment, the polytheism of values and the sacrifice of the intellect are known. It makes Weber the second representative of the regime of social function, confronts him with Durkheim on the return of the gods, notes that he had read Nietzsche and that the convergence with the Genealogy is not fortuitous, and wonders whether he is not the last Puritan.

In the essay: Weber · Norris and Inglehart · Marcel Gauchet · Assessment of Part Eight

Further reading: Max Weber, Die protestantische Ethik und der Geist des Kapitalismus (1904-1905, 1920) ; Wissenschaft als Beruf and Politik als Beruf (1919) ; Wirtschaft und Gesellschaft (posth., 1921-1922) ; Gesammelte Aufsätze zur Religionssoziologie (1920-1921) ; Raymond Aron, La Sociologie allemande contemporaine (1935) ; Catherine Colliot-Thélène, Max Weber et l'histoire (1990) ; Joachim Radkau, Max Weber (2005)

The thought of William James: pragmatism, psychology, religious experienceTheories of the authors

William James (1842-1910), son of a wealthy Swedenborgian theologian and elder brother of the novelist Henry James, trained as a physician, professor of physiology, of psychology and then of philosophy at Harvard, who emerged from a severe depressive crisis through a decision to believe in free will, is the founder of American psychology and, with Peirce and Dewey, of pragmatism.

Psychology

The Principles of Psychology (1890), twelve years in the making, describe mental life as a stream of consciousness, continuous and selective, and not as an association of ideas; they put forward the theory of the emotions that bears his name, according to which we are sad because we cry; they analyse habit, attention, the self and the will. In them James refuses to reduce the mind to the brain while describing their correlations, and leaves the metaphysical question open.

Pragmatism and radical empiricism

Pragmatism, a name borrowed from Peirce and set out in the lectures of 1907, is first a method: the meaning of an idea lies in its practical consequences, and two ideas that have no different consequences are the same idea. It is then a theory of truth: an idea is true if it works, if it leads us successfully through experience; truth happens to ideas, it is a process of verification. Its adversaries, Russell and Moore, saw in this a confusion of truth with utility; James replied that he was describing the way truths are actually accepted. Radical empiricism, set out in posthumous essays, holds that relations are given in experience as much as the terms they relate, and that pure experience is prior to the distinction between subject and object; Bergson, with whom James corresponded, and Whitehead are close to it. A Pluralistic Universe (1909) defends an unfinished, open world against the absolute monism of Bradley and Royce.

Religion

The Will to Believe, a lecture of 1896 collected in a volume in 1897, answers Clifford: when an option is living, forced and momentous and reason cannot decide it, we have the right to let our passional nature decide, at our own risk. The Varieties of Religious Experience, the Gifford Lectures given in Edinburgh in 1901-1902, ground the psychology of religion on personal documents, conversions, saints, mystics, defining religion as the feelings, acts and experiences of individuals in their solitude, in relation to whatever they consider divine. James rejects medical materialism, which explains an experience by its pathological origin, judges religions by their fruits, distinguishes healthy-minded souls from sick souls, describes mysticism by four marks, ineffability, noetic quality, transiency, passivity, and concludes, in his own name, with an over-belief: religious experience puts us in contact with something wider, of which the subconscious is the door, and God is real since he produces real effects. He was also interested in psychical research and in mediums.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known pragmatism, the individual definition of religion, set against Durkheim's in an attested dialogue, medical materialism, fruits and roots, the two types of soul, the four marks of mysticism and The Will to Believe. It makes James the representative of the regime of experience and the ally of its thesis in the order of belief, not an exception, and combines his answer to Clifford with Clifford's own demand.

In the essay: William James · Do we have the right to believe? Clifford, James and analytic philosophy · André Comte-Sponville

Further reading: William James, The Principles of Psychology (1890) ; The Will to Believe and Other Essays (1897) ; The Varieties of Religious Experience (1902) ; Pragmatism (1907) ; Essays in Radical Empiricism (posth., 1912) ; Ralph Barton Perry, The Thought and Character of William James (1935) ; Stéphane Madelrieux, William James. L'attitude empiriste (2008)

Rudolf Otto and The Idea of the Holy: the numinous, the wholly other, comparative mysticismTheories of the authors

Rudolf Otto (1869-1937), Lutheran theologian, professor at Göttingen, Breslau and then Marburg, traveller in North Africa, India, China and Japan, liberal deputy in the Prussian parliament, wrote in 1917 one of the most widely read books of twentieth-century theology, Das Heilige, which gave the phenomenology of religion its central concept, the numinous.

The Idea of the Holy

The book starts from an observation: theology and the philosophy of religion have treated the divine through rational concepts, goodness, omnipotence, personality, and have missed what makes up the proper object of religion, an irrational element, in the sense of non-conceptual, which Otto calls the numinous, from the Latin numen, to distinguish it from the holy, already charged with moral meaning. The numinous is not defined, it is awakened: Otto invites the reader who has never felt a religious emotion not to read on. It is described through its moments. The Kreaturgefühl, creature-feeling, is the echo in the subject of a reality that surpasses it, and Otto thereby corrects Schleiermacher: dependence is not primary, it presupposes the object. The mysterium tremendum combines awe, majesty and energy; the mysterium is the wholly other, ganz Andere, which fits into no category; the fascinans attracts, enraptures and promises bliss. The numinous is an a priori category of the mind, as in Kant and in Jakob Fries, whose philosophy of religion Otto follows; it is schematized in history through rational concepts, and Christianity, in which the numinous is fully moralized without being lost, is for Otto the most accomplished religion. The book follows the manifestation of the numinous in the Old Testament, the Gospels, Luther, the mystics, art and music.

Comparative mysticism and India

In Marburg Otto founds a collection of world religions and a Religious League of Mankind. Mysticism East and West (1926) compares Eckhart and Shankara, the master of non-dualist Vedanta, to show common structures and fundamental differences; India's Religion of Grace and Christianity (1930) brings Vaishnava bhakti close to Lutheranism. These works make him, with Söderblom and Heiler, a founder of the comparative history of religions on a phenomenological basis, which Van der Leeuw and Eliade will pursue.

Legacy

The numinous has passed into everyday vocabulary; Jung takes it up to define religion, Eliade for the sacred, Barth rejects it as a human experience, Levinas combats it as violence. Critics have pointed to the circularity of the method, which addresses only those who have already had the experience, and to the ranking of Christianity as the summit.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the numinous and its moments, the warning to the reader, the correction of Schleiermacher and the critique of James, which are attested responses, and the a priori category, from which it draws the conclusion that one cannot get out of the numinous but only let it lie fallow or cultivate it. It sees in Otto the paradoxical source of spirituality without a Church, places the oceanic feeling as a pure fascinans, and reports Levinas's response.

In the essay: Rudolf Otto · Levinas · Jung

Further reading: Rudolf Otto, Das Heilige (1917), trans. André Jundt, Le Sacré (1929) ; West-östliche Mystik (1926) ; Die Gnadenreligion Indiens und das Christentum (1930) ; Reich Gottes und Menschensohn (1934) ; Philip C. Almond, Rudolf Otto. An Introduction to His Philosophical Theology (1984) ; Todd A. Gooch, The Numinous and Modernity (2000)

Freud's psychoanalysis: the unconscious, Oedipus, Totem and Taboo, illusion, MosesTheories of the authors

Sigmund Freud (1856-1939), a Viennese neurologist of Jewish origin, a student of Charcot, inventor of psychoanalysis in the 1890s, head of an international movement from 1910, who was driven out by the Anschluss in 1938 and died in London, transformed the conception of the mind and gave the critique of religion the regime of illusion.

The discovery of the unconscious

The Studies on Hysteria (1895), with Breuer, then The Interpretation of Dreams (1900) establish the method and the theory: symptoms, dreams, slips of the tongue and bungled actions have a meaning, and this meaning points to unconscious wishes, repressed because they cannot be reconciled with moral conscience. Repression, the return of the repressed, transference and free association are the concepts of the cure. The Three Essays on the Theory of Sexuality (1905) describe an infantile sexuality; the Oedipus complex, sketched as early as 1900 and named in 1910, desire for the parent of the other sex and rivalry with the parent of the same sex, is resolved by the formation of the superego. The second topography (1923) distinguishes the id, reservoir of the drives, the ego and the superego; Beyond the Pleasure Principle (1920) introduces the death drive alongside the life drives.

Religion

Freud wrote on religion in every period of his life. Obsessive Actions and Religious Practices (1907) compares rites to the ceremonials of neurotics. Totem and Taboo (1913) grounds religion, morality and society in an original murder: the sons of the primal horde killed and ate the father who kept the women, then, seized by remorse, deified him as a totem and forbade themselves incest; the totemic meal is the first sacrifice, and Christianity, in which the Son atones, is its avowal. The Future of an Illusion (1927) explains belief by infantile helplessness and the need for a father's protection, defines illusion by the wish that commands it, makes religion the universal obsessional neurosis, and wagers on education to reality and on reason, our god Logos. Civilization and Its Discontents (1930) answers Romain Rolland on the oceanic feeling, which it traces back to primary narcissism, and describes culture as a renunciation of drives that is a source of guilt. Moses and Monotheism (1939) makes Moses an Egyptian killed by his people, whose monotheism would be the return of the repressed.

Dissent and readings

Jung breaks away in 1913 over libido and religion, Adler before him, Rank and Reich afterwards; Pfister, a pastor, answers Freud with The Illusion of a Future. Lacan, Ricoeur, who counts him among the masters of suspicion, Marcuse, Girard, who turns Totem and Taboo around, and the critics of the scientific status of psychoanalysis, Popper, Grünbaum, have carried the debate further.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the unconscious, repression, the Oedipus complex, Totem and Taboo, the definition of illusion, the universal obsessional neurosis, the god Logos and Rolland's letter. It holds Freud to be the representative of the regime of illusion, turns § 344 of The Gay Science against him and judges that he refounds a god, Logos, while granting both Freud and Rolland a share of the truth: religion without a father, but not without a mother.

In the essay: Freud · Jung · Girard · Ricoeur

Further reading: Sigmund Freud, Die Traumdeutung (1900) ; Totem und Tabu (1913) ; Die Zukunft einer Illusion (1927) ; Das Unbehagen in der Kultur (1930) ; Der Mann Moses und die monotheistische Religion (1939) ; Peter Gay, Freud. Une vie (1988) ; Paul Ricœur, De l'interprétation. Essai sur Freud (1965) ; Élisabeth Roudinesco, Sigmund Freud en son temps et dans le nôtre (2014)

Bergson's philosophy: duration, intuition, the élan vital, the Two SourcesTheories of the authors

Henri Bergson (1859-1941), a graduate of the École normale supérieure of Jewish origin, a lycée teacher and then professor at the Collège de France, where his lectures drew fashionable Paris, a member of the Académie française, Nobel laureate in literature in 1927 and a diplomat during the Great War, was the most famous philosopher of his time. Drawn towards Catholicism at the end of his life, he refused to convert so as to remain among the persecuted, and died in Paris under the Occupation.

Duration and memory

Time and Free Will (1889) distinguishes two orders: space, homogeneous and measurable, where intelligence and science cut up and count, and pure duration, the qualitative succession of interpenetrating states of consciousness, which is grasped by intuition. Clock time is space; freedom is the act that issues from the deep self in duration, and determinism rests on a confusion. Matter and Memory (1896) applies the distinction to the relation of soul and body: the brain is an organ of action, not a store of images; pure memory is distinct from habit memory, and the past is preserved in itself. Laughter (1900) analyses the comic as something mechanical encrusted on the living.

The élan vital

Creative Evolution (1907) extends duration to life: evolution is neither mechanistic nor finalist, it is the work of an élan vital, a creative impulse that passes through matter and divides into divergent lines, plants, instinct, intelligence. Intelligence is made to act on inert matter and to manufacture tools; it misses life and duration, which intuition, instinct become disinterested and conscious of itself, can grasp again. Metaphysics becomes possible again as the intuition of duration, and the book, which discusses Darwin, Spencer and Kant, criticizes the idea of nothingness as a pseudo-idea: nothing cannot be thought, and the question why there is something rather than nothing is a false problem. Bergson sees God in it as unceasing life, action, freedom.

The Two Sources

The Two Sources of Morality and Religion (1932), twenty-five years after Creative Evolution, distinguishes two sources. Closed morality is social pressure, obligation, which binds the individual to the group and the group against others; static religion, produced by the fabulating function, is a defensive reaction of nature against the dissolving power of intelligence and against the representation of death. Open morality is the call of heroes and saints, which aims at the whole of humanity; dynamic religion is mysticism, whose fulfilled examples are the great Christian mystics, Paul, Teresa, Joan of Arc: they rejoin the élan vital at its source, God is love and the object of love, and the universe is a machine for making gods. The closed society and the open society, and the supplement of soul that mechanism calls for, conclude the book, which answers Durkheim in the name of the second source and discusses Lévy-Bruhl, James and Delacroix.

Legacy

Bergsonism marked Péguy, Proust, Maritain, Jankélévitch, Merleau-Ponty, Deleuze, who reread it in the 1960s, and Popper, who borrows the open society from it. It was criticized by Russell, Politzer, Benda and Thomism, and placed on the Index in 1914.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with duration, intuition, the élan vital, the critique of nothingness, the fabulating function, the two moralities and the two religions, and the machine for making gods. It reports Bergson's attested response to Durkheim and his constructed responses to Freud and to Nietzsche, the superman is the mystic, and judges that Bergson escapes the divinization of man by a narrow margin, at the price of a metaphysics of life.

In the essay: Bergson · William James · Why do people believe? A constructed debate

Further reading: Henri Bergson, Essai sur les données immédiates de la conscience (1889) ; Matière et mémoire (1896) ; L'Évolution créatrice (1907) ; Les Deux Sources de la morale et de la religion (1932) ; Vladimir Jankélévitch, Henri Bergson (1931, 1959) ; Gilles Deleuze, Le Bergsonisme (1966) ; Frédéric Worms, Bergson ou les deux sens de la vie (2004)

Ricoeur's philosophy: symbol, suspicion, text, narrativeTheories of the authors

Paul Ricoeur (1913-2005), a war orphan brought up in Reformed Protestantism, agrégé in philosophy, a prisoner in Germany from 1940 to 1945, where he translated Husserl, professor at Strasbourg, at the Sorbonne, at Nanterre, of which he was dean in 1969, and at Chicago, built a body of work in which phenomenology passes through hermeneutics and through dialogue with the human sciences, psychoanalysis, linguistics, history, exegesis.

The will and the symbol

The Philosophy of the Will begins with a phenomenology of the voluntary and the involuntary (1950), then, in Finitude and Guilt (1960), approaches evil through man's fallibility and through The Symbolism of Evil: fault is expressed first neither in concepts nor in pure experiences, but in symbols, defilement, sin, guilt, and in myths, among them the Adamic myth; the symbol gives rise to thought, and philosophy must pass through the interpretation of symbols in order to recover, beyond criticism, a second naivety. This detour grounds the hermeneutic graft on phenomenology.

Suspicion and conflict

Freud and Philosophy: An Essay on Interpretation (1965) reads psychoanalysis as a hermeneutics of suspicion, which reduces the apparent meaning to the economy of the drives, and contrasts it with the hermeneutics of the restoration of meaning, that of Eliade's phenomenology of religion, which listens to the symbol; Marx, Nietzsche and Freud form the school of suspicion, masters who taught us to doubt consciousness, and Ricoeur holds that faith must pass through this critique instead of refusing it. The Conflict of Interpretations (1969), which includes Religion, Atheism, and Faith, adds that the idols must be allowed to die, the god of accusation and the god of consolation, in order to hear the symbols, and maintains that the rival hermeneutics, structuralism, psychoanalysis, phenomenology of religion, cannot be reduced to one another and that reflective philosophy must arbitrate between them without abolishing them. Ricoeur kept his philosophy separate from his faith, set out in articles of exegesis and biblical theology, while letting them answer each other.

The text, the narrative, the self

The Rule of Metaphor (1975) shows that metaphor creates meaning and redescribes reality; the theory of the text distinguishes the world of the text, opened up in front of it, from the intention of the author. Time and Narrative (1983-1985) maintains that human time becomes thinkable only when narrated, through emplotment, in history as in fiction, and that identity is narrative. Oneself as Another (1990) develops a hermeneutics of the self, between the exaltation of the cogito and its Nietzschean humiliation, and a little ethics: aiming at the good life, with and for others, in just institutions. Memory, History, Forgetting (2000) and The Course of Recognition (2004) complete the journey.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes for granted the school of suspicion, the distinction between the two hermeneutics, the second naivety, the symbol that gives rise to thought and the text Religion, Atheism, and Faith on the two gods rightly destroyed. It holds Ricoeur right on method and wrong about the object, the three regimes of suspicion remaining distinct, reports the objection that the second naivety might be merely a first naivety giving itself a clear conscience, and classes Ricoeur among the believing exceptions, prudent and unstable.

In the essay: Ricoeur · Jürgen Habermas · Freud

Further reading: Paul Ricœur, Philosophie de la volonté (1950-1960) ; De l'interprétation. Essai sur Freud (1965) ; Le Conflit des interprétations (1969) ; La Métaphore vive (1975) ; Temps et récit (1983-1985) ; Soi-même comme un autre (1990) ; François Dosse, Paul Ricœur. Les sens d'une vie (1997) ; Jean Greisch, Paul Ricœur. L'itinérance du sens (2001)

Tylor and Frazer: animism, survivals, The Golden BoughTheories of the authors

Edward Burnett Tylor (1832-1917) and James George Frazer (1854-1941) are the two great names of English evolutionist anthropology, which sought, in Darwin's wake, to write the natural history of religion on the basis of so-called primitive societies. Their books supplied two generations with the vocabulary of the critique of religion, before fieldwork refuted them.

Tylor and animism

The son of Quakers, without a university degree, a traveller in Mexico, Tylor published Primitive Culture in 1871, which opens with the first anthropological definition of culture, that complex whole which includes knowledge, belief, art, morals, law and custom. Culture evolves everywhere through the same stages, from savagery to civilization, and incomprehensible modern customs are explained as survivals of earlier states. Religion, defined minimally as belief in spiritual beings, arises from animism: the first humans, to explain dreams, fainting and death, conceived of a soul separable from the body, then attributed it to animals, plants and things, and from there came spirits, gods and finally the one God. This primitive philosophy is reasonable but false, and the science of culture, says the last page, is a reformer's science, which marks out harmful survivals for destruction. In 1896 Tylor obtained the first chair of anthropology at Oxford.

Frazer and The Golden Bough

A Cambridge Hellenist and editor of Pausanias, Frazer never did fieldwork. The Golden Bough, published in two volumes in 1890 and expanded to twelve by 1915, starts from a riddle: why did the priest of the grove at Nemi have to be killed by his successor? The answer mobilizes the whole of humanity: divine kings put to death before their strength declines, vegetation gods who die and are reborn, Adonis, Attis, Osiris, scapegoats laden with the ills of the community, taboos, fire festivals. Frazer orders this compilation according to a law of three stages: magic, which believes it can compel nature through similarity and contact, sympathetic magic, and which is the bastard sister of science; religion, which implores higher beings when magic has failed; science, which rediscovers the idea of law. The book, which places Christ among the dying gods in pages that the third edition relegates to an appendix, nourished the mythicists, Freud in Totem and Taboo, and literature, Eliot, Joyce, Lawrence. Frazer regarded his friend William Robertson Smith, who had placed communion sacrifice at the centre of Semitic religion, as his master.

The refutations

Durkheim objects that a universal, everyday illusion would not be one, and that religion is a social fact, not an individual error; Malinowski, that the succession of stages is not found in the field; Wittgenstein, in his Remarks on Frazer's Golden Bough, that Frazer is more savage than his savages, because he takes for mistaken theories rites that express; Evans-Pritchard sums up these criticisms in 1965. Anthropology has kept the definition of culture, the universality of belief in invisible beings, and a few themes taken up by Girard and by Descola.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with animism, survivals, the reformer's science, the sequence of magic, religion, science, and the scapegoats of The Golden Bough. It places Tylor and Frazer in the regime of error in its evolutionist form, notes that they explain the birth of beliefs and not their persistence, and reports the refutations by Durkheim, by Wittgenstein, an attested response, and by Malinowski.

In the essay: Tylor and Frazer · Wittgenstein · Boyer

Further reading: Edward B. Tylor, Primitive Culture (1871) ; James G. Frazer, The Golden Bough (1890, 3rd ed. in twelve volumes 1906-1915 ; abridged 1922) ; Robert Ackerman, J. G. Frazer. His Life and Work (1987) ; George W. Stocking, Victorian Anthropology (1987) ; E. E. Evans-Pritchard, Theories of Primitive Religion (1965)

Malinowski: fieldwork, functionalism, magic, science and religionTheories of the authors

Bronisław Malinowski (1884-1942), a Pole from Kraków, doctor in physics and philosophy, converted to anthropology by reading The Golden Bough, a student at the London School of Economics, is in New Guinea when the war of 1914 breaks out; an Austrian subject, he is allowed to remain and spends two years in the Trobriand Islands. He brings back from them a method, extended fieldwork, and a theory, functionalism, which founded British social anthropology.

Fieldwork and the Trobriands

Argonauts of the Western Pacific (1922) describes the kula, a circuit of ceremonial exchanges of necklaces and armbands between the islands, and formulates in its introduction the method of participant observation: living among the natives, speaking their language, recording the imponderabilia of everyday life, and grasping the native's point of view on his world. There follow Sex and Repression in Savage Society (1927), which disputes the universality of the Oedipus complex in a matrilineal society, The Sexual Life of Savages (1929), Coral Gardens and Their Magic (1935), on agriculture and magic, and essays on law and kinship. The private diary published in 1967, with its admissions of boredom and hostility, has troubled the image of the founder. Malinowski teaches in London from 1922 to 1938, trains Evans-Pritchard, Firth, Leach and Fortes, and dies at Yale.

Functionalism

Against the evolutionism of Tylor and Frazer, which classified customs as survivals of a reconstructed past, and against diffusionism, Malinowski holds that every institution must be understood through its function in the present culture, and that every culture answers, through its institutions, the biological and derived needs of human beings: food, reproduction, safety, and the needs for cohesion, education and meaning. Radcliffe-Brown will propose a structural version of it, based on the maintenance of the social structure rather than on individual needs.

Magic, science and religion

The essay of 1925 argues that the Trobrianders possess empirical and rational knowledge, a science, for everything that technique controls; magic comes in only where uncertainty and danger prevail, in deep-sea fishing and not in the lagoon, and it serves to ritualize optimism, to give confidence when knowledge is no longer enough. Religion, distinct from magic in that it is not a means to an end, answers the crises of life, and first of all death: funeral rites affirm the desire to live against decomposition and reintegrate the survivors; its source is individual before it is collective, against Durkheim. Myth (1926) is neither an explanation nor an allegory but a charter, myth as the charter of institutions, which founds and justifies rights, rites and rules by linking them to a sacred past.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the Trobriand fieldwork, the distinction between the lagoon and the open sea, the formula of ritualizing optimism, death as the source of religion, and myth as a charter. It regards Malinowski as solid, infers from him that belief recedes with uncertainty and not with knowledge, which Norris and Inglehart measure, and notes his responses to Tylor, to Frazer and to Durkheim.

In the essay: Malinowski

Further reading: Bronisław Malinowski, Argonauts of the Western Pacific (1922) ; Magic, Science and Religion (1925) ; Myth in Primitive Psychology (1926) ; A Diary in the Strict Sense of the Term (posth., 1967) ; Michael W. Young, Malinowski. Odyssey of an Anthropologist (2004) ; Raymond Firth (ed.), Man and Culture (1957)

Jung's psychology: archetypes, collective unconscious, individuation, Answer to JobTheories of the authors

Carl Gustav Jung (1875-1961), son of a Swiss Reformed pastor, psychiatrist at the Burghölzli asylum in Zurich, Freud's designated heir from 1907 to 1913, and after their break the founder of analytical psychology, built a depth psychology in which religion is no longer a neurosis to be cured but a function of the soul to be understood. His influence extends from psychotherapy to the history of religions and to mass culture.

The break with Freud

Wandlungen und Symbole der Libido (1912) widens Freud's libido into a general psychic energy and reads myths as expressions of the unconscious; Freud sees in it a desertion, and the break is consummated in 1913. Jung then passes through a crisis lasting several years, of which the Red Book, calligraphed and painted, published in 2009, preserves the trace, and from which his concepts emerge. Psychological Types (1921) distinguishes introversion and extraversion and four functions, thinking, feeling, sensation, intuition, from which personality tests derive.

Archetypes, collective unconscious, individuation

Beneath the personal unconscious, made of repressed contents, Jung posits a collective unconscious, inherited and common to the species, structured by archetypes, empty and universal forms filled by the images of each culture: the shadow, the anima and the animus, the great mother, the wise old man, the child, the hero, and the Self, centre and totality of the psyche, whose symbol is the mandala. The task of psychic life is individuation, the process by which the ego integrates the shadow and the other figures and enters into relation with the Self; the second half of life is devoted to it, and religions have been its traditional paths. Jung seeks evidence for these structures in his patients' dreams, in myths, in gnosis, in alchemy, which he reads as a projection of the process of individuation, in yoga and in the I Ching. Synchronicity, set out in 1952 in a volume shared with the physicist Pauli, posits meaningful coincidences without causal connection.

Religion

Psychology and Religion (1938), the Terry Lectures given at Yale, defines religion as the scrupulous observation of the numinosum, in the manner of Cicero and Otto, and asserts that the psyche has a natural religious function whose denial makes one ill. Jung refuses to make any statement about God in himself and speaks only of the image of God in the soul; asked on the BBC in 1959 whether he believes in God, he answers that he knows. Answer to Job (1952) reads the Bible as the drama of an unconscious God whom Job, through his moral consciousness, surpasses, and who must become incarnate in order to become conscious; the dogma of the Assumption, defined in 1950, seems to him the most important religious event since the Reformation. Buber reproaches him in 1952 with reducing God to the psyche and reviving gnosis. The article Wotan (1936) describes Nazism as the awakening of an archaic god in the German soul, and Jung's conduct in the 1930s remains debated.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the break with Freud, the archetypes, the Self, individuation, the definition of religion of 1938, the answer given on the BBC, Answer to Job and the controversy with Buber. It sides with Buber, judges that Jung makes religion necessary without making it true, Durkheim transposed to the psyche, and reproaches him with having believed that the religious disposition was in itself a wisdom.

In the essay: Jung · Freud · Rudolf Otto

Further reading: C. G. Jung, Wandlungen und Symbole der Libido (1912) ; Psychologische Typen (1921) ; Psychology and Religion (1938) ; Antwort auf Hiob (1952) ; Ma vie. Souvenirs, rêves et pensées (1962) ; Das rote Buch (publ. 2009) ; Deirdre Bair, Jung. A Biography (2003) ; Sonu Shamdasani, Jung and the Making of Modern Psychology (2003)

René Girard's mimetic theoryTheories of the authors

Mimetic theory is the overall hypothesis that René Girard (1923-2015) developed over half a century, from literary criticism to anthropology, then to the reading of the Bible and the theory of history. It starts from an observation about desire and claims to explain the origin of culture, religion and institutions. The essay retains only its anthropological level; this article presents the whole edifice.

Mimetic desire

The first book, Deceit, Desire and the Novel (1961), reads Cervantes, Stendhal, Flaubert, Proust and Dostoevsky. Their heroes do not desire spontaneously: they desire what a model desires, and the novel unmasks this mediation, which the romantic illusion of autonomy conceals. When the model is out of reach, mediation is external and peaceful; when he shares the subject's world, it is internal, and the model becomes a rival, an obstacle, then an obsession. Girard later extends the hypothesis to psychology, with the psychiatrist Jean-Michel Oughourlian, and to Shakespeare (A Theater of Envy, 1990).

The crisis and the surrogate victim

Violence and the Sacred (1972) carries mimetic desire over into stateless societies. Rivalry spreads by contagion, the differences that hold the community together are effaced, and reciprocal violence becomes a mimetic crisis. It is resolved by the spontaneous convergence of all against one, designated by some arbitrary sign: the collective murder of the scapegoat restores peace, and the community, unaware of what it has done, attributes the crisis and then the reconciliation to the victim, whom it divinizes. From this mechanism, according to Girard, derive the sacred, the sacrificial rite that repeats it, the prohibitions that forestall the crisis, the myths that recount it from the crowd's point of view, and even institutions: kingship would be a victim whose sacrifice is deferred, domestication and hunting forms of sacrifice. Things Hidden since the Foundation of the World (1978) makes the victimary mechanism the driving force of hominization itself.

The biblical revelation

The same book and The Scapegoat (1982) argue that the biblical texts, from Joseph to the Psalms and the suffering servant, and then the Gospels, recount the lynching from the victim's point of view and proclaim his innocence, unveiling what the myths conceal. Once unveiled, the mechanism loses its efficacy: Christian societies can no longer reconcile themselves over a victim in good conscience, hence the modern concern for victims, but also the loss of the brake that the sacred placed on violence. I See Satan Fall Like Lightning (1999) names Satan the principle of accusation; Battling to the End (2007) reads in the escalation to extremes of modern war an apocalypse without transcendence.

Extensions and criticisms

The theory has spread beyond its field: Jean-Pierre Dupuy and André Orléan apply it to economics (money, speculative bubbles as mimetic contagion), Dupuy to nuclear deterrence and catastrophe, theologians such as Raymund Schwager and James Alison to Christology, and neuroscientists have sought support for it in mirror neurons. An annual conference, the Colloquium on Violence and Religion, has been devoted to it since 1990. Anthropologists raise three objections: the hypothesis of the founding murder is unverifiable; sacrifice, in many societies, is a gift or a meal rather than a lynching; a theory that makes its own religion the key to all the others is open to the suspicion of circularity. Walter Burkert, in the same year 1972, had proposed a related origin of sacrifice in Palaeolithic hunting, without the revelation.

What the essay assumes

The essay sets out the theory in its section on Girard, connects it with Frazer, Freud, Durkheim and Nietzsche, and makes it the anthropological foundation of its sacrifice criterion, leaving aside the apologetics. It assumes familiarity with the vocabulary (mediation, double, undifferentiation, misrecognition) and with the breadth of the applications, from the novel to economics, which make mimetic theory a system and not a single thesis on religion.

In the essay: Girard · Part Nine · Assessment of the refoundations · What the exceptions have in common

Further reading: René Girard, Mensonge romantique et vérité romanesque (1961) ; La Violence et le sacré (1972) ; Des choses cachées depuis la fondation du monde (1978) ; Le Bouc émissaire (1982) ; Achever Clausewitz (2007) ; Jean-Pierre Dupuy, La Marque du sacré (2008)

Ernest Becker: the denial of death, the heroic system, the escape from evilTheories of the authors

Ernest Becker (1924-1974), an American cultural anthropologist, an unsettled teacher driven out of several universities for his independence, who died of cancer at forty-nine, two months before receiving the Pulitzer Prize for The Denial of Death, proposed a synthesis of psychoanalysis, Kierkegaard and anthropology in which the fear of death is the hidden mainspring of culture and religion. The essay reads him by paraphrase, not having consulted the original text.

The denial of death

The Denial of Death (1973) starts from a paradox: man is an animal that knows it will die, and this awareness, unbearable, must be repressed for life to be possible. Becker borrows from Otto Rank, Freud's dissident disciple, the idea that the fundamental anxiety is not sexual but existential, and from Kierkegaard the description of man as a synthesis of angel and worm, a spirit shut up in a body that rots. The repression of death works through the vital lie of character, an armour of defences that gives each person a sense of his own worth, and through the causa sui project, the effort to be one's own cause, to escape contingency and make oneself immortal. Culture supplies the means of this project: it is a heroic system, a set of roles, rites and narratives that allows each person to feel important, to take part in something greater than himself and to last symbolically beyond his death. Religion is the vastest of these systems, and Becker, a reader of Kierkegaard and Tillich, holds it to be the most honest answer, since it faces the creature in its finitude; psychoanalysis itself, romantic love and science are substitute heroic systems, often less lucid.

The escape from evil

Escape from Evil (1975), completed on his deathbed, draws the social consequences: heroism needs enemies, and evil in history comes less from wickedness than from the will to triumph over death by inflicting it on others; sacrifice, war, the scapegoat and nationalism are the forms through which human beings buy their symbolic immortality at the price of victims. Here Becker joins Rank, Norman O. Brown and, without citing him, Girard.

The experimental legacy

From 1986, three social psychologists, Greenberg, Solomon and Pyszczynski, drew from Becker terror management theory: cultural worldviews and self-esteem serve as a buffer against death anxiety, and reminders of death should strengthen attachment to one's own worldview and hostility to others. Hundreds of experiments have supported it; preregistered replications, among them Many Labs 4 in 2022, have not found the basic effect, and the theory is now disputed.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the denial of death, the causa sui project, the heroic system, Rank and Kierkegaard, Escape from Evil and the experimental fate of the theory. It retains the idea that refoundations are substitute heroic systems, reproaches Becker for confusing the need for meaning, which is universal, with the need for immortality, and notes that the spiritualities of exercise, which accept death without a system, put his theory to the test.

In the essay: Becker · Girard

Further reading: Ernest Becker, The Birth and Death of Meaning (1962, 1971) ; The Denial of Death (1973) ; Escape from Evil (posth., 1975) ; Otto Rank, Psychology and the Soul (1930) ; Sheldon Solomon, Jeff Greenberg and Tom Pyszczynski, The Worm at the Core (2015) ; Daniel Liechty (ed.), The Ernest Becker Reader (2005)

Berger and Luckmann: the social construction of reality and the sociology of religionTheories of the authors

Peter Berger (1929-2017), a Viennese émigré to the United States and a Lutheran, and Thomas Luckmann (1927-2016), Slovene by origin, professor in Frankfurt and then in Konstanz, both students of Alfred Schütz at the New School in New York, wrote together in 1966 a book that has become a classic and then, each on his own, two books on religion that shaped the sociology of the 1960s and 1970s.

The social construction of reality

The Social Construction of Reality (1966) applies Schütz's phenomenology of the lived world to the sociology of knowledge: what we take to be self-evident reality is produced socially, through a three-stage process, externalization, human beings produce a world through their activity, objectivation, this world faces them as a thing, internalization, they take it in through socialization. Institutions arise from typified habit and are legitimated by narratives and theories, and symbolic universes, religion among them, crown legitimation by integrating the whole of experience into an order of meaning. Man produces society and society produces man. The book, written in plain language, founded social constructionism, and its authors later distanced themselves from the relativist uses that were made of it.

Berger: the sacred canopy and after

The Sacred Canopy (1967), translated into French as La Religion dans la conscience moderne, applies the scheme to religion: every society builds a nomos, an order of meaning that protects against chaos, and religion is the sacred canopy that relates it to a cosmos; it holds through plausibility structures, and theodicy makes suffering bearable by giving it a meaning. The second part analyses secularization as an effect of pluralism and of Christianity itself, whose transcendent God disenchanted the world, and predicts the privatization of religion. Berger later qualified this, A Rumor of Angels (1969) looking for signals of transcendence in ordinary experience and The Heretical Imperative (1979) describing modernity as the obligation to choose, and in 1999 retracted the secularization thesis: the world is as furiously religious as ever, Western Europe and intellectuals being the exception. He also wrote on capitalism, development and humour.

Luckmann: the invisible religion

The Invisible Religion (1967), the English version of a German book of 1963, defines religion by its anthropological function, the transcendence of biological nature through a universe of meaning, and argues that this function, once performed by the Churches, is now performed by private systems of meaning, assembled from themes available on a market, self-realization, family, sexuality, mobility; secularization is therefore not the end of religion but its migration out of institutions. Luckmann also contributed to the sociology of language and communication.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with social construction, the nomos, the canopy, plausibility structures, the retraction of 1999, the remark that a choice does not have the authority of a world, and the invisible religion. It draws two terms from them, the shelter of meaning, which does not disappear, and the institution, which can recede, and judges that Luckmann's definition, by encompassing everything, no longer distinguishes what the sacrifice criterion seeks to distinguish.

In the essay: Berger and Luckmann · Hervieu-Léger · Charles Taylor

Further reading: Peter L. Berger and Thomas Luckmann, The Social Construction of Reality (1966) ; Peter L. Berger, The Sacred Canopy (1967) ; A Rumor of Angels (1969) ; The Heretical Imperative (1979) ; Peter L. Berger (ed.), The Desecularization of the World (1999) ; Thomas Luckmann, The Invisible Religion (1967) ; Peter L. Berger, La Religion dans la conscience moderne (trans., 1971)

Danièle Hervieu-Léger: religion as memory, the pilgrim and the convertTheories of the authors

Danièle Hervieu-Léger (b. 1947), a sociologist, director of studies and then president of the École des hautes études en sciences sociales, for many years director of the Archives de sciences sociales des religions, is the leading French theorist of religious modernity. Trained in the Durkheimian and Weberian tradition and a reader of Berger and Luckmann, she has proposed a definition of religion that makes it a matter of memory and transmission.

Religion as memory

Vers un nouveau christianisme ? (1986) describes the charismatic communities and the emotional revival of the 1970s as responses to secularization rather than as survivals. La Religion pour mémoire (1993) poses the problem of definition: substantive definitions, by the supernatural, miss religions without God, and functional definitions, by meaning, take in everything, like Luckmann's invisible religion. Hervieu-Léger proposes defining religious believing by a specific mode of legitimation: a believing is religious when it draws its authority from a tradition, that invokes the continuity of a believing lineage going back to founding witnesses, and that a chain of memory, an ideological, practical and symbolic apparatus, sustains and controls. Modernity is then a society of fragmented, amnesiac memory, where the chains break, not because people stop believing but because transmission no longer takes place; in return it produces recompositions, utopias, substitute memories, among them the nation and the revolution.

The pilgrim and the convert

Le Pèlerin et le Converti (1999) describes two figures of contemporary religiosity, which replace the regular churchgoer: the pilgrim, whose believing is mobile, individual, à la carte, made up of an itinerary and of experiences, validated by felt authenticity rather than by conformity; and the convert, who chooses a lineage instead of inheriting it, and whose intense commitment may be a change of religion, a return, or the affiliation of someone without religion. The validation of believing shifts from the institution to the individual, the group and the elective community. La Religion en miettes (2001) applies the analysis to sects and new movements, and Catholicisme, la fin d'un monde (2003) shows that the Church in France has lost, along with practice, its capacity to organize the common culture: the exculturation of Catholicism.

Method and debates

Hervieu-Léger works through qualitative surveys, interviews and monographs, and through the history of the social sciences of religion; she discusses Gauchet, whose diagnosis of the exit from religion she shares while insisting on recompositions, and the thesis of the religious market, which she judges ill-suited to Europe. Her definition has been criticized for leaving spiritualities without lineage outside religion, a consequence she accepts.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the definition by the believing lineage, amnesiac modernity, the pilgrim, the convert and exculturation. It infers from them that the convert touches on the sacrifice criterion, that there are substitute lineages, Nation, Revolution, Civilization, and that a spirituality without lineage escapes refoundation but is not passed on: the modern pilgrim has no lineage, he has a library.

In the essay: Hervieu-Léger · Berger and Luckmann

Further reading: Danièle Hervieu-Léger, Vers un nouveau christianisme ? (1986) ; La Religion pour mémoire (1993) ; Le Pèlerin et le Converti (1999) ; La Religion en miettes ou la question des sectes (2001) ; Catholicisme, la fin d'un monde (2003) ; Danièle Hervieu-Léger and Jean-Paul Willaime, Sociologies et religion. Approches classiques (2001)

Pascal Boyer: religion explained by cognitionTheories of the authors

Pascal Boyer (b. 1951), a French anthropologist trained in Paris and Cambridge, who did fieldwork among the Fang of Cameroon, professor at Washington University in St. Louis, is one of the founders of the cognitive science of religion. His 2001 book, Religion Explained, translated into French as Et l'homme créa les dieux, is its most widely read exposition.

From anthropology to cognition

Boyer's early work deals with oral tradition and transmission: why do some representations spread better than others? In the line of Dan Sperber's epidemiology of representations, he answers that transmission is selective and that the constraints of the human mind filter what is retained and communicated. The Naturalness of Religious Ideas (1994) applies this approach to religious concepts and formulates the thesis of violations of intuitive expectations.

Religion Explained

The 2001 book rejects the classical explanations, religion consoles, it explains the world, it founds society, not because they are false but because they do not say why religion takes the forms it takes. The human mind is equipped with specialized inference systems, products of evolution, which handle effortlessly physical objects, living things, persons, cooperation, contamination. Religious concepts are minimally counterintuitive concepts: they violate one expectation of an ontological category, a person without a body, an object that listens, and respect all the others, which makes them memorable and usable. Gods and spirits are, moreover, supernatural agents endowed with full strategic information, that is, they know everything that matters to social interactions, who cheated, who lied, and that is why they are relevant to morality, ritual and cooperation, not because they found morality, but because moral intuition pre-exists and recruits them. Rituals exploit the systems of precaution against contamination; religious specialists form guilds that defend their monopoly; theological doctrines, learned explicitly, differ from the intuitive representations that believers actually use, which Justin Barrett calls theological correctness, a notion Boyer takes up, as opposed to actual religion. Religion is thus a by-product of faculties that have other functions, and neither an adaptation nor a need; it is natural in the sense that it is easy to think, and science, which demands that intuitions be corrected, is unnatural.

Later work and debates

Minds Make Societies (2018) extends the method to social institutions as a whole. Boyer has been challenged by the adaptationists, for whom religion was selected for cooperation, by anthropologists, who reproach him for neglecting history and texts, and by philosophers, who ask whether explaining the causes of a belief says anything about its truth, the genetic fallacy, which Dennett sometimes forgets and which Boyer, according to the essay, avoids better.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known inference systems, minimally counterintuitive concepts, agents with strategic information, the by-product and the phrase about science being unnatural. It holds that Boyer adds no new regime of critique but explains why gods are easy to think, that he refutes the idea of an empty place of the exact shape of God, and objects to him, besides the genetic fallacy, that he explains beliefs and not exercises.

In the essay: Boyer · Hume · Tylor and Frazer

Further reading: Pascal Boyer, Tradition as Truth and Communication (1990) ; The Naturalness of Religious Ideas (1994) ; Religion Explained (2001), trans. Et l'homme créa les dieux (2001) ; Minds Make Societies (2018) ; Scott Atran, In Gods We Trust (2002) ; Justin L. Barrett, Why Would Anyone Believe in God? (2004)

Norris and Inglehart: existential security, the World Values SurveysTheories of the authors

Pippa Norris (b. 1953), a British political scientist at Harvard, and Ronald Inglehart (1934-2021), an American political scientist at the University of Michigan, founder and long-time director of the World Values Survey, proposed in Sacred and Secular (2004) a theory of secularization based on existential security and supported by the largest opinion surveys ever conducted. The essay makes it the empirical arbiter of its ninth part.

The World Values Surveys

As early as 1977, in The Silent Revolution, Inglehart had formulated a theory of value change: generations raised in prosperity and peace move from materialist values, economic and physical security, to postmaterialist values, self-expression, quality of life, autonomy. To test it, he launched from 1981, as an extension of the European Values Study, the World Values Surveys, repeated in waves in a growing number of countries, nearly a hundred today, on political, moral, family and religious attitudes. The resulting cultural map of the world sets two axes against each other, traditional values against secular-rational values, survival values against self-expression values, and shows that countries shift with development while keeping the imprint of their religious heritage, Protestant, Catholic, Orthodox, Islamic, Confucian.

Sacred and Secular

The 2004 book starts from a paradox: secularization is advancing in almost all advanced industrial societies, and yet the world has more believers than ever. The theory of existential security explains it: religiosity answers the need for certainty and protection, and it recedes where illness, poverty and violence are held at bay by development and the welfare state, generation after generation; it persists where existence remains precarious. Since poor and religious countries have higher fertility, the share of believers in humanity increases: this is the demographic paradox. Scandinavia, the most secure, is the most secularized; the United States, rich but unequal and offering little protection, remains religious, which according to the authors refutes the religious market theory of Stark and Finke, for whom competition among churches explains American vitality. Religion's Sudden Decline (2021), Inglehart's last book, observes that between 2007 and 2020 religiosity declined in most of the countries studied, the United States included, and attributes this to security, to the fall in fertility, which makes religious norms on sexuality unnecessary, and to individualization.

Criticisms

The objections bear on measurement, standardized questions translated into dozens of languages, on causality, security possibly being as much effect as cause, on the exceptions, and on the evolutionist inheritance of modernization theory. The sociologists of the religious market and the proponents of the return of religion, Berger, Casanova, contest its scope.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the world surveys, existential security, the demographic paradox, the comparison between Scandinavia and the United States, and the 2021 book. It makes them the arbiter of the debate on the causes of belief, proving Lucretius, Hume, Freud and Becker right and the Enlightenment wrong, since it is not critique that secularizes but health insurance, and concludes from them that exits from religion presuppose a security that only institutions guarantee.

In the essay: Norris and Inglehart · Malinowski · Why do people believe? A constructed debate

Further reading: Ronald Inglehart, The Silent Revolution (1977) ; Modernization and Postmodernization (1997) ; Pippa Norris and Ronald Inglehart, Sacred and Secular. Religion and Politics Worldwide (2004, 2nd ed. 2011) ; Ronald Inglehart, Religion's Sudden Decline (2021) ; Rodney Stark and Roger Finke, Acts of Faith (2000) ; Phil Zuckerman, Society without God (2008)

The thought of Heidegger: Dasein, Being and Time, the turn, technology, 1933Theories of the authors

Martin Heidegger (1889-1976), son of a Catholic sexton in Messkirch, a seminarian, a student of Husserl, professor at Marburg and then at Freiburg, rector of that university in 1933-1934 under the Nazi regime, barred from the university from 1945 to 1951, dominated twentieth-century continental philosophy through a single question, that of the meaning of being, and left a body of work, the complete edition, of more than a hundred volumes.

Being and Time

Sein und Zeit (1927), unfinished, starts from the observation that the question of being has been forgotten by metaphysics, which has thought only beings. To raise it again, one must analyse the being that asks the question, man, whom Heidegger calls Dasein, being-there, to avoid the words subject and consciousness. Dasein is being-in-the-world, always already thrown into a situation, busy among things and with others; its being is care. It mostly exists in the inauthenticity of the they, idle talk and curiosity; anxiety, which has no object, brings it face to face with its own being, and being-towards-death, the resolute anticipation of its ownmost possibility, restores it to authenticity. Temporality, the future first, is the meaning of the being of Dasein, and historicity follows from it. The method is a hermeneutic phenomenology, and the destruction of the history of ontology was to follow.

The turn

After 1930 comes the Kehre, the turn: Heidegger no longer starts from Dasein but from being itself, which gives itself and withdraws in a history whose epochs are the figures of metaphysics, from Plato to Nietzsche, the last metaphysician, in whom the will to power completes the overturning of Platonism. Metaphysics is onto-theology: it thinks beings in general and the supreme being, and forgets the ontological difference between being and beings. Modern technology, whose essence is the Gestell, the enframing that summons every thing to stand available as standing-reserve, is the accomplishment of this forgetting, and only a god can still save us, as the interview given to Der Spiegel in 1966 and published in 1976 will say. Thought must then prepare another beginning by listening to the poets, Hölderlin first, and to the Presocratics, and by practising Gelassenheit. The Letter on Humanism (1947) answers Sartre that existentialism remains metaphysical.

1933 and the Black Notebooks

Heidegger joins the Nazi party in May 1933, gives a rectorial address that places the university at the service of the movement, resigns in 1934 and remains a member until 1945. The debate on the link between his philosophy and this commitment, opened as early as 1946, was revived by Farias in 1987 and by the publication, from 2014 onwards, of the Black Notebooks, notebooks containing antisemitic passages tied to the history of being. Heidegger never expressed public regret.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with onto-theology, the death of God as the end of metaphysics, the causa sui, Gelassenheit, the waiting for a god, the rectorship and the Black Notebooks, and warns that it quotes Heidegger from no printed edition. It judges that Heidegger refounds, waiting for a god within an empty absolute whose priest is the thinker, while granting him that the critique of the God of the philosophers is not the critique of God, a point Marion will turn around.

In the essay: Heidegger · Marion · Why is there something rather than nothing? A constructed debate · Jaspers

Further reading: Martin Heidegger, Sein und Zeit (1927) ; Was ist Metaphysik? (1929) ; Brief über den Humanismus (1947) ; Holzwege (1950) ; Identität und Differenz (1957) ; Schwarze Hefte (publ. 2014-) ; Jean Greisch, Ontologie et temporalité (1994) ; Rüdiger Safranski, Heidegger et son temps (1994) ; Peter Trawny, Heidegger et l'antisémitisme (2014)

Wittgenstein's philosophy: the Tractatus, language games, ethics and the mysticalTheories of the authors

Ludwig Wittgenstein (1889-1951), son of one of the richest men in Vienna, engineer, pupil of Russell at Cambridge, volunteer soldier in 1914, village schoolteacher, architect, gardener in a monastery, then professor at Cambridge from 1939 to 1947, wrote two philosophies, that of the Tractatus and that of the Investigations, and published only one book of philosophy in his lifetime. He read the Gospels and Tolstoy, thought of entering holy orders, and said that his thought was religious without his being so.

The Tractatus

The Tractatus Logico-Philosophicus (1921), completed during the war, in 1918, sets out in seven numbered propositions a theory of language and the world: the world is the totality of facts, the proposition is a picture of facts, and logic shows the form common to language and the world without being able to say it. What can be said are the propositions of natural science; logic is tautology; metaphysics, ethics, aesthetics, which want to say what can only show itself, are nonsense, but nonsense that matters: the sense of the world must lie outside the world, God does not reveal himself in the world, it is not how the world is that is the mystical, but that it is, and one must keep silent about what one cannot speak of. The Vienna Circle saw in it an antimetaphysical charter; Wittgenstein wrote to his publisher that the essential part of the book was the unwritten part, ethics. The Lecture on Ethics (1929) says that ethics runs against the limits of language and names its experiences: wondering at the existence of the world, feeling absolutely safe.

The Investigations

Having returned to philosophy in 1929, Wittgenstein undoes his first doctrine. The Philosophical Investigations, Philosophische Untersuchungen, published in 1953, replace the single picture of language with a multiplicity of language games, rule-governed activities interwoven with forms of life, whose meaning is use; concepts hang together through family resemblances; there is no private language, and following a rule is a practice. Philosophy explains nothing, it describes and dissolves the confusions born of grammar; it leaves everything as it is. The Remarks on Frazer's Golden Bough argue that rites are not theoretical errors but expressions, and that Frazer is more savage than his savages; the lectures on religious belief treat faith as a picture that regulates a life, not as a hypothesis; On Certainty, notes from 1949-1951 published in 1969, examines the propositions that form the bedrock, beyond doubt, of our language games.

Posterity

The Tractatus nourished logical positivism, the Investigations ordinary language philosophy, Anscombe, Malcolm, Cavell, and a philosophy of religion, Phillips, Winch, that has been called fideist in spite of itself; Bouveresse and Monk have given the standard French and biographical readings of it.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes that propositions 6.4 to 7 of the Tractatus, the Lecture on Ethics and the Remarks on Frazer, an attested response, are known. It makes Wittgenstein the silent and strictest version of its exceptions, a mystic without God and without doctrine, a refusal to let an answer occupy the place of the question, and applies propositions 6.371 and 6.372 to science itself.

In the essay: Wittgenstein · Why is there something rather than nothing? A constructed debate

Further reading: Ludwig Wittgenstein, Tractatus logico-philosophicus (1921) ; Philosophische Untersuchungen (posth., 1953) ; Lecture on Ethics (1929, publ. 1965) ; Remarques sur Le Rameau d'or de Frazer (1931-1948, publ. 1967) ; Leçons et conversations sur l'esthétique, la psychologie et la croyance religieuse (publ. 1966) ; Ray Monk, Wittgenstein. Le devoir de génie (1990) ; Jacques Bouveresse, Wittgenstein. La rime et la raison (1973)

Barth's theology: the Römerbrief, dialectical theology, the Church DogmaticsTheories of the authors

Karl Barth (1886-1968), a Swiss from Basel, a student of Harnack and Herrmann, pastor in Safenwil from 1911 to 1921, professor in Göttingen, Münster and Bonn, driven out of Germany in 1935 and professor in Basel until 1962, is the most important Protestant theologian of the twentieth century. He broke with the liberal theology of the nineteenth century and put revelation back at the centre, in a work of thirteen volumes that remained unfinished.

The break and the Römerbrief

The war of 1914 and the manifesto of the German intellectuals, signed by his teachers, convince Barth that the theology which grounded faith in experience, culture and history had handed God over to man. His commentary on the Epistle to the Romans, the Römerbrief, whose second edition of 1922 is entirely rewritten, has, in Karl Adam's phrase, the effect of a bomb in the world of theologians: God is God, man is man, and between them lies Kierkegaard's infinite qualitative difference; revelation comes vertically from above, as the tangent touches the circle, and judges every religion, every culture, every ethics, Christian ones included. With Gogarten, Thurneysen, Brunner and Bultmann, Barth leads dialectical theology in the 1920s, so called because it can speak of God only by affirming and denying, around the journal Zwischen den Zeiten; the group breaks up after 1933.

The Church Dogmatics

From 1932, Barth undertakes the Kirchliche Dogmatik, the Church Dogmatics, a systematic exposition of the faith on the basis of the Word of God alone, in four parts: the doctrine of the Word, of God, of creation and of reconciliation, the part on redemption never having been written. § 17 of volume I/2 (1938) defines religion as unbelief, the concern of godless man, man's effort to take hold of God, and only revelation can raise religion up. Barth rejects the Catholic analogia entis and all natural theology, there being no knowledge of God outside Christ, which earns him a controversy with Brunner, to whom he replies in 1934 with a resounding No!, and with Przywara. His doctrine of election, Christocentric, makes Christ the elect and the reprobate for all, and leans towards a universal hope. Anthropology, ethics and creation are thought in it on the basis of the covenant. Barth drafts most of the Barmen Declaration (1934), the foundation of the Confessing Church, refuses the oath to Hitler and after 1945 takes positions against rearmament and the Cold War.

Legacy and critiques

Barth trained generations of pastors, influenced Bonhoeffer, who reproached him with a positivism of revelation, and the Catholicism of Balthasar and of Küng, and drew the critiques of Tillich, for whom he neglected culture, of Bultmann, and of those who, like Glasse, object that a theology intelligible only from inside faith cannot answer Feuerbach, whom Barth had nevertheless declared right all along the line in an essay of 1927.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the Römerbrief, the infinite qualitative difference, § 17 on religion as unbelief, the 1927 lecture on Feuerbach, an attested response, and the refusal of natural theology. It sees in Barth the exact inverse of Stirner, the pious man is godless, retains his critique of the appropriation of transcendence, and leaves him within revelation, without making him an exception.

In the essay: Barth · Bonhoeffer · Friedrich Schleiermacher

Further reading: Karl Barth, Der Römerbrief (1919, 2nd ed. 1922) ; Nein! Antwort an Emil Brunner (1934) ; Die Kirchliche Dogmatik (1932-1967) ; Die protestantische Theologie im 19. Jahrhundert (1947) ; Eberhard Busch, Karl Barth. Sa vie d'après ses lettres et ses écrits autobiographiques (1975) ; Henri Bouillard, Karl Barth (1957) ; Hans Urs von Balthasar, Karl Barth (1951)

The thought of Bonhoeffer: The Cost of Discipleship, the Ethics, the prison lettersTheories of the authors

Dietrich Bonhoeffer (1906-1945), son of a Berlin psychiatrist, doctor of theology at twenty-one, pastor in Barcelona and London, a student in New York, a lecturer in Berlin until he was banned, director of the Confessing Church's clandestine seminary at Finkenwalde, a member of the conspiracy against Hitler, arrested in April 1943 and hanged at Flossenbürg camp on 9 April 1945, left an interrupted body of work whose prison letters have become a classic of twentieth-century theology.

The Church and discipleship

The two dissertations, Sanctorum Communio and Act and Being, defended in 1927 and 1930, conceive the Church as Christ existing as community and confront revelation with philosophy. After 1933, Bonhoeffer is among those who, from the very first day, reject the Aryan paragraph in the Church and join the Confessing Church. Nachfolge (1937), Discipleship, translated into French as Le Prix de la grâce, opposes cheap grace, forgiveness without repentance, baptism without discipline, which has ruined German Protestantism, to costly grace, the grace that calls one to follow Christ, and comments on the Sermon on the Mount as an order to be carried out. Life Together (1939) describes the rule of Finkenwalde. Having returned from America in 1939 to share the fate of his people, Bonhoeffer enters the Abwehr resistance through his brother-in-law Hans von Dohnanyi and takes part in preparing the assassination attempt.

The Ethics

The Ethics, written between 1940 and 1943 and published in 1949 by his friend Eberhard Bethge, rejects an ethics of abstract principles: the question is not how to be good, but how the reality of God, revealed in Christ, becomes real among human beings; one must act responsibly, accept guilt, and the mandates, work, marriage, government, Church, structure common life. Tyrannicide is conceived as a guilty responsibility.

The prison letters

Widerstand und Ergebung, Letters and Papers from Prison, published by Bethge in 1951, collects the letters written from Tegel prison. From April 1944, Bonhoeffer sketches in them a new theology: the world has come of age and does without God as a working hypothesis; religion, understood as metaphysics and inwardness, which made God a stopgap at the limits of knowledge and distress, is coming to an end; Christianity must become non-religious, interpret the Bible in a worldly sense, and speak of a God who lets himself be pushed out of the world onto the cross, powerless and weak, and who helps by his weakness. He writes that before God and with God we live without God. Barth put a positivism of revelation in the place of religion, Bultmann remained a liberal; what is needed is an arcane discipline and a life for others. These fragments have nourished death of God theology, the political theology of Moltmann and Metz, and contradictory readings, secular and evangelical.

What the essay assumes

The essay draws only on the prison letters of 30 April, 16 and 18 July 1944, and takes as known Bonhoeffer's resistance and death, while stating that it has not verified the phrase religionless Christianity. It sees in them the reversal of Feuerbach, Marx and Nietzsche, a weak God being no longer a projection but a dispossession, and concludes that there is an exit, on condition of not leaving Christ: Bonhoeffer remains within revelation.

In the essay: Bonhoeffer · Simone Weil · Assessment of Part Ten

Further reading: Dietrich Bonhoeffer, Nachfolge (1937), trans. Le Prix de la grâce ; Gemeinsames Leben (1939) ; Ethik (posth., 1949) ; Widerstand und Ergebung (posth., 1951), trans. Résistance et soumission ; Eberhard Bethge, Dietrich Bonhoeffer. Theologe, Christ, Zeitgenosse (1967) ; Ferdinand Schlingensiepen, Dietrich Bonhoeffer 1906-1945 (2005) ; André Dumas, Une théologie de la réalité. Dietrich Bonhoeffer (1968)

The thought of Simone Weil: attention, affliction, decreation, rootednessTheories of the authors

Simone Weil (1909-1943), a graduate of the École normale supérieure, agrégée in philosophy, a pupil of Alain, a lycée teacher, a trade-union activist, a factory worker at Alsthom and Renault in 1934-1935, a volunteer in Spain in 1936, touched by Christ at Assisi and at Solesmes in 1937-1938, an exile in Marseille, New York and London, where she dies of tuberculosis and exhaustion at thirty-four, having refused to eat more than the people of occupied France, published only articles in her lifetime. Her friends drew from her notebooks a posthumous body of work that has become one of the most widely read of the twentieth century.

Work, force, affliction

The Réflexions sur les causes de la liberté et de l'oppression sociale (1934) criticize Marx and every revolutionary hope in the name of an analysis of oppression through the division between those who command and those who carry out, and propose a civilization of work in which the worker would understand what he does; the Journal d'usine, in La Condition ouvrière, describes the ordeal of the assembly line, which branded her soul like a slave's. The Iliad, or the Poem of Force (1940) reads the epic as the picture of force, which turns into a thing whoever undergoes it and whoever wields it. Affliction, distinct from mere suffering, is the state in which the soul is crushed, uprooted, degraded, and in which God seems absent; Weil sees in it the condition in which supernatural love, if it survives, reaches its purity.

Grace, attention, decreation

The Cahiers, from which Gustave Thibon extracted Gravity and Grace (1947), develop a religious metaphysics: gravity governs everything in the soul as in matter, except grace, which descends; God created by withdrawing, and the creature must respond by decreating itself, by renouncing being something, so that God may be through it; one must love God through affliction, consent to necessity without justifying it, and one must have no consolation. Attention, which the Réflexions sur le bon usage des études scolaires make the aim of school, is the empty and disinterested waiting that lets truth and others enter into us; prayer is its highest form. Waiting for God (1950) gathers her letters to Father Perrin, in which she explains why she remains on the threshold of the Church, out of solidarity with everything it excludes, and her essays on the love of God and affliction. Weil holds Greece, the Upanishads and the Cathars to be centres of truth, and reproaches Israel and Rome with the idolatry of the collective, Plato's Great Beast.

The Need for Roots
