The Need for Roots (1949), written in London for Free France, starts from the needs of the soul, order, liberty, obedience, responsibility, truth, rootedness, and describes modern uprootedness, of workers, of peasants, of the nation; it proposes a spiritual refoundation of France through work and sets duties against rights. Albert Camus, who published it in his Espoir collection, Iris Murdoch, who borrows attention from her, Bataille, who put her into a novel, and Levinas, who fought her, form her first posterity; Giorgio Agamben and Emmanuel Gabellieri reread her today.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes that decreation, attention, affliction, the two atheisms one of which is a purification, religion as consolation being an obstacle to faith, and praying while thinking that God does not exist are known. It holds Weil to be the principal believing candidate for the rank of exception, the most radical, because she refuses consolation and gives no meaning to affliction, holds the Nietzschean objection of the ascetic ideal to be partly right but judges that it misses the essential point, since Weil refuses any meaning to affliction, and turns Marx and Feuerbach around in constructed confrontations.

In the essay: Simone Weil · Iris Murdoch · Bataille · The sigh without a heaven

Further reading: Simone Weil, La Pesanteur et la grâce (1947, ed. Gustave Thibon) ; L'Enracinement (1949) ; Attente de Dieu (1950, ed. Joseph-Marie Perrin) ; La Condition ouvrière (1951) ; Cahiers (1951-1956) ; Œuvres complètes (Gallimard, 1988-) ; Simone Pétrement, La Vie de Simone Weil (1973) ; Miklos Vetö, La Métaphysique religieuse de Simone Weil (1971)

Bataille's thought: expenditure, the accursed share, sovereignty, the College of SociologyTheories of the authors

Georges Bataille (1897-1962), converted to Catholicism at seventeen and out of the faith around 1920, archivist-palaeographer, librarian at the Bibliothèque nationale and then in Carpentras and Orléans, the driving force behind journals, Documents, Acéphale, Critique, and a novelist under pseudonyms, built, away from the university, a thought of excess, transgression and the sacred without God that marked Foucault, Derrida and the whole of French thought after 1960.

Documents, expenditure, Acéphale

In Documents (1929-1930), Bataille sets against Breton's surrealism a base materialism, which looks at what idealism sets aside, the big toe, the formless, waste. The Notion of Expenditure (1933), read through Mauss's The Gift and the potlatch, argues that societies are governed not by utility and accumulation but by unproductive expenditure, luxury, mourning, war, cult, play, sacrifice, and that the bourgeoisie, ashamed to spend, has broken the link between wealth and glory. Contre-Attaque (1935) attempts a riposte to fascism on its own ground; Acéphale (1936-1939), a journal and a secret society, seeks to found a religion without God or leader, Nietzschean, centred on sacrifice, and the College of Sociology (1937-1939), with Caillois and Leiris, applies Durkheim and Mauss to a sacred sociology of the present, of power and of festival, in the hope of reactivating the sacred against disenchanted society.

Inner experience and the Summa

During the war, Bataille writes the Atheological Summa, Inner Experience (1943), Guilty (1944), On Nietzsche (1945): a mysticism without object, a method of ecstasy through anguish and non-knowledge, which borrows from the Christian mystics and from yoga and refuses all salvation; Sartre sees in it a new mystic who keeps the corpse of God. The novels, Story of the Eye (1928), Madame Edwarda (1941), Blue of Noon (1935, published in 1957), in which Simone Weil appears in the guise of Lazare, stage the same experience through eroticism and abjection.

General economy and the sacred

The Accursed Share (1949) extends expenditure to a general economy: the energy received from the sun always exceeds what a living system can use to grow, and the surplus must be spent, gloriously or catastrophically; Aztec sacrifice, Islam, Lamaism, the Reformation and the Marshall Plan are its successive solutions. Sovereignty is the moment when man consumes without regard for the future, outside the servitude of the project. Theory of Religion (posthumous, 1973) describes sacrifice as the restoration to the intimacy of the sacred world of what labour has reduced to the state of a thing, and Erotism (1957) makes the transgression of the prohibition, which confirms it, the heart of sacred experience as of erotic experience: eroticism says yes to life even in death. Critique, founded in 1946, remains his journal.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with expenditure, the College of Sociology, Acéphale, Inner Experience, Sartre's attested response and the portrait of Weil, and states that it has read no extract of Theory of Religion. It holds Bataille to be a crucial witness, not an exception, since he refounds a sacred of transgression and confirms Durkheim, and notes that Onfray takes up the word atheology in another sense.

In the essay: Bataille · Sartre

Further reading: Georges Bataille, La notion de dépense (1933) ; L'Expérience intérieure (1943) ; La Part maudite (1949) ; L'Érotisme (1957) ; Théorie de la religion (posth., 1973) ; Denis Hollier (ed.), Le Collège de sociologie (1979) ; Michel Surya, Georges Bataille, la mort à l'œuvre (1987) ; Jean-Michel Besnier, La Politique de l'impossible (1988)

Sartre's philosophy: being-in-itself, being-for-itself, freedom, bad faith, commitmentTheories of the authors

Jean-Paul Sartre (1905-1980), a graduate of the École normale supérieure, a lycée teacher, a reader of Husserl in Berlin in 1933-1934, a prisoner in 1940, a playwright, a novelist, founder of Les Temps modernes in 1945, a fellow traveller of the Communist Party from 1952 to 1956, who refused the Nobel Prize in 1964 and kept company with the Maoists after 1968, was for thirty years the very figure of the committed philosopher and of French intellectual atheism.

Phenomenology and Being and Nothingness

The early essays, The Transcendence of the Ego (1936) and The Imaginary (1940), apply phenomenology to consciousness, which is nothing other than its relation to the world, and Nausea (1938) makes Roquentin feel the raw contingency of existence before the root of a chestnut tree. Being and Nothingness (1943), an essay in phenomenological ontology, distinguishes two regions of being: being-in-itself, the being of things, full, opaque, identical with itself; being-for-itself, consciousness, which is what it is not and is not what it is, a nothingness at the heart of being, and therefore freedom. Man is condemned to be free, and he flees this freedom in bad faith, which plays at being a thing, the café waiter, or at being nothing but freedom. The gaze of the other constitutes me as an object and grounds the conflict of consciousnesses, the hell-is-other-people of No Exit. The desire to be God, in-itself-for-itself, the foundation of oneself, is man's useless passion. An ethics that was announced was never written; the Notebooks for an Ethics are posthumous.

Existentialism and commitment

Existentialism Is a Humanism (1946), a lecture that became a manifesto, sums up: existence precedes essence, there is no human nature because there is no God to conceive it, man makes himself through his choices and commits all humanity, and one must draw all the consequences of a coherent atheist position, Dostoevsky having written that if God did not exist everything would be permitted. What Is Literature? (1948) defines the committed writer. The Critique of Dialectical Reason (1960) attempts to ground Marxism, the unsurpassable horizon of our time, in an anthropology of praxis, scarcity, the group in fusion and the practico-inert. The existential biographies, Baudelaire, Saint Genet, The Family Idiot on Flaubert, apply existential psychoanalysis.

The Words and religion

The Words (1964) tells of a childhood in which writing took the place of God: brought up in a family half Catholic and half Protestant, Sartre loses his faith around the age of twelve without drama and understands late that atheism is a cruel and long-term undertaking, and that his religion of literature was its heir. He argued with Gabriel Marcel and the Christian existentialists, broke with Merleau-Ponty, and his quarrel with Camus in 1952, in Les Temps modernes, concerned The Rebel and the violence of history. Heidegger answered him as early as 1947 that existentialism remained a humanist metaphysics.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with being-in-itself and being-for-itself, freedom, bad faith, the 1946 lecture, the formula attributed to Dostoevsky and turned into a task, The Words and Heidegger's attested response. It treats Sartre as a lucid witness for the prosecution, not as an exception, without a spiritual remainder, a man of the marketplace of § 125, whose religion of writing is avowed, and it sides with Camus on substance and with Sartre on a point of method in the quarrel of 1952.

In the essay: Sartre · Camus · Iris Murdoch

Further reading: Jean-Paul Sartre, La Nausée (1938) ; L'Être et le Néant (1943) ; L'existentialisme est un humanisme (1946) ; Critique de la raison dialectique (1960) ; Les Mots (1964) ; Annie Cohen-Solal, Sartre (1985) ; Francis Jeanson, Le Problème moral et la pensée de Sartre (1947) ; Vincent de Coorebyter, Sartre face à la phénoménologie (2000)

The thought of Camus: the absurd, revolt, the cyclesTheories of the authors

Albert Camus (1913-1960), born in Algeria into a poor family, a war orphan, a pupil of Jean Grenier, a journalist at Alger républicain, a member of the Resistance and editor-in-chief of Combat, novelist, playwright, Nobel laureate in literature in 1957, killed in a car accident, built his work in cycles, each made up of a novel, an essay and plays, and thought as a moralist about the condition of a man without God in the face of injustice.

The cycle of the absurd

The Stranger (1942), The Myth of Sisyphus (1942), Caligula and The Misunderstanding form the first cycle. The absurd arises from the confrontation between the human call for meaning and the silence of the world; it justifies neither suicide, which abolishes one of the terms, nor the leap into faith or hope, a philosophical suicide for which Camus reproaches Kierkegaard, Shestov and Jaspers. One must live the absurd by maintaining it, in revolt, freedom and passion, and the essay ends on Sisyphus rolling his rock, whom one must imagine happy. Meursault, who refuses to lie about his feelings and dies without God, is its fictional hero. Nuptials (1939) had expressed, in the light of Tipasa, the sensual harmony with the world that underlies everything.

The cycle of revolt

The Plague (1947), The Rebel (1951), The State of Siege and The Just Assassins form the second. Revolt is the movement by which a man says no and, through this no, affirms a value common to all: I revolt, therefore we are. The Rebel retraces its history: metaphysical revolt, from Sade to Nietzsche by way of Stirner, Ivan Karamazov and surrealism, which contests creation; historical revolt, from the regicides of 1793 to the deicides and the State terrorism of the twentieth century, which ends by divinizing history and killing in the name of the future; the nihilism in which both end when they forget their origin. Against this drift, the thought of noon, a thought of limit and measure inherited from Greece and the Mediterranean, refuses divinity in order to share the common struggles and destiny. The book earns Camus the attack of Francis Jeanson in Les Temps modernes and the break with Sartre in 1952: Sartre reproaches him for his moralism and his refusal of history, Camus reproaches the intellectuals for their indulgence towards the camps. Doctor Rieux, in The Plague, embodies revolt without God, and Father Paneloux faith before the death of a child.

The last texts

The Fall (1956), the monologue of Clamence, judge-penitent, turns irony against good conscience; Exile and the Kingdom, the Reflections on the Guillotine (1957), the Chroniques algériennes (1958), the Stockholm sentence about his mother, preferred to justice, and The First Man, unfinished, published in 1994, close the work. Camus never ceased to engage in dialogue with Christianity, whose hope he rejects, without hostility, and his 1936 dissertation dealt with Plotinus and Augustine.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the absurd, philosophical suicide, Sisyphus happy, revolt as cogito, the divinization of history, the thought of noon, the 1952 quarrel and the critique of Jaspers, an attested response. It ranks Camus among the exceptions of attitude, unstable like Nietzsche, sees in the Mediterranean an aesthetic myth, the most discreet form of refoundation, a reservation that does not remove him from the exceptions, and borrows from him its answer to the sigh without a heaven, on condition that the we remains horizontal.

In the essay: Camus · The sigh without a heaven · Jaspers · Max Stirner

Further reading: Albert Camus, L'Étranger (1942) ; Le Mythe de Sisyphe (1942) ; La Peste (1947) ; L'Homme révolté (1951) ; La Chute (1956) ; Le Premier Homme (posth., 1994) ; Olivier Todd, Albert Camus, une vie (1996) ; Jean-Yves Guérin (ed.), Dictionnaire Albert Camus (2009)

Levinas's philosophy: the other, the face, ethics as first philosophy, the TalmudTheories of the authors

Emmanuel Levinas (1906-1995), born in Kaunas in Lithuania into a Jewish family, a student in Strasbourg and then in Freiburg under Husserl and Heidegger, whose phenomenology he introduced into France as early as 1930, a prisoner of war in a stalag while his family in Lithuania was massacred, director of the École normale israélite orientale, professor at Poitiers, Nanterre and the Sorbonne, made ethics first philosophy and the relation to the other the place where God comes to mind.

From phenomenology to ethics

The first books, On Escape (1935), Existence and Existents (1947), Time and the Other (1948), start from the experience of anonymous being, the there is, a nocturnal rustling from which the subject tears itself away by positing itself, and seek in time, fecundity and the other what delivers it from the solitude of being. Levinas breaks with Heidegger, whose ontology he judges a philosophy of power and of the neuter, without forgetting what he owes him, and in 1961 publishes Totality and Infinity: Western philosophy has been a thought of totality, which reduces the other to the same; the idea of the infinite, which Descartes found in himself without being able to produce it, designates on the contrary a relation in which the other overflows every idea, and this relation is the face-to-face with the face of the other, who speaks to me, commands me not to kill and institutes me as responsible prior to any freedom. Ethics precedes ontology, it is an optics, and justice is born with the third party.

Otherwise than Being

Otherwise than Being or Beyond Essence (1974) radicalizes the break with the language of being, which Derrida had reproached Totality and Infinity for retaining: subjectivity is described there as sensibility, exposure, substitution, the self as hostage of the other, responsible to the point of persecution, and the Saying as prior to the Said. God is neither a being nor a theme: he comes to mind in the trace left by the face, in illeity, and Of God Who Comes to Mind (1982) gathers the texts in which Levinas thinks a transcendence that cannot be thematized without being lost. Phenomenology is thus led to its limits, and Janicaud saw in this the beginning of the theological turn.

The Jewish writings

Levinas kept his confessional writings separate from his philosophy. Difficult Freedom (1963) gathers essays on Judaism as a religion for adults, which has disenchanted the world and refuses the numinous sacred as a violence, on Heidegger, Gagarin and pagan rootedness, on the Torah loved more than God, and on Simone Weil, an adversary. The Talmudic readings, given every year from 1960 at the colloquia of French-speaking Jewish intellectuals and published in several volumes, comment on pages of the Talmud as a philosopher would, and show the rabbinic source of Levinasian ethics. Maimonides, Rosenzweig and Buber, with whom he debates reciprocity, are his Jewish interlocutors.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the face, ethics as an optics, substitution and the hostage, the religion for adults, the attested response to Heidegger and what the essay reads as a response to Otto, the dialogue with Buber and the Torah loved more than God. It sets Levinasian asymmetry against Feuerbach's symmetry, hesitates over the verdict and leans towards a believing exception, Levinas not falling under the criterion, though the author is not entirely sure of it.

In the essay: Levinas · Rudolf Otto · Assessment of Part Ten

Further reading: Emmanuel Levinas, De l'existence à l'existant (1947) ; Totalité et infini (1961) ; Difficile liberté (1963) ; Quatre lectures talmudiques (1968) ; Autrement qu'être ou au-delà de l'essence (1974) ; De Dieu qui vient à l'idée (1982) ; Marie-Anne Lescourret, Emmanuel Levinas (1994) ; Catherine Chalier, Levinas. L'utopie de l'humain (1993)

The philosophy of Jean-Luc Marion: the idol and the icon, givenness, the saturated phenomenonTheories of the authors

Jean-Luc Marion (b. 1946), a graduate of the École normale supérieure, a pupil of Derrida, Althusser and Beaufret, a historian of Descartes, professor at the Sorbonne and in Chicago, a member of the Académie française since 2008, a Catholic engaged in the journal Communio, has pursued at once a body of work as a historian of metaphysics, a phenomenology of givenness and a theology that seeks to think God outside being. He extends Heidegger and Levinas in French thought about God.

The historian of Descartes

Sur l'ontologie grise de Descartes (1975), Sur la théologie blanche de Descartes (1981) and Sur le prisme métaphysique de Descartes (1986) reread Descartes in the light of the Heideggerian question of onto-theology: the creation of the eternal truths lets Descartes escape the univocity of being, but the causa sui, the metaphysical name of God, brings him back to it, and Cartesian metaphysics presents a double figure, the ego as the ground of thought and God as the ground of being. These works renewed Cartesian studies and gave Marion his thesis on the constitution of modern metaphysics.

God Without Being

The Idol and Distance (1977) and God Without Being (1982) distinguish two ways of aiming at the divine. The idol is an invisible mirror: the gaze stops and settles on it, and the god it sees there is made to the measure of that gaze; the icon, on the contrary, lets the invisible pass through the visible and reverses the gaze, so that it is the visible that looks at me. The concepts of God, supreme being, causa sui, moral God, are conceptual idols, and the death of God announced by Nietzsche is the death of these idols; Heidegger, by subjecting God to the question of being and making the divine god the one before whom one can dance, sets up a second idolatry. God is not said first in terms of being, but of love, agape, and of distance, and Marion writes the word God crossed out. The book provokes a debate with the Thomists, and in 1995 Marion acknowledges that the Thomist esse is not that of metaphysics.

The phenomenology of givenness

Reduction and Givenness (1989), Being Given (1997) and In Excess (2001) construct a phenomenology that goes further than Husserl's reduction to the object and Heidegger's reduction to being: the reduction to givenness, so much reduction, so much givenness. Every phenomenon gives itself, and some give themselves in excess of what intuition can receive and the concept contain: the saturated phenomena, the event, the idol in the sense of the painting, the flesh, the icon of the face, and, combining them all, revelation, thought as a phenomenological possibility and not as a fact of faith. The subject is no longer constituting but gifted, the one who receives himself from what he receives. Janicaud denounces a theological turn; Marion replies that description does not presuppose faith. The Erotic Phenomenon (2003) continues with a meditation on love as the first reduction, and works on Augustine and on faith complete the whole.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the idol and the icon, the conceptual idol with its definition by site, function and causa sui, the reversal of Heidegger and of the Zurich seminar, and the partial retraction before the Thomists; it does not draw on the phenomenology of givenness. It closes the Marion section with an assessment of twentieth-century Christianity, at once a victory and a capitulation, purified faith having renounced knowing, consoling, founding and instituting, so that what remains of it, according to the essay, is only a spirituality that atheists also claim.

In the essay: Marion · Assessment of Part Ten

Further reading: Jean-Luc Marion, L'Idole et la Distance (1977) ; Dieu sans l'être (1982) ; Sur la théologie blanche de Descartes (1981) ; Réduction et donation (1989) ; Étant donné (1997) ; De surcroît (2001) ; Le Phénomène érotique (2003) ; Dominique Janicaud, Le Tournant théologique de la phénoménologie française (1991)

Carl Schmitt's thought: decisionism, friend and enemy, political theologyTheories of the authors

Carl Schmitt (1888-1985), a Catholic jurist from Westphalia, professor of public law at Bonn, Berlin and Cologne, an adviser in the last years of Weimar, who joined the Nazi Party in May 1933 and was the regime's crown jurist until his fall from favour in 1936, barred from the university after 1945, living in retirement in Plettenberg until his death, is one of the most controversial political thinkers of the twentieth century, read on the right as on the left for his critique of liberalism.

Decision and the exception

Political Theology (1922) opens with the sentence: sovereign is he who decides on the state of exception. Against the normativism of Kelsen, for whom law is a system of norms closed upon itself, Schmitt maintains that the legal order rests on a decision that derives from no norm, and that the situation of exception, in which the norm is suspended, reveals the true sovereign: this is decisionism. The third chapter states that all the significant concepts of the modern theory of the state are secularized theological concepts, by their history and by their structure, the sovereign corresponding to God and the exception to the miracle; deism, which banishes the miracle from the world, corresponds to the constitutional state, which banishes the exception. Schmitt claims the counter-revolutionaries as his own, de Maistre, Bonald and above all Donoso Cortés, who had understood, in the aftermath of 1848, that the hour of decision against anarchy had come, dictatorship against discussion.

The political

The Concept of the Political, Der Begriff des Politischen (1927, revised in 1932), defines the political by the specific criterion of the distinction between friend and enemy: the political is not one domain among others but the degree of intensity of an association or dissociation of human beings, whose extreme case is war; the enemy is public, hostis, not private, inimicus, and a world without the possibility of war would be a world without politics. Liberalism, which seeks to dissolve the political into economics and discussion, is mistaken about human nature, and parliamentary democracy rests on a fiction. Political Romanticism (1919) and The Crisis of Parliamentary Democracy (1923) prepare these theses, Dictatorship (1921) and Constitutional Theory (1928) develop them. Leo Strauss, in his review of 1932, objects that Schmitt remains a prisoner of the liberalism he combats.

1933 and after the war

In 1934 Schmitt justifies the murders of the Night of the Long Knives in an article entitled The Führer Protects the Law, organizes a conference on Jewry in legal science, and is pushed aside by the SS in 1936. Interned in 1945, he refuses denazification. The Nomos of the Earth (1950) traces the spatial order of European international law and its dissolution; the Theory of the Partisan (1963) and Political Theology II (1970), a reply to Erik Peterson, who held political theology to be impossible in Trinitarian Christianity, and to Blumenberg, bring his work to a close. His influence extends to Kojève, Koselleck, Böckenförde, Agamben, Mouffe and the New Right.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the sentence on the sovereign, the theorem of secularized theological concepts, the analogy between the exception and the miracle, which it has not verified textually, Donoso Cortés, the commitment of 1933 and the correspondence with Blumenberg. With Hebekus, it reads Schmitt as a truth to be embraced rather than a denunciation, and ranks him among the strict refoundations, the limit case of refoundation in the sole form of authority.

In the essay: Carl Schmitt · Hans Blumenberg · Assessment of the quarrel

Further reading: Carl Schmitt, Politische Theologie (1922) ; Der Begriff des Politischen (1927, 1932) ; Politische Romantik (1919) ; Der Nomos der Erde (1950) ; Politische Theologie II (1970) ; Jean-François Kervégan, Que faire de Carl Schmitt ? (2011) ; Reinhard Mehring, Carl Schmitt. Aufstieg und Fall (2009) ; Heinrich Meier, Carl Schmitt, Leo Strauss et la notion de politique (1988)

Karl Löwith's thought: from Hegel to Nietzsche, history as secularized eschatologyTheories of the authors

Karl Löwith (1897-1973), a native of Munich of Jewish origin, wounded in 1915 on the Italian front, a pupil of Husserl and of Heidegger, whose close friend he was before becoming his most lucid critic, driven out of Marburg in 1934, exiled to Rome, to Japan, where he taught at Sendai, and then to the United States, back in Heidelberg in 1952, is the historian of philosophy who showed what the modern thought of history owed to theology.

From Hegel to Nietzsche

Nietzsche's Philosophy of the Eternal Recurrence of the Same (1935) reads the doctrine of recurrence as the attempt to recover, after the death of God, an ancient, cosmic and cyclical vision of the world, and is one of the first serious engagements with Nietzsche against the Nazi annexations. Von Hegel zu Nietzsche (1941), From Hegel to Nietzsche, retraces the revolutionary break in nineteenth-century thought: after Hegel's system, which reconciled reason and Christianity, mediation comes apart among the Young Hegelians, Feuerbach, Ruge, Marx, Stirner, Bauer, Kierkegaard, and ends in Nietzsche's radical critique of the bourgeoisie, of culture and of Christianity. The book, written in Japan, remains the great history of the Hegelian left and brings to light the series of authors whom the essay rereads as accusing one another of having remained religious.

Meaning in History

Meaning in History (1949), translated into German as Weltgeschichte und Heilsgeschehen, world history and salvation history, and into French as Histoire et salut, goes back in time from Burckhardt to the Bible: Marx, Hegel, Proudhon, Comte, Condorcet, Turgot, Voltaire, Vico, Bossuet, Joachim of Fiore, Augustine, Orosius. The thesis is that the modern philosophy of history, which attributes to history a meaning, a direction and an end, was born not of reason but of biblical eschatology, whose structure, the expectation of a final fulfilment, it has preserved, replacing providence by progress: it is a secularized eschatology. The Greeks, who thought in terms of the cosmos and the cycle, knew nothing of this question; Christianity, which hopes for a beyond of history, has no philosophy of history; the moderns, in seeking to realize Christian hope in the world, produced a hybrid form, unstable and dangerous, of which Marxism, Jewish messianism and prophetism transposed, and Comte's positivism, which ends in the founding of a religion, are the examples. Löwith concludes in favour of a withdrawal: neither faith nor progress, but a sceptical and Stoic wisdom before the cosmos, which Habermas called the Stoic withdrawal from historical consciousness.

Heidegger and the legacy

Löwith wrote Heidegger, Thinker in a Destitute Time (1953), the first book to link the commitment of 1933 to the philosophy of existence, and recounted in My Life in Germany Before and After 1933 his meeting in Rome with Heidegger wearing the party badge. Blumenberg made Meaning in History the target of The Legitimacy of the Modern Age; Voegelin and Taubes are close to him; his thesis remains the reference point of every discussion of secularization.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known From Hegel to Nietzsche, Meaning in History, the reading of Marx and of Comte, the Stoic withdrawal and the debate with Blumenberg, an attested response. It holds Löwith to be the best historical support for its thesis and an ambiguous ally, who refounds nothing but proposes no way of living, and specifies that its criterion owes more to Stirner than to Löwith.

In the essay: Karl Löwith · Hans Blumenberg · Assessment of the quarrel

Further reading: Karl Löwith, Nietzsches Philosophie der ewigen Wiederkehr des Gleichen (1935) ; Von Hegel zu Nietzsche (1941) ; Meaning in History (1949), French trans. Histoire et salut (2002) ; Mein Leben in Deutschland vor und nach 1933 (posth., 1986) ; Enrico Donaggio, Una sobria inquietudine. Karl Löwith e la filosofia (2004) ; Jürgen Habermas, Karl Löwith. Le retrait stoïcien devant la conscience historique (1963)

The thought of Hans Blumenberg: the legitimacy of the modern age, metaphorology, work on mythTheories of the authors

Hans Blumenberg (1920-1996), born in Lübeck to a Jewish mother, excluded from the universities under Nazism, interned in 1945, professor at Hamburg, Giessen, Bochum and Münster, withdrawn from academic life in his last decades, left an immense and winding body of work, at once history of ideas, phenomenology and anthropology, of which the 1966 book on modernity is the most discussed.

Metaphorology

The Paradigms for a Metaphorology (1960) argue that certain metaphors, naked truth, light, the book of the world, shipwreck with spectator, are not ornaments translatable into concepts but absolute metaphors, which answer questions that the concept cannot handle, and whose history sheds light on the history of thought. Die Lesbarkeit der Welt (1981), Shipwreck with Spectator (1979) and Höhlenausgänge (1989) are applied metaphorologies, and the theory of nonconceptuality is their culmination.

The legitimacy of the modern age

Die Legitimität der Neuzeit (1966) attacks the secularization theorem, in Löwith, Schmitt and the theologians, according to which modern ideas, progress, autonomy, the State, are secularized Christian contents, hence illegitimate, unpaid debts to their origin. Blumenberg shows that this thesis presupposes an identity of substance between the Christian idea and the modern idea, which it cannot establish; what is identical is the function, the place in a system of questions, and modernity has reoccupied, Umbesetzung, positions left empty by theology by putting new answers in them, often required to answer inordinate questions, such as that of the meaning of history as a whole, which it would not have asked of itself. The legitimacy of modernity comes from its self-assertion, Selbstbehauptung, a response to the theological absolutism of late medieval nominalism, which had made God an arbitrary will and withdrawn every guarantee from the world: man had to take charge of himself through science and technology. Modernity is also the second overcoming of Gnosticism, after Augustine's, which had left the problem of evil in the creature. Schmitt replies in Political Theology II (1970), and the second edition in turn answers Löwith, Schmitt and Gadamer.

The Copernican world and myth

The Genesis of the Copernican World (1975) follows the reception of Copernicus as a history of the self-consciousness of decentred man. Arbeit am Mythos, Work on Myth (1979), argues that myth is not a stage surpassed by logos but a lasting response to the absolutism of reality, the anxiety before an unmastered world, which it keeps at a distance by naming and narrating; the myth of Prometheus, from Hesiod to Goethe and Kafka, is its thread. The posthumous works, among them the Beschreibung des Menschen (2006), develop a phenomenological anthropology.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the secularization theorem and its critique, reoccupation, self-assertion, theological absolutism, the second overcoming of Gnosticism and the attested responses to Löwith and Schmitt. It concedes to Blumenberg that the extensive version of its thesis proves too much, makes the sacrifice criterion its answer, and regards Blumenberg as an unexpected ally, in the order of history.

In the essay: Hans Blumenberg · Assessment of the refoundations · Assessment of the quarrel

Further reading: Hans Blumenberg, Paradigmen zu einer Metaphorologie (1960) ; Die Legitimität der Neuzeit (1966, 2nd ed. 1973-1976), trans. La Légitimité des Temps modernes (1999) ; Die Genesis der kopernikanischen Welt (1975) ; Arbeit am Mythos (1979) ; Die Lesbarkeit der Welt (1981) ; Höhlenausgänge (1989) ; Jean-Claude Monod, Hans Blumenberg (2007) ; Rüdiger Zill, Der absolute Leser. Hans Blumenberg (2020)

The thought of Eric Voegelin: political religions, gnosticism, order and historyTheories of the authors

Eric Voegelin (1901-1985), born in Cologne, raised in Vienna, a pupil of Kelsen, whose adversary he became, professor in Vienna, driven out by the Anschluss and an émigré to the United States, where he taught in Louisiana from 1942 to 1958, back in Munich from 1958 to 1969 to found the institute of political science there, then professor at Stanford, sought all his life to understand the totalitarianisms as religious phenomena and to refound political science on a philosophy of order.

The political religions

Die politischen Religionen (1938), ready in Vienna in April 1938, seized after the Anschluss and republished in Stockholm at the end of the same year, argues that National Socialism and communism are not only ideologies but religions, intramundane ones, which divinize a reality of this world, the people, the race, the class, the State, and take over the structure of the sacred: hierarchy, ekklesia, apocalypse, kingdom. Voegelin goes back from Akhenaten and Egypt to the medieval religions of empire, to Hobbes and the modern State, to show that the political has always been religious and that secularization has only displaced the divine towards the world. He would later regret the word religion, too vague, and speak of experiences of order and their symbolizations.

The New Science of Politics and gnosticism

The New Science of Politics (1952), lectures given in Chicago, is his most widely read book. Political science must once again become a science of order, founded on the experience of transcendence that the Greeks and Christianity articulated. Christianity, by divesting the world of the divine, created a tension that many found unbearable, and the history of the West since Joachim of Fiore, with his three ages, is that of a relapse into gnosticism: the claim to certain knowledge of the meaning of history and to salvation through human action, the immanentization of the Christian eschaton. Puritanism, the Enlightenment, progressivism, Comte's positivism, Marxism, Nazism are gnostic movements, and totalitarianism is the final form of progressive civilization. Averroes, he says, had seen something. Science, Politics and Gnosticism (1959) refines the notion, and Voegelin would later extend it by speaking of egophany and of the refusal of the question.

Order and History

Order and History, five volumes from 1956 to 1987, covers Israel and revelation, the world of the polis, Plato and Aristotle, the ecumenic age and the search for order, to show how societies symbolize their experience of order and transcendence, from cosmological myth to philosophical and revealed differentiation; the fourth volume breaks with the linear plan. Voegelin was in dialogue with Strauss, Arendt, Aron, Kelsen, who answered him in a posthumous book, Secular Religion, and Blumenberg, who holds the modern age to be a victory over gnosticism, not a relapse.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes that the political religions of 1938, the immanentization of the eschaton, gnosticism, the sentence on totalitarianism and the remark about Averroes are known. It ranks these religions among the most massive cases of the criterion, holds Kelsen and Blumenberg to be right against Voegelin's gnostic genealogy and wrong against the function that Aron describes, and distinguishes secular religion in the functional sense, which it finds indispensable, from the Voegelinian genealogy, which proves everything.

In the essay: Voegelin and Aron · Assessment of the quarrel

Further reading: Eric Voegelin, Die politischen Religionen (1938), trans. Les Religions politiques (1994) ; The New Science of Politics (1952), trans. La Nouvelle Science du politique (2000) ; Order and History (1956-1987) ; Wissenschaft, Politik und Gnosis (1959) ; Autobiographical Reflections (1989) ; Thierry Gontier (ed.), Politique, religion et histoire chez Eric Voegelin (2011)

The thought of Raymond Aron: critical philosophy of history, secular religions, the opium of the intellectualsTheories of the authors

Raymond Aron (1905-1983), a normalien and fellow student of Sartre, trained in Germany, where he discovered Weber and phenomenology and watched the rise of Nazism, editor in London of La France libre during the war, journalist at Le Figaro and then at L'Express, professor at the Sorbonne and at the Collège de France, was for forty years the liberal sociologist and commentator of French political life, against the commitments of his contemporaries.

The critical philosophy of history

The Introduction to the Philosophy of History (1938), a thesis defended before a hostile jury, asks what historical knowledge can achieve: against positivism, which believes in brute facts, and against the philosophies of history that claim to know its meaning, Aron, a reader of Dilthey, Rickert and Weber, shows that history is understood from a present and from questions, that it admits a plurality of legitimate interpretations, and that its total meaning is not given: man is in history without mastering it, and choice, political and moral, remains open. This position, called probabilist, underlies his whole work: there are no laws of history, and prudence is the virtue of action.

Secular religions

In two articles in La France libre in 1944, L'avenir des religions séculières, Aron gives this name to the doctrines that occupy, among his contemporaries, the place left empty by faith and that locate the salvation of humanity in a social order to be built here below: communism and National Socialism, which have their dogmas, their prophets, their liturgies and their inquisitions, and whose disappearance does not follow from the end of the war, since they answer a need. The Opium of the Intellectuals (1955) applies the analysis to the French intellectuals of the left, who forgive the proletariat and History with a capital H everything: the myths of the left, of revolution and of the proletariat, the idolatry of history, the cleric who turns worshipper of a secular Church; the end of the ideological age is announced. The title turns Marx around, the opium now being the Marxism of the clerics. The book earned him Sartre's hostility, and the saying that it was better to be wrong with Sartre than right with Aron.

The sociologist and the commentator

Main Currents in Sociological Thought (1967) presents Montesquieu, Comte, Marx, Tocqueville, Durkheim, Pareto and Weber, and has trained generations of students; the lectures on industrial society, Eighteen Lectures on Industrial Society, La Lutte de classes, Democracy and Totalitarianism, compare regimes on the basis of their common infrastructure; Peace and War (1962) founds the theory of international relations; Clausewitz: Philosopher of War (1976) and the Memoirs (1983) complete the work. Aron was the reader of Weber, the interlocutor of Voegelin, the friend of Kojève and of Jaspers, and the constant critic of Sartre, with whom he was reconciled in 1979.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the 1944 definition of secular religions, which it paraphrases, The Opium of the Intellectuals, the critical philosophy of history and the opposition to Sartre. It distinguishes secular religion in Aron's functional sense, which it finds indispensable for separating modest reoccupations from sacrificial absolutes, from Voegelin's gnostic genealogy, and holds Kelsen and Blumenberg wrong against the function that Aron describes.

In the essay: Voegelin and Aron

Further reading: Raymond Aron, Introduction à la philosophie de l'histoire (1938) ; L'avenir des religions séculières (1944) ; L'Opium des intellectuels (1955) ; Les Étapes de la pensée sociologique (1967) ; Mémoires (1983) ; Nicolas Baverez, Raymond Aron. Un moraliste au temps des idéologies (1993) ; Serge Audier, Raymond Aron. La démocratie conflictuelle (2004)

The thought of Leo Strauss: natural right, esotericism, Athens and JerusalemTheories of the authors

Leo Strauss (1899-1973), a Jew from Hesse, a student of Cassirer who attended the lectures of Husserl and Heidegger, a researcher at the Academy for the Science of Judaism in Berlin, who emigrated in 1932 to Paris, London and then New York, and taught at the New School and above all at Chicago from 1949 to 1968, founded a school of reading classical texts and put the theologico-political problem, the quarrel between reason and revelation, back at the centre of political philosophy.

Spinoza, Maimonides and the theologico-political problem

Spinoza's Critique of Religion (1930) examines whether the modern critique of revelation, from Spinoza to the Enlightenment, has refuted orthodoxy, and concludes that it has not: reason cannot demonstrate the impossibility of a mysterious and omnipotent God, and Spinoza won by laughter and mockery, not by proof; the 1965 preface recounts the path of Strauss, a young Zionist, through this theologico-political predicament. Philosophy and Law (1935) discovers in Maimonides and the falasifa a forgotten position, philosophy under the revealed law, in which revelation founds the city and philosophy inquires freely within that framework. Hence the thesis set out in Persecution and the Art of Writing (1952): philosophers living under threat wrote on two levels, an exoteric teaching in keeping with opinion and an esoteric teaching reserved for attentive readers, and one must read between the lines of Maimonides, Halevi and Spinoza, but also of Plato, Xenophon and Machiavelli.

Natural right and history

Natural Right and History (1953) contrasts classical natural right, Plato, Aristotle, Aquinas, founded on a hierarchy of ends and knowledge of the best regime, with modern natural right, Hobbes, Locke, Rousseau, founded on the rights of the individual, and with historicism, which denies all natural right and leads to nihilism, Weber and Heidegger included. In a late essay, The Three Waves of Modernity (1975), Strauss describes modernity as built in three waves, Machiavelli, Rousseau, Nietzsche, each lowering the ends in order to ensure their realization. The quarrel of the Ancients and the Moderns must be reopened, and Strauss takes the side of the Ancients, not to return to them but to recover the questions they asked. Athens and Jerusalem, philosophy and revelation, are the two roots of the West, and neither has refuted the other: their tension is the secret of its vitality, and the philosopher must live within this open question.

The school and the controversies

At Chicago Strauss trained disciples, Bloom, Jaffa, Mansfield, Pangle, and the adjective Straussian designates both a method of reading and, since the 2000s, a supposed influence on American politics, which his students dispute. His 1932 review of Schmitt's The Concept of the Political and his correspondence with Löwith, with Kojève, around On Tyranny, and with Voegelin are major documents of the century's political thought.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the 1930 thesis and its 1965 preface, philosophy under the law, the art of writing, which it judges fragile in its general use, classical and modern natural right, Athens and Jerusalem, and the review of Schmitt. It regards Strauss as the adversary of the very idea of leaving religion, neither a refounder nor an exception, whose objection is not lifted: the exit is not an accomplished event but a question that remains open, and the Spinozist exception rests on an indemonstrable decision.

In the essay: Leo Strauss · Spinoza

Further reading: Leo Strauss, Die Religionskritik Spinozas (1930), trans. La Critique de la religion chez Spinoza (1996) ; Philosophie und Gesetz (1935) ; Persecution and the Art of Writing (1952) ; Natural Right and History (1953) ; What Is Political Philosophy? (1959) ; Jewish Philosophy and the Crisis of Modernity (1997) ; Heinrich Meier, Leo Strauss and the Theologico-Political Problem (2006) ; Daniel Tanguay, Leo Strauss. Une biographie intellectuelle (2003)

Marcel Gauchet's thought: the disenchantment of the world, the exit from religion, democracyTheories of the authors

Marcel Gauchet (b. 1946), historian and philosopher, a student of Claude Lefort, co-founder with Pierre Nora of the journal Le Débat in 1980, and director of studies at the École des hautes études en sciences sociales, proposed in The Disenchantment of the World a political history of religion that makes Christianity the religion of the exit from religion, and devoted his subsequent books to the democracy that this exit makes possible and fragile.

The Disenchantment of the World

The 1985 book takes up Weber's word while shifting it: disenchantment is not only the retreat of magic, it is the exhaustion of the reign of the invisible as the organizing principle of societies. Religion in its pure form, that of the stateless societies studied by Pierre Clastres, is heteronomy: the law comes from an untouchable founding past, people do not think of themselves as the authors of their order, and this dispossession protects them from division and from power. The appearance of the State, then of the great religions with a transcendent God, in Jaspers's axial age, breaks into this structure: the further God withdraws, the more the world is left to human beings. Christianity, through the incarnation of a God who enters history and through its absolute transcendence, carries this movement to its end: it makes thinkable an autonomous world, a State that detaches itself from the Church, an individual who relates directly to God, and thus produces the conditions of its own effacement. Democratic modernity is the fulfilment of this dynamic, not its negation. The exit from religion is completed around 1700, according to Gauchet, and we live in its aftermath.

Democracy and the religious after religion

La Révolution des droits de l'homme (1989), La Révolution des pouvoirs (1995) and L'Avènement de la démocratie, in four volumes from 2007 to 2017, trace the constitution of modern autonomy, the State, law, history, and its crises, from totalitarianism to the democracy of individuals. La Religion dans la démocratie (1998) specifies that the exit from religion does not mean the exit from religious belief: religion persists as identity and as private choice, and French laïcité changes meaning when the Church ceases to be a rival of the State. La Condition historique (2003), a book of interviews, sums up the trajectory; Le Religieux après la religion (2004), a dialogue with Luc Ferry that the essay says it has not read, sets two readings of what remains against each other. Gauchet has also written on schooling, on psychiatry, with Gladys Swain, on the unconscious and on the crisis of transmission.

Debates

Historians have discussed the dating, sociologists the absence of fieldwork, theologians a reading of Christianity that makes it the instrument of its own end; Pierre Manent, Charles Taylor and Hans Joas have engaged in dialogue with him, and the 1985 book remains the reference work of the French debate on secularization.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known heteronomy, the religion of the exit from religion, the role of the incarnation, the 1998 distinction between the exit from religion and the exit from belief, and the dialogue with Dumont. It brings Gauchet together with the third essay of Nietzsche's Genealogy, § 27, with § 357 of The Gay Science and with Girard, in constructed confrontations, uses him against Onfray's Décadence, and holds him to be the historian of the exit, not the thinker of what remains.

In the essay: Marcel Gauchet · Nietzsche

Further reading: Marcel Gauchet, Le Désenchantement du monde. Une histoire politique de la religion (1985) ; La Révolution des droits de l'homme (1989) ; La Religion dans la démocratie. Parcours de la laïcité (1998) ; La Condition historique (2003) ; L'Avènement de la démocratie (2007-2017) ; Le Religieux après la religion, with Luc Ferry (2004)

The thought of Charles Taylor: the sources of the self, the secular age, recognitionTheories of the authors

Charles Taylor (b. 1931), a Quebec philosopher, a Catholic, educated at Oxford, professor at McGill and at Oxford, a social democratic candidate in federal elections in the 1960s, co-chair in 2007 of a commission on reasonable accommodation in Quebec, winner of the Templeton and Kyoto prizes, has built a philosophy of modern identity, recognition and secularization in dialogue with Hegel, Heidegger, Wittgenstein and the social sciences.

Modern identity

After a thesis against behaviourism and a major book on Hegel (1975), Taylor published Sources of the Self (1989), a genealogy of modern identity: the inner self, from Plato to Augustine and Descartes, the affirmation of ordinary life, of work and the family, which comes from the Reformation, and the voice of nature, from Romanticism; behind every identity stand constitutive goods, moral sources, which naturalist modernity cannot articulate without borrowing from the theism or the Romanticism it believes it has left behind. The Malaise of Modernity (1991) defends the ideal of authenticity against its individualist degradations, and The Politics of Recognition (1992) grounds multiculturalism in the need of identities to be recognized, in the wake of Hegel and Herder. Taylor belongs to the so-called communitarian current, with Sandel, MacIntyre and Walzer, although he rejects the label.

A Secular Age

A Secular Age (2007), eight hundred pages drawn from the Gifford Lectures of 1999, asks how we passed from a society in which it was virtually impossible not to believe in God, in 1500, to a society in which belief is one option among others, often the hardest one. Taylor distinguishes three senses of the secular: the withdrawal of religion from institutions, the decline of practice, and the change in the conditions of belief, which alone interests him. Against subtraction stories, which see in secularization the unveiling of a human nature rid of illusion, he narrates the construction of an exclusive humanism, first through the Reformation and the discipline of morals, then through providential deism, finally through an immanent frame in which the world is understood without transcendence; the porous self of enchanted societies has become a buffered self, and the nova effect has multiplied spiritual positions. Belief persists, weakened and transformed, and Taylor argues, as a believer, for a fullness that exclusive humanism also seeks without naming it. Varieties of Religion Today (2002) rereads James; there followed debates with Habermas on religion in the public sphere and with sociologists on the empirical basis of the thesis.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the three senses of the secular, belief become an option, subtraction stories, the immanent frame, exclusive humanism and the nova effect, which it paraphrases. It shares the rejection of subtraction stories, which joins its own rejection of a total exit, but not Taylor's believing conclusion, and it regards him as a loyal believing adversary: Stirner turned around, you have built something else, answer for it.

In the essay: Charles Taylor · Jürgen Habermas

Further reading: Charles Taylor, Hegel (1975) ; Sources of the Self (1989) ; The Malaise of Modernity (1991) ; Multiculturalism and the Politics of Recognition (1992) ; Varieties of Religion Today (2002) ; A Secular Age (2007), trans. L'Âge séculier (2011) ; Ruth Abbey, Charles Taylor (2000) ; Michael Warner et al. (eds.), Varieties of Secularism in a Secular Age (2010)

Jürgen Habermas's thought: communicative action, public sphere, postmetaphysical reason, post-secular societyTheories of the authors

Jürgen Habermas (b. 1929), Adorno's assistant in Frankfurt, professor at Heidelberg, Frankfurt and Starnberg, heir and critic of the Frankfurt School, is one of the most influential German philosophers of the postwar period, at once the theorist of communicative reason and a public intellectual engaged in the debates of the Federal Republic, from the historians' dispute to Europe and genetic engineering.

The public sphere and critical theory

The Structural Transformation of the Public Sphere (1962) traces the birth in the eighteenth century of a bourgeois public sphere in which private persons reason about common affairs, and its decline in mass society. Knowledge and Human Interests (1968) distinguishes three knowledge-constitutive interests, technical, practical and emancipatory. The Theory of Communicative Action (1981), in two volumes, is the major work: against the instrumental reason that Adorno and Horkheimer had made the fate of the Enlightenment, Habermas distinguishes strategic action, oriented towards success, from communicative action, oriented towards mutual understanding, in which participants raise validity claims, to truth, rightness and sincerity, that they can justify with reasons; rationality lies in this structure of language. The lifeworld is colonized by systems, the economy and the administration, and critical theory must defend its communicative resources. Discourse ethics, developed with Apel, grounds norms in the possible agreement of all those concerned; Between Facts and Norms (1992) applies the model to the constitutional state.

Postmetaphysical thinking and religion

Postmetaphysical Thinking (1988) accepts that philosophy can no longer ground a total worldview and must become fallible and procedural, cooperating with the sciences. Describing himself, according to an often reported formula, as religiously unmusical, Habermas has taken religion seriously since the address Faith and Knowledge, delivered in Frankfurt on 14 October 2001: in a post-secular society, where religious communities persist in a secularized environment, secular reason must recognize its limits and translate into a language accessible to all the moral intuitions that religious traditions have preserved, such as creatureliness in the face of genetic engineering; a secularization that does not annihilate is accomplished in the mode of translation. The dialogue with Cardinal Ratzinger (2004), Between Naturalism and Religion (2005) and Also a History of Philosophy, Auch eine Geschichte der Philosophie (2019), in two volumes, which traces the genealogy of postmetaphysical thinking from the axial age onwards and the constellation of faith and knowledge, develop this position. Taylor, Rawls and theologians have discussed the asymmetrical burden that translation places on believers.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known communicative reason, the public sphere, postmetaphysical thinking, the 2001 address and translation, and specifies that it has read neither the dialogue with Ratzinger nor the 2019 history of philosophy. It sees in Habermas a third way between Löwith and Blumenberg, one that translates only normative contents and does not say how the individual lives without religion, and notes that for him Kant is the first example of a secularizing and saving deconstruction.

In the essay: Jürgen Habermas · Kant · Charles Taylor

Further reading: Jürgen Habermas, Strukturwandel der Öffentlichkeit (1962) ; Theorie des kommunikativen Handelns (1981) ; Nachmetaphysisches Denken (1988) ; Faktizität und Geltung (1992) ; Glauben und Wissen (2001) ; Zwischen Naturalismus und Religion (2005) ; Auch eine Geschichte der Philosophie (2019) ; Stefan Müller-Doohm, Jürgen Habermas. Eine Biographie (2014)

The thought of Pierre Hadot: ancient philosophy as a way of lifeTheories of the authors

Pierre Hadot (1922-2010), ordained a priest in 1944 and having left the Church in 1952, a philologist, a historian of ancient philosophy and of Latin patristics, director of studies at the École pratique des hautes études and then professor at the Collège de France from 1982 to 1991, elected with Foucault's support, transformed the reading of ancient philosophies by showing that they were first of all ways of life, and gave contemporary spiritualities without God their historical point of reference.

From the philologist to the spiritual exercises

His early work concerns Marius Victorinus, Porphyry and Plotinus; Plotinus or the Simplicity of Vision (1963), a short book written for a general readership, presents Plotinus's experience as an inner transformation that can be described. The article Spiritual Exercises (1977), included in Exercices spirituels et philosophie antique (1981), formulates the thesis: in antiquity, philosophy was not a theoretical discourse but a way of life, a choice of life that discourse justifies and that exercises make effective: meditation, examination of conscience, premeditation of evils, attention to the present, the view from above on human affairs, learning to die, reading, dialogue. The term spiritual exercises comes from Ignatius of Loyola, but the thing comes from the philosophers, Christianity having presented itself as a philosophy before reversing the debt. In this light Hadot also reads Wittgenstein, on whom he had published the first French articles as early as 1959, and Goethe.

Ancient philosophy as a way of life

What Is Ancient Philosophy? (1995) develops the thesis as history: from Socrates to the Hellenistic schools, Epicureans, Stoics, Sceptics, Cynics, Platonists, each school offers a form of life, and theory, physics, logic, ethics, serves that form; in the Middle Ages, philosophy becomes the handmaid of theology and is reduced to discourse, and the moderns inherited this reduction, with some exceptions: Montaigne, Spinoza, Nietzsche, Bergson. The Inner Citadel (1992) reads the Meditations of Marcus Aurelius as written exercises, not as the confidences of a melancholy emperor, structured by the three disciplines of Epictetus's Stoicism: judgement, desire, action. The Veil of Isis (2004) follows the history of the idea of nature, between the Promethean attitude, which unveils through technology, and the Orphic attitude, which contemplates. N'oublie pas de vivre (2008) meditates on Goethe and the tradition of the exercises.

The debate with Foucault

Foucault read Hadot and made use of him in his last lectures on the care of the self. Hadot, in two texts written after Foucault's death, expressed both his esteem and his disagreement: the Foucauldian culture of the self, in which the subject constitutes itself as a work, seems to him too aesthetic, a new form of dandyism, whereas the ancient exercises aimed at going beyond oneself towards the Whole, universal reason, the cosmos, and not at cultivating a self. Hadot himself professes a spirituality of the oceanic feeling, of wonder before existence, without a personal God or a Church, close to Spinoza and to Marcus Aurelius.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the thesis of the spiritual exercises, the debt reversed from Ignatius to the philosophers, the list of exercises, the attested disagreement with Foucault, the Whole as a horizon of lucidity and not as an object of adoration, and the 1963 reading of Plotinus. It places Hadot, together with Spinoza, among the exceptions, in the position of immanence, and holds his historical description to be the most exact account of the spirituality of exercise that the conclusion defends.

In the essay: Pierre Hadot · Michel Foucault · Plotinus · What the exceptions have in common

Further reading: Pierre Hadot, Plotin ou la simplicité du regard (1963) ; Exercices spirituels et philosophie antique (1981, expanded ed. 2002) ; La Citadelle intérieure. Introduction aux Pensées de Marc Aurèle (1992) ; Qu'est-ce que la philosophie antique ? (1995) ; Le Voile d'Isis (2004) ; La Philosophie comme manière de vivre (interviews, 2001) ; Arnold I. Davidson (ed.), Pierre Hadot. L'enseignement des antiques, l'enseignement des modernes (2010)

Foucault's thought: archaeology, power, care of the self, parrhesiaTheories of the authors

Michel Foucault (1926-1984), a graduate of the École normale supérieure, agrégé in philosophy, trained as a psychologist, professor at Clermont-Ferrand, Tunis and Vincennes, then at the Collège de France from 1970 until his death, and an activist for prisoners and minority struggles, renewed the history of knowledge, of power and of the subject, and devoted his last years to Antiquity and to the techniques of the self, where he encounters the question of spirituality.

The archaeology of knowledge

History of Madness (1961) shows how the great confinement of the seventeenth century constituted madness as an object before psychiatry medicalized it. The Birth of the Clinic (1963), The Order of Things (1966), an unexpected success, and The Archaeology of Knowledge (1969) describe epistemes, historical configurations that make possible the knowledge of an age, Renaissance, classical age, modernity, and announce that man, a recent invention, might be erased like a face drawn in sand. This archaeology seeks neither the authors nor the truth of statements, but the rules of their formation; it was classed with structuralism, which Foucault rejected.

The genealogy of power

With The Order of Discourse (inaugural lecture of 1970, published in 1971) and Discipline and Punish (1975), Foucault moves, in Nietzsche's wake, to genealogy: modern power is not only repressive and sovereign, it is disciplinary, it produces docile bodies through surveillance, examination and the norm, of which Bentham's panopticon is the diagram, and it runs through forms of knowledge, power-knowledge. The Will to Knowledge (1976), the first volume of the History of Sexuality, overturns the repressive hypothesis: sex has been put into discourse, and biopower manages populations. The Collège de France lectures, Society Must Be Defended, Security, Territory, Population, The Birth of Biopolitics, forge the notion of governmentality, the conduct of conducts, and analyse Christian pastoral power and liberalism.

The care of the self and parrhesia

The last volumes, The Use of Pleasure and The Care of the Self (1984), and Confessions of the Flesh, posthumous (2018), shift the inquiry to Antiquity and the first Christian centuries: how does the subject constitute itself through techniques of the self, practices of ascesis, examination and confession? The Hermeneutics of the Subject (lectures of 1982) contrasts the care of the self, epimeleia heautou, with the precept know thyself, defines spirituality as the transformations the subject must carry out on itself in order to gain access to truth, and places at the Cartesian moment the separation of philosophy from spirituality. The last lecture courses, The Government of Self and Others and The Courage of Truth (1984), study parrhesia, courageous truth-telling, from Athenian democracy to the Cynics, whose scandalous life is a manifestation of truth. Hadot, whom Foucault had read, would criticize him for an aesthetics of existence too close to dandyism.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the definition of spirituality of 6 January 1982, the care of the self, the Cartesian moment, which it paraphrases for want of a reliable page, parrhesia and the attested disagreement with Hadot. It borrows the definition from Foucault and the description from Hadot, notes that Foucault does not say what the true is or whether it goes beyond the subject, and uses the Cartesian moment to explain why modern critique targeted religion as belief.

In the essay: Michel Foucault · Pierre Hadot

Further reading: Michel Foucault, Histoire de la folie à l'âge classique (1961) ; Les Mots et les Choses (1966) ; Surveiller et punir (1975) ; Histoire de la sexualité (1976-1984, 2018) ; L'Herméneutique du sujet. Cours au Collège de France 1981-1982 (2001) ; Le Courage de la vérité (2009) ; Didier Eribon, Michel Foucault (1989) ; Frédéric Gros, Michel Foucault (1996)

The philosophy of Karl Jaspers: psychopathology, existence, philosophical faith, the Axial AgeTheories of the authors

Karl Jaspers (1883-1969), a psychiatrist in Heidelberg who moved to psychology and then to philosophy, professor at Heidelberg, dismissed in 1937 because his wife was Jewish and he refused to separate from her, reinstated in 1945, emigrated to Basel in 1948, friend and then adversary of Heidegger, teacher of Hannah Arendt, founded with Heidegger the German philosophy of existence and proposed a philosophical faith between revealed religion and unbelief.

From psychiatry to existence

General Psychopathology (1913), constantly revised, introduces into psychiatry the method of understanding of Dilthey and Weber, distinguishes understanding from explaining, and remains a classic. Psychology of Worldviews (1919) describes attitudes and pictures of the world and introduces limit situations, death, suffering, struggle, guilt, in which man runs up against what he cannot change and in which existence awakens. Philosophy (1932), in three volumes, Philosophical World Orientation, Illumination of Existence, Metaphysics, sets out the system: objective knowledge does not exhaust being; existence, Existenz, is what man is in freedom, in communication with others, in historicity and in limit situations; transcendence is given only in failure, Scheitern, and through ciphers, Chiffern, scripts that one deciphers without ever possessing them. The Encompassing, das Umgreifende, developed in Reason and Existenz (1935) and Von der Wahrheit (1947), names the modes of the being that encompasses us, world, consciousness, spirit, existence, transcendence.

Philosophical faith and history

The Question of German Guilt, Die Schuldfrage (1946), distinguishes criminal, political, moral and metaphysical guilt and calls on the Germans to examine themselves. Philosophical Faith (1948) defines a faith without revelation, without dogma and without Church, which holds transcendence to be real without knowing it, and Philosophical Faith in the Face of Revelation (1962) confronts it with Christianity: revelation freezes the cipher into an objective reality, and Jaspers, in his debate with Bultmann on demythologization (1954), defends myth against its translation. The Origin and Goal of History, Vom Ursprung und Ziel der Geschichte (1949), proposes the Axial Age, Achsenzeit, between 800 and 200 BCE, in which China, India, Iran, Israel and Greece independently discovered man and transcendence, the axis of a universal history that would not be Christian. The Great Philosophers (1957) and political essays, on the atomic bomb, on the Federal Republic, complete the work.

Debates

Camus sees in Jaspers's leap a philosophical suicide and the absurd become god; Barth reproaches him with a transcendence without content; Heidegger, whose correspondence with Jaspers has been published, shares according to the essay the structure of expectation, without the communication that saves Jaspers; Arendt, Ricoeur and Jeanne Hersch made him known in France and in the United States.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known limit situations, the Encompassing, ciphers, failure, philosophical faith, the attested debate with Bultmann, the Axial Age and Camus's attested critique. It ranks Jaspers among the cautious believing exceptions, judges Camus's critique sound in logic but not with regard to the criterion, since the leap leads to no obedience, and expresses a reservation about the inflation of ciphers.

In the essay: Jaspers · Assessment of Part Ten

Further reading: Karl Jaspers, Allgemeine Psychopathologie (1913) ; Psychologie der Weltanschauungen (1919) ; Philosophie (1932) ; Die Schuldfrage (1946) ; Der philosophische Glaube (1948) ; Vom Ursprung und Ziel der Geschichte (1949) ; Der philosophische Glaube angesichts der Offenbarung (1962) ; Jeanne Hersch, Karl Jaspers (1978) ; Hans Saner, Karl Jaspers (1970)

The thought of Iris Murdoch: the novels, the Good, attentionTheories of the authors

Iris Murdoch (1919-1999), born in Dublin, educated at Oxford, a civil servant at the Treasury during the war and then at the United Nations working with refugees, later at Cambridge, where she met Wittgenstein, a philosophy tutor at Oxford from 1948 to 1963, and the author of twenty-six novels, among them the Booker Prize winner The Sea, the Sea, pursued side by side a body of fiction and a moral philosophy which, against the analytic philosophy and the existentialism of her time, puts the Good, attention and vision back at the centre.

Against the will-centred subject

Her first book, Sartre, Romantic Rationalist (1953), is the first English study of Sartre and already a critique: existentialism makes man a naked will choosing its values in a void. Oxford moral philosophy, Hare, Hampshire, shares this picture in another form, reducing morality to choices and public acts. Murdoch objects that moral life is played out mostly before the choice, in the way we see the world and others, and that this vision is clouded by the fat, relentless ego, which projects its fantasies and its consolations. Moral progress is an effort of attention, a word she borrows from Simone Weil, a just and loving gaze directed upon an individual reality; the example of the mother-in-law who learns to see her daughter-in-law differently, without any act changing, illustrates it. Art, and the great novel in particular, Tolstoy, is the school of this disinterested attention, and the example of the kestrel, glimpsed through the window, in which the self forgets itself, is that of unselfing.

The sovereignty of the Good

The Sovereignty of Good (1970), three essays, argues that the Good, taken from Plato and the image of the sun in the Republic, is the centre of moral life, transcendent, unique, unrepresentable, necessarily real, and that it has taken the place God used to occupy: an object of attention that transforms the one who looks at it, of which it can be said that it judges the world without blessing it, and towards which a prayer without an addressee remains possible. The Fire and the Sun (1977) discusses Plato's hostility to artists. Metaphysics as a Guide to Morals (1992), the Gifford Lectures, a summa of more than five hundred pages, takes up the whole through Plato, Kant, Schopenhauer, Wittgenstein, Heidegger, Derrida and Buddhism, defending a religion without a personal God, centred on the Good, attention and the death of the self.

The novels

From Under the Net (1954), a picaresque comedy, to Jackson's Dilemma (1995), Murdoch's novels, The Unicorn, The Bell, The Black Prince, The Sea, the Sea, stage enchanters, false prophets, contingent loves and characters caught in their fantasies, and give flesh to her philosophy: the real resists the self, and love in them is the difficult discovery that something other than oneself is real. Suffering from Alzheimer's disease, she died in 1999; her husband John Bayley told the story of this end.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the critique of Sartre and of analytic philosophy, the fat ego, attention borrowed from Weil, an attested debt, unselfing, the kestrel, the Good in the place of God, the prayer without an addressee, which it knows from a reference-work entry, and the constructed objection to Comte-Sponville. It classes Murdoch among the exceptions, the position of transcendence without God, an absolute that demands nothing but attention, and notes her weakness, a morality of vision before which Marx would ask what one does.

In the essay: Iris Murdoch · Simone Weil · Plato

Further reading: Iris Murdoch, Sartre, Romantic Rationalist (1953) ; Under the Net (1954) ; The Sovereignty of Good (1970) ; The Fire and the Sun (1977) ; The Sea, the Sea (1978) ; Metaphysics as a Guide to Morals (1992) ; Peter J. Conradi, Iris Murdoch. A Life (2001) ; Justin Broackes (ed.), Iris Murdoch, Philosopher (2012)

The thought of Comte-Sponville: materialism, wisdom, spirituality without GodTheories of the authors

André Comte-Sponville (b. 1952), a graduate of the École normale supérieure, agrégé in philosophy, lecturer at the Sorbonne until 1998, member of the National Consultative Ethics Committee, is one of the most widely read French philosophers of his generation. Brought up a Catholic and having left the faith at eighteen, he defends a non-dogmatic materialism, a morality without foundation and a spirituality without God, in the tradition of Epicurus, Spinoza and Montaigne.

Despair and beatitude

The Traité du désespoir et de la béatitude (1984-1988), in two volumes, Le Mythe d'Icare and Vivre, sets out the theses: materialism, everything is matter or depends on it, and thought is only a function of the body; despair, not as sadness but as the renunciation of hope, which in Spinoza is an anxious sadness; beatitude, joy in what is, in the present, without expectation. Comte-Sponville calls himself a materialist in knowledge and a spiritualist in practice, and reads Epicurus, Lucretius, Spinoza, Montaigne, Pascal and the Eastern wisdoms as philosophies of salvation without God. Une éducation philosophique (1989) and Valeur et vérité (1994) clarify the relation between knowledge, which is not a value, and morality, which is not knowledge.

Morality and the virtues

A Small Treatise on the Great Virtues (1995), an international success, reviews eighteen virtues, from politeness, which is not one but prepares them, to love, which dispenses with them, by way of fidelity, prudence, justice, compassion, gentleness and humility, and proposes a morality without metaphysical foundation, which holds through fidelity to what civilization has handed down to us: one can be an atheist and faithful to Christian values, because they are not Christian but human. Le Capitalisme est-il moral ? (2004) distinguishes four orders, the technical and economic, the legal and political, the moral, and the ethical, whose confusion produces ridicule and tyranny. The Présentations de la philosophie (2000) and the Dictionnaire philosophique (2001) set out the whole for a wide public.

L'Esprit de l'athéisme

L'Esprit de l'athéisme (2006) answers three questions. Can we do without religion? Yes, but not without communion, fidelity and love: fidelity is what remains of faith once it has been lost, and the faithful atheist keeps what Christianity taught him. Does God exist? Probably not: no proof holds, and evil, human mediocrity and the all too desirable character of the hypothesis tip the balance, but atheism is a belief, not knowledge. What spirituality for atheists? A spirituality of immanence, grounded in the mystical experience of the All, the oceanic feeling of Romain Rolland, which Comte-Sponville knew one night in a forest: fullness, simplicity, eternity, acceptance, in which the Kingdom is here and now. La Sagesse des Modernes (1998), a dialogue with Luc Ferry, sets this Spinozist acceptance against Ferry's Kantian transcendence.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes the reader knows the materialism, the fidelity, the three questions of 2006, the oceanic feeling taken over from Rolland, the formulas about the Kingdom and about the universe that suffices, the quarrel with Ferry and Hausemer's criticism concerning the disappearance of evil. It holds that Comte-Sponville refounds no sacrificial absolute and tends towards the position of immanence of Spinoza and Hadot, but that his oceanic experience consoles without protesting: he lacks Marx's Protestation, and he does not refound without being an exception.

In the essay: André Comte-Sponville · Comte-Sponville and Ferry · Spinoza

Further reading: André Comte-Sponville, Traité du désespoir et de la béatitude (1984-1988) ; Petit traité des grandes vertus (1995) ; Présentations de la philosophie (2000) ; Dictionnaire philosophique (2001) ; L'Esprit de l'athéisme (2006) ; Le Goût de vivre (2010) ; André Comte-Sponville and Luc Ferry, La Sagesse des Modernes (1998)

The thought of Luc Ferry: transcendental humanism, the Man-God, the revolution of loveTheories of the authors

Luc Ferry (b. 1951), agrégé in philosophy and in political science, professor at Caen and then at Paris VII, chair of the Conseil national des programmes, Minister of National Education from 2002 to 2004, columnist and best-selling author, has built since the 1980s a philosophy of Kantian inspiration that he calls transcendental humanism, and has devoted several books to what becomes of the meaning of life after religion.

Humanism against the thought of 68

With Alain Renaut, Ferry published in 1985 La Pensée 68, an attack on Foucault, Derrida, Bourdieu, Lacan and Althusser, accused of antihumanism, of dissolving the subject and of contempt for democracy; there followed Système et critique, Heidegger et les Modernes (1988) and a Philosophie politique in three volumes, which defend, in the line of Kant and Fichte, a free subject, capable of tearing itself away from nature and history, the foundation of human rights. Homo Aestheticus (1990) traces the invention of taste and of the modern individual; Le Nouvel Ordre écologique (1992) criticizes deep ecology as an antihumanism that sacralizes nature. The constant thesis is that of a transcendence within immanence: values are neither founded in God nor reducible to nature and society; they impose themselves on consciousness as coming from elsewhere, and human freedom is this capacity to exceed everything that conditions it.

The Man-God

L'Homme-Dieu ou le Sens de la vie (1996) describes a double movement of modernity: the humanization of the divine, by which Christianity became ethics and love, and the divinization of the human, by which man has become the object of the sacred; people now die for freedom, for their loved ones, for human rights, and these devotions, which reason does not command, bear witness to a horizontal transcendence, in other human beings. The sacred has not disappeared, it has moved, and post-traditional identity must take on this meaning without tradition. Qu'est-ce qu'une vie réussie ? (2002) and Apprendre à vivre (2006), a history of philosophy for everyone, make philosophy a doctrine of salvation without God, which answers the question of death otherwise than religions do.
