La Révolution de l'amour (2010) sees in the love match and the modern family the source of a new sacredness and of a politics. Ferry has held dialogues with Comte-Sponville in La Sagesse des Modernes (1998), Spinoza against Kant, and with Gauchet in Le Religieux après la religion (2004).

Criticisms

Agnès Antoine asked whether the title Man-God was not naive or perverse, Serge Cantin whether a transcendence lodged in consciousness still deserved the name; opponents of La Pensée 68 reproached the authors with conflating different philosophies.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes for granted transcendental humanism, the double movement of L'Homme-Dieu, horizontal transcendence, post-traditional identity, philosophy as a doctrine of salvation without God, the attested criticisms by Antoine and Cantin, and the dialogues with Comte-Sponville and Gauchet, the latter not read. It concludes that this is a soft refoundation, consecrating without demanding, the most liberal form of refoundation, and opposes Ferry's transcendence without God to Comte-Sponville's acceptance without transcendence.

In the essay: Luc Ferry · Comte-Sponville and Ferry · Ludwig Feuerbach

Further reading: Luc Ferry and Alain Renaut, La Pensée 68 (1985) ; Luc Ferry, Homo Aestheticus (1990) ; Le Nouvel Ordre écologique (1992) ; L'Homme-Dieu ou le Sens de la vie (1996) ; Qu'est-ce qu'une vie réussie ? (2002) ; Apprendre à vivre (2006) ; La Révolution de l'amour (2010) ; Luc Ferry and Marcel Gauchet, Le Religieux après la religion (2004)

The thought of Mohammed Arkoun: the critique of Islamic reason, the unthought, applied IslamologyTheories of the authors

Mohammed Arkoun (1928-2010), a Kabyle from Taourirt-Mimoun, educated at the lycée of Oran and at the Sorbonne, agrégé in Arabic, professor of the history of Islamic thought at the Sorbonne nouvelle from 1972 to 1992, sought to apply to Islam the human sciences of his time, history, anthropology, linguistics, semiotics, in order to reopen a tradition he judged closed in on itself. The essay knows him at second hand, through Tauil and Demorgon.

Arab humanism and closure

Arkoun's thesis, L'Humanisme arabe au IVe/Xe siècle (1970), studies Miskawayh and his circle, the century of Baghdad in which philosophers, theologians, men of letters and jurists debated freely, the Mu'tazilites included: an Arab humanism existed before being repressed. La Pensée arabe (1975) and Lectures du Coran (1982) trace the formation of what he calls Islamic reason, the system of thought constituted between the eighth and the eleventh centuries, which fixed the Qur'an as a closed official corpus and the religious sciences as orthodoxy, and excluded whatever did not conform to them. This dogmatic closure, of which the profession of faith of al-Qadir is a milestone, produced a sphere of the unthought and the unthinkable: the unthought is what the tradition has not yet thought, the unthinkable what it has forbidden itself to think, the lost debates, the abandoned possibilities, the question of the historicity of the sacred text.

The critique of Islamic reason

Pour une critique de la raison islamique (1984), a collection of articles, gives the programme its name: to subject Islamic reason to historical and anthropological critique, as Kant criticized pure reason and as biblical criticism treated Scripture, in order to distinguish the Qur'anic fact, the event of the prophetic word, from the Islamic fact, its construction by the following generations, and to free Muslims from dogmatism without handing them over to positivism. Arkoun gives the name applied Islamology to this discipline, which does not merely describe Islam from outside, as Orientalism does, but intervenes in its debates; he speaks of a plural, emergent reason, which would go beyond both Islamic reason and the reason of the Enlightenment. His vocabulary, closure, the unthought, archaeology, is indebted to Foucault, Bourdieu and Derrida. He argued for the teaching of the history of religions, for laïcité and for a Mediterranean of learning.

Reception

Little read in the Arab world during his lifetime, where his work, written in French, was translated late and judged too Western, contested by Islamists as well as by certain Orientalists, Arkoun influenced Abdou Filali-Ansary, Rachid Benzine and contemporary reformists, and his name remains attached to the idea that Islam can reappropriate its own history. He is buried in Casablanca.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known Islamic reason, dogmatic closure, the unthought and the unthinkable, the Mu'tazilites as a lost possibility and the vocabulary borrowed from French thought, while stating that it has read none of Arkoun's books. It concludes that Arkoun leaves the closure, not religion, on the side of Spinoza and Ricoeur, and ranks the closure of al-Qadir among the strict refoundations.

In the essay: Islam before the exit from religion · Averroes

Further reading: Mohammed Arkoun, L'Humanisme arabe au IVe/Xe siècle (1970) ; La Pensée arabe (1975) ; Lectures du Coran (1982) ; Pour une critique de la raison islamique (1984) ; Ouvertures sur l'islam (1989) ; The Unthought in Contemporary Islamic Thought (2002) ; Humanisme et islam (2005) ; Ursula Günther, Mohammed Arkoun. Ein moderner Kritiker der islamischen Vernunft (2004)

The thought of Abdennour Bidar: self islam, leaving religion, man as khalîfTheories of the authors

Abdennour Bidar (b. 1971), a graduate of the École normale supérieure, agrégé and doctor in philosophy, raised by a French mother who had converted to Islam and initiated into Sufism, a secondary-school teacher, then an adviser at the French Ministry of Education and a member of the Observatoire de la laïcité, has proposed a reform of Islam through the individual and a philosophy of the exit from religion that keeps from the religious the idea of a human superpower. The essay knows him at second hand, through Tauil and Demorgon.

Self islam

Self islam (2006), an autobiographical narrative and a manifesto, defends a personal Islam: each Muslim must reappropriate his faith in conscience, choose what he keeps of the tradition and become the author of his relation to God, against communal Islam and the authority of the doctors of the law. L'Islam sans soumission (2008) gives this programme its philosophical foundation, a Muslim existentialism: rereading Iqbal, the Indo-Pakistani thinker of The Reconstruction of Religious Thought in Islam, Bidar argues that the Quran calls man to become khalîf, God's lieutenant on earth, heir to his attributes, creative and free, and that submission, the meaning of the word islam, must be understood as the fulfilment of this vocation and not as obedience; God withdraws so that man may take over, and faith becomes confidence in human superpower.

Comment sortir de la religion

Comment sortir de la religion (2012) widens the question to the West and enters into dialogue with Gauchet: the exit from religion is not complete, because modern man has kept the need for the infinite without having a place for it, and because renouncing the gods remains a psychically unbearable sacrifice. What is needed, then, is an exit from above, which inherits from religion its aim at the infinite by transferring it to man, and a secular spirituality that cultivates this capacity for self-surpassing; the Histoire de l'humanisme en Occident (2014) looks to the Renaissance, the Enlightenment and Romanticism for the milestones of such a divinization of the human. The Lettre ouverte au monde musulman, written in October 2014, widely reread after the attacks of January 2015, published as a book the same year and translated into several languages, asks Muslims to face the sickness of their civilization instead of blaming it on the West, and the Plaidoyer pour la fraternité (2015) proposes a spiritual humanism common to believers and unbelievers. Bidar runs meditation and fraternity workshops and takes part in the debate on laïcité.

Reception

Hailed in the French media as the voice of a liberal Islam, Bidar has been criticized by Islamicists for an unhistorical reading of the Quran, by Muslims for a reform they consider corrosive, and by philosophers for the ambiguity of a human superpower inherited from God.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known self islam, man as khalîf, superpower, the dialogue with Gauchet and the sentence on the psychically unbearable sacrifice, while stating that it has read none of Bidar's books. It concludes, cautiously and by way of a constructed confrontation, that Bidar falls under the criterion, on the side of Feuerbach and Kirillov, and that the exit from religion is first of all Christian but not only Christian.

In the essay: Islam before the exit from religion · Luc Ferry

Further reading: Abdennour Bidar, Self islam. Histoire d'un islam personnel (2006) ; L'Islam sans soumission. Pour un existentialisme musulman (2008) ; Comment sortir de la religion (2012) ; Histoire de l'humanisme en Occident (2014) ; Lettre ouverte au monde musulman (2015) ; Plaidoyer pour la fraternité (2015) ; Leïla Tauil, Féminismes arabes and works on Islamic reason, and Jacques Demorgon, review of Comment sortir de la religion (second-hand sources cited by the essay)

The thought of Michel Onfray: hedonism, Traité d'athéologie, Décadence, Théorie de JésusTheories of the authors

Michel Onfray (b. 1959), son of a Norman farm labourer, brought up in a Salesian orphanage, a doctor of philosophy, a teacher at a technical secondary school in Caen until 2002, founder of the Université populaire de Caen, an omnipresent media polemicist and the author of more than a hundred books, defended a materialist and libertarian hedonism before moving towards a defence of Judaeo-Christian civilization that surprised his readers.

Hedonism and the Contre-histoire

The first books, Le Ventre des philosophes (1989), L'Art de jouir (1991), La Sculpture de soi (1993), winner of the Prix Médicis for non-fiction, propose a hedonist morality, to enjoy and give enjoyment without harming, nourished by Epicurus, Lucretius, Nietzsche and the libertines, and an aesthetics of existence. La Politique du rebelle (1997) extends it into a Nietzschean anarchism. The Contre-histoire de la philosophie, in twelve volumes from 2006 to 2020, drawn from the lectures of the Université populaire de Caen, founded in 2002 against the rise of the Front national, rehabilitates the philosophers whom the idealist tradition is said to have obscured, Cyrenaics, Epicureans, Montaigne, Gassendi, libertines, d'Holbach, Helvétius, Bentham, Feuerbach, Nietzsche, Bataille, Camus, against Plato, Descartes, Kant and Hegel.

The Traité d'athéologie

The Traité d'athéologie (2005), which sold several hundred thousand copies and was translated throughout the world, is the manifesto of French New Atheism. Taking the word atheology from Bataille in the opposite sense, Onfray attacks the three monotheisms as death drives, hatred of reason, of the body, of women and of pleasure, and deconstructs the figure of Jesus, a conceptual character rather than a historical man; he denounces in the philosophers a Christian atheism, which keeps the values of Christianity while abandoning God, and calls for a post-Christian atheism. The book drew responses from René Rémond, Matthieu Baumier and Irène Fernandez, and a Spanish critique, by Jaime Alvar, which turns the charge of Christian atheism back against its author.

Décadence and Théorie de Jésus

After Cosmos (2015), a materialist ontology and a celebration of nature, Décadence (2017) tells of the birth, greatness and death of Judaeo-Christian civilization, from Jesus, who did not exist, to Islam, which would replace it: it is religion that produces a civilization, and ours is dying with its own. In 2020 Onfray founded the review Front populaire, and in 2021 he defended the Latin Mass against the pope as liturgical heritage, declaring that his world is the one made possible by the God of the Christians. Théorie de Jésus (2023) takes up the mythicist thesis: Jesus is a concept, manufactured by Paul and the evangelists from the prophecies, and his historical existence matters little. The trajectory, from libertarian hedonist to defender of Christian civilization, is the object of many criticisms, which point to errors of scholarship and to the contradiction between atheology and the defence of the Church.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the Traité d'athéologie, Christian atheism, Bataille's atheology turned around, Décadence, the 2021 text in Le Figaro and Théorie de Jésus, which it connects with Strauss's gesture and Bauer's path. It sees in Onfray an inverted Marx, a contemporary Grand Inquisitor, for whom religion is false and necessary, a Christian atheist in a different way from Comte-Sponville, a Church without spirituality, and the contemporary case of Stirner's law: Onfray refounds a collective absolute, Civilization, and has taken the step from soft refoundation to strict refoundation.

In the essay: Contemporary militant atheists · André Comte-Sponville

Further reading: Michel Onfray, Le Ventre des philosophes (1989) ; La Sculpture de soi (1993) ; Traité d'athéologie (2005) ; Contre-histoire de la philosophie (2006-2020) ; Cosmos (2015) ; Décadence (2017) ; Théorie de Jésus (2023) ; Jaime Alvar, review of the Traité d'athéologie, Dialogues d'histoire ancienne 32/2 (2006)

Biblical chronology: the time of the story and the time of the writingBible: the texts

The Bible tells a story that runs from the creation of the world to the first Churches, but it was written in a different order and much later than the events it reports. Distinguishing these two times is the first step of historical reading.

Two clocks

The table sets side by side what the books narrate, with the dates historians accept when they can be verified, and the moment when the texts took shape according to most exegetes. Dates in the story earlier than the tenth century BCE correspond to no external evidence: they are the dates the story assumes, not established facts.

What the story narratesWhen, according to the story or to historyWhen the text takes shape
Creation, Flood, Babel (Gn 1 to 11)outside historyPersian period, 6th and 5th centuries BCE, on older traditions
Abraham, Isaac, Jacob, Josephthe story places them around the 2nd millennium; no external tracecycles gathered between the 8th and the 5th century
Exodus from Egypt, Sinai, wildernessthe story, c. 13th century; no archaeological tracefounding composition between the 8th and the 5th century
Settlement in Canaan, Judges12th and 11th centuries, gradual settlement according to archaeologyDeuteronomistic history, late 7th century, reworked during the exile
David and Solomonc. 1000 and 950; house of David mentioned c. 840 at Tel Dansame Deuteronomistic history
Schism, kingdoms of Israel and Judahc. 930; fall of Samaria c. 722first prophetic collections, Amos, Hosea, Isaiah
Capture of Jerusalem, exile in Babylon587 to 538Jeremiah, Ezekiel, Second Isaiah, first major redactions of the Torah
Return, Second Temple, Ezra and Nehemiah538, Temple dedicated c. 515, 5th centuryTorah essentially fixed, Chronicles, Job, Qoheleth

Life of Jesus narratedMark c. 70; Matthew and Luke c. 80 to 90; John c. 90 to 100
End of time, heavenly Jerusalemstill to come for the storyRevelation, c. 95; 2 Peter, c. 120 to 150

What the gap means

Three consequences follow. First, the stories oldest in subject are often the most recent in composition: the creation in the first chapter of Genesis is written after the exile, by priests who had seen the Temple fall. Second, each book also speaks of its own time: Exodus is reread by a deported people awaiting a new return, Daniel speaks of Antiochus under the features of Nebuchadnezzar, the gospels speak of communities separated from the synagogue. Finally, the order of the books in the Bible is not the order of their composition: Paul's letters, placed after the gospels and Acts, are the oldest Christian texts.

Schools sometimes differ by a century on these dates; the gap itself is a matter of consensus, and it says nothing about the religious value of the texts, only about where they speak from.

What the essay assumes

The essay works with this gap without presenting it. It reports that Porphyry dated the composition of Daniel to the years 167 to 164 BCE, as modern exegesis does; it makes Spinoza's Theological-Political Treatise the book that asks for Scripture to be read according to the history of each book; it takes Paul's seven letters to be earlier than all the gospels and places Mark c. 70, at the threshold of the series. Part Two, which recounts the birth of Christianity, assumes the reader knows that the texts of the New Testament are spread over nearly a century.

In the essay: Part Two · Spinoza · Porphyry and Augustine

Further reading: Israel Finkelstein and Neil Asher Silberman, La Bible dévoilée (2001) ; Thomas Römer, Jean-Daniel Macchi and Christophe Nihan (eds), Introduction à l'Ancien Testament (2004) ; Daniel Marguerat (ed.), Introduction au Nouveau Testament (2000) ; Mario Liverani, La Bible et l'invention de l'histoire (2003)

The Bible: books, canons and languagesBible: the texts

The word Bible comes from the Greek ta biblia, the books, in the plural: what goes by that name is a library composed over nearly a millennium, not a book written in one stroke. Almost all the authors of the essay, from Philo to Spinoza and Leo Strauss, relate to this library, but they read it neither in the same version nor in the same language.

The Tanakh and its three parts

The Hebrew Bible, which Judaism calls the Tanakh after the initials of its three parts, comprises the Torah, the Prophets and the Writings. The Torah, which the Greeks called the Pentateuch, the five scrolls, runs from Genesis to Deuteronomy and tells of the creation, the patriarchs, the exodus from Egypt and the giving of the Law. The Prophets bring together the historical books, from Joshua to Kings, and the prophets proper, from Isaiah to the twelve minor prophets. The Writings run from the Psalms to Chronicles, with Job, Qoheleth and Daniel. The Jewish canon numbers twenty-four books by the traditional count; its closure is gradual, the Torah being regarded as fixed from the Persian period, while some of the Writings were still debated at the beginning of the common era.

The Septuagint and the Vulgate

In Alexandria, from the third century BCE, the Greek-speaking Jewish community translates the Torah, then the other books: this is the Septuagint, named after the legend of its seventy-two translators. It contains books absent from the Hebrew Bible, Tobit, Judith, Wisdom, Sirach, Baruch and the two books of Maccabees: Catholics call them deuterocanonical, Protestants apocryphal. Philo reads it, and the New Testament quotes it most often. At the end of the fourth century, Jerome retranslates the Old Testament into Latin from the Hebrew and revises the Gospels; his version, which became the Vulgate, is the Bible of the medieval West, and the Council of Trent declares it authentic in 1546.

Four canons

The confessions do not agree on the extent of the Old Testament. The Protestant canon follows the Hebrew Bible, thirty-nine books by its count, Luther having set the deuterocanonical books apart; the Catholic canon numbers forty-six; the Orthodox Churches accept a few more, among them the third book of Maccabees and Psalm 151. The major confessions agree on the twenty-seven books of the New Testament, whose list was settled in the fourth century.

The languages

The Old Testament is written in Hebrew, with a few chapters in Aramaic in Daniel and Ezra; several deuterocanonical books survive only in Greek. The New Testament is entirely in common Greek, the koine, whereas Jesus spoke Aramaic.

Composition and critical reading

Most exegetes date the writing of the texts between the Israelite monarchy, in the ninth and eighth centuries BCE, and the Hellenistic period, with a decisive moment in the Persian period, after the exile in Babylon, when the Torah takes its written form. The setting down in writing is late: apart from two small silver amulets from Ketef Hinnom, c. 600 BCE, which bear the priestly blessing, the oldest biblical manuscripts are the Qumran scrolls, from the third century BCE to the first century CE. The documentary hypothesis, formulated in the nineteenth century, explains the doublets and variations of the Pentateuch by the combination of several sources; its current forms and its critics are presented in the article on readings of the Bible.

What the essay assumes

The essay recalls that Philo reads the Bible in the Greek translation of the Septuagint and comments on it alongside the Timaeus; it presents the Theological-Political Treatise as the book in which Spinoza subjects Scripture to historical criticism, against Maimonides' method of reading, and says that the exit from religion begins there with a quarrel between readers; it places the birth of Christianity between Athens and Jerusalem, following Tertullian, and gives this subtitle to Leo Strauss, for whom revelation and philosophy do not refute each other. It therefore assumes it known that there are several Bibles, in several languages, and that the question of who wrote it and how to read it comes before any critique of religion.

In the essay: Part Two · Spinoza · Leo Strauss

Further reading: Thomas Römer, Jean-Daniel Macchi and Christophe Nihan (eds.), Introduction à l'Ancien Testament (2004) ; Marguerite Harl, Gilles Dorival and Olivier Munnich, La Bible grecque des Septante (1988) ; Pierre Gibert, L'Invention critique de la Bible (2010) ; Jean-Louis Ska, Introduction à la lecture du Pentateuque (2000)

Genesis 1 to 3: the creation, Adam and Eve, the fallBible: the texts

The first three chapters of Genesis open the Bible and tell of the creation of the world, the forming of the first couple and its disobedience. Few pages have been commented on so much, from Augustine's original sin to human dignity in Habermas.

The first narrative: the seven days

Gn 1, 1 to 2, 4a tells how God creates in six days, by his word alone, by separating: light from darkness, the waters above from the waters below, the land from the sea; then he fills these spaces with stars, fish and birds, and land animals. On the sixth day he creates the human being in his image, male and female, and entrusts to it dominion over living things (Gn 1, 27). Each stage is declared good; on the seventh day God rests and hallows the sabbath.

The second narrative: the garden

Gn 2, 4b to 25 starts again differently. The Lord God fashions the man, adam, from the dust of the ground, adama, and breathes into him the breath of life; he plants a garden in Eden, with the tree of life and the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, whose fruit he forbids; he forms the animals, which the man names, then the woman, taken from the side of the sleeping man. Tone, vocabulary and the order of creation differ: since the eighteenth century criticism has seen here two sources, Priestly for the first narrative, Yahwist for the second, combined by a redactor.

The disobedience and the expulsion

Gn 3 tells how the serpent persuades the woman that the forbidden fruit makes one like God; she eats of it and gives some to the man; their eyes are opened and they discover that they are naked. God questions them, each puts the blame on the other, and the sentences fall: the serpent will crawl, the woman will give birth in pain, the man will toil on a cursed soil and return to dust. God drives them out of the garden, whose entrance cherubim guard. The word sin does not appear in the chapter, and the woman receives the name Eve only at the end.

Composition and readings

Most exegetes date the Priestly narrative to the exile or the return, in the sixth or fifth century BCE, and the garden narrative to an older tradition, with no agreement on its date. Judaism reads Gn 3 as the story of a fault and an exile, not of a hereditary corruption: it knows no original sin. Paul, in Rm 5, contrasts Adam, through whom sin and death entered the world, with Christ, through whom grace comes. Augustine, against Pelagius, draws from it the doctrine of original sin transmitted by generation, an expression not found in Genesis. Creation out of nothing is not stated by Gn 1 either, which opens on a watery chaos; it appears in 2 M 7, 28, then among the Fathers, against the eternal matter of the Greeks. Philo, before them, reads the narrative together with Plato's Timaeus. The Reformation keeps Augustine; the moderns, from Kant to Ricoeur, read Adam as a symbol of the human condition rather than as an ancestor.

What the essay assumes

The essay draws on these chapters from several sides. In the section on Habermas, Gn 1, 27, man created in the image of God, is the example of a religious content that secularization must translate, into dignity and into the prohibition on making a human being the product of another's design. In Philo, Genesis and the Timaeus say the same thing, man made in the image of God is made after the Logos, and the Garden of Eden becomes, allegorically, virtue. The constructed debate on existence rather than nothing notes that the Greeks had no idea of absolute nothingness and that creation ex nihilo was needed for the question to take its modern form. Finally, the section on Augustine recalls that The City of God argues with the Platonists about the creator God, a legacy that Part Eleven follows into the quarrel over secularization.

In the Gallery:The Creation of Adam, Sistine Chapel ceiling (c. 1511): man created in the image of God (Gn 1, 27), which Habermas seeks to translate into the secular language of dignity

From Cain to Babel: the fratricide, the Flood, the towerBible: the texts

Between the expulsion from the garden and the calling of Abraham, Gn 4 to 11 tells of the earliest times of humankind: a murder, a universal flood, an unfinished tower. These chapters, the primeval history, pose in narrative form the question of human violence and excess, and the moderns still draw their figures from them.

Cain and Abel

The narrative relates that Eve gives birth to Cain, a tiller of the soil, then to Abel, a shepherd. Each brings an offering, the fruits of the ground for the one, the firstborn of the flock for the other; God accepts Abel's and not Cain's. Cain leads his brother out into the field and kills him. Questioned, he answers that he is not his brother's keeper; the blood cries out from the ground, Cain is cursed and condemned to wandering, but God puts a mark on him so that no one will kill him. Cain settles east of Eden and builds the first city, which he names after his son Enoch.

The generations and the Flood

Ten generations lead from Adam to Noah through Seth, the son born after Abel's death; Gn 6 relates briefly that sons of God unite with the daughters of men and beget giants. Faced with human wickedness, God decides to blot them out; Noah alone finds favour. He receives the order to build an ark of wood and to bring into it his family and a pair of every species. The rain falls for forty days, the waters cover the mountains, everything perishes. Noah releases a raven, then a dove that returns with an olive branch. Having come out of the ark, he offers a sacrifice; God promises never again to curse the ground, makes a covenant with every living creature and gives as its sign the bow in the cloud.

The Tower of Babel

Gn 11 relates that the whole earth has one language. People settle in the plain of Shinar and decide to build a city and a tower whose top reaches the heavens. God comes down to see, confuses their language and scatters them over the whole earth; the city is given the name Babel, linked to the Hebrew verb meaning to confuse. The text is aimed at Babylon and its stepped towers, the ziggurats.

Parallels and composition

Mesopotamian texts shed light on the composition. The Epic of Gilgamesh contains a flood narrative in which Utnapishtim builds a boat, releases birds and offers a sacrifice; the older poem of Atrahasis explains the flood by the noise of humans, which keeps the gods from sleeping. Most exegetes regard the biblical narrative as a rereading of these traditions, composed during or after the exile in Babylon, in the 6th century BCE, in two interwoven strands whose doublets, the number of animals, the duration of the waters, preserve the trace. No geological evidence attests a universal flood.

Readings

Augustine, in Book XV of The City of God, makes Cain the founder of the earthly city and Abel the pilgrim of the city of God. Girard sees in Cain and Abel the founding murder from which culture and the city are born, told for once from the point of view of the victim, whose blood cries out. Among the moderns Babel becomes the figure of political excess, and the tower painted by Bruegel in 1563 fixes its image.

What the essay assumes

The essay does not set out these chapters; it uses their figures. The section on Girard takes from them the point that the Bible tells the lynching from the point of view of the victim and that the title of the 1978 book comes from the things hidden since the foundation of the world; the Girardian reading of Cain is presupposed there. In the section on Voegelin and Aron, Bruegel's The Tower of Babel illustrates the immanentization of the eschaton, salvation carried from heaven into history. Part Two recalls that Origen distinguishes two cities and that Augustine gives this idea its finished form; finally, two literary boxes take up Cain: the one in the section on Leibniz, with Byron's Cain, who rejects inherited guilt, and another with Hugo's La Conscience, in which an eye open in the sky pursues him even into the tomb.

In the Gallery:The Tower of Babel (1563, Vienna, Kunsthistorisches Museum): the human work that claims to reach heaven, a figure of what Voegelin calls the immanentization of the eschaton, salvation carried from heaven into history and entrusted to human hands

In the essay: Girard · Voegelin and Aron · Part Eleven

Further reading: Jean Bottéro, Naissance de Dieu : la Bible et l'historien (1986) ; Jean Bottéro and Samuel Noah Kramer, Lorsque les dieux faisaient l'homme (1989) ; René Girard, Des choses cachées depuis la fondation du monde (1978) ; Augustine, La Cité de Dieu (413-426)

The patriarchs: Abraham, the sacrifice of Isaac, JacobBible: the texts

Gn 12 to 50 tells the story of a family, Abraham, Isaac, Jacob and his twelve sons, whom tradition calls the patriarchs. It is to their God that Pascal appeals in the Memorial, and the sacrifice of Isaac gave Kierkegaard and Girard their decisive scene.

Abraham: the promise and the covenant

The narrative relates that God commands Abram to leave his country for a land that he will show him, and promises him descendants and a blessing for all the families of the earth (Gn 12). Abram sets out with his wife Sarai and his nephew Lot. God makes a covenant with him, first through the rite of the divided animals (Gn 15), then by changing his name to Abraham and instituting circumcision as its sign (Gn 17). Sarah, who is barren, gives him her servant Hagar, mother of Ishmael, who is sent away into the desert with her son after the birth of Isaac. Abraham bargains with God over the fate of Sodom, from fifty righteous men down to ten; the city is destroyed.

The binding of Isaac

Gn 22, which Judaism calls the Aqedah, the binding, relates that God puts Abraham to the test: he asks him to take his only son and offer him as a burnt offering on a mountain in the land of Moriah. Abraham sets out with Isaac and two servants; on the third day he leaves the servants behind, lays the wood on Isaac and carries the fire and the knife. Isaac asks where the lamb is; Abraham answers that God will provide. He builds the altar, binds his son, lays him on the wood and stretches out his hand. The angel of the Lord stops him, and a ram caught by its horns in a thicket is offered in his place. The text is silent about Abraham's thoughts and about Isaac's reaction.

Jacob

Isaac marries Rebekah, who gives birth to twins, Esau and Jacob. Jacob buys the birthright for a dish of lentils and obtains his blind father's blessing by deceit. While fleeing, he sees in a dream a ladder between earth and heaven. In the house of his uncle Laban he serves fourteen years for Leah and Rachel. On the night before he meets Esau again, left alone at the ford of the Jabbok, he wrestles until dawn with a stranger who puts his hip out of joint and renames him Israel (Gn 32). His twelve sons give their names to the tribes; Joseph, sold by his brothers, becomes Pharaoh's minister in Egypt, where the whole family joins him: the Exodus follows on.

Composition and readings

No external source attests the patriarchs; most exegetes see them as ancestral figures carried by distinct cycles, Abraham in the south, Jacob in the north, brought together between the eighth and the fifth centuries BCE. Judaism reads the Aqedah as the merit of the fathers, invoked in the liturgy of the New Year; Christianity sees in it a figure of Christ carrying the wood; the Qur'an relates it without naming the son. Kierkegaard, in Fear and Trembling (1843), makes it the paradox of faith, a suspension of the ethical that cannot be justified before others. Girard reads it as the moment when human sacrifice is suspended and replaced by the animal. Caravaggio and Rembrandt painted the instant when the angel stays the hand.

What the essay assumes

The essay cites Pascal's Memorial, the God of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob set against the God of the philosophers and the scholars, and reads in it a critique of religion made in the name of faith, the true God being the God of a history; Rembrandt's The Sacrifice of Abraham illustrates the section, and the transition of Part Two says that Pascal appeals to the God who came before the Greek translation. The section on Kierkegaard opens with Delacroix's Jacob Wrestling with the Angel, an image of faith as a singular ordeal; Fear and Trembling is not analysed there, but is named later among the books that marked Iris Murdoch. In Girard, Caravaggio's The Sacrifice of Isaac is a key moment of his reading of the Bible. The section on Maimonides, finally, reads the Bible as a text whose anthropomorphisms call for interpretation: the stories of the patriarchs are full of them.

Further reading: Søren Kierkegaard, Crainte et tremblement (1843) ; Shalom Spiegel, The Last Trial (1967) ; Thomas Römer, L'Invention de Dieu (2014) ; Jean-Louis Ska, Introduction à la lecture du Pentateuque (2000)

Exodus: Moses, the departure from Egypt, Sinai and the LawBible: the texts

Exodus, the second book of the Pentateuch, tells of the liberation of the Hebrews from Egypt and the giving of the Law at Sinai; the following books continue it up to the death of Moses. It is the founding narrative of Israel, which the Jewish Passover commemorates every year.

From oppression to the burning bush

The narrative tells how the descendants of Jacob are reduced to servitude by a Pharaoh who orders the newborn boys to be drowned. A Levite child is hidden in a basket on the Nile, taken in by Pharaoh's daughter and named Moses. As an adult he kills an Egyptian and flees to the land of Midian. Near Horeb he sees a bush that burns without being consumed; God calls him from it and answers the request for his name with an enigmatic formula, often rendered as I am who I am (Ex 3).

The plagues, the Passover, the sea

Pharaoh refuses to let the people go; ten plagues strike Egypt. On the night of the last, each family slaughters a lamb and marks its door with the blood: this is the Passover. Pharaoh gives way, then gives chase; the sea opens before Israel and closes over the chariots (Ex 14). In the desert, God feeds the murmuring people with manna.

Sinai and the Law

At Sinai, God utters the ten words, the Decalogue: no other gods, no image, respect for the name and for the sabbath, the honour due to parents, the prohibitions of murder, adultery, theft, false witness and covetousness (Ex 20). Deuteronomy gives a second version, which grounds the sabbath differently (Dt 5). The covenant code follows, civil and ritual laws; Moses then goes up the mountain for forty days.

The golden calf and the broken tables

In his absence, the people ask Aaron for gods to go before them; Aaron melts down the gold rings into a calf, which the people acclaim as the god who brought them out of Egypt (Ex 32). Coming down with the tables written by the finger of God, Moses sees the dancing and breaks the tables. He intercedes, is allowed to see God only from behind (Ex 33) and cuts two new tables. Leviticus then regulates sacrifices and purity; Deuteronomy rereads the history in the discourses of Moses, with the Shema, the call to love a single God, and tells of his death on Mount Nebo, in sight of a land he does not enter.

History and readings

No Egyptian source or archaeological trace attests a mass departure; most historians regard the narrative as a founding composition elaborated between the eighth and the fifth century BCE, some admitting a historical core, a small group come from Egypt. Maimonides explains the sacrifices as a divine pedagogy adapted to a people accustomed to idolatrous cults (Guide for the Perplexed, III, 32). Freud, in Moses and Monotheism (1939), makes Moses an Egyptian close to Akhenaten, killed by his people, and monotheism the return of this repressed murder. The golden calf has become the very image of idolatry.

What the essay assumes

The section on Maimonides sets out the thesis of the Guide III, 32, the sacrifices transferred to the name of God rather than abolished, like the detour through the desert (Ex 13, 17-18); its illustration, Rembrandt's Moses, recalls that it is debated whether he is breaking the tables or presenting them, and reads in the God seen from behind (Ex 33, 23) only the attributes of action of the Guide I, 54. In the section on Feuerbach, Poussin's The Adoration of the Golden Calf figures projection, man adoring what he has made. Philo makes the exodus the departure of the soul from the body. The section on Freud keeps to The Future of an Illusion and Civilization and Its Discontents, without discussing the book on Moses; Part One asks what becomes of the revealed Law when a philosopher reads it, and Part Two situates the birth of Christianity between a God of the Law come from Jerusalem and a God of reason come from Athens.

In the Gallery:Moses Breaking the Tablets of the Law (1659, Berlin, Gemäldegalerie): it is debated whether he is breaking them (Ex 32, 19) or presenting them to the people; Moses is the prophet to whom God shows only his back (Ex 33, 23), that is, according to the Guide for the Perplexed (I, 54), only his attributes of actionThe Adoration of the Golden Calf (c. 1633-1634, London, National Gallery): the god made by human hands (Ex 32), an ancient figure of what Feuerbach generalizes: in God, man worships what he has himself projected

Further reading: Thomas Römer, Moïse : lui que Yahvé a connu face à face (2002) ; Israel Finkelstein and Neil Asher Silberman, La Bible dévoilée (2001) ; Jan Assmann, Moïse l'Égyptien (1997) ; Sigmund Freud, L'Homme Moïse et la religion monothéiste (1939)

From the Judges to the Kings: Samuel, Saul, David and Goliath, Solomon and the TempleBible: the texts

The books of Joshua, Judges, Samuel and Kings tell the history of Israel from the entry into Canaan to the destruction of Jerusalem by the Babylonians. They gave the West its royal figures par excellence, David and Solomon, and gave Christianity the idea of a messiah who is the son of David.

From the conquest to the Judges

The book of Joshua relates that the tribes, led by the successor of Moses, cross the Jordan, take Jericho, then subdue the land and divide it among themselves. Archaeology does not confirm this picture: Jericho appears uninhabited at the supposed period, and most specialists describe a gradual settlement of populations originating in Canaan itself, around the twelfth century BCE. The book of Judges then presents a period without a king, punctuated by a cycle: the people abandon their God, are handed over to an oppressor, cry out to him, and a deliverer arises, Deborah against Sisera, Gideon against Midian, Samson against the Philistines.

Samuel, Saul and the demand for a king

Samuel, the last of the judges and a prophet, is asked for a king, such as all the nations have. The narrative of 1 S 8 gives him a speech of warning: the king will take their sons for his chariots, their daughters for his kitchens, a tenth of their harvests. God nevertheless consents, and Samuel anoints Saul. After Saul's disobedience, Samuel secretly anoints a young shepherd of Bethlehem, David, the youngest of the sons of Jesse.

David

Chapter 17 of 1 Samuel tells of the combat against Goliath: a Philistine champion of gigantic stature defies the army of Israel for forty days; David, who has come to bring provisions to his brothers, refuses Saul's armour, steps forward with his sling and five stones from the brook, fells the giant with a stone to the forehead and cuts off his head with his own sword. There follow the friendship with Jonathan, Saul's jealousy, the flight and the death of the king at Gilboa. David reigns seven years at Hebron over Judah, then takes Jerusalem, makes it his capital and brings the ark of the covenant there. Then David takes Bathsheba, wife of Uriah the Hittite, and has the husband placed in the front line so that he will die; the prophet Nathan tells him the parable of the rich man who steals the poor man's only ewe lamb, and David condemns himself before understanding that it is about him. Bathsheba will later give birth to Solomon. The end of the reign is marked by the revolt of Absalom.

Solomon, the Temple and the schism

Solomon asks God for wisdom rather than riches, and shows it by deciding between two women who claim the same child. He builds the Temple of Jerusalem in seven years (1 R 6 to 8), where the ark rests. The narrative reproaches him for his foreign wives, who draw him towards their gods. At his death, around 930, the northern tribes break away: Israel, with Samaria as its capital, and Judah, around Jerusalem. The books of Kings follow them until the capture of Samaria by the Assyrians around 722 and that of Jerusalem in 587 by Nebuchadnezzar, who destroys the Temple and deports the elites to Babylon.

Composition and reading

Since Martin Noth (1943), criticism has seen in the whole stretching from Joshua to Kings a Deuteronomistic history, composed under King Josiah at the end of the seventh century, then reworked during the exile to explain the catastrophe by the infidelity of the kings. The historical David remains debated: the Tel Dan stele, discovered in 1993, mentions around 840 a house of David, but the Jerusalem of the tenth century looks modest to the excavators. The genealogies of Matthew and Luke make Jesus descend from him.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the Davidic descent of the messiah when, in Part Five, it sets out how Strauss explains the birth in Bethlehem and the descent from David by the faith of a community awaiting a Messiah; it opposes, with Tertullian and then Leo Strauss, Jerusalem to Athens, the revealed Law to Greek reason; and, in the section on Girard, it draws on Frazer's sacrificed kings and on the biblical texts that give the victim a voice, among them the Psalms attributed to David.

In the essay: Part One · Part Five · Ernest Renan · Leo Strauss

Further reading: Israel Finkelstein and Neil Asher Silberman, La Bible dévoilée (2001) ; Thomas Römer, La Première Histoire d'Israël (2007) ; Baruch Halpern, David's Secret Demons (2001) ; Mario Liverani, La Bible et l'invention de l'histoire (2003)

The prophets of Israel, the exile and the book of DanielBible: the texts

The prophets occupy a whole section of the Hebrew Bible, and their books are among those most often cited by the New Testament. Isaiah, Jeremiah, Ezekiel and the twelve minor prophets, to whom the Christian Bible adds Daniel, shaped the ideas of covenant, judgement and messiah.

What a prophet is

In the Bible, the prophet is not first of all a soothsayer but a spokesman: he speaks in the name of God, often against the king, the priests and the people. A distinction is drawn between court prophets, such as Nathan before David, and the so-called writing prophets. Elijah and Elisha, in the ninth century, wrote nothing; the books of Kings recount Elijah's challenge to the prophets of Baal and his being taken up in a chariot of fire.

From the eighth century to the exile

Amos denounces the luxury of Samaria and the oppression of the poor: worship without justice displeases God. Hosea depicts the covenant as a marriage betrayed by the wife. Isaiah of Jerusalem preaches trust in God alone and announces to King Ahaz the birth of a child named Emmanuel (Is 7). Jeremiah preaches submission to Babylon, sees Jerusalem fall in 587 and announces a new covenant written on hearts. Ezekiel, deported as early as 597, prophesies in Babylon through visions. The exile (587 to 538) is the crucible of the Bible: the deportees gather their traditions together. Cyrus authorizes the return in 538; the Second Temple is dedicated around 515.

The three Isaiahs and the suffering servant

Since Bernhard Duhm (1892), criticism has distinguished three blocks in the book of Isaiah: chapters 1 to 39, for the most part from the prophet of the eighth century; chapters 40 to 55, an anonymous Second Isaiah who speaks to the exiles and hails Cyrus as God's anointed; chapters 56 to 66, a Third Isaiah after the return. Second Isaiah contains four songs of the servant, the last of which (Is 52 and 53) describes a despised man, struck for the faults of others, whose suffering obtains the justification of many. The same block affirms that God hides himself (Is 45).

Messianism

The word messiah means anointed and first designates the reigning king. After the end of the monarchy, hope shifts to a descendant of David yet to come who would restore justice.

Daniel

The book of Daniel presents itself as the work of a Jewish sage at the court of Babylon in the sixth century. Its first part recounts court episodes: Nebuchadnezzar's dreams interpreted, the three companions in the furnace, Daniel in the lions' den. The second part contains visions: four beasts rising from the sea, then an Ancient of Days and, coming on the clouds, one like a son of man who receives eternal kingship (Dn 7); a succession of empires told with increasing precision up to the reign of Antiochus IV Epiphanes, the Seleucid king who profanes the Temple in 167; and finally the announcement of a resurrection of the dead. Porphyry, in the third century, had noticed that the precision stops at Antiochus, and concluded that the book, written under this king, told the past in the form of prediction. Modern exegesis likewise dates its final redaction to the years 167 to 164, during the Maccabean revolt.

Composition and Christian rereading

The prophetic books are collections of oracles supplemented over several centuries by disciples and redactors. The first Christians read them as announcements of Christ: Matthew cites the Emmanuel of Is 7 from the Septuagint, which translates the Hebrew word for young woman as virgin; the Passion is told with the features of the servant of Is 53.

What the essay assumes

The essay draws on Porphyry, dating Daniel to the time of Antiochus Epiphanes, as an ancestor of critical exegesis; it cites in Pascal the verse of Isaiah on the hidden God, which the Pensées turn into a thesis; it places, in Girard, the suffering servant, Job and the Psalms among the texts that proclaim the innocence of the victim; and it presents, with Schweitzer and Sanders, Paul's Christianity as an eschatology, an expectation of an imminent end for which Daniel and the prophets of the exile supplied the images.

In the essay: Porphyry and Augustine · Pascal

Further reading: Joseph Blenkinsopp, Une histoire de la prophétie en Israël (1983) ; André Neher, L'Essence du prophétisme (1955) ; John J. Collins, The Apocalyptic Imagination (1984) ; Thomas Römer, Jean-Daniel Macchi and Christophe Nihan (eds.), Introduction à l'Ancien Testament (2004)

Psalms, Job, Qoheleth and the wisdom booksBible: the texts

The poetic and wisdom books form the heart of the Writings, the third part of the Hebrew canon after the Law and the Prophets. Less narrative than the others, they give voice to prayer, to lament and to reflection on the human condition.

The Psalms

The Psalter gathers one hundred and fifty poems. Since Hermann Gunkel, exegetes have classified them by genre: hymns of praise, supplications, thanksgivings, royal psalms. Nearly half bear the name of David, whom tradition holds to be their author; criticism sees in them a collection formed over several centuries, from the monarchy to the Persian period. They became the daily prayer of Christian monks and the book of the Old Testament most often quoted in the New.

Proverbs

Attributed to Solomon, Proverbs collects maxims of practical wisdom. The first nine chapters stage personified Wisdom, begotten before the world: a figure that the Jewish and Christian traditions would later associate with the Torah and with the Logos.

Job

The Book of Job raises the problem that Proverbs seemed to solve, that of retribution. A prose prologue relates that Job, an upright man, is put to the test following a wager between God and the Satan: he loses his flocks, his children, his health, and refuses to curse God. Three friends come to console him and maintain that he must have sinned since he suffers; Job protests his innocence, accuses God of injustice and asks to plead his case. A fourth, Elihu, intervenes. God finally answers out of the whirlwind (Jb 38-41), not with an explanation but with a series of questions about creation; Job falls silent and retracts. The epilogue vindicates Job against his friends and restores to him twice his former goods. The question of the suffering of the righteous remains open.

Qoheleth and the Song

Qoheleth, in Greek Ecclesiastes, presents himself as son of David and king in Jerusalem. He repeats that all is vanity, vapour, a chasing after wind; that there is a time for everything; that the wise man and the fool die the same death; and he advises eating, drinking and enjoying one's labour, for such is man's portion. The Song of Songs is a collection of love poems between a young woman and her beloved, with no mention of God; the rabbis and then the Fathers read it as the allegory of the love between God and Israel, or between Christ and the Church. The Wisdom of Solomon and Sirach are deuterocanonical, absent from the Hebrew Bible.

Composition and readings of Job

Criticism dates Job and Qoheleth to the Persian or Hellenistic period; the dialogue of Job may have been inserted into an older tale. Gregory the Great makes it, in the Moralia, a moral allegory; Maimonides, in the Guide III, 22 and 23, sees in it a parable on providence in which the friends represent philosophical opinions; Leibniz counts it among the sources of the Theodicy; Kant, in 1791, contrasts Job's sincerity with the speeches of his friends; Jung, in Answer to Job (1952), reads in it a God unaware of his own shadow, morally surpassed by his creature; Girard, in Job, the Victim of His People (1985), sees in Job the scapegoat of a unanimous community whose spokesmen are the friends.

What the essay assumes

The essay sets out Jung's reading, for whom Job becomes morally superior to the God who crushes him to win a wager, and compares it with Ivan Karamazov siding with the child against universal harmony; it presents Leibniz's Theodicy and the best of all possible worlds as a way of thinking that asks the individual to sacrifice his complaint; it places Job, in Girard's reading, among the biblical texts that proclaim the innocence of the victim; and it notes, in the debate on wonder, that Schelling quotes Qoheleth, the old book according to which all is toil under the sun, before raising the question of being.

In the essay: Jung · Leibniz · Dostoevsky · Why is there something rather than nothing? A constructed debate

Further reading: Jean Lévêque, Job et son Dieu (1970) ; Gerhard von Rad, Israël et la Sagesse (1970) ; Hermann Gunkel, Introduction aux Psaumes (1933) ; Jean Bottéro, Naissance de Dieu (1986)

The short books: Ruth, Esther, Jonah, Tobit, Judith, Lamentations, Ezra and NehemiahBible: the texts

Between the great collections of the Law, the Prophets and the Psalms, the Bible contains short books, often narrative, which have had a considerable fortune in liturgy, art and literature. Most date from the Persian or Hellenistic period and raise, each in its own way, the question of the faithfulness of a scattered people.

Ruth

In the time of the Judges, a famine drives a family from Bethlehem into Moab. The men die; Naomi returns, and her Moabite daughter-in-law, Ruth, refuses to leave her and adopts her people and her God. She gleans in the field of Boaz, a kinsman of her husband, who marries her according to the custom of redemption. Their son is the grandfather of David. The book, dated by many to the Persian period, makes a foreign woman the ancestor of the king, at a time when other texts forbid mixed marriages. It is read at the Jewish feast of Weeks.

Jonah

God sends Jonah to preach against Nineveh, the Assyrian capital; the prophet flees by ship, is thrown into the sea during the storm, swallowed by a great fish and cast up on the shore three days later. He preaches at last, Nineveh repents, and God forgives, to the great vexation of Jonah, to whom he teaches the lesson of a castor-oil plant that withers. A story with a didactic aim rather than a chronicle, probably from the Persian or Hellenistic period, it opens mercy to the nations. The gospels make the sign of Jonah a figure of Jesus's three days in the tomb.

Esther

At the court of the Persian king, a young Jewish woman, Esther, who has become queen without revealing her origin, foils with her uncle Mordecai the plan of the minister Haman to exterminate the Jews of the empire; Haman is hanged on the gallows he had set up, and the Jews take revenge on their enemies. The Hebrew text never names God; the Greek version adds prayers. The book founds the feast of Purim, and critical scholarship sees in it a diaspora story from the Persian or Hellenistic period, without historical support.

Tobit and Judith

These two books, absent from the Hebrew Bible, are deuterocanonical. Tobit tells of a pious Jew of the deportation, struck blind, whose son travels in the company of the angel Raphael, heals his father and marries Sarah, delivered from a demon: a tale of family piety and almsgiving. Judith, a widow of Bethulia besieged by Holofernes, general of Nebuchadnezzar, goes to his camp, seduces him and cuts off his head; the town is saved. The anachronism, Nebuchadnezzar as king of Assyria, signals an edifying story from the time of the Maccabees. Judith and Holofernes have become a major subject of painting, from Caravaggio to Artemisia Gentileschi.

Lamentations, Ezra and Nehemiah

Lamentations are five poems of mourning over Jerusalem destroyed in 587, four of which are acrostics; tradition attributes them to Jeremiah, critical scholarship to anonymous authors of the exile. Ezra and Nehemiah recount the return under the Persian kings, the rebuilding of the Temple and then of the walls, the public reading of the Law by Ezra and the sending away of foreign wives. Ezra, priest and scribe, became in Jewish tradition the second Moses, the one who restored the Torah; Spinoza, following Ibn Ezra, would later make him the probable compiler of the Pentateuch. Chronicles retells the history from Adam to the exile, centred on David and the Temple.

What the essay assumes

The essay cites none of these books. They nevertheless shed light on two of its threads: Part One, which asks what becomes of the Law when a philosopher reads it, and the section on Maimonides, which distinguishes the apparent sense of Scripture, for the multitude, from the sense reserved for the sage; Ezra, restorer of the Torah according to tradition and compiler of the Pentateuch according to Spinoza, stands at the meeting point of the two. Above all, these books show how the Bible tells the survival of a people without king or land, a question that precedes that of the exit from religion.

In the essay: Part One · Spinoza

Further reading: Thomas Römer, Jean-Daniel Macchi and Christophe Nihan (eds.), Introduction à l'Ancien Testament (2004) ; André LaCocque, Subversives ou un Pentateuque de femmes (1992) ; Pierre Grelot, Introduction à la Bible (1961) ; Shaye J. D. Cohen, From the Maccabees to the Mishnah (1987)

Between the two Testaments: the Maccabees, Enoch, Qumran and apocalyptic expectationBible: the texts

Between the last books of the Hebrew Bible and the first Christian writings stretch nearly four centuries that tradition calls intertestamental. Today they are seen as the matrix from which rabbinic Judaism and Christianity emerge.

The period

Alexander conquers Judaea in 332 BCE. The country passes to the Ptolemies and then, around 200, to the Seleucids. In 167, Antiochus IV Epiphanes forbids Jewish practices and installs a pagan cult in the Temple; the revolt led by Mattathias and his sons, the Maccabees, leads to the purification of the Temple in 164, and then to independence under the Hasmoneans. In 63, Pompey enters Jerusalem; Rome installs Herod the Great (37 to 4 BCE), then Roman prefects govern Judaea, among them Pontius Pilate (26 to 36). The revolt of 66 ends with the destruction of the Temple in 70.

The books

The books of the Maccabees, Tobit, Judith, the Wisdom of Solomon, Sirach and Baruch are received in the Septuagint and in the Catholic and Orthodox canon, where they are called deuterocanonical, but not in the Hebrew Bible nor among Protestants. Other writings, called pseudepigrapha because they are attributed to ancient figures, enter no canon except in Ethiopia: the Book of Enoch, which tells of the fall of the angels, the patriarch's heavenly journeys and the judgement by a Son of Man; Jubilees; the Testaments of the Twelve Patriarchs; the Psalms of Solomon, which await a messiah son of David.

The currents

The historian Flavius Josephus, at the end of the first century, distinguishes three schools that he compares to philosophies: the Pharisees, masters of the oral tradition, who believe in the resurrection; the Sadducees, a priestly aristocracy bound to the Temple, who accept only the written Law and deny the resurrection; the Essenes, a community of shared goods and purity living apart. He adds a fourth philosophy, that of the Zealots, who acknowledge no master but God.

Qumran

In 1947, a Bedouin shepherd finds scrolls in a cave near Qumran, on the shore of the Dead Sea; eleven caves will yield some nine hundred manuscripts. About a quarter are biblical texts, among them a complete scroll of Isaiah, a thousand years older than the Hebrew manuscripts known until then; the rest comprises writings already known, such as Enoch, and texts specific to one community: the Community Rule, the Damascus Document, the Hymns, commentaries that read the prophets as the announcement of their own time and of a persecuted Teacher of Righteousness. Most specialists identify this community with Josephus's Essenes, a hypothesis still debated. The manuscripts show the plurality of ancient Judaism, the expectation of two messiahs, a priest and a king, and a biblical text still fluid before its fixing by the Masoretes. They mention neither Jesus nor the Christians.

Apocalyptic expectation and the Septuagint

From the Book of Daniel to Enoch and Qumran, a worldview called apocalyptic takes shape: the present age is evil and dominated by hostile powers; God will soon intervene to judge; the dead will rise; a heavenly envoy or a messiah will establish the reign of God. John the Baptist, Jesus and Paul speak this language, and historians see in it the matrix of Christianity. In parallel, the diaspora of Egypt translates the Bible into Greek in Alexandria from the third century onwards: the Septuagint is the Bible of Philo, of Paul and of the first Christians.

What the essay assumes

The essay presents Philo as a Jew of Alexandria who reads the Bible in the Septuagint and comments on it by allegory, without having heard of Jesus; it recalls that Paul calls himself a Jew of the elite, more advanced in Judaism than his contemporaries, and places him, with Sanders, within the Judaism of his time; it adopts, with Schweitzer, Sanders, Meier, Fredriksen and Ehrman, the image of Jesus as an apocalyptic prophet awaiting the imminent end of the world. These three points assume familiarity with the Greek diaspora, the currents of Judaism and eschatological expectation.

In the essay: Philo of Alexandria · Paul of Tarsus

Further reading: Geza Vermes, Les Manuscrits du désert de Juda (1953) ; André Paul, Qumrân et les esséniens (2008) ; John J. Collins, The Apocalyptic Imagination (1984) ; Marcel Simon and André Benoit, Le Judaïsme et le christianisme antique (1968)

The New Testament: twenty-seven books, their dates and their authorsBible: the texts

The New Testament brings together twenty-seven books written in Greek between about 50 and 150, which the churches gradually recognized as Scripture. Their order in the Bible is not the order in which they were written, and almost none bears the name of its real author.

Four genres, twenty-seven books

Four Gospels, Matthew, Mark, Luke and John, tell the life, death and resurrection of Jesus; the Acts of the Apostles, the sequel to Luke; twenty-one letters, thirteen under the name of Paul, an anonymous one to the Hebrews, seven called catholic; and finally the Revelation of John. The word gospel, good news, first designates the message, then the books.

The order of composition

The oldest texts are the letters of Paul, written between about 50 and 60: seven are held to be authentic by almost all specialists (Romans, the two to the Corinthians, Galatians, Philippians, the first to the Thessalonians, Philemon); the others are thought to come from disciples. Mark is the oldest Gospel, written c. 70; Matthew and Luke c. 80 to 90; John c. 90 to 100. The Pastoral Epistles, the second letter of Peter and a few others reach down to the beginning or the middle of the second century.

Authors and attributions

The Gospels are anonymous; the titles according to Matthew, according to Mark were added in the second century. The traditional attributions go back to Papias of Hierapolis, c. 120 to 130, who says that Mark put Peter's recollections into writing and that Matthew composed the sayings in the Hebrew language, then to Irenaeus of Lyon, c. 180, who names the four evangelists. Critical scholarship judges these testimonies late and partly incompatible with the texts, since Matthew is written in Greek and depends on Mark.

Language, manuscripts, variants

The New Testament is written in koine, the common Greek of the eastern Mediterranean. The oldest witness is a papyrus fragment of the Gospel of John, P52, usually dated to around 125 to 150; the first complete manuscripts, the Sinaiticus and the Vaticanus, date from the fourth century. The thousands of Greek manuscripts present countless variants, which textual criticism compares in order to reconstruct the oldest text. Two famous passages are missing from the best witnesses: the longer ending of Mark and the episode of the woman taken in adultery in John 8.

The synoptic problem

Matthew, Mark and Luke are called synoptic because they can be read in parallel columns: they share the order of episodes and often the same words, whereas John differs. Explaining these agreements and divergences is the synoptic problem. The dominant solution since the nineteenth century is the two-source hypothesis: Mark was used by Matthew and by Luke; these two also drew on a collection of sayings of Jesus now lost, designated by the letter Q; each finally has material of its own, such as the infancy narratives. The priority of Mark is very widely accepted.

What the Gospels are

The Gospels are not biographies in the modern sense but narratives of faith, composed forty to seventy years after the events, from oral traditions. Historians use them with criteria: embarrassment, that is, what Christians would not have invented, such as the baptism by John or the crucifixion; multiple attestation; coherence with the Judaism of the time. An article in these annexes is devoted to this exegesis.

What the essay assumes

The essay states that Paul's seven authentic letters, written between 50 and 60, are the oldest Christian texts, earlier than all the Gospels; for the existence of Jesus it relies on Paul, on the elements of the Gospels that embarrassed the first Christians, on Josephus and on Tacitus; it sets out the notion of myth in Strauss, a narrative produced without intent to deceive by the faith of a community, and Bauer's thesis that the Gospels are the conscious literary work of one author, the evangelist Mark, on whom the others depend. This last point assumes familiarity with the priority of Mark.

In the essay: Part Two · Did Jesus exist? The Jesus of history and the mythicist thesis · David Friedrich Strauss and Bruno Bauer

Further reading: Raymond E. Brown, Que sait-on du Nouveau Testament ? (1997) ; Daniel Marguerat (ed.), Introduction au Nouveau Testament (2000) ; Bart D. Ehrman, The New Testament (1997) ; Bruce M. Metzger, The Text of the New Testament (1964)

The Gospel of Mark: the oldest accountBible: the texts

The shortest of the four Gospels is also, according to almost all exegetes, the oldest. In sixteen chapters, at a hurried pace, it tells of Jesus' activity in Galilee, his journey up to Jerusalem and his death, and it stops before an empty tomb.

An anonymous text, around 70

The tradition that attributes it to Mark goes back to Papias, bishop of Hierapolis at the beginning of the second century, quoted by Eusebius: Mark is said to have been Peter's interpreter and to have written down his recollections, without order. Historians regard this notice as uncertain and speak of an anonymous Greek-speaking author, writing for non-Jewish readers to whom he explains customs and translates Aramaic words. The most common dating places it around 70, just before or just after the capture of Jerusalem by Titus, since the discourse of chapter 13 announces the ruin of the Temple. The place, Rome according to tradition, or Syria, remains disputed.

The plan

The narrative opens with neither infancy nor genealogy, with John the Baptist in the desert, the baptism of Jesus in the Jordan and the temptation in a single sentence. Galilee follows: the calling of the disciples, exorcisms, healings, meals with tax collectors, controversies with the Pharisees over the sabbath and purity, parables of the Kingdom, the multiplication of the loaves. At the centre of the book, at Caesarea Philippi (Mc 8, 27-30), Peter recognizes Jesus as the Messiah; Jesus orders him to be silent and immediately announces that the Son of Man must suffer. The journey up to Jerusalem is punctuated by three predictions of the Passion, each time misunderstood. The Passion narrative (Mc 14 and 15) takes up nearly a third of the book. On Easter morning, women find the tomb open; a young man in white tells them that Jesus is risen and sends them to Galilee; they flee, seized with fear, and say nothing to anyone (Mc 16, 8). There the text of the oldest manuscripts stops. The longer ending (Mc 16, 9-20) is a second-century addition.

Distinctive features

Mark's Jesus acts more than he speaks; the adverb immediately recurs dozens of times. He is fully human: he grows angry, is astonished, does not know the day of the end, prays in anguish at Gethsemane and dies crying out his abandonment by God. His disciples understand nothing and all desert him. He calls himself the Son of Man, a title drawn from Daniel 7, and imposes silence on the demons, on those he heals and on Peter. William Wrede, in 1901, named this feature the messianic secret and saw in it a construction of the evangelist meant to explain why Jesus had not been recognized as Messiah in his lifetime; the hypothesis, though disputed, left its mark on twentieth-century exegesis.

Composition and reading

Mark assembles units first transmitted orally, which he links with summaries and indications of place. The two-source theory, dominant since the nineteenth century, makes him the common source of Matthew and Luke, who take over most of his text while correcting it. Long held to be an abridgement of Matthew, as Augustine claimed, it was rehabilitated by German criticism, which saw in its priority the way of access to the Jesus of history, before Wrede showed that it too is a work of faith and Schweitzer, in 1906, drew from its eschatology a Jesus who was a prophet of the end.

What the essay assumes

In the section on the existence of Jesus, the essay places Mark, around 70, at the threshold of the series of Gospels running from Mark to John, and recalls that these are texts of faith composed for communities. It sets out the thesis of Strauss, for whom the Gospel narratives are myths formed without intention in a community awaiting the Messiah, and that of Bauer, who on the contrary makes the evangelist Mark the conscious author on whom the others depend. It quotes Renan seeing Galilee as a fifth Gospel and recovering, through the narratives of Matthew and Mark, a human figure. It takes for granted the order in which the Gospels were composed and the fact that Mark has neither an infancy narrative nor an appearance of the risen Jesus.

In the essay: Ernest Renan · David Friedrich Strauss and Bruno Bauer

Further reading: Camille Focant, L'Évangile selon Marc (2004) ; Raymond E. Brown, Que sait-on du Nouveau Testament ? (1997) ; Daniel Marguerat (ed.), Introduction au Nouveau Testament (2000) ; William Wrede, Das Messiasgeheimnis in den Evangelien (1901)

Matthew and Luke: the infancy, the Sermon on the Mount, the parablesBible: the texts

Matthew and Luke, written a generation after Mark, take over the substance of his narrative and enrich it with sayings of Jesus, infancy narratives and parables that have become the best-known pages of the Gospels. They give two distinct portraits of him, one turned towards Israel, the other towards the Greek world.

Two books built on Mark

Historians date both Gospels to the years 80 to 90. They are anonymous: the attribution to Matthew, the tax collector who became an apostle, and to Luke, the companion of Paul, comes from the tradition of the second century. According to the two-source theory, each used Mark, keeping his order and most of his text, and a collection of sayings now lost, which exegetes call Q, from the German Quelle, the source of the Beatitudes, the Lord's Prayer and the love of enemies. Each adds material of his own.

Matthew, the Gospel of Israel

Matthew writes for Christians of Jewish origin, probably in Syria, after the break with the synagogue. He opens with a genealogy going back to Abraham, tells of the visit of the magi, the massacre of the children of Bethlehem and the flight into Egypt, and shows at every stage that the Scriptures are being fulfilled. He groups the teaching of Jesus into five great discourses, like the five books of the Law. The first is the Sermon on the Mount (Mt 5 to 7): the Beatitudes, which call blessed the poor in spirit, the meek, the merciful, the peacemakers, the persecuted; a rereading of the Law that radicalizes its demands; the love of enemies; prayer in secret, with the Lord's Prayer. The last discourse culminates in the judgement scene (Mt 25), in which the Son of Man separates the nations according to what they did to the least. The book ends with the sending of the disciples to all nations.

Luke, the Gospel of the poor and of the nations

Luke, a cultivated Greek writer, opens with a historian's preface. His infancy narrative centres on Mary: the annunciation, the Magnificat, in which God casts down the mighty and lifts up the lowly, the birth in a manger, the shepherds. Luke alone reports the Good Samaritan, who makes a despised foreigner the model of the neighbour, the prodigal son welcomed by his father, Lazarus and the rich man, the good thief. The poor, women and sinners hold a place there that they do not have in the other Gospels. Luke is also the author of the Acts of the Apostles, the second volume of a single work, which carries the proclamation from Jerusalem to Rome.

The temptations in the wilderness

Matthew and Luke develop into three scenes the temptation that Mark mentions in a single sentence (Mt 4, 1-11 ; Lc 4, 1-13). After forty days of fasting, the devil proposes that Jesus turn the stones into bread, throw himself down from the top of the Temple so that the angels will bear him up, and receive all the kingdoms of the world in exchange for a prostration. Each time Jesus answers with a verse from Deuteronomy and refuses bread, wonder and power.

Composition and reading

The infancy narratives do not agree, with two genealogies, magi on one side and shepherds on the other: criticism sees in them theological compositions, which say who Jesus is rather than what happened. The sayings common to the two Gospels, on the contrary, are held to be one of the oldest points of access to the preaching of Jesus.

What the essay assumes

Dostoevsky's Grand Inquisitor, whom the essay regards as the centre of gravity of its question, comments on the three temptations: the cardinal reproaches Christ for having refused bread, miracle and power in the name of freedom; the essay illustrates this with Duccio's panel of the temptation on the mountain. It takes for granted, in Kierkegaard, the gap between the demanding Christianity of the New Testament and the Christendom that abolished it by flourishing; in Engels, the reading of early Christianity as a movement of slaves and the poor, for which Luke's Beatitudes, which call the poor blessed without further qualification, are the usual support; in Simone Weil, who commented on the Lord's Prayer in Waiting for God, a prayer made while thinking that God does not exist.

In the Gallery:The Temptation of Christ on the Mountain (1308-1311, New York, Frick Collection), a panel from the Siena Maestà: the temptations that the Grand Inquisitor takes upIvan Kramskoy, Christ in the Desert (1872, Moscow, Tretyakov Gallery)

In the essay: Dostoevsky · Søren Kierkegaard · Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels · Simone Weil

Further reading: Raymond E. Brown, The Birth of the Messiah (1977) ; Pierre Bonnard, L'Évangile selon saint Matthieu (1963) ; François Bovon, L'Évangile selon saint Luc (1991-2009) ; Daniel Marguerat (ed.), Introduction au Nouveau Testament (2000)

The Gospel of John: the Word made fleshBible: the texts

The fourth Gospel differs from the other three in its language, its plan and its theology: it begins before creation, with the Word beside God, and presents a Jesus who says openly who he is. It is through this Gospel that the Greek vocabulary of the Logos entered the Christian faith.

A milieu, successive stages

The text claims the authority of a witness, the disciple whom Jesus loved, never named, whom the tradition of the second century identifies with the apostle John, writing at Ephesus in his old age. Historians prefer to speak of a Johannine milieu, a community whose founding figure this disciple would have been, and distinguish several stages of composition, of which chapter 21 is an appendix. The final redaction is placed around 90 to 100; a papyrus copied in Egypt in the first half of the second century shows that the book circulated early.

The prologue

Eighteen verses open the book like a hymn. In the beginning was the Word, the Logos; he was with God, he was God; all things were made through him; in him was life, and the life was the light of men (Jn 1, 1-5). Then the Word became flesh and dwelt among us (Jn 1, 14). The vocabulary meets that of personified Wisdom in the wisdom books and that of Philo of Alexandria, for whom the Logos is the intermediary between God and the world; the affirmation of the incarnation has no equivalent in either.

Signs and discourses

John reports neither parables nor exorcisms, but seven signs chosen for their meaning: the water changed into wine at Cana, the multiplication of the loaves, the man born blind, the raising of Lazarus (Jn 11), which leads the authorities to resolve on his death. The signs come with long discourses: to Nicodemus on being born from above, to the Samaritan woman on living water, to the crowd on the bread of life, to the Pharisees on the good shepherd. In the I am sayings, Jesus declares himself the bread of life, the light of the world, the resurrection and the life. The farewell discourse (Jn 13 to 17) promises the Spirit.

The Passion and the appearances

The Johannine Jesus remains master of events: he gives himself up, carries his cross alone, entrusts his mother to the disciple and dies saying that all is accomplished. The dialogue with Pilate concerns kingship and truth. On Easter morning, Mary Magdalene finds the tomb empty, then recognizes Jesus when he calls her by name. In the evening he appears to the disciples; Thomas, who is absent, declares that he will not believe without putting his finger into the wounds. Eight days later Jesus invites him to do so; Thomas confesses him as his Lord and his God, and Jesus calls blessed those who believe without having seen (Jn 20, 24-29).

Composition and reading

Compared with the synoptics, John spreads the ministry over three Passovers, places the cleansing of the Temple at the beginning rather than the end, has Jesus die on the eve of Passover, at the hour when the lambs are slaughtered, and gives the teaching a meditative rather than proverbial form. Clement of Alexandria called it the spiritual Gospel. The gnostics were the first to comment on it, Origen and Augustine made it the book of the theology of the Word, Bultmann read a gnostic milieu in it; recent scholarship stresses its Jewish roots.

What the essay assumes

The essay quotes Jn 1, 1 and Jn 1, 14 in the section on Philo: the concept of the Logos was available to the Jewish philosopher, but the sentence on the Word made flesh is what Philo could never have written, and it is through this encounter that Christianity entered philosophy. In the section on Hume, Caravaggio's painting of the incredulity of Thomas illustrates the faith that demands proof, set against the question of testimony to miracles. Part Ten, which opposes the God of the philosophers to a God before whom one can pray and dance, takes for granted that it was from John's Word that the Fathers united biblical faith with the Greek vocabulary of being.

In the Gallery:The Incredulity of Saint Thomas (c. 1601-1602, Potsdam, Sanssouci, Picture Gallery): faith that demands proof (Jn 20, 24-29), set against Hume's question of testimony to miracles

In the essay: Philo of Alexandria · Hume · Part Ten · Heidegger
