Further reading: Raymond E. Brown, The Gospel According to John (1966-1970) ; Rudolf Bultmann, Das Evangelium des Johannes (1941) ; Xavier Léon-Dufour, Lecture de l'Évangile selon Jean (1988-1996) ; Jean Zumstein, L'Évangile selon saint Jean (2007-2014)

The Passion and the Resurrection: the four accounts and what history retainsBible: the texts

The four Gospels converge in their final part: a week in Jerusalem, an arrest at night, a trial, a crucifixion, a tomb found empty. It is the oldest core of the Christian tradition and the point at which history and faith part most clearly.

The week

Jesus enters Jerusalem before Passover, riding a donkey, acclaimed by pilgrims. He drives the merchants out of the Temple. On the Thursday evening he takes a last meal with the Twelve, the Last Supper: in the Synoptic Gospels, he breaks the bread and passes the cup, identifying them with his body and his blood, a gesture that Paul already reports around 55 (1 Co 11, 23-25); John puts the washing of the feet in its place. In the garden of Gethsemane he prays in anguish while the disciples sleep; Judas arrives with a troop and points him out with a kiss. The disciples flee; Peter denies knowing him three times. Before the Sanhedrin, Jesus is questioned about the Temple and his messianic claim, then taken in the morning to Pontius Pilate, prefect of Judaea. Pilate offers to release a prisoner, the crowd demands Barabbas. Jesus is scourged, then crucified at Golgotha between two robbers, under an inscription that calls him king of the Jews. The words on the cross differ: Mark and Matthew keep only the cry of Psalm 22 about being forsaken by God, Luke the forgiveness of the executioners, John the completion. Joseph of Arimathea lays the body in a tomb closed with a stone.

The Easter accounts

On Sunday at dawn some women, Mary Magdalene at their head, find the stone rolled away and the tomb empty; one or two messengers announce to them that Jesus has risen. Mark stops there, on their flight and their silence. Matthew places the appearances in Galilee, Luke in Jerusalem, with the disciples at Emmaus, John in the garden and then before Thomas. The witnesses, the places and the duration do not agree; the four accounts agree on the empty tomb, on the women as the first witnesses and on a presence that the disciples do not at first recognize.

What history retains

Historians, whatever their convictions, hold it as established that Jesus was crucified in Jerusalem on the orders of Pontius Pilate, around 30, during a Passover festival. Tacitus reports it around 116, Josephus around 93, and crucifixion, a punishment for slaves and a mark of infamy, is not one of those facts that a community invents for its Messiah. The cross is a Roman punishment; the Gospels, written after the break with the synagogue, shift the responsibility towards the Jewish authorities and the crowd. The resurrection belongs to another order: the historical method can record a tomb found empty, experiences of appearances and the birth of a faith, without being able to pronounce on their cause. The oldest testimony is not a Gospel but a formula that Paul says he received and that he passes on around 55: Christ died for our sins according to the Scriptures, he was buried, he was raised on the third day, he appeared to Cephas, then to the Twelve (1 Co 15, 3-5).

What the essay assumes

The essay retains, in the section on the existence of Jesus, the crucifixion under Pontius Pilate as the consensus of historians, attested by Tacitus, who gives Pilate an inaccurate title, and by Josephus, and it takes the formula of 1 Co 15 as the oldest testimony to the content of the faith. In Hegel, it reads in Faith and Knowledge (1802) the sentence on the death of God himself, which Hegel will connect with the Lutheran hymn only in his lectures, and the speculative Good Friday as a death of God followed by a resurrection in the concept. In the section on Dostoevsky, Holbein's dead Christ (1521-1522), a copy of which hangs in Rogozhin's house, is the painting before which Prince Myshkin says, in The Idiot, that one could lose one's faith. In Girard, the Passion is a unanimous lynching, in which even the disciples fail, but told by those who know the victim to be innocent. In Nietzsche, the essay quotes the sentence from The Antichrist about the only Christian, who died on the cross.

In the Gallery:The Last Supper (1495-1498, Milan, refectory of Santa Maria delle Grazie): the bread and the wine, which the last pages of Das Wesen des Christenthums restore to their natural and human meaningChrist Crucified (c. 1632, Madrid, Prado): the speculative Good Friday, in which Hegel takes up the “Gott selbst ist tot” of the Lutheran hymnThe Body of the Dead Christ in the Tomb (1521-1522, Basel, Kunstmuseum): before a copy of this painting in Rogozhin's house, Prince Myshkin says, in The Idiot (1868-1869), that it could make one lose one's faiththe Crucifixion from the Isenheim Altarpiece (1512-1516, Colmar, Musée Unterlinden): Karl Barth kept a reproduction of this panel above his desk, and the outstretched index finger of John the Baptist served him as a figure of witnessThe Resurrection (c. 1463-1465, Sansepolcro, Museo Civico): the Christ of Easter faith, whom historical research distinguishes from the Jesus of historyThe Magdalen with the Smoking Flame, known as the Terff Magdalen (c. 1640-1645, Paris, Musée du Louvre): the skull, the books and the flame, a meditation on death that joins the ancient spiritual exercise in Hadot's sense

In the essay: Did Jesus exist? The Jesus of history and the mythicist thesis · Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel · Dostoevsky · Girard · Nietzsche

Further reading: Raymond E. Brown, The Death of the Messiah (1994) ; Simon Légasse, Le Procès de Jésus (1994-1995) ; Paula Fredriksen, Jesus of Nazareth, King of the Jews (1999) ; Dale C. Allison, Resurrecting Jesus (2005)

The teaching of Jesus: the Kingdom, the parables, the miraclesBible: the texts

Before being the Christ of the creeds, Jesus is, in the Gospels, an itinerant preacher from Galilee who proclaims the Kingdom of God, speaks in parables, heals and eats with the excluded. These notes summarize what the first three Gospels report of his teaching and the way historians read it.

John the Baptist and the Kingdom

The Gospels begin Jesus' activity with his baptism in the Jordan, received from John, an ascetic prophet who announces an imminent judgement and calls for repentance, and whom Josephus also knows. After John's arrest, Jesus preaches in Galilee that the Kingdom of God has come near (Mc 1, 15); Matthew writes Kingdom of Heaven out of respect for the divine name. The Kingdom is not a place but the reign of God that is coming: one prays for its coming in the Lord's Prayer, and yet it is said to be already present in the exorcisms and in the midst of the listeners. Historians have been divided for a century: Albert Schweitzer (1906) sees in it the expectation of an imminent end of the world, which did not happen; C. H. Dodd (1935) a realized eschatology, the Kingdom already there in the person of Jesus; most today hold the two aspects together.

The parables

Jesus teaches in parables, short stories taken from the life of fields, houses and markets: the sower, the mustard seed that becomes a tree, the tares left until the harvest, the hidden treasure, the talents, the labourers of the eleventh hour paid like the first. The parable does not define the Kingdom; it gives a glimpse of it through a comparison that surprises and overturns expectation, and it leaves the listener to decide.

The miracles

The Gospels report healings, exorcisms, raisings of the dead and nature miracles. Historians observe that the reputation of healer and exorcist is one of the best-attested traits, acknowledged even by adversaries, who explain it by magic, like Celsus, or by the prince of demons, like the scribes in Mark; they leave the interpretation of the facts open. In the Gospels, the miracles are signs of the Kingdom rather than proofs, and Jesus refuses to give any to those who demand them.

The meals, the Law, the enemies

Jesus eats with publicans, tax collectors in the service of Rome, and with sinners, which scandalizes the Pharisees; women follow him and support him. He does not reject the Law but discusses it from within: the sabbath is made for man, purity comes from the heart, the greatest commandment is the love of God and of one's neighbour. He calls for love of one's enemies and for forgiveness without limit. He most often calls himself the Son of Man, an expression that can mean simply a human being, but which also refers to the figure coming on the clouds in Daniel 7, to whom an everlasting kingdom is given.

Composition and reading

These materials come from Mark and from the sayings source shared by Matthew and Luke; John replaces them with signs and discourses. Each age has drawn from them a different Jesus, a teacher of morality, a gentle Galilean, a prophet of the end.

What the essay assumes

The essay draws on this teaching through its readers. Kant makes Christ the ideal of humanity well-pleasing to God, which reason carries within itself and recognizes in the Gospel narrative, so that historicity becomes indifferent. Renan holds miracle to be impossible to establish, because no wonder-worker has ever performed before a commission of scientists, and rediscovers in Galilee a human figure in the narratives of Matthew and Mark. Nietzsche, in The Antichrist, describes a Jesus without resentment or dogma who lives in an inner kingdom without preaching it. The section on the existence of Jesus retains Schweitzer's apocalyptic prophet, taken up by Meier and Fredriksen. Simone Weil, finally, is cited for her refusal of consolation; attention, which Murdoch borrows from her, is for her a religious category, in its highest degree identical with prayer.

In the Gallery:The Good Shepherd, painting in the Crypt of Lucina, Catacomb of Callixtus (Rome, 3rd century CE), plate from Joseph Wilpert, Die Malereien der Katakomben Roms (1903): the imagery of the Church that Celsus attacks and that Origen defends in Against Celsus (c. 248)

In the essay: Kant · Ernest Renan · Nietzsche · Did Jesus exist? The Jesus of history and the mythicist thesis · Simone Weil

Further reading: E. P. Sanders, Jesus and Judaism (1985) ; John P. Meier, A Marginal Jew (1991-2016) ; Joachim Jeremias, Les Paraboles de Jésus (1947) ; C. H. Dodd, The Parables of the Kingdom (1935)

The Acts of the Apostles: from Jerusalem to RomeBible: the texts

The Acts of the Apostles is the only book of the New Testament that narrates what follows the death of Jesus: the birth of a community in Jerusalem, then the spread of the message as far as Rome. The second volume of the Gospel of Luke, it was composed c. 80 to 90 by an anonymous author whom tradition identifies with the physician who was Paul's companion.

Jerusalem: the Spirit and the community

The narrative opens with the Ascension: forty days after Easter, the risen Christ is taken up into heaven and promises the Spirit (Ac 1). At Pentecost, a Jewish feast celebrated fifty days after Passover, the Spirit descends on the disciples in the form of tongues of fire; pilgrims who have come from the whole diaspora each hear them in their own language (Ac 2). Peter preaches and baptizes. Luke then describes the Jerusalem community in idealized tableaux: prayer, the breaking of bread, and believers who sell their goods and lay the proceeds at the apostles' feet, so that no one is in need (Ac 2 and 4). This community of goods has its reverse side: Ananias and Sapphira, who keep back part of the price of their field while claiming to give everything, fall dead (Ac 5). Seven men, among them Stephen and Philip, are chosen to serve the Greek-speaking widows; Stephen, after a speech that rereads the history of Israel as a series of refusals, is stoned before the eyes of a young man named Saul (Ac 6 and 7).

Saul, Cornelius and the assembly of Jerusalem

The conversion of Saul on the road to Damascus is told three times, by the narrator and then by Paul himself (Ac 9, 22 and 26): a light, a voice, three days of blindness, baptism. Peter, warned by a vision, baptizes the centurion Cornelius, the first pagan received without circumcision (Ac 10). The question of the pagans is settled at the assembly that tradition calls the Council of Jerusalem, c. 48 or 49: Peter, James and Paul agree not to impose circumcision on converts from the nations, asking them instead to abstain from meat sacrificed to idols, from blood and from sexual immorality (Ac 15).

Paul's journeys and the road to Rome

The second half follows Paul: Asia Minor, Philippi, Athens, where he delivers before the Areopagus a speech on the unknown god (Ac 17), Corinth, Ephesus. Back in Jerusalem, he is arrested in the Temple and held for two years at Caesarea. As a Roman citizen, he appeals to Caesar; the crossing ends in a shipwreck on Malta. The book closes on Paul preaching in Rome; his death is not narrated.

History and apology

Historians compare Acts with Paul's letters, which are older and direct. The agreements are many, such as the proconsul Gallio, whom an inscription at Delphi dates to c. 51, but so are the discrepancies: in the letter to the Galatians, Paul insists on his independence from Jerusalem, where Luke shows him compliant; the conflict at Antioch between Peter and Paul is passed over in silence; the speeches are the author's compositions, after the custom of ancient historians. Luke writes an apologetic history: a unanimous Church, led by the Spirit, harmless to Rome, whose magistrates always release the Christians.

Readings

Engels, in 1894, rereads the first communities as a movement of the oppressed comparable to the workers' movement; the community of goods in Ac 2 and 4 nourished, from Thomas More to the Christian socialisms of the nineteenth century, the idea of an original communism, which historians reduce to a local and short-lived practice. The Athens speech served as a model for the apologists of the second century, Justin first among them, who present the faith in the language of the philosophers.

What the essay assumes

The essay reads Paul through his letters and treats Acts, written a generation later, as a narrative to be handled with caution; it draws on the Areopagus speech, which it takes to be Luke's composition, as the scene in which Athens laughs at the resurrection, and on Engels's formula about early Christianity. It takes as known the setting of Paul's mission, and martyrdom as the refusal of a cult, of which Stephen is the first case.

In the Gallery:The Conversion of Saint Paul (1600-1601, Rome, Santa Maria del Popolo, Cerasi Chapel): Paul, the “Dysangelist” of Der Antichrist

In the essay: Paul of Tarsus · Justin Martyr · Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels · Auguste Comte

Further reading: Daniel Marguerat, La Première Histoire du christianisme. Les Actes des apôtres (1999) ; Jacques Dupont, Études sur les Actes des Apôtres (1967) ; Ernst Haenchen, Die Apostelgeschichte (1956) ; Richard I. Pervo, Acts. A Commentary (2009)

The letters of Paul: Romans, Corinthians and the othersBible: the texts

The letters of Paul, written around 50 to 60, are the oldest surviving Christian writings. Addressed to particular communities, they have become the foundation of Christian theology and the object of its greatest quarrels.

The corpus

Thirteen letters bear Paul's name. Critical scholarship holds seven to be authentic: the First Letter to the Thessalonians, the oldest, c. 50; Galatians; the two letters to the Corinthians; Philippians; the note to Philemon; Romans, c. 56 to 58. Three are disputed and called deutero-Pauline: Colossians, Ephesians, 2 Thessalonians, perhaps written by disciples. The three pastoral epistles, 1 and 2 Timothy and Titus, addressed to leaders and concerned with Church organization, are held to be late, from the beginning of the second century. The Letter to the Hebrews is no longer attributed to Paul. The letters, dictated to a secretary, are read to the assembly and exchanged between Churches.

Romans

The longest and most carefully constructed letter, addressed to a community that Paul did not found. All, Jews and Greeks, are under sin; the justice of God is manifested through faith in Christ, independently of the works of the Law (Rm 3 and 4); Adam and Christ correspond to each other (Rm 5); the divided self does not do the good it wants (Rm 7). Chapters 9 to 11 deal with Israel: Paul affirms that the gifts and the calling of God are irrevocable and that all Israel will be saved. Then comes the exhortation: to offer one's body as a living sacrifice, a reasonable or spiritual worship depending on the translation (Rm 12, 1), then submission to the authorities, which come from God (Rm 13).

Corinthians, Galatians, Philippians

The First Letter to the Corinthians deals with the divisions between partisans of Paul, of Apollos and of Cephas, and sets the folly of the cross against the wisdom the Greeks seek and the signs the Jews demand (1 Co 1); then with marriage, with the Lord's supper, of which it transmits the oldest account (1 Co 11), with the body of many members (1 Co 12), with love, which never passes away (1 Co 13), and with the resurrection of the dead, with the received creed and the list of appearances (1 Co 15). Galatians is a letter of anger against those who impose circumcision: Paul's calling, the meeting with Cephas and James, the Law as a pedagogue, neither Jew nor Greek. Philippians, written in prison, is a letter of joy; its chapter 2 quotes a hymn, probably earlier than Paul, in which Christ, being of divine condition, empties himself, takes the condition of a slave even unto death on the cross, and is then exalted: this is kenosis, from the Greek kenôsis, emptying.

The great readings

Augustine reads Rm 7 and 9 in the Pelagian controversy and draws from them grace and predestination. Luther finds in Rm 1, 17 justification by faith alone; the Reformation is born of a reading of Romans. Barth, in 1919 and then 1922, breaks with liberal theology through his Römerbrief. Nietzsche, in The Antichrist, makes Paul the inventor of Christianity, who replaced the practice of Jesus with the cross and the resurrection. Since Sanders (1977), a body of research often called the new perspective places Paul back within the Judaism of his time: justification concerns the admission of the Gentiles, not a religion of performance; philosophers (Taubes, Badiou, Agamben) have reread Paul as a thinker of the universal.

What the essay assumes

The essay adopts the consensus on the seven letters, the dating between 50 and 60, the creed of 1 Co 15 and the meeting of Ga 1, 19 as testimonies about Jesus, the folly of the cross of 1 Co 1, 22-23 as the formula of the impossible meeting of Athens and Jerusalem, Sanders's reading against the Lutheran image, and Rm 12, 1 as the text in which bloody sacrifice becomes self-offering. It reads Barth through the Römerbrief and the infinite qualitative difference, Nietzsche through paragraph 42 of The Antichrist, and Weil through decreation, a God who renounces being everything, which is compared with the hymn of Philippians.

In the Gallery:Rembrandt (and workshop?), The Apostle Paul (c. 1657, Washington, National Gallery of Art): the apostle meditating before his letters, the sword of his martyrdom in his hand

In the essay: Paul of Tarsus · Barth · Nietzsche · Simone Weil

Further reading: E. P. Sanders, Paul and Palestinian Judaism (1977) ; Simon Légasse, Paul apôtre (1991) ; Jacob Taubes, La Théologie politique de Paul (1993) ; Daniel Marguerat (ed.), Introduction au Nouveau Testament (2000)

Hebrews, James, Peter, John, Jude: the other lettersBible: the texts

Alongside the gospels and the letters of Paul, the New Testament contains eight shorter and less read letters. Seven of them have been called, since Eusebius, the Catholic Epistles, that is, universal, because they are addressed to all the Churches and not to a single community; the eighth, the Epistle to the Hebrews, was long counted among Paul's.

Hebrews: Christ the high priest

The text is anonymous; Origen already said that God alone knows who wrote it. It is a learned homily, in the most polished Greek of the New Testament, composed before 95, the date at which Clement of Rome quotes it. Its thesis is that Christ surpasses all the former mediators: the angels, Moses, the priests of the line of Levi. He is high priest according to the order of Melchizedek, the priest-king whom Genesis has meet Abraham and whom Psalm 110 evokes, and he has entered once for all into the heavenly sanctuary with his own blood. This single sacrifice renders unnecessary the sacrifices that the Temple repeated every year (He 7 to 10) and inaugurates the new covenant announced by Jeremiah. Chapter 11 unfolds the gallery of the ancients who believed without seeing the promise.

James: faith without works

Attributed to James, whom tradition identifies with the brother of the Lord, the epistle is a series of wisdom exhortations close to the Sermon on the Mount: trial, the poor given preference in the assembly, the tongue that sets ablaze, the stolen wages of the harvesters. Its second chapter maintains that faith without works is dead and that Abraham was justified by works, which seems to contradict Rm 3 and 4; many see in it a response to a distorted reading of Paul. Luther, in 1522, judged it an epistle of straw compared with the writings that preach Christ, and relegated it to the end of his New Testament together with Hebrews, Jude and Revelation.

Peter, John, Jude

The First Epistle of Peter is addressed to the elect strangers scattered through Asia Minor: exile, unjust suffering in the image of Christ, and the royal priesthood of the baptized (1 P 2, 9). The Second Epistle of Peter, which takes up Jude, is the latest text of the canon, from around 120 to 150: it answers the scoffers who ask where the promise of the Lord's coming is, and recalls that a thousand years are to him as one day. The three letters of John, close to the fourth gospel, affirm that God is love, denounce antichrists who have gone out from the community and require that Christ be confessed as come in the flesh. Jude, brief and vehement, quotes the book of Enoch.

Pseudepigraphy

Writing under the name of a master was common in Jewish and Greek antiquity: the Pythagorean schools, Daniel, Enoch. That 1 and 2 Peter, James or Jude are probably pseudepigraphical tells us about their date and milieu; it does not imply that their content is worthless or that their traditions are late. Ehrman nonetheless judges the practice closer to forgery than classical criticism admits.

Readings

The quarrel over faith and works set, in the sixteenth century, the Lutheran reading of Romans against James, which the Council of Trent invoked in its decree on justification (1547). Girard, in Things Hidden since the Foundation of the World (1978), rejected Hebrews as the text that reintroduced a sacrificial reading of the Passion; he went back on this judgement in the 1990s and admitted that the epistle conceives sacrifice in a new sense, that of the gift of self which puts an end to sacrifices.

What the essay assumes

The essay reports that Girard long refused to call the Passion a sacrifice, then accepted the word in a new sense after his exchange with Raymund Schwager: the vocabulary at stake is that of Hebrews, which the essay does not name. It reads in Paul, in Rm 12, 1, the spiritualized sacrifice; in Weber, a Puritanism that abolishes the sacraments as means of salvation; in Barth, religion defined as an attempt to justify oneself by works, rites and thoughts.

In the essay: Weber · Barth

Further reading: Albert Vanhoye, Prêtres anciens, prêtre nouveau selon le Nouveau Testament (1980) ; Raymond E. Brown, Que sait-on du Nouveau Testament ? (1997) ; Bart D. Ehrman, Forgery and Counterforgery (2013) ; Ceslas Spicq, L'Épître aux Hébreux (1952)

The Revelation of John: visions, the beast and the heavenly JerusalemBible: the texts

The last book of the New Testament, the Revelation of John is also the strangest: a series of coded visions announcing the fall of an empire and the descent of a heavenly city. Its Greek title, apokalupsis, means unveiling, and it is from this book that the word took on its sense of catastrophe.

A genre and an author

Apocalyptic is a Jewish genre born in the second century BCE, with the book of Daniel and the books of Enoch: a seer receives visions, an angel interprets them, empires are beasts, numbers have a meaning, and the present crisis is the prelude to a divine intervention. The author names himself John, exiled on Patmos, and presents himself as a prophet, not as an apostle. Justin and Irenaeus identify him with the apostle, the son of Zebedee; as early as the third century, Dionysius of Alexandria distinguishes the seer from the evangelist on grounds of style, and most critics follow him. The most commonly accepted date is c. 95, under Domitian, on the testimony of Irenaeus; some place it under Nero or Vespasian.

The structure

The book opens with seven letters to the Churches of Asia, from Ephesus to Laodicea (Ap 2 and 3). Then the seer is carried before the throne of God; only the slain Lamb is worthy to open the sealed book (Ap 4 and 5). Three series of seven plagues follow one another: the seals, the first four of which release the horsemen, the trumpets, the bowls. At the centre, a woman clothed with the sun gives birth to a child whom the dragon seeks to devour (Ap 12); a beast rises from the sea, another from the earth, and the number of the beast, 666, corresponds in Hebrew letters to Nero Caesar according to the most common reading (Ap 13). Babylon the great, a harlot seated on seven hills, falls (Ap 17 and 18). The rider named the Word of God wins the battle; Satan is bound for a thousand years while the risen martyrs reign with Christ (Ap 20), the passage from which millenarianism comes; there follow the judgement before the book of life, then a new heaven and a new earth, and the heavenly Jerusalem coming down from God, without a temple, where every tear is wiped away (Ap 21 and 22).

Aim and reception

The book consoles Churches exposed to the imperial cult and to local persecutions, and denounces Rome under the name of Babylon; it calls for the endurance of the martyrs, not for armed revolt. Its reception was difficult in the East, where Eusebius ranks it among the disputed books; it was accepted early in the West. Augustine, in book XX of The City of God, reads the thousand years as the present time of the Church and puts an end to the literal millenarianism that Papias, Justin and Irenaeus had professed. Luther, in 1522, denies it apostolic character; Calvin did not comment on it.

Political and philosophical legacy

Millenarianism revives in the Middle Ages: Joachim of Fiore, who died in 1202, divides history into three ages, of the Father, of the Son and of the Spirit, and announces for the last a spiritual Church; the Franciscan Spirituals, the Taborites, Thomas Müntzer and the Anabaptists of Münster (1534-1535) await the reign of the saints on earth, movements that Norman Cohn traced in The Pursuit of the Millennium (1957). Hence the twentieth-century debate on the secularization of eschatology: Löwith and Voegelin see in the philosophies of history the end of time transposed into history; Blumenberg replies that the apocalyptic end breaks in from outside, whereas modern progress is immanent.

What the essay assumes

The essay uses the words eschatology, eschaton, end of time and imminent expectation without describing the book they come from: in Löwith, the philosophy of history is Christian eschatology minus God; in Voegelin, the genealogy runs through Joachim; in Blumenberg, three conditions are lacking for speaking of a transfer. In the section on the existence of Jesus, it retains from Schweitzer a Jesus who is an apocalyptic prophet awaiting the imminent end of the world, and in Paul an expectation of Christ's return within the lifetime of his readers.

In the essay: Karl Löwith · Voegelin and Aron · Hans Blumenberg · Part Eleven

Further reading: Pierre Prigent, L'Apocalypse de saint Jean (1981) ; Norman Cohn, The Pursuit of the Millennium (1957) ; Elisabeth Schüssler Fiorenza, The Book of Revelation. Justice and Judgment (1985) ; Jean Delumeau, Mille ans de bonheur (1995)

The apocryphal gospels: Thomas, Peter, Mary, Judas and the othersBible: the texts

The name apocryphal gospels is given to the narratives or collections of sayings of Jesus that the Churches did not receive into their canon. The Greek word apokryphos means hidden: it first designates books reserved for initiates, a sense some gnostics claimed, then, among the Fathers, books not read in the assembly and of suspect origin.

The main families

The sayings gospels have no Passion narrative. The most important is the Gospel of Thomas, 114 sayings attributed to Jesus, known from Greek fragments found at Oxyrhynchus since 1897 and from a complete Coptic version discovered at Nag Hammadi in 1945; half of its sayings have a parallel in the synoptics, the other half have an ascetic and gnosticizing colouring. Its date divides the schools, from 60 to 150, depending on whether it is held to be independent of the canonical Gospels or dependent on them. The Gospel of Peter, a fragment of which was found at Akhmim in 1886, tells the Passion with a cross that speaks; around 200, Bishop Serapion of Antioch forbade its reading. The gnostic gospels have the risen Jesus speak to a few disciples: the Gospel of Mary, in which Mary Magdalene receives a revelation that Peter contests; the Gospel of Philip, a Valentinian collection on the sacraments; the Gospel of Judas, in Coptic, published in 2006, in which Judas is read now as the disciple who understands, now as a demon. The infancy gospels fill the silences: the Protevangelium of James, from the second century, tells of the birth of Mary, of her parents Anne and Joachim, of her presentation in the Temple, of old Joseph the widower, and it is the source of several Marian feasts and of their iconography; the Infancy Gospel of Thomas shows a child Jesus bringing clay birds to life. The Jewish-Christian gospels, of the Hebrews, of the Nazarenes, of the Ebionites, are known only through quotations in Clement, Origen, Jerome and Epiphanius. Marcion, around 140, had retained only an abridged Luke. To these are added the agrapha, sayings of Jesus quoted outside the Gospels, as in Ac 20, 35, and the Egerton papyrus, around 150.

What they contribute

On the Jesus of history, little: historians date these texts to the second and third centuries, and only Thomas is debated, some looking in it for a few independent sayings. On second-century Christianity, a great deal: the diversity of currents, the conflicts of authority between Peter and Mary or Thomas, popular piety and its taste for the marvellous, the sources of Christian iconography, the ox and the ass of the crib, the descent into hell, Veronica, which come from yet other apocrypha.

Why outside the canon

These writings are later than the four Gospels, tied to particular currents, gnostic or encratite, without wide liturgical use, and held to be non-apostolic despite the names they bear. The Churches retained the books read everywhere and for a long time, in conformity with the rule of faith; the rest was copied in the margins, translated, read in private, and sometimes buried, as at Nag Hammadi.

Modern readings

Strauss, in 1835, works on the four canonical Gospels and draws nothing from the apocrypha; nor does Bauer, who looks for the conscious author behind the synoptics; Renan barely cites them. The discoveries of Nag Hammadi and the publication of Judas have fed a contemporary fascination, of which The Da Vinci Code (2003) is the example, for gospels hidden by the Church; historians, Ehrman among them, reply that these late texts teach nothing new about Jesus and a great deal about his readers.

What the essay assumes

The essay reasons on the canonical sources, the letters of Paul, the Gospels from Mark around 70 to John at the end of the first century, Josephus and Tacitus, and does not mention the apocrypha; it takes from Ehrman the consensus on the existence of Jesus, and from Celsus a polemic, Jesus son of a soldier named Panthera, which circulates outside the Gospels. When it opposes Strauss's collective myth to Bauer's conscious author, or describes in Onfray a Jesus as concept, it takes for granted this profusion of second-century gospels, which shows how the life of a founder was written at the time.

In the essay: Celsus and Origen · David Friedrich Strauss and Bruno Bauer

Further reading: François Bovon and Pierre Geoltrain (eds.), Écrits apocryphes chrétiens (1997-2005) ; Bart D. Ehrman, Lost Christianities (2003) ; Elaine Pagels, The Gnostic Gospels (1979) ; Jean-Daniel Kaestli and Daniel Marguerat (eds.), Le Mystère apocryphe (1995)

How the canon was formed: criteria, lists and excluded textsBible: the texts

The canon of the New Testament, that is, the list of the twenty-seven books that the Churches hold to be Scripture, was not fixed by a decision but by a usage of more than two centuries, which lists later recorded. The Greek word kanôn designates the mason's rule, then the norm, then the catalogue.

The stages

Paul's letters circulated very early from one Church to another and were gathered into a collection before the end of the first century; the second letter of Peter already counts them among the Scriptures. Around 150, Justin reports that on Sundays the memoirs of the apostles, that is, the gospels, are read alongside the prophets. The first to draw up a closed list is Marcion, c. 140: a single gospel, an abridged Luke, and ten letters of Paul, excluding the whole Old Testament; his gesture forces the Churches to say what they receive. Irenaeus of Lyon, c. 180, asserts that there can be only four gospels, as there are four winds, against the Gnostic writings. The Muratorian Canon, a Latin fragment published in Milan in 1740 and dated by most scholars to the late second century, lists the bulk of the present collection, without Hebrews, James or the letters of Peter, and with an Apocalypse of Peter; it admits the Shepherd of Hermas for private reading, because it is recent. Origen, in the third century, distinguishes the books acknowledged by all, the disputed books and the spurious ones; Eusebius, c. 325, takes up this classification. The first list exactly matching ours is the thirty-ninth festal letter of Athanasius of Alexandria, announcing the date of Easter for the year 367. The African synods of Hippo (393) and Carthage (397), in which Augustine took part, confirm it for the West. In Syria, the Peshitta long ignores five of these books; the Church of Ethiopia keeps a broader canon.

The criteria

Four criteria recur among the Fathers: apostolicity, a book written by an apostle or one of his companions, Mark for Peter, Luke for Paul; conformity with the rule of faith handed down in the Churches; reception, that is, ancient and widespread liturgical use; antiquity, which excludes Hermas. They serve less to choose than to justify after the fact a usage already established.

The disputed books and the excluded books

Seven books were slow to gain acceptance: Hebrews, Revelation, James, 2 Peter, 2 and 3 John, Jude. Four others were for a time read as Scripture and then set aside: the Shepherd of Hermas and the letter of Barnabas, copied in the Codex Sinaiticus in the fourth century; the Didache; the first letter of Clement, present in the Codex Alexandrinus. The apocryphal gospels never appeared in any Church list.

The Reformations and Trent

Luther, in 1522, puts Hebrews, James, Jude and Revelation at the end of the volume, unnumbered, but does not remove them; the Reformed exclude from the Old Testament the Greek books absent from the Hebrew Bible, Tobit, Judith, Wisdom, Sirach, Baruch, Maccabees, which they call apocrypha and print separately. The Council of Florence had given the list in 1442; the Council of Trent, on 8 April 1546, defines it for the Catholic Church, deuterocanonical books included, with the Vulgate as the authentic text.

A legend to dismiss

The Council of Nicaea, in 325, did not fix the canon: its acts deal with Arius and the date of Easter. The idea of a sorting of the books at Nicaea comes from a late anecdote, mocked by Voltaire and popularized by The Da Vinci Code.

What the essay assumes

The essay follows the dialogue between Celsus and Origen, and later Porphyry contesting the date of Daniel, without saying which books were then received; it reports that Augustine grounds understanding on prior faith and that religion, according to Montaigne, is received through the custom of the country, which applies first of all to its books. It itself works with a canon sorted by criticism: seven letters of Paul held to be authentic, Mark c. 70, John at the end of the first century, and it contrasts, in Strauss and Bauer, two ways of explaining the formation of the gospels.

In the essay: Celsus and Origen · Porphyry and Augustine · Part Two · Montaigne

Further reading: Bruce M. Metzger, The Canon of the New Testament (1987) ; Hans von Campenhausen, Die Entstehung der christlichen Bibel (1968) ; Yves-Marie Blanchard, Aux sources du canon, le témoignage d'Irénée (1993) ; Lee Martin McDonald, The Biblical Canon (2007)

How the Bible has been read: allegory, the four senses, historical criticismBible: the texts

The Bible has never been read in a single way. Jews and Christians have sought in it, in turn, a law, a hidden meaning, a figure of Christ, a rule of life, and then, from the seventeenth century onwards, a document whose authors and dates had to be established.

Jewish readings

Rabbinic Judaism reads the Torah through midrash, an inquiry that scrutinizes every word: halakha draws norms from it, haggadah narratives and lessons. The kabbalists of the thirteenth century sum up four levels in the word Pardes, the orchard: the plain sense, the allusion, the homiletic interpretation and the secret.

Allegory, letter and charity

Philo of Alexandria, in the first century, distinguishes the literal sense, good for the multitude, from the figurative sense, in which Eden becomes virtue and the exodus the departure of the soul from the body. Origen systematizes three senses, the body, the soul and the spirit of the text, and holds certain passages to be impossible in the letter so as to force the reader towards the spirit. The school of Antioch, on the contrary, defends the literal and historical sense. Augustine, in On Christian Doctrine, lays down a rule: any interpretation that does not build up the love of God and of neighbour has not understood the text.

The four medieval senses

Cassian and then the medieval masters fix four senses of Scripture: literal, allegorical, moral or tropological, anagogical. A Latin distich of the thirteenth century sums them up: the letter teaches the facts, allegory what one must believe, the moral sense what one must do, anagogy what one must strive towards. Jerusalem is thus the city, the Church, the soul and the heavenly city. Thomas Aquinas requires the spiritual senses to rest on the literal one.

Luther and the clarity of Scripture

The Reformation lays down the principle of sola scriptura: Scripture alone has authority, it is clear in what concerns salvation, it interprets itself and every believer can read it, hence Luther's German translation (1522-1534). Luther distrusts allegory and looks for Christ everywhere.

The birth of criticism

Spinoza, in chapter VII of the Theological-Political Treatise (1670), wants to read Scripture as one reads nature: to establish the language, the history of each book, its author, its addressees, and to draw from it only what the text says, without bending reason to it as Maimonides did; in the following chapter, he concludes with Ibn Ezra that Moses did not write the Pentateuch, of which Ezra would be the compiler. The Oratorian Richard Simon, in the Histoire critique du Vieux Testament (1678), destroyed at Bossuet's instigation, attributes the text to successive public scribes. After Jean Astruc (1753), who distinguishes two documents in Genesis according to the divine name, German criticism culminates in Julius Wellhausen, whose Prolegomena to the History of Israel (1878) fix the documentary hypothesis: four sources, the Yahwist J, the Elohist E, the Deuteronomist D and the Priestly writing P, the last being the latest, after the exile. Since the 1970s the model has been contested, above all for J and E. On the side of the New Testament, Strauss introduces myth in 1835; Gunkel, Dibelius and Bultmann found form criticism; Bultmann proposes in 1941 to demythologize the message. The Catholic Church, after condemning modernism, accepts literary genres with Pius XII (1943), and at the Second Vatican Council the constitution Dei Verbum (1965) asks that the intention of the human authors be sought. Conversely, American fundamentalism, born at the end of the nineteenth century, holds the inerrancy of Scripture to be an article of faith.

A Muslim parallel

Averroes, in the twelfth century, distinguishes in the Qur'an an apparent sense and an inner sense reached through taʾwīl, interpretation, which is reserved for those capable of demonstration; this is the division, between the letter for the crowd and the meaning for the learned, that Spinoza rejects.

What the essay assumes

The essay describes in Philo the regime of allegory and its two senses, in Origen the three-level reading inherited from Philo, in Averroes taʾwīl and the three classes of men, in Spinoza the rejection of Maimonides' method as useless, dangerous and absurd, and in Strauss myth without intention; it takes as known the four medieval senses, sola scriptura and the documentary hypothesis, which it describes nowhere.

In the essay: Celsus and Origen · Spinoza · Averroes

Further reading: Henri de Lubac, Exégèse médiévale. Les quatre sens de l'Écriture (1959-1964) ; Pierre Gibert, L'Invention critique de la Bible (2010) ; Thomas Römer, Jean-Daniel Macchi and Christophe Nihan (eds.), Introduction à l'Ancien Testament (2004) ; Gilbert Dahan, L'Exégèse chrétienne de la Bible en Occident médiéval (1999)

Sacrifice in the Bible: from Abel's altar to self-offeringBible: the texts

Sacrifice runs through the Bible from the first offering of Cain and Abel to the Lamb of the Apocalypse. Following this thread shows how a religion of the Temple becomes a religion without blood sacrifices, and how the word sacrifice changes its meaning along the way.

The stories of origins

The first murder follows the first offering: God accepts Abel's, not Cain's, and the story does not say why (Gn 4). Noah, having left the ark, offers burnt offerings, and God promises never again to curse the ground (Gn 8). On Mount Moriah, God asks Abraham for his son, then stays his hand and provides a ram (Gn 22). Exegetes often read this story as the memory of a rejection of child sacrifice, which the prophets and Deuteronomy expressly condemn as a practice of the nations and at times of Israel, around the place called Tophet.

The system of Leviticus

Leviticus organizes a complete cult, reserved to the priests and, after the reform of Josiah, to the Temple of Jerusalem alone. The burnt offering burns the whole victim for God; the communion sacrifice divides its meat between the altar, the priests and the offerer; the sin offerings and guilt offerings purify the sanctuary and make good transgressions, often involuntary ones; grain offerings complete the whole. Blood, the seat of life, belongs to God and is not eaten. On the Day of Atonement, Yom Kippur, the high priest enters the Holy of Holies, sprinkles the mercy seat and lays the sins of the people on a goat that is driven out into the desert, to Azazel (Lv 16): this is the origin of the expression scapegoat.

The prophetic critique

The prophets do not always demand an end to sacrifices, but they subordinate them to justice. Amos reports a God who hates the feasts and offerings of a people that oppresses the poor; Hosea, a God who desires faithfulness rather than sacrifice, words Jesus takes up twice in Matthew; Micah, a God who asks for neither rams nor the firstborn, but for justice, kindness and humility; a psalm declares that the acceptable sacrifice is a broken spirit. Second Isaiah, finally, describes a servant whose life, offered as a sacrifice for guilt, justifies the many.

After the Temple

The destruction of the Temple in 70 puts an end to sacrifices. Rabbinic Judaism replaces them with study, with prayer at the hours of the former sacrifices, and with works of mercy. Christians read the death of Jesus in the vocabulary of the Temple: the Last Supper takes up the blood of the covenant, John presents Jesus as the lamb of God who takes away the sin of the world and has him die at the hour when the Passover lambs are slaughtered; the Epistle to the Hebrews makes this death the single sacrifice that renders all the others useless. Paul, in Rm 12, 1, calls believers to offer their bodies as a living sacrifice, a worship in keeping with reason.

Readings

Maimonides explains the sacrifices as a pedagogical concession to a people accustomed to the cults of its time; Hubert and Mauss, in 1899, see in them a mechanism of communication between the sacred and the profane; Girard, from 1972 onwards, a ritual repetition of the founding murder, whose victim's innocence the Bible and the Passion would unveil. Historians stress above all the diversity of practices and the slowness of the transformations.

What the essay assumes

The essay makes Rm 12, 1 a turning point: Paul spiritualizes sacrifice instead of abolishing it, an immense advance over the burnt offerings and the birth certificate of a religion that will be able to demand that the individual offer himself entirely. It sets out, in Maimonides, the sacrifices transferred to the name of God rather than abolished, in Girard the scapegoat and the Passion, and concludes, in the assessment of the refoundations, that no exit can avoid refounding unless it refuses the scapegoat. Its criterion, the absolute in whose name the sacrifice of the individual can be demanded, presupposes this biblical history of sacrifice.

In the Gallery:Agnus Dei (c. 1635-1640, Madrid, Prado): the bound lamb of the sacrificial victim (Jn 1, 29), in which Girard reads the Christian revelation of the innocence of the scapegoat victimThe Sacrifice of Isaac (c. 1603, Florence, Uffizi Gallery): human sacrifice suspended and replaced by the ram (Gn 22), a key moment in Girard's reading of the Bible

In the essay: Girard · Paul of Tarsus · Maimonides · Assessment of the refoundations

Further reading: Alfred Marx, Les Systèmes sacrificiels de l'Ancien Testament (2005) ; Henri Hubert and Marcel Mauss, Essai sur la nature et la fonction du sacrifice (1899) ; René Girard, La Violence et le sacré (1972) ; Jon D. Levenson, The Death and Resurrection of the Beloved Son (1993)

The God of the Bible: his names, his images and the formation of monotheismBible: the texts

The God whom the Bible calls by several names was not conceived from the outset as the one God of the philosophers. Historians reconstruct a long history, from the god of one people among other gods to the one and invisible God, and this history is itself inscribed in the texts.

The names

The Hebrew Bible first uses El, the common noun for god in the Semitic languages and the proper name of the head of the Canaanite pantheon, and Elohim, a plural form construed with a singular verb. The proper name of the God of Israel is the tetragrammaton, the four consonants YHWH; its pronunciation, probably Yahweh, ceased to be spoken in the Second Temple period, and Jewish readers replace it with Adonai, the Lord, which the Septuagint translates as Kyrios and French Bibles as the Eternal or the Lord. The story of the burning bush links this name to the verb to be, an enigmatic formula that has nourished the whole of Christian metaphysics. To these are added titles: Shaddai, Sabaoth, the god of hosts, the Most High.

A god among the gods

Several texts assume the existence of other gods: the Decalogue forbids having others before the God of Israel, a psalm shows God judging in the midst of the gods, and Jephthah speaks of the god of the Ammonites as a rival. Historians call this worship of a single god without denial of the others monolatry. Archaeology sheds light on it: inscriptions from Kuntillet Ajrud, c. 800 BCE, mention Yahweh and his Asherah, and the prophets fight cults of Baal practised in Israel. Monotheism proper, the assertion that there is no other god, is expressed clearly in Second Isaiah, during the exile, when Yahweh is said to be the creator of all things, of light and of darkness.

Images and the ban on images

The Bible forbids representing God, yet it describes him constantly: shepherd, king, judge, husband, father, warrior, potter; he walks in the garden, repents, grows angry. At the same time it asserts that no one can see his face and live, that Moses sees him only from behind, and that he is a hidden God. This tension between the anthropomorphism of the stories and the transcendence they affirm lies at the heart of every learned reading.

From the biblical God to the God of the philosophers

Philo reads the name from the bush as being itself and makes God the unknowable, of whom only the Logos is within reach. Maimonides devotes the opening of the Guide for the Perplexed to showing that the bodily terms applied to God are homonyms and that only negative attributes are fitting. The Fathers and the scholastics identify the God of the Bible with the first principle of Aristotle and Plotinus. Pascal, in 1654, on the contrary sets the God of Abraham, Isaac and Jacob against the God of the philosophers and scholars. Historians of monotheism, from Hume to Jan Assmann, have finally discussed its link with intolerance.

What the essay assumes

The essay cites, in Pascal, the hidden God of Isaiah and the God of Abraham set against that of the philosophers; it sets out, in Maimonides, the homonyms and the negative attributes; it reports, in Hume, monotheism as a late product of fear, intolerant because a single god suffers no rivals; it cites, in Levinas, the idea that monotheism, measured against the mythical gods, is nothing but atheism, and, in Weber, the return of a polytheism of values. It assumes the reader knows the history that leads from the god of one people to the one God.

In the essay: Pascal · Maimonides · Hume · Levinas

Further reading: Thomas Römer, L'Invention de Dieu (2014) ; Mark S. Smith, The Early History of God (1990) ; Jan Assmann, Le Prix du monothéisme (2003) ; André Lemaire, Naissance du monothéisme (2003)

Evil, Satan and the afterlife: from Sheol to the Last JudgementBible: the texts

The Bible has no single doctrine of death, the devil and the afterlife: its representations change over nearly a millennium, and most of those that Christianity has made familiar, hell, the devil as chief of the demons, the judgement of souls, appear late in the texts.

Sheol and the silence of the dead

In most of the books of the Old Testament, the dead go down to Sheol, an underground and indistinct abode where the righteous and the wicked share the same shadow, far from God, without praise or reward. Retribution takes place here below, in long life, descendants and prosperity, which is precisely what Job and Qoheleth dispute. There is neither paradise nor hell for individuals.

The serpent, the Satan and the fallen angels

The serpent of Genesis is not the devil: it is a cunning animal, and its identification with the devil comes later, in the Wisdom of Solomon and then in the Apocalypse. In Job and Zechariah, the Satan, the adversary, is a member of the heavenly court who accuses human beings before God, with his permission. The Persian and Hellenistic period, probably under the influence of Iranian dualism, develops darker figures: the book of Enoch tells of the fall of the Watchers, angels who united with women, the source of the demons, and the Qumran manuscripts set the Prince of Light against Belial. The New Testament inherits this world: Satan tempts Jesus in the desert, exorcisms deliver the possessed, and the Apocalypse shows the dragon, the ancient serpent, cast down from heaven.

Resurrection and judgement

The hope of a resurrection of the dead appears clearly in the book of Daniel, c. 165 BCE: many of those who sleep in the dust will awake, some to everlasting life, others to shame. The second book of Maccabees links it to the martyrs. In the time of Jesus, the Pharisees believe in it, the Sadducees do not. The gospels announce the judgement of the nations by the Son of Man (Mt 25), speak of Gehenna, the name of a valley at Jerusalem associated with child sacrifice that became the image of the fire that is never quenched, and of Lazarus carried to Abraham's bosom while the rich man suffers. Paul awaits the resurrection of bodies at the return of Christ.

What tradition has built

Hell as an eternal place of punishment, purgatory, whose birth Jacques Le Goff places in the twelfth century, limbo, the natural immortality of the soul taken over from Plato, Dante's geography, are later developments that read the biblical texts in the light of Greek philosophy and medieval pastoral care. Origen hoped for a final restoration of all, condemned in the sixth century; Augustine fixes the eternity of punishment for the West. The problem of evil, finally, is not treated by the Bible as a theoretical question: Job raises it without resolving it, and theodicy is a philosophical invention, which Leibniz names in 1710.

What the essay assumes

The essay draws on these figures in several places. In Dostoevsky, Zosima defines hell as the suffering of no longer being able to love, the reverse of a place of punishment; in Feuerbach, the flames of hell are only the sparks of the furious gaze that faith casts on unbelievers; in Rousseau, civil religion replaces hell with exile and the scaffold; in Girard, Satan is the accuser, the principle of the unanimous crowd, whose fall is the end of the power to accuse without being unmasked. It assumes familiarity with the distance between these images and the texts from which they come.

In the essay: Dostoevsky · Ludwig Feuerbach · Rousseau · Girard

Further reading: Alan E. Bernstein, The Formation of Hell (1993) ; Jacques Le Goff, La Naissance du Purgatoire (1981) ; Elaine Pagels, The Origin of Satan (1995) ; Jon D. Levenson, Resurrection and the Restoration of Israel (2006)

Messiah, Son of Man, Son of God: the titles of JesusBible: the texts

The first Christians said who Jesus was through titles borrowed from the Jewish Scriptures and from the Greek world. Their history shows how a crucified Galilean prophet received, within a few decades, the names that would lead to the definitions of the councils.

Messiah and Christ

Messiah, in Hebrew, and Christ, in Greek, mean anointed. The word first designates the king, and sometimes the priest, consecrated by anointing; after the end of the monarchy, it names the descendant of David awaited to restore Israel, a figure that remains varied in the time of Jesus, a king, a priest, sometimes both at Qumran. The gospels show Jesus reticent about the title, and the inscription on the cross, king of the Jews, indicates that Rome executed him as a messianic claimant. In Paul, Christ has become almost a proper name, Jesus Christ, which presupposes an already long-standing usage.

Son of Man

The expression, which can simply mean a man in Aramaic, is the one Jesus uses most often to speak of himself, and others almost never. It also refers to the vision of Daniel, in which one like a son of man receives everlasting kingship. The gospels use it for his present activity, for the announced suffering and for the coming in glory. Historians have debated for a century which of these uses go back to Jesus.

Son of God

In the Hebrew Bible, son of God is said of the angels, of the people of Israel and of the king, adopted on the day of his enthronement. The title first expresses a relation of election and obedience. The gospels have it spoken by the voice at the baptism, by the demons and, in Mark, by the centurion before the cross. John gives it a new meaning, that of the only Son come from the Father, and Paul quotes an ancient formula in which Jesus is established Son of God by the resurrection. The ontological sense, the same nature as the Father, is fixed in the fourth century at Nicaea.

Lord, Logos, Lamb

Lord, Kyrios, is the word by which the Septuagint renders the divine name; applying it to Jesus, as the Aramaic prayer Marana tha, which Paul preserves, already does, amounts to giving him a place beside God in worship, which Larry Hurtado regards as the most remarkable fact of nascent Christianity. The Logos of the prologue of John, the creative Word, intersects with Jewish Wisdom and with the philosophy of Philo. The Lamb of God, in John and in the Apocalypse, unites the paschal sacrifice and the suffering servant of Isaiah.

From title to dogma

The titles do not form a system in the New Testament; they accumulate. The quarrels of the following centuries, over the divinity of the Son and then over the union of his two natures, consist in drawing a coherent doctrine from them, from Nicaea in 325 to Chalcedon in 451. Historians thus distinguish what Jesus may have said of himself, what the first communities confessed, and what the councils defined.

What the essay assumes

The essay recalls, in the section on the existence of Jesus, that Reimarus saw in Jesus a political Jewish messiah who failed and that Albert Schweitzer made him a prophet of the end; in Strauss, the community awaiting a Messiah gives Jesus the features that the Scriptures attributed to the Messiah, birth in Bethlehem and descent from David; in Philo, the Logos becomes flesh; in Nietzsche, the Idiot and the only Christian, who died on the cross, stand against the titles of glory. It assumes familiarity with the passage from the crucified preacher to the Christ of the creeds.

In the essay: Did Jesus exist? The Jesus of history and the mythicist thesis · David Friedrich Strauss and Bruno Bauer · Nietzsche · Philo of Alexandria

Further reading: Oscar Cullmann, Christologie du Nouveau Testament (1957) ; Martin Hengel, Le Fils de Dieu (1977) ; Larry W. Hurtado, Lord Jesus Christ (2003) ; Paula Fredriksen, From Jesus to Christ (1988)

History

Christianity in the Roman Empire: from Nero to DiocletianHistory

For three centuries, from the death of Jesus (c. 30) to the agreement of Milan (313), Christianity was a religion without status in the Roman Empire, tolerated by default and punished intermittently. The persecutions, less continuous than legend claims, shaped its vocabulary, its cult of the martyrs and its first literature, apologetics.

A religion without status

Rome did not persecute cults as such, and Judaism enjoyed a long-standing tolerance that exempted Jews from the imperial cult. Christians lost this benefit as they separated from the synagogue, especially after the destruction of the Temple in 70. They refused to sacrifice to the gods of the city and to the genius of the emperor, which Rome took as a refusal of civic loyalty; they were accused of atheism, of hatred of the human race and of secret crimes. The offence was not belief but the obstinate refusal to perform the public gesture.

The first episodes were local. In 64, after the fire of Rome, Nero laid the blame on the Christians of the city and had a great many of them tortured to death; Tacitus, who reports the fact around 116 in the Annals (XV, 44), notes in passing that their name comes from a certain Christus executed under Tiberius by the procurator Pontius Pilate. Around 112, Pliny the Younger, governor of Bithynia, asked Trajan how to deal with the Christians denounced to him; Trajan's answer set the rule for a century: do not seek them out, reject anonymous denunciations, punish those who, once denounced, refuse to sacrifice. The martyrs of the second century, Polycarp at Smyrna (c. 155), Justin at Rome (c. 165), the Christians of Lyon (177), Perpetua at Carthage (203), died under this regime of denunciation, not under a general law.

The imperial persecutions

The turning point came in the middle of the third century. In 250 Decius ordered all the inhabitants of the Empire to sacrifice to the gods and to obtain a certificate of it; this was the first general persecution, and it produced more apostates than martyrs. Valerian, in 257-258, targeted the clergy; Cyprian of Carthage was executed. Gallienus, in 260, restored the places of worship: forty years of peace followed. Diocletian's Great Persecution, launched in 303 by a series of edicts (destruction of churches and books, arrest of the clergy, obligation to sacrifice), was the harshest, especially in the East; Galerius's edict of toleration (311) brought it to an end.

The word martyr, in Greek the witness, first designates the one who attests his faith before the tribunal; the cult of their tombs made them the first saints. The apologists, Justin, Athenagoras, Tertullian, addressed the authorities to claim the protection of the ordinary law and to present Christianity as the true philosophy.

A slow, urban spread

Christianity spread through the cities, along the roads and the networks of the Jewish diaspora: Antioch, Alexandria, Asia Minor, Rome, then North Africa and Gaul. Its followers were at first artisans, women, slaves and freedmen, then men of letters and notables. Around 300 they were still a minority, a tenth of the population, but an organized one, with bishops, property and schools. Celsus wrote around 178, when this minority was beginning to cause concern; Origen answered him around 248, on the eve of the edict of Decius.

What the essay assumes

The essay cites Tacitus on Nero as non-Christian testimony to the existence of Jesus, mentions the martyrdom of Justin, a case in which it is Rome that demands the sacrifice, and reports Celsus's mockery of the religion of slaves and Engels's thesis of a movement of the oppressed. It takes it as known that the Empire demanded the gesture of sacrifice, not inner adherence.

Key dates

  • 64: fire of Rome, first executions of Christians under Nero.
  • c. 112: Pliny's letter to Trajan and the imperial rescript.
  • c. 165: martyrdom of Justin at Rome.
  • 250: edict of Decius; 257-258: persecution of Valerian; 260: peace of Gallienus.
  • 303-311: Diocletian's Great Persecution; 311: edict of Galerius; 313: agreement of Milan.

In the essay: Part Two · Justin Martyr · Celsus and Origen · Did Jesus exist? The Jesus of history and the mythicist thesis

Further reading: Peter Brown, The World of Late Antiquity (1971) ; Robin Lane Fox, Pagans and Christians (1986) ; Marie-Françoise Baslez, Les Persécutions dans l'Antiquité (2007) ; Paul Veyne, Quand notre monde est devenu chrétien (2007)

Judaism: from the Second Temple to emancipationHistory

Christianity is born within Judaism, and several authors of the essay, Philo, Paul, Maimonides, Spinoza, Levinas, Buber, come from it. Yet Judaism did not remain what it was in the time of Jesus: it rebuilt itself after the destruction of the Temple, produced the Talmud, a philosophy and a mysticism, then passed through modern emancipation. The essay takes these landmarks as known.

The Second Temple and its currents

The Second Temple of Jerusalem, rebuilt after the return from the Babylonian exile (c. 515 BCE) and enlarged by Herod, is the centre of the sacrificial cult. Around it, in the time of Jesus, Judaism is divided into currents: the Sadducees, a priestly aristocracy attached to the Temple and to the written Torah alone; the Pharisees, masters of the oral law, who believe in the resurrection and practise a piety of everyday life; the Essenes, a separate community whose manuscripts were found at Qumran in 1947; the Zealots, partisans of revolt against Rome. A large diaspora lives in Egypt, in Asia Minor and in Rome; in Alexandria the Bible has been translated into Greek, the Septuagint, and Philo reads it alongside Plato. The revolt of 66 ends with the capture of Jerusalem and the burning of the Temple by Titus in 70; that of Bar Kokhba (132-135) with the ban on Jews residing in Jerusalem.

Rabbinic Judaism

Without Temple or sacrifices, Judaism rebuilds itself around the Torah, the synagogue and study, under the leadership of the heirs of the Pharisees, the rabbis. The oral law is set down in writing in the Mishnah (c. 200), on which the Gemara of Palestine and that of Babylon comment: the Babylonian Talmud, completed around 500, becomes the text of reference. Rabbinic Judaism ignores Philo, who was handed down by Christians, and defines itself against Christianity as much as Christianity defines itself against it. In the Middle Ages, in the lands of Islam and then in Christian Spain, there develop a philosophy, Saadia Gaon, Judah Halevi, Maimonides (1138-1204), whose Guide for the Perplexed reconciles the Torah with Aristotle and whose Mishneh Torah codifies the law, and a mysticism, the Kabbalah, whose major book is the Zohar, in Castile at the end of the thirteenth century. The expulsion from Spain in 1492 scatters the Sephardim towards the Ottoman Empire, Italy and Amsterdam, where Spinoza will be born, to be expelled from his community by the herem of 1656.

Emancipation and modernity

From the eighteenth century onwards, the Haskalah, the Jewish Enlightenment of Moses Mendelssohn in Berlin, opens Judaism to European culture; the French Revolution emancipates the Jews of France in 1791, and the German states follow in the nineteenth century. Judaism then divides into Reform Jews, who adapt the liturgy, Orthodox and Conservative Jews. In Eastern Europe, Hasidism, born in the eighteenth century around the Baal Shem Tov, offers a piety of joy and fervour, which Buber studied. The science of Judaism applies historical criticism to the texts. The twentieth century is that of the Shoah, which destroys the Judaism of Eastern Europe, and of the founding of the State of Israel in 1948. Levinas, Buber, Rosenzweig and Scholem think within this heritage.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the pluralism of first-century Judaism, in which Paul is a Pharisee, the break of 70, the difference between Philo's Judaism and rabbinic Judaism, Spinoza's herem, and the fact that Maimonides, Spinoza and Levinas think within a tradition of the Law and of commentary, not of dogma. It says itself that it treats Judaism only in passing and knows it is centred on Christian Europe.

Key dates

  • C. 515 BCE: dedication of the Second Temple.
  • 70: destruction of the Temple; 135: end of the Bar Kokhba revolt.
  • C. 200: Mishnah; c. 500: Babylonian Talmud.
  • 1204: death of Maimonides; 1492: expulsion from Spain.
  • 1656: Spinoza's herem in Amsterdam.
  • 1791: emancipation of the Jews of France.
  • 1948: founding of the State of Israel.

In the essay: Philo of Alexandria · Paul of Tarsus · Maimonides · Levinas

Further reading: Mireille Hadas-Lebel, Jérusalem contre Rome (1990) ; Gershom Scholem, Les Grands Courants de la mystique juive (1941) ; Maurice-Ruben Hayoun, Maimonides (1987) ; Michael A. Meyer, Response to Modernity (1988) ; Shmuel Trigano (ed.), La Société juive à travers l'histoire (1992)

Islam: birth, expansion and thought, from Muhammad to AverroesHistory

Islam is born in Arabia in the seventh century and within a century rules a territory stretching from Spain to the Indus. It is in this territory, between the ninth and the twelfth centuries, that Greek philosophy is translated, commented upon and carried further, before returning to Latin Europe by way of Spain. The essay treats Islam only in passing, through Averroes, Maimonides, Arkoun and Bidar, but it takes the main features of this history as known.

Birth and expansion

Muhammad (c. 570-632), a merchant of Mecca, receives from about 610 onwards revelations that he proclaims as the word of the one God; in 622 he emigrates to Medina, where he founds a community that is at once religious and political: this is the hijra. At his death Arabia is unified. The Qur'an, the collection of these revelations, is set down in writing under the caliph Uthman, around 650. The caliphs conquer Syria, Egypt, Persia and, at the beginning of the eighth century, Spain. The succession to Muhammad divides Muslims between Sunnis, the majority, and Shiites, partisans of Ali and his descendants. The Umayyads reign from Damascus (661-750), then the Abbasids from Baghdad (750-1258), while an emirate, then an independent Umayyad caliphate, holds on in Cordoba. Christians and Jews, people of the Book, have there a protected and inferior status, that of dhimmi.

The translations and falsafa

In Baghdad, under the Abbasids, a vast translation movement carries into Arabic, often by way of Syriac, Aristotle, Plato, Galen, Euclid and Ptolemy. From this reception is born falsafa, philosophy in the Greek tradition written in Arabic: al-Kindi in the ninth century, al-Farabi in the tenth, Avicenna (Ibn Sina, 980-1037) in Persia, whose metaphysics of the necessary being and of possible beings will mark the whole of Latin scholasticism, and finally Averroes (Ibn Rushd, 1126-1198) in Cordoba, the Commentator on Aristotle par excellence. Alongside the philosophers, the theologians of kalam debate the justice of God, human freedom and the status of the Qur'an. The Mu'tazilites, dominant in Baghdad at the beginning of the ninth century, hold that the Qur'an is created, that God is just in the sense in which reason understands justice, and that man is free; the caliph al-Ma'mun imposes their doctrine through an inquisition, the mihna. The reversal comes with their adversaries: Ash'arism, which affirms the omnipotence of God against human reason, and the profession of faith of the caliph al-Qadir, at the beginning of the eleventh century, which declares the Qur'an uncreated and condemns the Mu'tazilite theses. Arkoun sees in this moment the dogmatic closure of Sunni Islam.

Al-Ghazali and Averroes

Al-Ghazali (1058-1111), theologian and mystic, writes the Tahāfut al-falāsifa, The Incoherence of the Philosophers, which condemns twenty theses of falsafa, three of them as impious: the eternity of the world, God's ignorance of particulars, the denial of the resurrection of bodies. Averroes answers him with the Tahāfut al-tahāfut, The Incoherence of the Incoherence, and, in the Faṣl al-maqāl, The Decisive Treatise, maintains that the revealed Law itself commands philosophizing and that truth does not contradict truth. His work is little read in the Muslim world after him, where Sufism and theology prevail, and widely read in Latin Europe, where it nourishes Parisian Averroism and the condemnations of 1277. Philosophy continues in Shiite Iran, with Suhrawardi and Mulla Sadra, in a tradition that Europe long ignored.

What the essay assumes

The essay uses without explaining them the distinction between falsafa and kalam, the memory of the Mu'tazilites as the lost possibility of a rationalist Islam, the closure of al-Qadir, which it ranks among the strict refoundations, and the quarrel between al-Ghazali and Averroes, an attested response. It recalls that the double truth attributed to Averroes is a Latin legend, and that Maimonides, a Jew of Cordoba writing in Arabic, belongs to the same intellectual world.

Key dates

  • 622: hijra, founding of the community of Medina.
  • 632: death of Muhammad; 711: landing in Spain.
  • 750: accession of the Abbasids; Baghdad founded in 762.
  • 833-848: mihna, the Mu'tazilite inquisition.
  • 1018: profession of faith of al-Qadir.
  • 1037: death of Avicenna; 1111: death of al-Ghazali; 1198: death of Averroes.
  • 1258: capture of Baghdad by the Mongols.

In the essay: Averroes · Maimonides · Islam before the exit from religion · Part One

Further reading: Maxime Rodinson, Mahomet (1961) ; Henry Corbin, Histoire de la philosophie islamique (1964) ; Dimitri Gutas, Greek Thought, Arabic Culture (1998) ; Ali Benmakhlouf, Averroes (2000) ; Christian Jambet, Qu'est-ce que la philosophie islamique ? (2011)

From Constantine to Theodosius: Christianity as the religion of the EmpireHistory

In eighty years, from Constantine's victory at the Milvian Bridge (312) to the ban on pagan sacrifices by Theodosius (391-392), Christianity passes from the status of a persecuted religion to that of the religion of the Empire. This is not a single act but a series of imperial decisions, which the bishops inspired and from which they profited.

Constantine: favour, not obligation

Constantine, master of the West after the Milvian Bridge, attributes his victory to the God of the Christians. In 313, at Milan, he agrees with Licinius to grant freedom of worship to all and to restore to the Churches their confiscated property. He does not make Christianity the official religion, but he heaps favours on the Church: tax exemptions for the clergy, jurisdiction for bishops, the building of basilicas in Rome (the Lateran, St Peter's) and in Jerusalem, Sunday rest in 321. Sole emperor from 324, he founds Constantinople and in 325 convenes the Council of Nicaea to settle Arius's quarrel over the divinity of the Son: the first time an emperor assembles the Church and guarantees its decisions. He receives baptism only on his deathbed, in 337.

His sons continue the policy and harden it: Constantius II bans sacrifices (341) and orders the closing of the temples (356). The Church then divides as much as it grows, and the emperor becomes the arbiter of its quarrels.

Julian: the reverse attempt

Julian, Constantine's nephew, brought up a Christian and secretly converted to Neoplatonism, reigns from 361 to 363. He restores paganism, but in a new form copied from the adversary: a hierarchical clergy, a morality, works of charity, a theology borrowed from Iamblichus. He forbids Christians to teach classical letters (362) and writes an Against the Galileans whose arguments echo those of Celsus and Porphyry. His death in Persia, after twenty months of rule, ends the experiment; Christian tradition nicknamed him the Apostate.

Theodosius: compulsory religion

His successors return to Christianity. Gratian renounces the title of pontifex maximus and has the altar of Victory removed from the Senate (382); in 384 the prefect Symmachus will plead in vain for its restoration, and Ambrose of Milan will answer him. In 380, by the Edict of Thessalonica, Theodosius orders all the peoples of the Empire to follow the faith of Nicaea as professed by the bishops of Rome and Alexandria, and calls the others madmen and heretics, liable to divine and then imperial punishment. The Council of Constantinople (381) confirms Nicaea. In 391 and 392 a series of laws bans sacrifices, visits to temples and domestic worship; the Serapeum of Alexandria is destroyed in 391. After the massacre of Thessalonica (390), Ambrose imposes a public penance on Theodosius: the bishop now judges the emperor.

The Empire is Christian, but pagans remain numerous, especially in the countryside, hence the word paganus according to the most common etymology, and in the senatorial aristocracy; Christianization still takes centuries. Repression turns towards heretics and schismatics: it is against the Donatists of Africa that Augustine, c. 405-412, justifies recourse to the secular arm.

What the essay assumes

The essay sums up this period in one sentence, the Christian emperors, from Constantine to Theodosius, carrying out the strict refoundation Plato had dreamed of, a rational theology armed by the state; it mentions Lactantius as tutor to Constantine's son, Julian as the refounder of a restored paganism, and Augustine against the Donatists. It assumes the reader knows that the alliance of Church and Empire, and not the conversion of individuals alone, made Christianity the religion of Europe.

Key dates

  • 312: victory at the Milvian Bridge; 313: agreement of Milan.
  • 325: Council of Nicaea; 330: founding of Constantinople; 337: death of Constantine.
  • 341 and 356: laws of Constantius II against sacrifices and temples.
  • 361-363: reign of Julian.
  • 380: Edict of Thessalonica; 381: Council of Constantinople.
  • 382: removal of the altar of Victory; 390: penance of Theodosius.
  • 391-392: ban on pagan cults; 395: death of Theodosius, division of the Empire.

In the essay: Porphyry and Augustine · Part Two · Assessment of the refoundations · Plotinus

Further reading: Peter Brown, The Rise of Western Christendom (1996) ; Paul Veyne, Quand notre monde est devenu chrétien (2007) ; Ramsay MacMullen, Christianizing the Roman Empire (1984) ; Pierre Maraval, Théodose le Grand (2009)

Why Christianity prevailed: the historians' explanationsHistory

How did a Jewish sect from Galilee become, in three centuries, the religion of the Empire, and then of Europe? The question is as old as critical history, and the answers have followed one another without cancelling each other out: each singles out one factor, doctrine, organization, society, power, and the debate concerns their respective weight.

Gibbon and the secondary causes

In chapter XV of his History of the Decline and Fall of the Roman Empire (1776), Gibbon sets aside the first cause, providence, and retains only the secondary causes. He counts five: the uncompromising zeal inherited from Judaism, which refused any compromise with other cults; the doctrine of a future life, promised with a precision that philosophy did not offer; the miracles ascribed to the primitive Church; the austere morals of the first Christians; and finally the discipline and government of a community that formed, within the Empire, an independent state. His list has remained the starting point.

Harnack and Nock: networks and conversion

At the beginning of the twentieth century, Harnack reconstructs the geography of the mission: Christianity follows the routes of the Jewish diaspora, whose synagogues and pagan sympathizers serve it as a bridge, and it settles in the cities before reaching the countryside. He stresses care for the poor, widows and the sick, monotheism, simpler than polytheism for a world already worked on by philosophy, and universalism, which addressed everyone regardless of origin. Nock, in 1933, adds a decisive observation: the ancient cults were cumulative, one could honour Isis without leaving Jupiter; only the philosophical schools and the religions stemming from Judaism demanded exclusive adherence, a conversion, that is, a change of life. Christianity offered what philosophy offered the educated, but to everyone.

Sociology: Stark and networks

Rodney Stark, a sociologist of religion, proposed in 1996 an explanation through the ordinary mechanisms of religious movements. A growth of forty per cent per decade is enough to go from a thousand believers in 40 to six million around 300. Conversions happen through networks of kinship and friendship, not through mass preaching. The Christians, by nursing their sick during the epidemics of 165 and 251, had a higher survival rate and won grateful survivors.
