Women, more numerous in the communities, enjoyed a better status there and married pagans whom they converted; the rejection of infanticide and abortion favoured demographic growth. Martyrdom, finally, was a signal of sincerity that made the doctrine credible. Stark disputes that Christianity was the religion of slaves: its recruits came mainly from the urban middle classes.

Power: Veyne and MacMullen

Other historians shift the emphasis towards the end of the process. For Paul Veyne, Christianity in 312 was a minority of one tenth, and it was the conversion of Constantine, the personal decision of an emperor attracted by an ambitious and organized religion, that tipped the Empire; without it, nothing guaranteed victory. Ramsay MacMullen stresses the part played by miracle in popular conversions, and then, after 312, that of constraint, favours and prohibitions. Peter Brown describes a world of Late Antiquity in which bishops become the patrons of cities and Christianity provides a common culture for an Empire in transformation. The old thesis of an age of anxiety, which supposedly made people eager for salvation, is now disputed: the paganism of the 3rd century was alive, and the Christians did not fill a void.

What the essay assumes

The essay reports three old readings of this spread: Celsus mocking the audience of ignorant people and the religion of slaves, Engels seeing in it a movement of the oppressed comparable to the labour movement, Nietzsche making Paul the inventor of Christianity. It does not decide between the historians, but its ninth part, with Norris and Inglehart, assumes that belief recedes with existential security; Antiquity, a world of insecurity, is its implicit counter-example. Stark appears in it as a theorist of the religious market, which is the other side of his sociology.

In the essay: Part Two · Paul of Tarsus · Norris and Inglehart

Further reading: Edward Gibbon, The History of the Decline and Fall of the Roman Empire, chapter XV (1776) ; Adolf von Harnack, Die Mission und Ausbreitung des Christentums (1902) ; Arthur Darby Nock, Conversion (1933) ; Rodney Stark, The Rise of Christianity (1996) ; Paul Veyne, Quand notre monde est devenu chrétien (2007)

Councils and the formation of dogma: from Nicaea to ChalcedonHistory

Early Christianity had neither a fixed creed nor a closed Scripture. Between the second and the fifth century, it gave itself a canon, a rule of faith and dogmatic definitions, in councils convened by the emperors, at the end of quarrels that lasted for generations. This work was carried out with the vocabulary of Greek philosophy.

The canon and the rule of faith

The communities of the first century read the Jewish Bible and a few letters of apostles. Around 140, Marcion, who rejects the Old Testament and keeps only a Luke and ten letters of Paul, both expurgated, forces the Church to define what it receives. Irenaeus of Lyon, c. 180, defends the four gospels and a rule of faith handed down through the succession of bishops. The list of the twenty-seven books of the New Testament becomes stable in the fourth century; the festal letter of Athanasius, in 367, gives the first complete enumeration of it. The word heresy, which in Greek means choice or school, first designates any current of thought; with Irenaeus and Tertullian it becomes the name of deviation. Walter Bauer argued in 1934 that in many regions the forms later called heretical had preceded orthodoxy, which would thus be the result of a victory and not an origin.

The Trinity: Nicaea and Constantinople

The central question is that of Christ: how can Jesus be God if God is one? The theology of the Logos, in Justin and Origen, made the Son a second God, subordinate to the Father. Around 318, Arius, a priest of Alexandria, draws the consequence: the Son is a creature, and there was a time when he was not. The Council of Nicaea (325), convened by Constantine, condemns him and proclaims the Son consubstantial (homoousios) with the Father, begotten, not made. The word, foreign to Scripture, divides the East for fifty years; the emperors back the parties in turn, and Athanasius is exiled five times. The Cappadocian Fathers, Basil of Caesarea, Gregory of Nazianzus and Gregory of Nyssa, find the formula that prevails: one essence (ousia), three hypostases. The Council of Constantinople (381) extends it to the Holy Spirit; the creed known as the Niceno-Constantinopolitan Creed is its expression.

The Incarnation: Ephesus and Chalcedon

It remains to say how the divine and the human are united in Christ. Nestorius, patriarch of Constantinople, refuses to call Mary mother of God and sharply distinguishes the two natures; Cyril of Alexandria has him condemned at Ephesus (431). Eutyches falls into the opposite excess, a single nature after the union. The Council of Chalcedon (451) defines a single person in two natures, without confusion or separation; the Churches of Egypt, Syria and Armenia, which reject this formula, separate for good. Origen himself, three centuries after his death, is condemned for some of his theses (553).

Greek philosophy and dogma

Essence, hypostasis, nature, person, consubstantial: the terms of dogma come from Plato, Aristotle, the Stoics and Plotinus. The Fathers defend themselves by saying that they name a revealed mystery with human words; their pagan adversaries, Celsus and Porphyry, and later the moderns, see in this the proof that Christianity thought itself in Greek. Dogma also has a political face: from Theodosius onwards, adherence to the Nicene creed is compulsory, and the heretic is an offender.

What the essay assumes

The essay follows the Logos from Philo to the incarnate Word of John and Augustine, and notes that Augustine found the Logos among the Platonists but not the Incarnation; it calls the fusion of the God of the philosophers and the God of the Bible the major fact of the intellectual history of the West. It mentions that Jaspers reads the Trinity as a set of ciphers, and that rational theology was armed by the state. It assumes the reader knows the names of the councils and the meaning of the words consubstantial, heresy and orthodoxy.

Key dates

  • c. 140: Marcion; c. 180: Irenaeus, Against Heresies.
  • 325: Nicaea; 367: festal letter of Athanasius.
  • 381: Constantinople; 431: Ephesus; 451: Chalcedon.
  • 553: Constantinople II, condemnation of Origenism; 787: Nicaea II.

In the essay: Celsus and Origen · Porphyry and Augustine · Philo of Alexandria · Transition (part two) · Jaspers

Further reading: Jaroslav Pelikan, The Emergence of the Catholic Tradition (1971) ; R. P. C. Hanson, The Search for the Christian Doctrine of God (1988) ; Bernard Sesboüé and Joseph Wolinski, Le Dieu du salut (1994) ; Walter Bauer, Rechtgläubigkeit und Ketzerei im ältesten Christentum (1934)

Medieval Christendom: from the fall of Rome to the stakeHistory

From the ninth century onwards, the word Christendom (christianitas) designates the whole of the peoples of the West united by the same faith, the same clergy and the same Roman authority: a society in which the Church is everywhere, in law, in the calendar, in the school, at birth and at death. In Kierkegaard, it also designates that religion turned into custom which Christianity must fight. The history of medieval Christendom is the history of how this whole was built, and then of how its borders were policed.

From the fall of Rome to Charlemagne

After the end of the Western Empire (476), the barbarian kingdoms are often Arian; the baptism of Clovis, king of the Franks, c. 500, brings the most powerful kingdom over to Catholicism. The bishops, heirs of the Roman administration, and the monks, who follow the Rule of Benedict of Nursia (c. 530), preserve books and evangelize the countryside; Irish and then Anglo-Saxon missionaries win over Germany. With Gregory the Great (590-604), the papacy becomes the centre of the Latin West. The coronation of Charlemagne by the pope, in Rome, on Christmas Day 800, founds the alliance of Empire and Church; the Carolingian renaissance unifies liturgy and script.

The Gregorian reform and conquering Christendom

In the eleventh century, the reforming papacy seeks to free the Church from lay powers: Gregory VII forbids the investiture of bishops by princes and humiliates the emperor Henry IV at Canossa (1077); the conflict ends with the Concordat of Worms (1122). The Church equips itself with a law, canon law, a theology, scholasticism, and a capital. The break with Constantinople (1054) and the crusades (1095-1291) give Latin Christendom its outer borders; Cluny, Cîteaux, then the mendicant orders, Franciscans (1209) and Dominicans (1216), renew religious life. The Fourth Lateran Council (1215) requires every believer to confess once a year and defines transubstantiation: Christendom is henceforth a discipline of consciences.

Heresies, Inquisition, the stake

The same period invents organized exclusion. The Cathars of Languedoc and the Waldensians are fought first by preaching, then by the Albigensian Crusade (1209-1229). In 1231 Gregory IX entrusts the search for heresy to delegated judges, often Dominicans: this is the Inquisition, a procedure of inquiry with denunciation, interrogation, torture (authorized in 1252), and the handing over of relapsed heretics to the secular arm, which burns them. Jews are marked out and expelled from England (1290), France (1306, 1394) and Spain (1492). The Spanish Inquisition, created in 1478 at the request of the Catholic Monarchs, is an institution of the state, led by Torquemada, and its autos-da-fé target converts suspected of judaizing; it is this Inquisition that Dostoevsky transposes to Seville. The stake accompanies the whole of the late Middle Ages: Jan Hus at Constance (1415), Joan of Arc at Rouen (1431), then Savonarola (1498) and Giordano Bruno (1600). Meister Eckhart, prosecuted in Cologne and then in Avignon (1326-1329), dies before the sentence; the bull In agro dominico condemns his propositions, not his person.

Crisis and piety

The fourteenth and fifteenth centuries are those of the Black Death (1347-1352), the Avignon papacy (1309-1377) and the Great Schism (1378-1417), in which two and then three popes contend for the Church. Piety becomes more inward, with Rhineland mysticism and the devotio moderna, and more anxious: Jean Delumeau has described this pastoral of fear, sin and death, which prepares the Reformation.

What the essay assumes

The essay mentions the bull of 1329 against Eckhart, the Inquisition of the Grand Inquisitor, the burnings at the stake and the Inquisitors of historical Christianity, and the Christendom that Kierkegaard sets against Christianity. It assumes the reader knows that the Law of Plato's Laws found in Christendom its most lasting realization, and that Eckhart's negative theology was judged by a tribunal.

Key dates

  • 476: end of the Western Empire; c. 500: baptism of Clovis.
  • 800: coronation of Charlemagne.
  • 1054: break between Rome and Constantinople; 1077: Canossa; 1095: First Crusade.
  • 1215: Lateran IV; 1231: papal Inquisition.
  • 1329: bull against Eckhart; 1478: Spanish Inquisition.

In the essay: Meister Eckhart · Dostoevsky · Part One · Transition (part one)

Further reading: Jacques Le Goff, La Civilisation de l'Occident médiéval (1964) ; Jean Delumeau, Le Péché et la peur (1983) ; Robert Ian Moore, The Formation of a Persecuting Society (1987) ; André Vauchez, La Spiritualité du Moyen Âge occidental (1975)

Byzantium and Russian Orthodoxy: the other ChristianityHistory

The Christianity that Dostoevsky reads is not that of Rome. It comes from Constantinople, the second Rome, and it took on in Russia a form of its own, made of liturgy, icons, monasticism and a relation to the State different from that of the West. Without this history, the monks of The Brothers Karamazov and the author's polemic against Catholicism remain obscure.

Constantinople and the symphony

Founded by Constantine in 330, Constantinople remains the capital of a Christian Roman Empire until 1453. There the emperor convenes the councils and protects the Church, and the patriarch crowns the emperor: this relationship, called symphony, knows nothing of the separation of the two swords that the Gregorian reform imposes in the West. Greek theology, that of the Cappadocian Fathers, of John of Damascus, of Maximus the Confessor, is contemplative and apophatic; it conceives salvation as the deification of man (theosis) and holds rational speculation in suspicion. The iconoclast crisis (726-843) ends in the triumph of images, which Orthodoxy still celebrates. In the 14th century, Gregory Palamas defends, against Latin-influenced theologians, hesychasm, the prayer of the heart of the monks of Athos, and the distinction between the unknowable essence of God and his uncreated energies.

The schism and the Slavic mission

The two halves of Christendom drift apart through language, rite, the celibacy of priests, the addition of the filioque to the creed and, above all, the primacy that Rome claims. The mutual excommunications of 1054 are only an episode; the sack of Constantinople by the crusaders (1204) makes the break definitive, and the union of Florence (1439) is rejected by the people. In the meantime, the brothers Cyril and Methodius have created an alphabet for the Slavs (863), and Bulgaria, Serbia and then Kievan Rus', with the baptism of Vladimir (988), have received Greek Christianity in a Slavic language.

Moscow, the third Rome

After 1453, Russia regards itself as the heir of Byzantium: a monk of Pskov writes c. 1520 that two Romes have fallen, that the third stands and that there will be no fourth. The patriarchate of Moscow is created in 1589. The liturgical reforms of Patriarch Nikon (1653-1667), which bring the Russian books into line with the Greek ones, provoke the Raskol: the Old Believers, led by the archpriest Avvakum, burned in 1682, refuse the new rites and form a dissenting body persecuted until the twentieth century. Peter the Great, in 1721, abolishes the patriarchate and entrusts the Church to a Holy Synod presided over by a lay official, the chief procurator: the Church becomes a department of the State, until 1917.

The startsy and Dostoevsky

Nineteenth-century Russia experiences a spiritual revival coming from the monasteries. Paisius Velichkovsky translated the Philokalia (1793), a collection of the hesychast Fathers; Seraphim of Sarov (1754-1833) embodies popular sanctity; the monastery of Optina, near Kozelsk, shelters a line of startsy (singular starets), elder monks who receive visitors, hear confessions and give counsel. Gogol, Tolstoy, Soloviev and Dostoevsky go there. Dostoevsky spends two days there in June 1878, after the death of his son Alyosha, and meets the starets Ambrose; the starets Zosima of The Brothers Karamazov owes him his features. The opposition between the Slavophiles, for whom Orthodoxy and the peasant commune carry a truth that the West has lost, and the Westernizers nourishes his Russian idea; his distrust of Roman Catholicism, which he accuses of having traded Christ for power, explains why the Grand Inquisitor is a cardinal of Seville.

What the essay assumes

The essay names Zosima, Markel and the starets, and reads the Grand Inquisitor as a parable of religious power; Dostoevsky's anti-Catholicism is its context. It takes as known the difference between a State Church without scholasticism, whose theology is negative and monastic, and the Roman Church to which almost all its authors address themselves.

Key dates

  • 330: foundation of Constantinople; 843: end of iconoclasm.
  • 863: mission of Cyril and Methodius; 988: baptism of Vladimir.
  • 1054: mutual excommunications; 1204: sack of Constantinople; 1453: fall of the city.
  • 1589: patriarchate of Moscow; 1653-1667: Nikon's reforms and the Raskol; 1721: Holy Synod.
  • 1878: Dostoevsky at Optina.

In the essay: Dostoevsky · Part Two

Further reading: John Meyendorff, Byzantine Theology (1974) ; Olivier Clément, L'Église orthodoxe (1961) ; Pierre Gonneau, Histoire de la Russie, d'Ivan le Terrible à Nicolas II (2016) ; Georges Florovsky, Les Voies de la théologie russe (1937)

Scholasticism and the condemnations: faith and reason in the medieval universityHistory

Scholasticism is the philosophy and theology taught in the schools and then the universities of the Latin West, from the eleventh to the fourteenth century, by a method built on the reading of authorities, disputed questions and summae. It settled the relation between faith and reason for centuries, and then undid it itself; Arab and Jewish authors played a part in it that the essay recalls in connection with Averroes and Maimonides.

Anselm, the translations, the universities

Anselm of Canterbury (1033-1109) gives the programme its motto, faith seeking understanding, and the so-called ontological argument of the Proslogion. In the twelfth century, translations made in Toledo and Sicily bring almost the whole of Aristotle into Latin, with his Arab commentators, Avicenna and Averroes, and Maimonides' Guide for the Perplexed (c. 1220-1240). The universities of Bologna, Paris and Oxford arise around 1200; in Paris, the faculty of arts teaches philosophy, the faculty of theology sacred doctrine, and the tension between the two shapes the history of the century. Aristotle's physics and metaphysics are banned there in 1210 and in 1215, then tolerated, then made compulsory around 1255.

Thomas Aquinas: the two orders

Albert the Great and his pupil Thomas Aquinas (1225-1274), Dominicans, set out to integrate Aristotle into theology. The Summa Theologiae distinguishes what natural reason can demonstrate, the preambles of faith, the existence of God by the five ways (motion, efficient cause, the contingent and the necessary, the degrees of perfection, finality), from what revelation alone teaches, the Trinity and the Incarnation, which reason can defend against objections without proving them. One truth, two orders of knowledge, ranked in a hierarchy: this is the solution the essay calls the scholastic regulation of the demand. Thomas calls Maimonides Rabbi Moyses and Averroes the Commentator, while writing against him.

Latin Averroism and the condemnations

In the faculty of arts, masters such as Siger of Brabant and Boethius of Dacia teach Aristotle as Averroes reads him: the eternity of the world, the unity of the intellect for all men, the happiness of the philosopher in contemplation. They claim to speak as philosophers, according to the principles of reason, without denying the faith. Thomas attacks them in 1270 in On the Unity of the Intellect; the bishop of Paris, Étienne Tempier, condemns thirteen propositions in 1270, then two hundred and nineteen on 7 March 1277, some of which target Thomas himself, who had died three years earlier. The prologue to the condemnation reproaches the masters with maintaining that things are true according to philosophy and not according to faith, as if there were two contrary truths: this is the origin of the legend of the double truth, which Averroes did not teach. Pierre Duhem argued that 1277, by asserting God's omnipotence against Aristotelian necessity, had freed the imagination of physicists.

Duns Scotus, Ockham and nominalism

The fourteenth century draws the consequences. John Duns Scotus (d. 1308) places the divine will above the intellect; William of Ockham (c. 1287-1347), a Franciscan prosecuted at Avignon, maintains that universals are only names or concepts, that only individuals exist, and that God, by his absolute power, could have made everything quite otherwise: theology is no longer demonstrated, it is believed, and reason falls back on logic and experience. This via moderna shaped Luther, and Blumenberg sees in it the theological absolutism against which modernity asserts itself.

What the essay assumes

The essay mentions the condemnation of 1277, the five ways, the abyss of will that Duns Scotus and Ockham would recognize in Descartes, and nominalism in Blumenberg; it takes as known that scholasticism had regulated the rational demand received from the Greeks, and that the classical age set it free.

Key dates

  • 1078: Anselm's Proslogion; c. 1200: universities of Paris and Oxford.
  • 1210, 1215: bans on Aristotle in Paris; c. 1255: Aristotle on the curriculum.
  • 1270 and 1277: Tempier's condemnations; 1274: death of Thomas Aquinas.
  • 1308: death of Duns Scotus; 1347: death of Ockham.

In the essay: Averroes · Maimonides · Hans Blumenberg · Transition (part one) · Descartes

Further reading: Étienne Gilson, La Philosophie au Moyen Âge (1922) ; Alain de Libera, La Philosophie médiévale (1993) ; Luca Bianchi, Censure et liberté intellectuelle à l'université de Paris (1999) ; Kurt Flasch, Introduction à la philosophie médiévale (1987)

Renaissance, humanism and printing: the return to the sourcesHistory

Between the middle of the fourteenth and the end of the sixteenth century, Europe rediscovers the texts of Antiquity, invents printing and transforms its relation to religion without yet criticizing it head-on. Montaigne, with whom the third part of the essay opens, is the heir of this movement, and Descartes its late contemporary.

The humanists and the return to the sources

The word humanist first designates a profession: the teacher of the humanities, grammar, rhetoric, poetry, history and moral philosophy, studied in the ancient authors. Petrarch, in the fourteenth century, searches for the manuscripts of Cicero and sets the tone; in the fifteenth, the Italians recover Lucretius (the De rerum natura, copied by Poggio Bracciolini in 1417), Plato, whom Marsilio Ficino translates into Latin in Florence, and the Greek Fathers. The return to the sources, ad fontes, is at once philological and religious: Lorenzo Valla demonstrates in 1440 that the Donation of Constantine is a forgery, and applies criticism to the text of the New Testament; Erasmus (c. 1466-1536) publishes in 1516 the first printed Greek New Testament, with a new Latin translation, and proposes in The Praise of Folly and the Colloquies an inward piety that mocks monks, relics and pilgrimages, and that prepares the Reformation without joining it: Erasmus breaks with Luther in 1524 over the question of free will. Pico della Mirandola, in his Oration on the Dignity of Man (1486), makes man the being without a fixed nature who shapes himself. Humanism is not an atheism; it is another way of being Christian, nourished by the Ancients, and sometimes a dreamed-of concord between Plato, Moses and Christ.

Printing

Gutenberg prints in Mainz, around 1455, the first Bible in movable type. By 1500, more than two hundred towns have presses and some twenty million volumes have been produced. Printing fixes texts, multiplies readers, lowers the price of books and makes possible the rapid spread of ideas: Luther's theses go round Germany in a few weeks, and Bibles in the vernacular put Scripture into the hands of the laity. It also creates modern censorship: the Index of Forbidden Books is instituted by Rome in 1559. The culture of the printed book is that of Montaigne, who reads Plutarch, Seneca and Sextus Empiricus in his library, and whose Essays (1580, 1588, 1595) are among the first great books written in the first person for a public of anonymous readers.

Scepticism and custom

The rediscovery of ancient scepticism, with Sextus Empiricus translated into Latin in 1562 and 1569, supplies the thinkers of the end of the century with a new instrument. Faced with the Wars of Religion, Montaigne draws from Pyrrhonism a lesson of modesty: reason cannot ground faith, which depends on custom and birth, and this is what he calls being Christian as one is a native of Périgord. This position, fideism, is held by sincere believers, Montaigne, Charron, and also serves, a century later, the libertines. Descartes, trained by the Jesuits of La Flèche in humanist culture, will turn away from it to seek in reason alone a foundation that custom does not provide.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes for granted the meaning of the word humanist in the Renaissance, different from Feuerbach's humanism, the return of the Ancients, Lucretius and Plato, whom Montaigne reads, the role of printing in the Reformation, and the genealogy of Montaigne's scepticism, which it reads as a sincere fideism whose unintended effect is custom as the sole explanation of faith.

Dates

  • 1417: rediscovery of Lucretius' De rerum natura.
  • c. 1455: Gutenberg Bible.
  • 1484: Ficino's Latin translation of Plato.
  • 1516: Erasmus' Greek New Testament.
  • 1559: first Roman Index of Forbidden Books.
  • 1562: Sextus Empiricus in Latin; 1580: Montaigne's first Essays.

In the essay: Montaigne · Part Three

Further reading: Jacob Burckhardt, La Civilisation de la Renaissance en Italie (1860) ; Lucien Febvre and Henri-Jean Martin, L'Apparition du livre (1958) ; Eugenio Garin, L'educazione in Europa (1957) ; Elizabeth Eisenstein, The Printing Press as an Agent of Change (1979) ; Jean Delumeau, La Civilisation de la Renaissance (1967)

The Protestant Reformation: Luther, Calvin and the break-up of ChristendomHistory

The Reformation is the rupture which, in the sixteenth century, divides Latin Christendom into rival Churches. It arises from a question of salvation, raised by a German monk, and within a generation becomes an affair of princes, cities and wars. Almost all the German, Danish and Swiss authors of the essay, from Kant to Bonhoeffer, are heirs of this rupture.

Luther

Martin Luther (1483-1546), an Augustinian friar and professor of Scripture at Wittenberg, posts or sends on 31 October 1517 ninety-five theses against the trade in indulgences. His doctrine fits into a few formulas: man is justified by faith alone, not by his works; by grace alone; Scripture alone has authority, neither tradition nor the pope; every baptized person is a priest. Excommunicated in 1521, placed under the ban of the Empire at the Diet of Worms, he translates the New Testament (1522) and then the Bible (1534) into German, and printing, invented some sixty years earlier, spreads his writings in tens of thousands of copies: the Reformation is the first mass movement of the age of print. Luther condemns the Peasants' Revolt (1525) and entrusts to the princes the organization of the territorial Churches; the Augsburg Confession (1530) fixes Lutheran doctrine.

Calvin and the Reformed tradition

Zwingli in Zurich, then John Calvin (1509-1564), a French jurist in exile, give the Reformation its second form. The Institutes of the Christian Religion (1536 in Latin, 1541 in French) systematizes the doctrine: the absolute sovereignty of God, predestination, a sense of the divine inscribed in every human being, a Church governed by pastors and elders. In Geneva, from 1541 until his death, Calvin makes the city a disciplined commonwealth; Michael Servetus, an antitrinitarian, is burned there in 1553. Calvinism wins over France (the Huguenots), the Netherlands, Scotland with John Knox, and nourishes English Puritanism. England follows its own path: Henry VIII makes himself head of the Church (1534), Elizabeth sets a compromise in 1559, Anglicanism. The radical reformers, Anabaptists and spiritualists, who reject infant baptism and any state Church, are persecuted on all sides.

Counter-Reformation and confessions

Rome responds with the Council of Trent (1545-1563), which reaffirms tradition, the sacraments and works, reforms the clergy and creates the seminaries; the Jesuits (1540) and the Index (1559) are its instruments. The Peace of Augsburg (1555) leaves each prince of the Empire the choice of his subjects' religion. Europe divides into confessions, each with its catechisms, its schools and its control of morals; historians speak of confessionalization. The wars that follow, in France, in the Netherlands, then the Thirty Years' War, are its consequence.

Legacy

Lutheranism becomes the state religion in the Scandinavian kingdoms: the Church of Denmark that Kierkegaard attacks in 1855 is that of the Reformation of 1536. Pietism in the seventeenth century, then the liberal theology of the nineteenth, from Schleiermacher to Harnack, and Barth's dialectical theology in the twentieth, are moments of Protestant history; Bonhoeffer is a Lutheran pastor. Weber sought in Calvinist asceticism the origin of the spirit of capitalism, and in Protestantism the last act of disenchantment.

What the essay assumes

The essay says that the Reformation broke the unity of Christendom, sets Kierkegaard against the established Church, cites, through Plantinga, Calvin's sense of the divine and connects Weber with Puritanism; it takes for granted the principles of faith alone and Scripture alone, and the difference between Lutherans and the Reformed.

Key dates

  • 1517: the Wittenberg theses; 1521: Diet of Worms.
  • 1530: Augsburg Confession; 1534: Luther's Bible, Act of Supremacy in England.
  • 1536: Calvin's Institutes, Reformation in Denmark; 1541: Calvin in Geneva.
  • 1545-1563: Council of Trent; 1555: Peace of Augsburg.

In the essay: Weber · Søren Kierkegaard · Barth · Bonhoeffer · Part Three

Further reading: Lucien Febvre, Un destin : Martin Luther (1928) ; Jean Delumeau, Naissance et affirmation de la Réforme (1965) ; Diarmaid MacCulloch, Reformation : Europe's House Divided (2003) ; Pierre Chaunu, Le Temps des Réformes (1975)

Wars of Religion and the birth of toleration: from St Bartholomew's Day to BayleHistory

Between 1560 and 1690, Europe learns at its own cost that a society divided into confessions cannot be governed by a single truth. From this experience come the two ideas the essay follows in Montaigne, Spinoza and Bayle: that religion is not the necessary foundation of political order, and that conscience cannot be coerced.

France: eight wars and an edict

From 1562 to 1598, Catholics and Huguenots wage eight wars interrupted by precarious peaces. The St Bartholomew's Day massacre, in Paris on 24 August 1572 and then in the provinces, leaves several thousand dead; Montaigne, who writes the Essays during these years and will be mayor of Bordeaux (1581-1585), draws from it his distrust of any certainty that arms men. A party of jurists, the Politiques, among them Jean Bodin, maintains that the State must survive religious division. Henry IV, a Protestant converted to Catholicism (1593), promulgates the Edict of Nantes (1598): freedom of conscience for the Reformed, worship in designated places, places of safety. This is not toleration as a principle, but coexistence imposed by weariness.

The Empire and the United Provinces

The Thirty Years' War (1618-1648) devastates Germany; the treaties of Westphalia (1648) establish the parity of the confessions within the Empire and transfer Church property to the princes: this is where the legal use of the word secularization appears, which the confiscations of the Napoleonic era will generalize. In the Low Countries, the provinces in revolt against Spain proclaim as early as 1579 that no one shall be persecuted for his religion. Amsterdam welcomes the Jews expelled from the Iberian Peninsula, among them Spinoza's family, Protestant dissenters and printers from all over Europe. Toleration there has its limits: Spinoza is struck in 1656 by the herem of his community, his friend Koerbagh dies in prison in 1669, and the Theological-Political Treatise, published anonymously in 1670, is banned in 1674.

The Revocation and the Refuge

Louis XIV, absolute and Most Christian King, curtails the rights of the Reformed, then persecutes them: dragonnades, forced conversions, and the revocation of the Edict of Nantes by the Edict of Fontainebleau (1685). Two hundred thousand Huguenots flee to the United Provinces, England, Prussia and Switzerland. Pierre Bayle, a pastor's son, converted to Catholicism and then returned to Protestantism, is one of these exiles: a refugee in Rotterdam since 1681, he learns that his brother has died in a French prison. His Philosophical Commentary on the words of the Gospel, compel them to come in (1686), maintains that conscience, even when mistaken, must not be forced; the Various Thoughts on the Comet (1682-1683) had already argued that a society of atheists could subsist. His quarrel with Pierre Jurieu, a pastor of the Refuge who demands against Rome the intolerance of the righteous, shows that toleration divided the persecuted themselves.

Libertines, Locke and toleration

In Paris, between 1620 and 1650, the erudite libertines, Gabriel Naudé, La Mothe Le Vayer, Gassendi, readers of Montaigne and Charron, practise a discreet scepticism and treat religion as a matter of public order; clandestine manuscripts, among them the Treatise of the Three Impostors, circulate until the eighteenth century. In England, after the civil war and the revolution of 1688, Locke's Letter Concerning Toleration (1689) separates the ends of the State, which are civil, from those of the Church, which concern salvation; it still excludes Catholics and atheists. The Enlightenment will inherit these three justifications of freedom of conscience, sceptical, political and theological.

What the essay assumes

The essay names the Wars of Religion, Bayle's exile, the herem and Spinoza's Amsterdam, and the libertines who made Montaigne's Apology their breviary; it takes as known St Bartholomew's Day, the Edict of Nantes and its revocation, and the legal origin of the word secularization.

Key dates

  • 1562-1598: Wars of Religion; 1572: St Bartholomew's Day; 1598: Edict of Nantes.
  • 1618-1648: Thirty Years' War; 1648: Westphalia.
  • 1656: Spinoza's herem; 1670: Theological-Political Treatise.
  • 1685: revocation; 1686: Philosophical Commentary; 1689: Letter Concerning Toleration.

In the essay: Bayle · Spinoza · Part Three · Montaigne

Further reading: Denis Crouzet, Les Guerriers de Dieu (1990) ; Elisabeth Labrousse, Pierre Bayle (1963-1964) ; Jonathan Israel, Radical Enlightenment (2001) ; Steven Nadler, Spinoza, a Life (1999) ; René Pintard, Le Libertinage érudit (1943)

From absolutism to the Revolution: the Church of France, the Enlightenment and the cult of the Supreme BeingHistory

The France of the Ancien Régime is a monarchy by divine right in which the Catholic Church is the first order of the realm; in ten years the Revolution strips it of its property, its clergy and its calendar, then attempts to replace it. The revolutionary cults of 1793 and 1794 are, for Durkheim as for Comte, the experiment that shows what becomes of religion when one tries to do without it.

The Gallican Church and its quarrels

The king, anointed at Reims, holds his power from God alone; Bossuet sets out the theory of this in the Politique tirée de l'Écriture sainte (1709). The Church of France is Gallican: the declaration of 1682 limits the pope's authority over the realm, and the king appoints the bishops. It is riven by the Jansenist quarrel: the Augustinian theology of grace, defended at Port-Royal and by Pascal in the Provinciales (1656-1657), is condemned by Rome and by the king, the monastery is razed in 1710, and the bull Unigenitus (1713) divides the clergy for half a century. The parlements, Jansenist and Gallican, obtain the expulsion of the Jesuits (1764). Protestantism has been outlawed since 1685; the edict of 1787 restores civil status to the Reformed.

The Enlightenment and the Infamous

Royal and ecclesiastical censorship strikes the Encyclopédie (1759), Emile and The Social Contract (1762, condemned in Paris and in Geneva), and the Système de la nature (1770). Voltaire campaigns against the Infamous, fanaticism and the Church, around the Calas affair (1762); d'Holbach and his coterie privately profess an atheism that Voltaire judges dangerous for the people. Historians have shown that religious practice was already declining, slowly, among the urban elites, but that the mass of the realm remained faithful.

The Revolution against the Church

The Assembly nationalizes the property of the clergy (2 November 1789) and votes the Civil Constitution of the Clergy (12 July 1790), which makes priests elected public officials; the oath required in 1791 splits the clergy into jurors and refractories, and the pope condemns the Constitution. War and the Terror turn the rupture into persecution: priests deported or executed, civil registration secularized (1792), the republican calendar (October 1793), dechristianization carried out by the representatives on mission, the closing of churches, the festival of Reason at Notre-Dame on 10 November 1793. Robespierre, who regards atheism as aristocratic and venerates Rousseau, has the Convention decree, on 18 Floréal Year II (7 May 1794), that the French people recognizes the existence of the Supreme Being and the immortality of the soul, and presides over the festival of the Supreme Being on 20 Prairial (8 June 1794). Thermidor, seven weeks later, ends the cult together with its founder. The Directory separates Church and State (February 1795) and lets theophilanthropy, a natural religion with a civic liturgy, and the decadal cult flourish.

The Concordat

Bonaparte signs the Concordat with Pius VII (15 July 1801): Catholicism, the religion of the great majority of the French, regains public worship, bishops are appointed by the State and instituted by the pope, and the clergy is salaried; the Organic Articles (1802) add Gallicanism to it. This regime lasts until the separation of 1905. The nineteenth century will oscillate between the alliance of throne and altar and anticlericalism; Saint-Simon, Comte and their disciples will seek a new religion in that century.

What the essay assumes

The essay quotes the decree of 18 Floréal and the festival of the Supreme Being, compares Robespierre's cult with Rousseau's civil religion, recalls that Durkheim sees in the Revolution the proof of society's capacity to create gods and that Comte judges the revolutionary cults too metaphysical; it mentions the condemnation of Emile and the refuge of Port-Royal. It assumes familiarity with Gallicanism, Jansenism and the dechristianization of Year II.

Key dates

  • 1682: Gallican declaration; 1713: bull Unigenitus; 1764: expulsion of the Jesuits.
  • 1790: Civil Constitution of the Clergy; 1793: republican calendar, festival of Reason.
  • 7 May and 8 June 1794: decree and festival of the Supreme Being; 27 July 1794: fall of Robespierre.
  • 1795: separation; 1801: Concordat.

In the essay: Rousseau · Voltaire · Durkheim · Part Four · Auguste Comte

Further reading: Michel Vovelle, La Révolution contre l'Église (1988) ; Bernard Plongeron, Théologie et politique au siècle des Lumières (1973) ; Mona Ozouf, La Fête révolutionnaire (1976) ; Jean-Marie Mayeur (ed.), Histoire du christianisme, vol. X (1997)

The nineteenth century: the Church against modernity, theology before criticismHistory

The nineteenth century is at once the century in which the critique of religion takes its modern forms, from Feuerbach to Nietzsche, and the century of a massive religious revival. The Catholic Church sets itself against the world that issued from the Revolution; German Protestantism invents the historical criticism of the Bible and liberal theology. Almost all the authors of the fifth and sixth parts of the essay write in this context.

Religious revival and Catholic intransigence

After the Revolution and the Concordat of 1801, Catholicism undergoes a renewal in France and in Europe: refounded congregations, missions, pilgrimages, the apparitions of Lourdes in 1858, Marian piety and devotion to the pope. This Catholicism is ultramontane, that is, turned towards Rome against the national Churches, and intransigent towards political modernity. Pius IX, pope from 1846 to 1878, proclaims the dogma of the Immaculate Conception in 1854 and publishes in 1864 the Syllabus, a catalogue of eighty modern errors, the last of which condemns the idea that the Roman pontiff can reconcile himself with progress, liberalism and modern civilization. The First Vatican Council (1869-1870) defines papal infallibility in matters of faith and morals; it is interrupted by the capture of Rome, which puts an end to the Papal States. In Germany, Bismarck launches the Kulturkampf against the Church (1871-1878), expelling the Jesuits and imprisoning bishops, before backing down. Leo XIII (1878-1903) opens the Church to the social question with Rerum novarum (1891) and revives Thomism, but Pius X's condemnation of modernism in 1907 closes the way to critical exegesis among Catholics for half a century.

Tübingen, biblical criticism and liberal theology

In the German Protestant universities, the Bible becomes an object of history. After Reimarus, whose fragments Lessing publishes in 1774-1778, the Tübingen school around Ferdinand Christian Baur applies a Hegelian reading to the history of early Christianity: the opposition between the Jewish Christianity of Peter and the universalism of Paul, then the Catholic synthesis. David Friedrich Strauss, a former student at Tübingen, publishes in 1835 the Life of Jesus, which reads the gospels as myths, and loses his chair. Julius Wellhausen establishes the documentary hypothesis on the Pentateuch in 1878. At the same time, liberal theology, heir to Schleiermacher, seeks to reconcile faith and culture: Albrecht Ritschl reduces Christianity to a moral experience, Adolf von Harnack, in What Is Christianity? (1900), to the fatherhood of God and the infinite value of the soul; this Kulturprotestantismus, cultural Protestantism, will be Barth's target after 1914. In France, Renan's Life of Jesus (1863) transposes German criticism into a best-selling narrative and costs its author his chair at the Collège de France.

The birth of the sciences of religion

The century sees the birth of the disciplines that study religion as a fact: the comparative philology of Max Müller, the anthropology of Tylor, the history of religions, whose first French chair is created in 1880 at the Collège de France, then the sociology of Durkheim. Comte's positivism, which announces the end of the theological age and founds a religion of Humanity, is its most ambitious form.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes knowledge of the climate of Catholic intransigence, which explains the violence of the conflict between science and faith, the difference between Strauss's historical criticism and the liberal theology it shakes, the scandal of Renan, the position of the cultural Protestantism against which Barth will rise, and the fact that the Young Hegelians write in a Prussian State that calls itself Christian.

The dates

  • 1835: Strauss's Life of Jesus; 1863: Renan's Life of Jesus.
  • 1854: Immaculate Conception; 1858: Lourdes.
  • 1864: Syllabus; 1870: papal infallibility and the capture of Rome.
  • 1871-1878: Kulturkampf.
  • 1891: Rerum novarum; 1900: Harnack's What Is Christianity?.
  • 1907: condemnation of modernism.

In the essay: Part Five · Ernest Renan · Part Six · Auguste Comte

Further reading: Émile Poulat, Église contre bourgeoisie (1977) ; Owen Chadwick, The Secularization of the European Mind in the Nineteenth Century (1975) ; Gérard Cholvy and Yves-Marie Hilaire, Histoire religieuse de la France contemporaine (1985-1988) ; Jacques Gadille and Jean-Marie Mayeur (eds.), Histoire du christianisme, vol. 11 (1995) ; Albert Schweitzer, Von Reimarus zu Wrede (1906)

French laïcité: from Jules Ferry's school to the law of 1905History

Laïcité is the French form of the exit of religion from the State, built between 1880 and 1905 against a Catholic Church then hostile to the Republic. It is the framework of Durkheim, of Gauchet and of contemporary debates on the religious, and the essay takes it as known.

The school and the Republic

The word laïcité appears in the 1870s. The Third Republic, founded in 1870 and consolidated in 1879 by the victory of the republicans, comes up against a Church that had supported the Moral Order and had condemned modern liberalism in the Syllabus of 1864. The first front is the school. The Jules Ferry laws make primary education free (1881), then compulsory and secular (1882): religious instruction leaves the curriculum, replaced by moral and civic instruction, and one day off is left for catechism outside school. The Goblet law of 1886 entrusts public education to lay staff. The school of the Republic's black hussars trains citizens; secular morality, of which Durkheim is one of the theorists at the Sorbonne, seeks to ground duty without God, on society. At the same time, divorce is restored (1884), and cemeteries and hospitals are secularized.

The failed rallying and the Dreyfus affair

In 1892 Leo XIII invites French Catholics to rally to the Republic. The rallying largely fails, and the Dreyfus affair, from 1894 to 1906, reopens the rift: part of the clergy and the Catholic press, the Assumptionists' La Croix, side with the anti-Dreyfusard and antisemitic camp. The victory of the Dreyfusards brings the Bloc des gauches to power; the law of 1901 on associations makes religious congregations subject to authorization, and Émile Combes, head of government from 1902 to 1905, closes thousands of their establishments, forbids members of congregations to teach (1904) and breaks with the Vatican.

The law of 1905

The law of 9 December 1905 on the separation of the Churches and the State, which Briand and Jaurès carried against the partisans of a punitive separation, rests on two articles of principle: the Republic ensures freedom of conscience and guarantees the free exercise of worship; it recognizes, pays and subsidizes no religion. The Concordat of 1801 is abrogated, and Church property is entrusted to associations for worship. Pius X condemns the law (Vehementer nos, 1906) and forbids the associations; the quarrel over the inventories causes incidents, after which the buildings remain at the disposal of the faithful. Appeasement comes after 1914: the union sacrée, the restoration of diplomatic relations (1921), Pius XI's acceptance of diocesan associations (1924). Alsace-Moselle, German in 1905, keeps the concordat regime. Laïcité becomes a constitutional principle in 1946 and 1958.

Models and debates

French laïcité differs from the American model, where separation protects the Churches from the State as much as the reverse, and from the European regimes of an established Church or of the recognition of religions. The debate revives from 1989 onwards, with the headscarf affairs at school, the 2004 law on religious symbols and the discussions on Islam, in which a laïcité of neutrality and a laïcité of combat confront each other. Gauchet sees in it the effect of an exit from religion that is not an exit from belief: religion returns in democracy as identity and as choice.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the conflict between the Republic and the Church, which explains the tone of the French sciences of religion and Durkheim's secular morality, the content of the law of 1905, and the difference between French laïcité and the secularization that Taylor or Habermas describe for other countries. It relies on Gauchet to think what remains of the religious in a secular society.

The dates

  • 1882: compulsory and secular school.
  • 1892: encyclical of the rallying.
  • 1894-1906: Dreyfus affair.
  • 1901: law on associations ; 1904: congregations forbidden to teach.
  • 9 December 1905: law of separation.
  • 1924: diocesan associations ; 1946: laïcité in the Constitution.
  • 2004: law on religious symbols at school.

In the essay: Marcel Gauchet · Part Eleven

Further reading: Jean Baubérot, Histoire de la laïcité en France (2000) ; Émile Poulat, Liberté, laïcité (1987) ; Jean-Marie Mayeur, La Séparation des Églises et de l'État (1966) ; Jacqueline Lalouette, La République anticléricale (2002) ; Marcel Gauchet, La Religion dans la démocratie (1998)

The Churches and the totalitarian regimes: Barmen, the concordats, state atheismHistory

Between 1917 and 1945, the Christian Churches faced regimes that laid claim to the whole of man. They resisted, accommodated themselves or collaborated, and contemporary thinkers, Voegelin and Aron, described these regimes as religions without God, while Camus would speak of the divinization of history. Barth, Bonhoeffer and Schmitt, read in the essay, took positions within this history.

The Soviet Union and state atheism

The October Revolution made atheism a policy. The decree of January 1918 separated the Church from the State and the school from the Church, confiscated ecclesiastical property and stripped the Churches of legal personality. Persecution struck the Orthodox clergy from the civil war onwards, then peaked in the 1930s: the closure or destruction of most churches, tens of thousands of priests and monks shot or deported, and the League of Militant Godless, founded in 1925, entrusted with anti-religious propaganda. The Orthodox Church survived by submitting, through Metropolitan Sergius's declaration of loyalty in 1927, then enjoyed a respite during the war, when Stalin restored the patriarchate in 1943. After 1945 state atheism was extended to the people's democracies, with uneven violence, from Albania, which proclaimed itself the first atheist state in 1967, to Poland, where the Church remained powerful.

Nazi Germany: concordat, Deutsche Christen, Barmen

Hitler came to power in January 1933. In July the Holy See signed with the Reich a concordat guaranteeing freedom of worship and denominational education in exchange for the clergy's withdrawal from politics; the Catholic Centre Party had dissolved itself two weeks earlier. The concordat of 1933 was violated almost at once, and Pius XI protested in 1937 with the encyclical Mit brennender Sorge, read from the pulpit throughout Germany. On the Protestant side, the movement of the Deutsche Christen, the German Christians, wanted a Reich Church conforming to Nazism, purged of the Old Testament and of pastors of Jewish origin. Against them arose Martin Niemöller's Pastors' Emergency League, then the Confessing Church, whose synod at Barmen, in May 1934, adopted a theological declaration drafted for the most part by Karl Barth: Jesus Christ is the one Word of God, and the Church cannot recognize other events, powers or figures as revelation. Barth, who refused the oath to the Führer, was expelled from his chair at Bonn in 1935 and returned to Switzerland. Bonhoeffer directed the clandestine seminary of Finkenwalde, entered the resistance through the Abwehr, was arrested in 1943 and hanged at Flossenbürg on 9 April 1945. Carl Schmitt, a Catholic jurist, joined the party in May 1933 and justified the murders of June 1934 in an article asserting that the Führer protects the law, before being sidelined in 1936.

Italian Fascism and political religions

In 1929 Mussolini signed the Lateran Accords, which created the State of Vatican City and made Catholicism the religion of the Italian State; conflicts over youth followed in 1931. Faced with these regimes, contemporary observers forged the concepts the essay discusses: Voegelin, in The Political Religions (1938), describes the divinization of the people and of the State; Aron speaks in 1944 of secular religions, doctrines that take the place of faith and locate salvation here below; Berdyaev saw in communism an inverted religion; Emilio Gentile would speak of the sacralization of politics.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the Barmen Declaration as the context of Barth's theology, with Bonhoeffer's commitment and death, which give the prison letters their weight, with Schmitt's adherence to Nazism, and with the fact that the secular religions of Aron and Voegelin refer first of all to communism and Nazism, the most massive cases of the sacrifice criterion.

Dates

  • 1918: Soviet separation; 1925: League of the Godless.
  • 1929: Lateran Accords.
  • 1933: Reich Concordat; 1934: synod of Barmen.
  • 1937: Mit brennender Sorge.
  • 1938: Voegelin's The Political Religions.
  • 1943: restoration of the Moscow patriarchate.
  • 9 April 1945: execution of Bonhoeffer.

In the essay: Barth · Bonhoeffer · Voegelin and Aron · Carl Schmitt · Part Eleven

Further reading: Klaus Scholder, Die Kirchen und das Dritte Reich (1977-1985) ; Eberhard Bethge, Dietrich Bonhoeffer (1967) ; Nikolai Berdyaev, Les Sources et le sens du communisme russe (1938) ; Emilio Gentile, Les Religions de la politique (2001) ; Philippe Chenaux, L'Église catholique et le communisme en Europe (2009)

Vatican II, the secularization of Europe and the return of the religiousHistory

The second half of the twentieth century sees the Catholic Church reconcile itself with modernity at the very moment when Europe, within a generation, stops practising. Then the religious returns, in other forms, and a new militant atheism answers it. This is the present in which the authors of the eleventh and twelfth parts of the essay write.

Vatican II

John XXIII, elected in 1958, convenes a council for an aggiornamento, an updating of the Church. Vatican II sits from 1962 to 1965 under John XXIII and then Paul VI and brings together more than two thousand bishops. Its major texts reform the liturgy, now in the vernacular and facing the congregation (Sacrosanctum concilium, 1963), redefine the Church as the people of God (Lumen gentium, 1964), recognize religious freedom as a right of the person (Dignitatis humanae, 1965), open dialogue with other religions and release the Jews from the charge of deicide (Nostra aetate, 1965), and affirm the solidarity of the Church with the world of its time (Gaudium et spes, 1965). The council closes the intransigence of the nineteenth century: it accepts democracy, the autonomy of the political and critical exegesis. It also provokes a crisis: mass departures of priests and religious, quarrels over the liturgy, the traditionalist schism of Archbishop Lefebvre (1988), and the restoration of order carried out by John Paul II (1978-2005) and Benedict XVI (2005-2013).

The rupture of the 1960s

Throughout Western Europe, religious practice collapses between 1965 and 1975: Sunday Mass, attended by a quarter of the French at the beginning of the 1960s, is attended by only about one in ten around 1980, and ordinations fall by half; the same curves can be read in the Netherlands, Belgium and Quebec. Historians debate the causes: prosperity and the welfare state, sexual liberation and contraception, condemned by Humanae vitae in 1968, a culture of autonomy, and the effects of the council itself. Family transmission breaks down, and the following generations are no longer disaffiliated but simply without religion. The sociology of secularization, Berger, Luckmann, Bryan Wilson, theorizes this development, which Norris and Inglehart will measure and which Taylor will call the secular age.

The return of the religious and the new atheists

From the late 1970s onwards, the religious returns to the public stage in forms that secularization had not foreseen: the Islamic revolution in Iran (1979), the election of John Paul II (1978) and the role of the Church in the fall of Polish communism, the Christian right in the United States, evangelical and Pentecostal movements in the global South, a revival of pilgrimages and of spiritualities outside institutions. In 1991 Gilles Kepel speaks of the revenge of God, in 1999 Berger retracts his secularization thesis, in 2001 Habermas coins the word postsecular. The attacks of 11 September 2001 give birth to a militant atheism: Sam Harris (2004), Richard Dawkins and Daniel Dennett (2006), Christopher Hitchens (2007), the new atheists, and in France Onfray's Traité d'athéologie (2005). At the same time, the share of people without religion keeps growing in Europe, and Islam becomes the second religion of several countries.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the turning point of Vatican II, which makes possible a Catholicism reconciled with democracy, the background to the positions of Taylor and Habermas, the collapse of European practice, which Hervieu-Léger describes as the end of a world, the return of the religious, which motivates postsecular theories, and the context of the new atheists, whom it places in the poorest form of the regime of error.

The dates

  • 1962-1965: Second Vatican Council.
  • 1968: Humanae vitae ; 1965-1975: collapse of practice.
  • 1978: election of John Paul II ; 1979: Iranian revolution.
  • 1988: schism of Archbishop Lefebvre.
  • 1999: Berger's retraction ; 2001: Habermas's Glauben und Wissen.
  • 2004-2007: books of the new atheists ; 2005: Traité d'athéologie.

In the essay: Part Eleven · Part Twelve · Contemporary militant atheists · Hervieu-Léger

Further reading: Giuseppe Alberigo (ed.), Histoire du concile Vatican II (1995-2005) ; Hugh McLeod, The Religious Crisis of the 1960s (2007) ; Gilles Kepel, La Revanche de Dieu (1991) ; Danièle Hervieu-Léger, Catholicisme, la fin d'un monde (2003) ; Guillaume Cuchet, Comment notre monde a cessé d'être chrétien (2018)

Disciplines and methods

The sociology of religion: from Comte to secularization theoryDisciplines and methods

The sociology of religion studies religion as a social fact: what it does to groups, what groups make of it, and how it changes along with societies. Born with sociology itself, it supplies the essay with several of its major authors and with the background of its eleventh part.

The founders

Comte, who coins the word sociology in 1839, makes religion the first state of the human mind and ends by founding one. Tocqueville observes in America a religion that supports democracy instead of fighting it. But the discipline truly takes shape with two contemporary works. Durkheim, in The Elementary Forms of Religious Life (1912), defines religion by the distinction between the sacred and the profane and by the Church that unites the faithful, and seeks in Australian totemism the simplest form of the phenomenon: religion is society representing itself, and its rites periodically remake the group. Weber, in The Protestant Ethic and the Spirit of Capitalism (1904-1905) and then in his studies of the religions of China, India and ancient Judaism, asks conversely what religious ideas do to the economy and to the conduct of life: Calvinist asceticism shaped the spirit of capitalism, and the disenchantment of the world is the work of the prophetic religions themselves. He constructs types, priest, prophet, magician, Church and sect, and a comparative sociology of the paths of salvation. Ernst Troeltsch extends the typology of Church, sect and mysticism; Marcel Mauss, Durkheim's nephew, studies sacrifice, prayer and the gift.

Secularization theory and its crisis

After 1945, the discipline develops mainly around one thesis: modernization, the differentiation of spheres, rationalization, urbanization, pluralism, weakens religion. Bryan Wilson in England, Thomas Luckmann in Germany and Peter Berger in the United States, in the line of Weber, give it its classic formulations: religion loses its social functions, becomes privatized, and pluralism undermines the plausibility of beliefs. As early as the 1980s, this theory is contested. In the United States, Rodney Stark and his colleagues propose a model of the religious market: religious vitality depends on supply, and competition between Churches, not monopoly, stimulates it; this is rational choice applied to religion. Berger himself recants in 1999 in the face of a world that has remained religious. José Casanova distinguishes three senses of secularization, of which only the first, differentiation, seems to him to hold; Grace Davie describes a Europe that believes without belonging, and then belongs without believing; Norris and Inglehart defend a corrected version of the thesis, based on existential security. In France, Hervieu-Léger analyses religious modernity through memory and lineage, and Jean-Paul Willaime the recompositions of Protestantism.

Methods and objects

The sociology of religions works through statistical surveys of practice and belief, monographs on communities, and comparative history. Its objects have widened: new religious movements, spiritualities without institutions, religion and politics, Islam in Europe, global Pentecostalism, civil religion in the sense of Robert Bellah, who takes up Rousseau's term for the United States.

What the essay assumes

The essay draws on Durkheim and Weber as the two founders of one and the same regime of critique, social function, and sets them in dialogue with James and Bergson. It assumes familiarity with secularization theory, of which the eleventh part examines the philosophers rather than the sociologists, with Stark's critique, which Norris and Inglehart refute, and with Berger's reversal. It treats secularization as a measurable fact in Norris and Inglehart, and the exit from religion as a philosophical question in its eleventh part.

In the essay: Durkheim · Weber · Berger and Luckmann · Hervieu-Léger · Part Eight

Further reading: Max Weber, Gesammelte Aufsätze zur Religionssoziologie (1920-1921) ; Peter L. Berger, The Sacred Canopy (1967) ; Bryan Wilson, Religion in Secular Society (1966) ; Danièle Hervieu-Léger and Jean-Paul Willaime, Sociologies et religion (2001) ; Rodney Stark and Roger Finke, Acts of Faith (2000)

The psychology and psychoanalysis of religion: from James to experimental psychologyDisciplines and methods

The psychology of religion asks what happens in the individual who believes, prays, converts or loses faith. It emerges in the United States at the end of the nineteenth century, takes with Freud and Jung the form of an interpretation of the depths, and becomes in the twentieth century an experimental science. Four of the essay's authors fall within it.

American religious psychology

The first studies are American and empirical: Edwin Starbuck surveys the conversion of adolescents by questionnaire (1899), James Leuba the belief of scientists (1916). William James, in the Gifford Lectures of 1901-1902 published under the title The Varieties of Religious Experience, founds the discipline by choosing to study personal religion, the experience of individuals in their solitude, on the basis of autobiographical documents: conversions, mystical states, healthy-minded and sick souls. His pragmatism leads him to judge these experiences by their fruits, not by their causes, and to reject the medical materialism that reduces a vision to a pathology. This tradition continues with Gordon Allport, who contrasts as early as 1950 a mature and an immature religion, then, in the 1960s, intrinsic religiosity, lived for its own sake, and extrinsic religiosity, used for other ends, a distinction that has become standard in measurement scales.

Psychoanalysis

Freud takes up religion as early as 1907, comparing rites to obsessional ceremonials, then in Totem and Taboo (1913), which derives the sacred from the murder of the primal father, The Future of an Illusion (1927), which explains belief by infantile helplessness and the need for paternal protection, Civilization and Its Discontents (1930), which discusses Romain Rolland's oceanic feeling, and Moses and Monotheism (1939). In these works religion is an illusion, defined by the desire that produces it, and a collective neurosis from which humanity can be cured by education to reality. Jung, after his break with Freud in 1913, takes the opposite path: the psyche has a natural religious function, religious images express archetypes of the collective unconscious, and individuation, the relation of the ego to the Self, accomplishes psychologically what religions called salvation; he refuses to say anything about God outside the psyche. Other psychoanalysts extend the debate: Oskar Pfister, a pastor and friend of Freud, answers him in 1928 with The Illusion of a Future; Erik Erikson studies Luther and Gandhi; Donald Winnicott and Ana-Maria Rizzuto locate the representation of God in the transitional space of childhood; Antoine Vergote, at Louvain, builds a psychology of faith and unbelief.

Experimental psychology

From the 1960s onwards, the discipline adopts the methods of social and cognitive psychology: scales of religiosity, studies on prayer, religious coping in the face of illness, the relation between religion and health, meditation experiences. Ernest Becker, a cultural anthropologist, proposes in 1973 a synthesis of Kierkegaard, Rank and Freud in which culture and religion answer the terror of death; terror management theory draws from it, from 1986 onwards, an experimental programme whose results have been debated in the light of the replications of the 2010s and 2020s. The cognitive science of religion, born in the 1990s, forms a distinct branch.

What the essay assumes

The essay makes James the representative of the regime of experience and the ally of its thesis in the order of belief, Freud the regime of illusion, Jung a transfer of the sacred onto the religious function of the psyche, Becker an explanation by death. It takes for granted the method of the Varieties, the difference between Freud and Jung on the origin of religious images, and the existence of an experimental psychology that puts these theories to the test.

In the essay: William James · Freud · Jung · Becker · Part Nine

Further reading: William James, The Varieties of Religious Experience (1902) ; Sigmund Freud, Die Zukunft einer Illusion (1927) ; C. G. Jung, Psychology and Religion (1938) ; Antoine Vergote, Religion, foi, incroyance (1983) ; Ralph W. Hood, Peter C. Hill and Bernard Spilka, The Psychology of Religion (4th ed., 2009)

The anthropology of religion: from Tylor to GirardDisciplines and methods

The anthropology of religion studies the beliefs and rites of human societies, first those that Europe called primitive, then all of them. It supplied the critique of religion with its theories of origin and sociology with its materials, and several authors of Part Nine of the essay belong to it.

Victorian evolutionism

The discipline was born in Darwin's England. Edward Tylor, in Primitive Culture (1871), defines religion at its minimum as belief in spiritual beings and derives all its forms from animism, the philosophy of the first humans, who explained dreams and death by a separable soul; religions evolve from animism to polytheism and then to monotheism, and leave survivals in modern societies. James Frazer, in The Golden Bough (1890-1915), arranges an immense compilation according to a sequence of magic, religion, science, and studies sacred kings put to death, gods who die and rise again, scapegoats. Robertson Smith, as early as 1889, puts sacrifice and the communion meal at the centre, which Durkheim and Freud would take up. This armchair anthropology works from the accounts of missionaries and travellers, and judges the beliefs it classifies.

Fieldwork and function

The twentieth century breaks with evolutionism through fieldwork. Bronisław Malinowski, stranded in the Trobriand Islands during the First World War, founds participant observation and describes magic, religion and science as three domains coexisting within one society: magic steps in where technique no longer suffices, religion answers death, myth is the charter of institutions. Radcliffe-Brown and British functionalism, Evans-Pritchard among the Azande and the Nuer, show the internal coherence of belief systems and refuse to treat them as errors. Durkheim, who did no fieldwork but read the ethnographers of Australia, draws from totemism a general theory of religion as a social fact. In France, Mauss, Lévy-Bruhl, who attributed a prelogical mentality to primitive peoples before retracting it, then Lévi-Strauss, who analyses myths as structures of thought, form another lineage.

Symbols, violence, ontologies

After 1960, the interpretive anthropology of Clifford Geertz defines religion as a system of symbols that establishes dispositions and conceptions of a general order of existence; Victor Turner studies ritual and liminality, Mary Douglas purity and pollution. René Girard, a literary critic who came to anthropology, proposes in 1972 a theory of the origin of the sacred in the lynching of a scapegoat victim, which professional anthropologists have received with reservation. More recently, Philippe Descola and the anthropology of ontologies take up Tylor's animism and make of it not an error but one of four ways of distributing interiority and physicality among beings. Talal Asad has shown that the very notion of religion, as an inner belief separate from the political, is a construction of the modern West.

What the essay assumes

The essay places Tylor and Frazer in the regime of error in its evolutionist form, retains from Malinowski that belief recedes with uncertainty and not with knowledge, reads Durkheim through totemism, and keeps the anthropological layer of Girard while leaving aside the apologetics. It takes as known the break between armchair anthropology and field anthropology, and the refutation of evolutionism by Durkheim and Wittgenstein.

In the essay: Tylor and Frazer · Malinowski · Durkheim · Girard · Part Nine

Further reading: Edward B. Tylor, Primitive Culture (1871) ; Bronisław Malinowski, Magic, Science and Religion (1925) ; E. E. Evans-Pritchard, Theories of Primitive Religion (1965) ; Clifford Geertz, The Interpretation of Cultures (1973) ; Philippe Descola, Par-delà nature et culture (2005)

The cognitive science of religion: why gods are easy to thinkDisciplines and methods

The cognitive science of religion is a research programme born in the 1990s, which explains the recurrence of religious beliefs by the ordinary workings of the human mind as studied by cognitive psychology, anthropology and evolutionary psychology. Boyer, whom the essay reads in its ninth part, is one of its founders.

The programme

Its guiding idea is that religion needs no special explanation, whether by a need, a function or a faculty of its own: it is a by-product of mental systems that evolved for other tasks. The human mind is made up of specialized inference systems, intuitive and fast, that handle physical objects, living beings, persons and social relations. Religious concepts exploit these systems: they respect almost all of their expectations and violate one or two, which makes them both memorable and usable. Stewart Guthrie, in Faces in the Clouds (1993), starts from anthropomorphism: we see faces in the clouds and intentions in the wind, because it costs less to take a rock for a bear than the reverse; gods are the product of this systematic bias, which Hume had described in 1757. Justin Barrett names the corresponding faculty the hypersensitive agency detection device. Pascal Boyer, in Religion Explained (2001), and Scott Atran, in In Gods We Trust (2002), develop the theory of minimally counterintuitive concepts and of supernatural agents endowed with strategic information about what matters to human beings, which makes them relevant to morality and cooperation. Robert McCauley and Thomas Lawson analyse rituals as actions whose form is constrained by cognition.

Internal debates

Two positions clash over function. For the by-product theory, religion was selected for nothing; for adaptationist theories, David Sloan Wilson or Ara Norenzayan, the great moralizing and watchful gods favoured cooperation in large societies, and religious groups thus survived better. Developmental psychology, with Barrett and Deborah Kelemen, studies children's tendency towards teleology and the attribution of intentions. Daniel Dennett, in Breaking the Spell (2006), popularizes the programme and draws from it a plea for the naturalistic study of religion, taking care to distinguish the explanation of causes from the question of truth, the genetic fallacy. The discipline has been criticized for its universalism, for the thinness of its data outside the West and for the replication crisis that has struck experimental psychology.

What the essay assumes

The essay retains from Boyer that gods are easy to think, that science is as unnatural as religion is natural, and that there is in man no empty place of the exact shape of God; it concludes that Boyer adds no regime to critique but explains the religious disposition. It recalls, after Dennett, the risk of the genetic fallacy, and reproaches him for explaining beliefs, not exercises: a spirituality without belief escapes his model. It assumes familiarity with the vocabulary of inference systems and the by-product, Hume's place as precursor, and Dennett's membership of both camps, the cognitive sciences and the new atheism.

In the essay: Boyer · Why do people believe? A constructed debate · Part Nine · Tylor and Frazer

Further reading: Stewart Guthrie, Faces in the Clouds (1993) ; Pascal Boyer, Religion Explained (2001) ; Scott Atran, In Gods We Trust (2002) ; Justin L. Barrett, Why Would Anyone Believe in God? (2004) ; Daniel Dennett, Breaking the Spell (2006) ; Ara Norenzayan, Big Gods (2013)

Historical-critical exegesis and the quests for the Jesus of historyDisciplines and methods

Historical-critical exegesis reads the Bible as a body of ancient documents, with the methods of history and philology, without presupposing its inspiration. Applied to the Gospels, it has produced over two centuries several quests for the Jesus of history, whose results the essay summarizes in its section on the existence of Jesus.

The method

Historical criticism took shape in the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, with Spinoza, Richard Simon and the German scholars, then settled in the nineteenth into a set of procedures: textual criticism, which establishes the text from the manuscripts; source criticism, which identifies the documents an author used, and which established the priority of Mark and the probable existence of a sayings source, called Q, behind Matthew and Luke; form criticism, Formgeschichte, which, with Bultmann and Dibelius around 1920, classifies the units of the oral tradition according to their genre and their function in the communities; redaction criticism, which studies the particular theology of each evangelist; and finally the literary, sociological and narrative approaches of recent decades. The quests for the historical Jesus add criteria of authenticity: multiple attestation; embarrassment, for what the first Christians had no interest in inventing, such as the baptism by John or the crucifixion; dissimilarity; coherence; plausibility within first-century Judaism.

The three quests

The first quest begins with Reimarus, whose fragments Lessing published from 1774: Jesus was, on this account, a political messianic claimant, and his disciples invented the resurrection. Strauss, in 1835, puts myth in the place of imposture; Renan, in 1863, writes a historical novel; the liberal lives of the end of the century make Jesus a teacher of morality. Schweitzer, in 1906, closes this period by showing that each author painted his own face, and that the real Jesus is an apocalyptic prophet awaiting the imminent end of the world. A period of scepticism follows: Bultmann judges that almost nothing can be known of the life of Jesus and that only the preaching, the kerygma, matters. The second quest opens in 1953 with Ernst Käsemann, his pupil, who argues that a continuity between the Jesus of history and the Christ of faith is both necessary and possible. The third quest, from the 1980s, places Jesus back in the Judaism of his time and draws on archaeology and the Qumran texts: E. P. Sanders makes him a prophet of the restoration of Israel, John P. Meier a marginal Jew whose life he reconstructs in five volumes, Geza Vermes a Galilean hasid, the American Jesus Seminar a subversive sage, a minority reading that Sanders and Meier contest.

The results and the mythicist thesis

On the existence of Jesus, agreement is almost unanimous among believing, agnostic and atheist historians: a Jew from Galilee, baptized by John, a preacher of the reign of God, crucified under Pontius Pilate around 30, attested by Paul as early as the 50s, by the Gospels, by Josephus and by Tacitus. The mythicist thesis, sketched by Bruno Bauer, then put forward by Arthur Drews in 1909 and Paul-Louis Couchoud in 1924, and taken up by Richard Carrier in 2014, remains marginal; Bart Ehrman, an agnostic, refuted it in 2012. The disagreement concerns the portrait: apocalyptic or sapiential, and what can be said about the sayings and the miracles.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes for granted the vocabulary of the method, the criteria of authenticity, which it applies to the baptism and the crucifixion, the sequence of the three quests, from Reimarus to Schweitzer and then to Sanders and Meier, and the state of the debate on mythicism, which it places in the regime of error and, in the case of Drews, among the refoundations.

In the essay: Did Jesus exist? The Jesus of history and the mythicist thesis · David Friedrich Strauss and Bruno Bauer · Ernest Renan · Paul of Tarsus

Further reading: Albert Schweitzer, Von Reimarus zu Wrede (1906), 2nd ed. under the title Geschichte der Leben-Jesu-Forschung (1913) ; Rudolf Bultmann, Die Geschichte der synoptischen Tradition (1921) ; E. P. Sanders, Jesus and Judaism (1985) ; John P. Meier, A Marginal Jew (1991-2016) ; Bart D. Ehrman, Did Jesus Exist? (2012) ; Daniel Marguerat (ed.), Introduction au Nouveau Testament (2000)

Analytic philosophy of religion: from the problem of evil to Reformed epistemologyDisciplines and methods

Analytic philosophy of religion applies to religious beliefs the tools of logic, the theory of knowledge and the analysis of language, in the English-speaking tradition stemming from Frege, Russell and Wittgenstein. It provides the essay with the second half of its section on the right to believe.

From logical positivism to the revival

In the first half of the twentieth century the tradition is hostile or indifferent to religion. Russell, in Why I Am Not a Christian (1927), refutes the classical proofs and explains religion by fear; the image of the teapot in orbit, in a text of 1952, illustrates that the burden of proof lies on whoever makes the assertion. The logical positivism of the Vienna Circle and of Ayer holds theological statements to be meaningless, since they cannot be verified; the 1955 debate on falsification, between Flew, Hare and Mitchell, asks what could refute the assertion that God loves humankind. Wittgenstein, in his lectures on religious belief, and his heirs, Rush Rhees and then D. Z. Phillips, suggest that belief is a language game and a form of life, not a hypothesis, a so-called fideist reading, which its opponents hold against it. From the 1960s onwards the discipline is reborn with believing authors who defend the rationality of theism with full analytic rigour: Alvin Plantinga, Richard Swinburne, William Alston, Nicholas Wolterstorff.

The problem of evil

The problem of evil is the most closely followed debate. J. L. Mackie, in 1955, argues that the existence of an omnipotent and perfectly good God is logically incompatible with the existence of evil. Plantinga replies in 1974 with the free will defence: it is possible that a world containing free creatures and evil is better than a world without freedom, which suffices to remove the logical contradiction.
