The debate then shifts to the probabilistic or evidential problem, raised by William Rowe in 1979: do the quantity and gratuitousness of suffering make the existence of God improbable? Theists respond with theodicies, John Hick's on soul-making, or with sceptical theism, which denies that we can judge God's reasons. Mackie, in The Miracle of Theism (1982), examines all the arguments and concludes in favour of atheism; Swinburne, through Bayesian reasoning, concludes that theism is probable.

Evidentialism and Reformed epistemology

The second debate concerns the right to believe. Evidentialism, to which Clifford gave its strictest formulation in 1877, requires proportionate evidence for every belief. Plantinga's Reformed epistemology, from 1983 onwards, maintains that belief in God can be properly basic, rational without being inferred, in the manner of belief in other minds or in the past, and that it is warranted if it is produced by a reliable faculty, the sense of the divine of which Calvin spoke. The Great Pumpkin objection and the question of religious disagreement follow from it. Other fields, the divine attributes, miracles, W. L. Craig's kalam cosmological argument, the fine-tuning of the constants of the universe, religious experience as perception in Alston, make up an active discipline, above all in the United States.

What the essay assumes

The essay draws on Russell and the teapot, Mackie and Plantinga on evil, and Reformed epistemology to conclude that this school saves the coherence and rationality of faith at the price of its universality, and that there still remains Ivan Karamazov. It assumes familiarity with evidentialism as a position, the difference between the logical and the evidential problem of evil, and the fact that these authors reason about propositions, which the section on Foucault connects with the Cartesian moment.

In the essay: Do we have the right to believe? Clifford, James and analytic philosophy · Leibniz · Bayle · Wittgenstein · Part Nine

Further reading: Bertrand Russell, Why I Am Not a Christian (1927) ; Antony Flew, R. M. Hare and Basil Mitchell, Theology and Falsification (1955) ; J. L. Mackie, The Miracle of Theism (1982) ; Alvin Plantinga, Warranted Christian Belief (2000) ; Richard Swinburne, The Existence of God (1979) ; Roger Pouivet, Épistémologie des croyances religieuses (2013)

Phenomenology: from Husserl to the theological turnDisciplines and methods

Phenomenology is the philosophical movement founded by Edmund Husserl at the beginning of the twentieth century, which seeks to describe phenomena as they are given to consciousness, prior to any theory. Heidegger, Levinas and Marion, three authors of Part Ten of the essay, come from it, and the debate on what phenomenology can say about God runs through their works.

Husserl

Husserl (1859-1938), a mathematician by training, publishes the Logical Investigations in 1900-1901, against the psychologism that reduces logic to laws of the mind, then in 1913 the Ideas, which fix the method. His watchword is the return to the things themselves. Consciousness is intentionality: it is always consciousness of something, and the phenomenon is neither a thing in itself nor a subjective impression, but the thing as it appears. The phenomenological reduction brackets, epoché, the thesis of the existence of the world in order to describe the way in which the world is constituted for consciousness; the eidetic reduction brings out essences through imaginative variation. In his last works, the Krisis (1936), Husserl describes the life-world, Lebenswelt, which the sciences cover over and forget. He leaves few texts on God, whom he regards as a limit idea, but his method was taken up by believing philosophers, Max Scheler, who describes the religious act, Edith Stein, and Rudolf Otto, a phenomenologist in all but name, whose analysis of the numinous proceeds by describing states of consciousness.

Heidegger and existence

Heidegger, Husserl's assistant, transforms phenomenology in Being and Time (1927): the phenomenon to be described is no longer consciousness but Dasein, the human existent, in its being-in-the-world, its care, its being-towards-death, and the guiding question becomes that of the meaning of being. Phenomenology becomes a hermeneutics of existence, then, after the turn of the 1930s, a thinking of being and of its history, of which metaphysics is the forgetting. Heidegger separates philosophy from faith, and his critique of onto-theology maintains that the God of metaphysics is not a God before whom one can pray. Sartre and Merleau-Ponty introduce the movement into France, the former in an ontology of freedom, the latter in a philosophy of perception and the body.

The theological turn

From the 1960s onwards, part of French phenomenology turns towards what exceeds the phenomenon: Levinas describes the face of the other as what overflows all intentionality and where the trace of God is found; Michel Henry seeks in the self-affection of life a phenomenology of Christianity; Jean-Luc Marion develops a phenomenology of givenness, in which the saturated phenomenon, the event, the idol, the flesh, the icon, exceeds the concepts that would contain it, and in which revelation is thought as a possibility. Jean-Louis Chrétien and Jean-Yves Lacoste continue along this path. Dominique Janicaud denounces this theological turn in 1991 as an abandonment of Husserl's methodical neutrality; those concerned reply that description does not presuppose faith. Paul Ricoeur, for his part, grafts hermeneutics onto phenomenology.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the meaning of the phenomenological method, the line running from Husserl to Heidegger, Levinas and Marion, the Heideggerian critique of onto-theology as the background of Part Ten, and the debate on the theological turn, which gives its context to Marion's idol and icon and to Levinas's face. It does not settle the quarrel over method; it asks only whether these philosophies install an absolute that demands.

In the essay: Heidegger · Levinas · Marion · Rudolf Otto · Part Ten

Further reading: Edmund Husserl, Ideen zu einer reinen Phänomenologie (1913) ; Martin Heidegger, Sein und Zeit (1927) ; Emmanuel Levinas, Totalité et infini (1961) ; Jean-Luc Marion, Étant donné (1997) ; Dominique Janicaud, Le Tournant théologique de la phénoménologie française (1991) ; Jean-François Lavigne, Husserl et la naissance de la phénoménologie (2005)

Hermeneutics: from Schleiermacher to RicoeurDisciplines and methods

Hermeneutics is the art, and then the theory, of the interpretation of texts and, by extension, of every human expression. Born of biblical exegesis and philology, it becomes in the nineteenth century a method of the human sciences, and in the twentieth a philosophy. Ricoeur, from whom the essay takes the name school of suspicion and the idea of second naivety, is its heir.

From rules of interpretation to method

Antiquity and the Middle Ages already practised a hermeneutics without the word: the allegory of Philo and Origen, the four senses of Scripture, the taʾwīl of Averroes. The word appears in the seventeenth century to designate the rules for interpreting sacred, legal and classical texts. Schleiermacher, at the beginning of the nineteenth century, makes it a general discipline: to understand is to reconstruct the meaning of a discourse from the language, grammatical interpretation, and from the individuality of the author, psychological interpretation, and the aim is to understand an author as well as, and even better than, he understood himself. He formulates the hermeneutic circle: one understands the whole only from the parts and the parts only from the whole. Wilhelm Dilthey, at the end of the century, makes understanding, Verstehen, the proper method of the human sciences, as opposed to the explanation of the natural sciences: nature is explained, the life of the mind is understood in its expressions.

Philosophical hermeneutics

Heidegger shifts the question: understanding is not a method but a mode of being of Dasein, which always already finds itself within a pre-understanding, and the circle is not a defect but the very structure of existence. Hans-Georg Gadamer, in Truth and Method (1960), draws from this a philosophical hermeneutics: all understanding is caught up in a tradition and carried by prejudices, in the sense of prior judgements, which the Enlightenment was wrong to discredit wholesale; to understand a text is to bring about a fusion of horizons between its world and ours, and the history of a work's effects is part of its meaning. Habermas objects, in a debate begun in 1967, that tradition can be ideology and that a critique going beyond it is needed; Gadamer replies that critique itself speaks from within a tradition. Emilio Betti defends against Gadamer a methodical and objective hermeneutics.

Ricoeur and the conflict of interpretations

Paul Ricoeur grafts hermeneutics onto phenomenology and routes it through the human sciences. The Symbolism of Evil (1960) starts from symbols and myths, which give rise to thought; De l'interprétation (1965) confronts Freud's psychoanalysis, a hermeneutics of suspicion that demystifies, with the hermeneutics of the restoration of meaning, that of Eliade and of the phenomenology of religion, and names Marx, Nietzsche and Freud the school of suspicion. The Conflict of Interpretations (1969) holds that rival readings do not cancel each other out. Ricoeur then develops a theory of the text, of metaphor and of narrative, and a biblical hermeneutics that distinguishes the world of the text from the author's intention. Second naivety is the name of the faith that has passed through critique without renouncing it.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the line of Schleiermacher, Dilthey, Heidegger, Gadamer and Ricoeur, with the sense of understanding as opposed to explanation, with the idea that interpretation has no end, through which the essay has Ricoeur answer the objection it raises against him on the basis of Hegel and Strauss, and with the fact that Habermas, in Faith and Knowledge, proposes a translation of religious contents, a gesture that belongs to hermeneutics. It judges that Ricoeur is right about the method and wrong about the object, the three regimes of suspicion remaining distinct.

In the essay: Ricoeur · Friedrich Schleiermacher · Jürgen Habermas · Philo of Alexandria · Part Eight

Further reading: Friedrich Schleiermacher, Hermeneutik und Kritik (posth. ed., 1838) ; Wilhelm Dilthey, Die Entstehung der Hermeneutik (1900) ; Hans-Georg Gadamer, Wahrheit und Methode (1960) ; Paul Ricoeur, Le Conflit des interprétations (1969) ; Jean Grondin, L'Universalité de l'herméneutique (1993)

The vocabulary of Christian theology: revelation, grace, salvation, original sinDisciplines and methods

The essay uses, without defining them, the terms of Christian theology that its authors criticize, defend or turn around. This vocabulary became fixed between Augustine and the Reformation, and each word in it carries a debate. The main ones are presented here, in their most common sense among theologians.

Revelation, faith, reason

Revelation is the act by which God makes himself known to human beings, through the history of Israel, through Christ and through the Scripture that bears witness to it; it is opposed to what reason can reach by itself. Hence the distinction, fixed by scholasticism, between natural theology, the knowledge of God through reason starting from the world, and revealed theology, which receives its principles from Scripture and tradition. Faith is assent to revelation; Augustine and Anselm make it precede understanding, Thomas Aquinas distinguishes it from knowledge while holding the two to be compatible, Luther defines it as trust, Barth rejects all natural theology. A dogma is a truth of faith defined by the Church, whose denial is heresy; a mystery, a revealed truth that reason can neither demonstrate nor fully understand, such as the Trinity or the Incarnation. The supernatural designates what exceeds the powers and the demands of created nature, grace and the vision of God; the dispute over its relation to nature, revived by Henri de Lubac in 1946, runs through Catholic theology in the twentieth century.

Sin, grace, salvation

Original sin is the doctrine, formulated by Augustine against Pelagius at the beginning of the fifth century on the basis of Paul (Rm 5, 12), according to which Adam's fault corrupted human nature and is passed on to all his descendants, who cannot save themselves by their own powers. Grace is the free gift by which God saves; its relation to human freedom divides Augustine and Pelagius, Luther and Erasmus, the Jansenists and the Jesuits, Pascal writing the Provincial Letters in this last dispute. Predestination is the doctrine according to which God has chosen from all eternity those who will be saved; Augustine teaches it, Calvin pushes it as far as double predestination, to salvation and to damnation, and Weber makes it the psychological mainspring of Puritan asceticism. Salvation is deliverance from sin and death, through the redemption accomplished in the death and resurrection of Christ; justification, at the centre of the Reformation, is the act by which God declares or makes the sinner just, by faith alone according to Luther, by faith quickened by charity and works according to the Council of Trent. Eschatology deals with the last things, judgement, resurrection, the kingdom of God, and apocalyptic with their imminence.

God, Christ, Church

The Trinity, fixed at the councils of the fourth century, confesses one God in three persons; the Incarnation, the Word made flesh, and Chalcedon in 451 defines Christ as one person in two natures. Negative or apophatic theology says what God is not; affirmative, or cataphatic, theology proceeds by analogy. The Church is the people of the baptized and the institution that governs it; the sacraments, seven for Catholics, two for Protestants, are the efficacious signs of grace. Theodicy, a word of Leibniz's, is the justification of God in the face of evil; apologetics, the rational defence of the faith; kenosis, the self-abasement of God in the Incarnation, on which Bonhoeffer and Weil meditate under the name of the weakness of God.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes this vocabulary is known when it opposes the God of revelation to the God of reason, when it reads Barth's critique of religion as a theology of revelation alone, when it reads in Pascal the hidden God and the wager, or when it distinguishes, in Bonhoeffer and Weil, a faith without religion. It itself recalls that religion in the strong sense involves dogmas, a community and an external absolute, features that this vocabulary helps to recognize.

In the essay: Part Two · Porphyry and Augustine · Pascal · Part Ten

Further reading: Augustine, De civitate Dei (413-426) ; Thomas Aquinas, Summa theologiae (1266-1273) ; Jean Calvin, Institution de la religion chrétienne (1536-1560) ; Henri de Lubac, Surnaturel (1946) ; Bernard Sesboüé (ed.), Histoire des dogmes (1994-1996) ; Jean-Yves Lacoste (ed.), Dictionnaire critique de théologie (1998)

Metaphysics and the proofs of the existence of God: from Anselm to KantDisciplines and methods

Since Aristotle, metaphysics has been the science of being as being and of the supreme being; the proofs of the existence of God are its most debated component, from scholasticism to Kant. The essay meets them in Descartes, sees them dismantled by Kant, and reads in their very structure, with Heidegger, what he calls onto-theology.

The vocabulary of metaphysics

The title Metaphysics designates the books of Aristotle placed after the Physics; there the discipline is called first philosophy, or theological philosophy. Its concepts form the vocabulary of the whole tradition: being and beings, that which is; substance, what exists in itself, and accidents, what exists in something else; essence, what a thing is, and existence, the fact that it is; act and potency; the four causes; the necessary and the contingent. Scholasticism divides metaphysics into ontology, the science of being in general, and special metaphysics, which treats the soul, the world and God; Christian Wolff fixes this division in the eighteenth century, and it is this division that Kant criticizes. Causa sui, cause of itself, is the name that Spinoza, after Descartes's Replies to Objections, gives to the being that exists by the necessity of its own essence.

The three proofs

The tradition produced several arguments, which Kant reduces to three. The ontological argument, formulated by Anselm in the Proslogion: God is that than which nothing greater can be thought; but what exists in reality is greater than what exists in the understanding alone; therefore God exists. The monk Gaunilo objects to it with the perfect island, Thomas Aquinas rejects it, Descartes takes it up again in the fifth Meditation, existence belonging to the essence of the perfect being as the sum of its angles belongs to the triangle, and Leibniz demands that the possibility of God be proved first. The cosmological proof starts from the world: every motion, every cause, every contingent being points back to a first mover, a first cause, a necessary being, failing which one would regress to infinity; these are the first three of Aquinas's five ways, inherited from Aristotle and Avicenna, and the principle of sufficient reason gives its Leibnizian version. The physico-theological proof, or argument from design, infers an intelligent author from the order and purposiveness of the world; it is the most popular in the eighteenth century, the one Hume discusses in the Dialogues and that Darwin will shake.

Kant's critique and its aftermath

Kant, in the Transcendental Dialectic of the Critique of Pure Reason (1781), argues that existence is not a real predicate, a hundred real thalers containing no more than a hundred possible thalers, which ruins the ontological argument; that the cosmological proof presupposes it in passing from the necessary being to the perfect being; and that the physico-theological proof proves at best an architect of the world, not its creator. God becomes a regulative idea of reason, then a postulate of practical reason. After Kant, Hegel rehabilitates the proofs as elevations of the spirit, neo-Thomism defends them, Heidegger sees in their structure, the supreme being grounding beings in general, the essence of metaphysics as onto-theology, and analytic philosophy takes them up again in modal and probabilistic forms, in Plantinga and Swinburne.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the vocabulary of being and beings, the difference between the three proofs, the place of Descartes, who takes from theology the task of proving God and entrusts it to philosophy, Kant's critique, which dismantles that component, and Heidegger's reading, which makes the causa sui a god before whom one can neither pray nor dance. It holds the fusion of the God of the proofs and the God of the Bible to be the major fact that Pascal and Heidegger each undo in their own way.

In the essay: Descartes · Kant · Heidegger · Aristotle · Part Three

Further reading: Anselme de Cantorbéry, Proslogion (1077-1078) ; Thomas d'Aquin, Summa theologiae (1266-1273) ; René Descartes, Meditationes de prima philosophia (1641) ; Emmanuel Kant, Kritik der reinen Vernunft (1781) ; Étienne Gilson, L'Être et l'essence (1948) ; Jean-Luc Marion, Sur le prisme métaphysique de Descartes (1986)

The philosophy of history: from Augustine to LöwithDisciplines and methods

The philosophy of history asks whether human history has a meaning, a direction and an end. The expression is Voltaire's, but the question comes from Christian theology, and it is this link that Löwith, Voegelin and Blumenberg discuss in Part Eleven of the essay.

The history of salvation

The Greeks thought of time as a cycle and of history as repetition; the Bible introduces an oriented time, from creation to judgement, in which God acts through unique events. Augustine, in The City of God (413-426), writing against the pagans who blamed Christianity for the sack of Rome, produces the first theology of history: two cities, that of God and that of men, intermingled until the end of time, and a history divided into six ages, on the model of the six days; the meaning of history is invisible and reserved to God. Joachim of Fiore, a Calabrian abbot who died in 1202, breaks with this reserve by announcing a third age, that of the Spirit, after those of the Father and the Son, which is to be realized within history itself; his scheme will haunt millenarian movements and, according to Löwith and Voegelin, the modern philosophies of progress. Bossuet, in the Discourse on Universal History (1681), gives the last great Christian version: Providence guides empires in order to prepare and preserve the Church.

Progress and reason in history

Vico, in the New Science (1725), proposes a history of nations in three ages, of gods, of heroes and of men, governed by an immanent providence, and founds the idea that man can know history because he makes it. The Enlightenment replaces Providence with progress: Turgot, and Condorcet in the Sketch for a Historical Picture of the Progress of the Human Mind (1795), written under the Terror, describe a march of humanity towards enlightenment and happiness. Kant looks for a guiding thread in a purpose of nature; Herder sees in each people a singular expression of humanity. Hegel, in his lectures on the philosophy of history, makes universal history the progress in the consciousness of freedom, in which Spirit realizes itself through the cunning of reason, which uses the passions of individuals: the end of history is the modern State in which freedom is recognized. Comte gives it the form of the law of the three stages, Marx that of historical materialism, in which class struggle leads to the classless society; both, according to Löwith, transpose eschatology.

The critiques of the twentieth century

After the catastrophes of the century, the philosophy of history is put on trial. Löwith, in Meaning in History (1949), shows, by going back from Burckhardt and Marx to Augustine and the Bible, that the modern idea of progress is a secularized eschatology, and concludes with a withdrawal towards the ancient wisdom of the cosmos. Voegelin sees in it a gnosis. Aron, as early as 1938, holds that history has no single meaning and that the choice of one always remains open; Popper denounces, in a book of 1957 drawn from articles of 1944-1945, the poverty of historicism, the claim to predict the course of history; Camus speaks of the divinization of history. Blumenberg replies to Löwith that progress is not a disguised eschatology but a new answer to an old question. Francis Fukuyama revives in 1989 the Hegelian theme of the end of history.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the passage from cyclical to oriented time, Augustine's theology of history and Joachim of Fiore's scheme, the Hegelian doctrine of reason in history, and the debate between Löwith and Blumenberg on secularization, which it settles, with Blumenberg against Löwith, in favour of function rather than filiation: the History of the revolutionaries is a strict refoundation because it demands sacrifice, not because it descends from eschatology.

In the essay: Karl Löwith · Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel · Karl Marx and Friedrich Engels · Voegelin and Aron · Part Eleven

Further reading: Augustin, De civitate Dei (413-426) ; Jacques-Bénigne Bossuet, Discours sur l'histoire universelle (1681) ; Giambattista Vico, Scienza nuova (1725-1744) ; G. W. F. Hegel, Vorlesungen über die Philosophie der Geschichte (posthumous ed., 1837) ; Karl Löwith, Meaning in History (1949) ; Raymond Aron, Introduction à la philosophie de l'histoire (1938)

Currents and doctrines

Platonism and Neoplatonism: from the Academy to the RenaissanceCurrents and doctrines

Platonism is the longest tradition in Western philosophy: from the Academy founded by Plato around 387 BCE to the Florentine Renaissance, it supplied Hellenized Judaism, Christianity and Islam with the framework in which to think a transcendent God, an immortal soul and an intelligible world. The essay encounters it at every stage of its first and second parts.

The Academy and Middle Platonism

The Academy, the school Plato founded in a gymnasium in Athens, passes after him to Speusippus and Xenocrates, who systematize the doctrine of Forms and numbers, then, in the 3rd and 2nd centuries BCE, with Arcesilaus and later Carneades, to scepticism: the New Academy suspends judgement and combats Stoic dogmatism, and it is this Platonism that Cicero knew and handed on. In the 1st century BCE, Antiochus of Ascalon returns to dogmatism, and what the moderns call Middle Platonism begins: Philo of Alexandria, Plutarch, Apuleius, Numenius, Celsus and Alcinous read Plato as a theologian, place the Forms in the intellect of a transcendent God, rank the divine into a first God, a demiurge and a world soul, and absorb Aristotelian and Stoic elements. It is this school Platonism that the Christian apologists and the Gnostics found confronting them, and that Celsus defends against them.

Neoplatonism

Plotinus, in the 3rd century, gives Platonism its most rigorous form: the three hypostases, the One, Intellect, Soul, procession and conversion, and union with the One. Porphyry edits and popularizes; Iamblichus, in Syria, and Proclus, in Athens in the 5th century, multiply the intermediaries, systematize, and integrate theurgy, a ritual practice of salvation, in a last effort to give paganism a philosophical foundation; the school of Athens is closed by Justinian in 529, while that of Alexandria survives by becoming Christian. The Neoplatonists read Aristotle as an introduction to Plato, and it is through their commentaries, relayed by Boethius, that Aristotelian logic passed through the Middle Ages.

Christian, Jewish and Muslim Platonism

Augustine, through the books of the Platonists translated by Marius Victorinus, finds in Plotinus the Word without the Incarnation, and inwardness; Pseudo-Dionysius, an anonymous author of the early 6th century who passes himself off as the disciple of Paul, Christianizes Proclus and hands down to the Middle Ages negative theology and the hierarchy of angels; Eriugena, the school of Chartres, Bonaventure, Eckhart and Nicholas of Cusa draw their life from it. In Islam, the Theology of Aristotle, extracts from Plotinus attributed to Aristotle, and the Book of Causes, drawn from Proclus, nourish al-Farabi and Avicenna; in the Jewish world, Ibn Gabirol and the Kabbalah. The Renaissance, with Gemistus Plethon, Ficino, who translates Plato and Plotinus in Florence, and Pico della Mirandola, dreams of a Platonic theology and a concord of wisdoms; the Cambridge Platonists in the seventeenth century, then Schelling, Hegel, Bergson, Simone Weil and Murdoch carry the lineage forward.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the distinction between Middle Platonism and Neoplatonism, Nietzsche's formula of Christianity as Platonism for the people, the role of the books of the Platonists in Augustine, the Christianization of Proclus by Pseudo-Dionysius, and the Platonic genealogy of negative theology and of the spirituality of exercise. It regards Plotinus as the consummate example of a spirituality that might not have become religion.

In the essay: Plato · Plotinus · Part One

Further reading: A. H. Armstrong (ed.), The Cambridge History of Later Greek and Early Medieval Philosophy (1967) ; Pierre Hadot, Qu'est-ce que la philosophie antique ? (1995) ; John Dillon, The Middle Platonists (1977) ; Luc Brisson, Lectures de Platon (2000) ; Werner Beierwaltes, Platonismus und Idealismus (1972) ; Endre von Ivánka, Plato christianus (1964)

The Hellenistic schools: Stoicism, Epicureanism, Scepticism, wisdom as a way of lifeCurrents and doctrines

After the death of Alexander in 323 BCE, in a Greek world enlarged to the dimensions of an empire, three new schools share Athens with the Academy and the Lyceum: the Porch, the Garden and Scepticism. All three offer a wisdom, an art of living happily in a world the citizen no longer governs, and it is of them that Hadot thinks first when he defines ancient philosophy as a way of life.

Stoicism

Founded around 300 by Zeno of Citium, who taught under the Painted Porch, the Stoa, Stoicism was developed by Cleanthes and Chrysippus, then adapted to Rome by Panaetius and Posidonius, and illustrated under the Empire by Seneca, Epictetus, a freed slave, and Marcus Aurelius, an emperor. The doctrine is divided into logic, physics and ethics. The world is a single living being, pervaded by a divine breath, the logos, reason and artistic fire, which orders everything according to providence and fate; the sage consents to it, and the universe is periodically consumed and reborn. Ethics teaches one to distinguish what depends on us, our judgements and our desires, from what does not, the body, reputation, fortune; virtue alone is a good, the passions are false judgements, and the sage, apathetic in the sense of free from passion, lives according to nature and reason, a citizen of the world. The Meditations of Marcus Aurelius and the Handbook of Epictetus are written exercises, and Stoic theology, pantheist, rational, tolerant of cults reinterpreted through allegory, nourished the Christian apologists and Spinoza.

Epicureanism

Founded in 306 in Epicurus's Garden, Epicureanism offers an atomist and materialist physics, indifferent gods living in the intermundia, a mortal soul, and a happiness defined as the absence of disturbance, obtained through knowledge of nature, the limitation of desires and friendship. Lucretius is its poet, and the school, slandered as libertine, lasted until the fourth century CE before being reborn with Gassendi.

Scepticism

Pyrrho of Elis, who wrote nothing, taught the suspension of judgement, epochè, as the source of tranquillity; the New Academy of Arcesilaus and Carneades practised it against the Stoics; Aenesidemus and then Sextus Empiricus, in the second century CE, systematized the modes of suspension, the tropes, in the Outlines of Pyrrhonism, whose rediscovery in the sixteenth century supplied Montaigne and the fideists with their arguments, and the moderns, Descartes, Bayle, Hume, with the problem of certainty. Ancient Scepticism is also a way of life, without dogma, following appearances and customs.

Legacies

The three schools supplied nascent Christianity with concepts, the logos, providence, moral conscience, conscientia, and with adversaries; the Renaissance and the classical age, Justus Lipsius, Montaigne, Charron, Gassendi, Spinoza, with the model of a wisdom without revelation; Hadot and Foucault, then Comte-Sponville and contemporary Stoics, with the idea of secular spiritual exercises.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the framework of the three schools, Stoic theology, Epicurean ataraxia, sceptical suspension and its rediscovery by Montaigne, and the reading of Hadot, for whom ancient philosophy is a choice of life. It finds there the origin of the spiritualities of exercise that its twelfth part examines, and recalls, as early as the transition of its first part, that these wisdoms presupposed an elite.

In the essay: Epicurus and Lucretius · Pierre Hadot

Further reading: A. A. Long and D. N. Sedley, Les Philosophes hellénistiques (1987) ; Pierre Hadot, Qu'est-ce que la philosophie antique ? (1995) ; Jean Brun, Le Stoïcisme (1958) ; Carlos Lévy, Les Philosophies hellénistiques (1997) ; Martha Nussbaum, The Therapy of Desire (1994) ; Richard Popkin, Histoire du scepticisme (1960, 2003)

Gnosis and Gnosticism: from Valentinus to Hans Jonas and VoegelinCurrents and doctrines

Gnosticism is the name given to a set of religious currents of the second and third centuries, most of them Christian, which taught salvation through gnosis, a revealed knowledge of the soul's divine origin and of its fall into an evil world. Fought by the Fathers as the first heresy and rediscovered in the twentieth century through the Nag Hammadi texts, the word became, in Jonas and Voegelin, and then in Blumenberg's reply, a category for thinking about modernity.

The ancient doctrines

The Gnostic systems, long known through the refutations of Irenaeus, Hippolytus and Epiphanius, vary in detail but share a structure: the supreme God, unknowable and good, is a stranger to the world; the world was created by an inferior demiurge, ignorant or wicked, often identified with the God of the Old Testament, following a fall within the divine pleroma, that of Wisdom, Sophia, among the Valentinians; man carries within him a divine spark imprisoned in matter, and salvation comes from a revealer, Christ, who teaches him where he comes from and where he is going. Valentinus, in Rome c. 140, and his school, Basilides in Alexandria, the Sethians and the Ophites are its representatives; Marcion, c. 144, who opposes the just God of the Old Testament to the good God of the Gospel and rejects the Jewish Scriptures, is related to them without being a Gnostic in the strict sense; the Manichaeism of Mani, in the third century, is its dualist heir, which Augustine followed for nine years. The discovery in 1945, at Nag Hammadi in Upper Egypt, of thirteen Coptic codices, among them the Gospel of Thomas, the Gospel of Truth and the Apocryphon of John, gave access to the texts themselves. Contempt for the world, a negative theology pushed to dualism, and the elitism of the pneumatics against the psychics and the hylics characterize these currents, which Plotinus also combats in the treatise Against the Gnostics, in the name of the beauty of the cosmos.

Gnosis as a category

Hans Jonas, a student of Heidegger and Bultmann, reads ancient gnosis in 1934 in the light of existentialism, as the revolt of a man thrown into an alien world, and later reverses the comparison: modern nihilism is a gnosis without God. Eric Voegelin makes gnosis the essence of modernity, from Joachim of Fiore to the totalitarianisms, the claim to a knowledge of the meaning of history and to salvation through action; Blumenberg replies that modernity is on the contrary the second victory over gnosis, Augustine having left the problem of evil within the creature. Buber accuses Jung of a psychological gnosis; Simone Weil, in her letters of 1941 to Déodat Roché, admired the Cathars, medieval heirs of dualism; the word also serves historians of esotericism. In 1996 Michael Williams proposed abandoning the category of Gnosticism as too heterogeneous.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the Gnostic schema, the demiurge, Marcion, Nag Hammadi, Plotinus's treatise, Voegelin's polemical use of the term, Blumenberg's reply and Buber's criticism of Jung. It sides with Kelsen and Blumenberg against the Gnostic genealogy of modernity, while keeping the function of secular religions.

In the essay: Voegelin and Aron · Jung

Further reading: Hans Jonas, Gnosis und spätantiker Geist (1934-1954) ; La Religion gnostique (1958) ; Henri-Charles Puech, En quête de la gnose (1978) ; Kurt Rudolph, Die Gnosis (1977) ; Jean-Pierre Mahé and Paul-Hubert Poirier (eds.), Écrits gnostiques (2007) ; Michael Allen Williams, Rethinking Gnosticism (1996)

Christian mysticism: from Dionysius to John of the Cross and quietismCurrents and doctrines

Christian mysticism designates the experience of union with God in this life and the writings that describe it, from late Antiquity to the seventeenth century. The essay meets in it the precursors of negative theology and of the spiritualities without God, Eckhart first, and the models to which James, Bergson, Bataille and Simone Weil refer.

The sources and the Middle Ages

The word comes from the Greek mustikos, hidden, and first designates the hidden sense of Scripture and the sacraments. Origen and Gregory of Nyssa describe the ascent of the soul towards God in the darkness, like Moses on Sinai; Augustine, the inner illumination and the ecstasy at Ostia; Pseudo-Dionysius, at the beginning of the sixth century, in the Mystical Theology, the negative theology that goes beyond every affirmation and every negation to reach God in unknowing, and he hands down to the Middle Ages the word hierarchy, which he coined, and the very title of mystical theology. Bernard of Clairvaux, in the twelfth century, sings the love of the soul for the Word in his sermons on the Song of Songs; the Victorines systematize contemplation; women, Hildegard of Bingen, Hadewijch, Mechthild of Magdeburg, Marguerite Porete, burned in 1310, write in the vernacular a mysticism of love and annihilation. Rhineland mysticism, Eckhart, Tauler, Suso, then Ruusbroec in Flanders, reflects on the birth of God in the soul and on detachment; the Devotio moderna and The Imitation of Christ give it its practical version, and Nicholas of Cusa, On Learned Ignorance.

The Spanish golden age and the French seventeenth century

In Spain, in the sixteenth century, Teresa of Ávila describes in her Life and The Interior Castle the degrees of prayer up to the spiritual marriage, and reforms the Carmelite order; John of the Cross, her companion, composes the poems of the Dark Night and the Spiritual Canticle and comments on them: the soul must pass through the night of the senses and the night of the spirit, a stripping away of every support, to be united with God in naked faith. Ignatius of Loyola gives the Spiritual Exercises their Jesuit form. The French seventeenth century, which Bremond called the mystical invasion, sees Francis de Sales, Bérulle, Pascal with the Memorial, Marie de l'Incarnation, Surin, then the quietist controversy: Madame Guyon and Fénelon defend pure love, passive abandonment to God without desire for reward, Bossuet fights them, and Rome condemns Fénelon in 1699. After this controversy mysticism is held in suspicion, and Michel de Certeau read in this century the moment when mysticism, turned into a noun, becomes a separate body of knowledge, detached from theology.

Modern rereadings

James classifies mystical states by four marks and makes them the ground of personal religion; Bergson places the great Christian mystics at the summit of dynamic religion; Schopenhauer, Bataille and Weil read them outside the Church; Otto compares Eckhart and Shankara; Hadot recalls what Christian exercises owe to the philosophers.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the negative theology of Dionysius, Eckhart and Rhineland mysticism, John of the Cross, quietism and the controversy over pure love, and the uses made of them by James, Bergson and Bataille. It sees in mysticism one of the anticipations of the regime of experience, in Eckhart the ancestor of the spiritualities without God, and in Weil's decreation a mysticism that refuses consolation.

In the essay: Meister Eckhart · Simone Weil · Bergson

Further reading: Pseudo-Denys l'Aréopagite, Œuvres complètes, trans. Maurice de Gandillac (1943) ; Jean de la Croix, Œuvres (1618) ; Michel de Certeau, La Fable mystique (1982) ; Bernard McGinn, The Presence of God. A History of Western Christian Mysticism (1991-) ; Henri Bremond, Histoire littéraire du sentiment religieux en France (1916-1933) ; Alain de Libera, La Mystique rhénane (1984)

Pietism and German Romanticism: the religion of the heart and of the infiniteCurrents and doctrines

Two German movements, a century apart, shifted religion from doctrine towards feeling, and so prepared both Schleiermacher's regime of experience and Feuerbach's critique: Pietism, a Protestant revival of the seventeenth and eighteenth centuries, and Romanticism, the literary and philosophical revolution of the years 1795-1830. Schleiermacher and Nietzsche were sons of pastors; Kant, Hegel and Hölderlin were trained in Pietist schools or seminaries, the Collegium Fridericianum in Königsberg, the Stift in Tübingen.

Pietism

Against a Lutheran orthodoxy frozen in dogmatic controversy after the Thirty Years' War, Philipp Jakob Spener proposed in 1675, in the Pia Desideria, a reform of the Reformation: Bible reading in small groups, the collegia pietatis, an effective priesthood of the laity, piety of the heart and holy conduct rather than doctrine. August Hermann Francke made Halle, with its orphanage, its schools and its missions, the centre of the movement; Count Zinzendorf founded in 1722 the community of the Moravian Brethren at Herrnhut, a piety of love for the Saviour and of lived conversion, in which Schleiermacher would be brought up and which Wesley would frequent, the Methodist revival bearing its mark. Pietism insists on the new birth, the personal experience of grace, self-examination, the spiritual diary and autobiography, and sanctification. Kant, raised in Königsberg in a severe Pietism, kept its morality and rejected its practices; Lessing, Herder, Hamann and Jacobi, who set a leap of faith against the reason of Spinoza and Kant, were marked by it; Hegel saw in it a religion of feeling without concept, and Nietzsche is, through his family, its rebellious heir.

Romanticism

Early Romanticism, that of the circle of Jena and Berlin around the journal Athenaeum (1798-1800), Friedrich and August Wilhelm Schlegel, Novalis, Tieck, Schleiermacher, is a revolt against the Enlightenment and against classicism in the name of the infinite, of feeling, of poetry and of individuality; it rediscovers Spinoza, the Middle Ages, mythologies and the East, and dreams of a new mythology and a universal poetry. Schleiermacher's Speeches on Religion are its religious manifesto; Novalis's Christendom or Europe (1799) idealizes medieval Catholic unity; the Hymns to the Night blend love, death and Christ. Later Romanticism, in Heidelberg and then in Berlin, Brentano, Arnim, Görres, Eichendorff, turns towards the people, the nation and Catholicism; several convert, Friedrich Schlegel in 1808, and political Romanticism, which Carl Schmitt would analyse in 1919, serves the Restoration. Schelling gives the movement its philosophy of nature and of art, then its philosophy of mythology and of revelation; Hölderlin, standing apart, its poetry of the absent gods.

Legacies

Romanticism founded modern hermeneutics, renewed philology, launched the comparative history of religions, the music and painting of the infinite, and a religiosity without dogma that recurs in Carlyle, Emerson, Renan and as far as the New Age; Feuerbach and Marx came out of it, Kierkegaard fought it within himself, and Weber inherited its pessimism in the face of disenchantment.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known Pietism as a religion of the heart and as the milieu in which Kant, whom the essay calls a Lutheran of Pietist upbringing, and later Schleiermacher and Nietzsche were formed, the Romantic circle in Berlin where the Speeches were born, the rediscovery of Spinoza, whom Novalis called a God-intoxicated man, and the conversion of Friedrich Schlegel. It places in this climate the birth of the regime of experience and the weakness that the essay attributes to it: experience proves that one feels, not what one feels.

In the essay: Friedrich Schleiermacher · Part Five · Kant

Further reading: Philipp Jakob Spener, Pia Desideria (1675) ; Novalis, Die Christenheit oder Europa (1799) ; Friedrich Schlegel, Athenaeum-Fragmente (1798) ; Ernst Troeltsch, Protestantisches Christentum und Kirche in der Neuzeit (1906) ; Georges Gusdorf, Le Romantisme (1993) ; Jacques Droz, Le Romantisme allemand et l'État (1966) ; Pierre Deghaye, La Doctrine ésotérique de Zinzendorf (1969)

German idealism: Kant, Fichte, Schelling, HegelCurrents and doctrines

German idealism is the name given to the philosophical movement which, from the Critique of Pure Reason (1781) to the death of Hegel (1831), sought to complete Kant's undertaking by grounding all knowledge in the activity of the mind, and whose great names are Fichte, Schelling and Hegel. It supplied the nineteenth-century critique of religion with both its vocabulary and its target.

After Kant

Kant had limited knowledge to phenomena and left the thing in itself unknowable; his successors regard this limit as an inconsistency. Reinhold seeks a single principle; Jacobi maintains that every consistent rationalism leads to Spinozism, and will later denounce Fichte's idealism as a nihilism; and the pantheism controversy (1785), born of Jacobi's revelation of Lessing's Spinozism, puts Spinoza at the centre of the debate: the Tübingen generation, Hölderlin, Schelling, Hegel, reads him with enthusiasm. Fichte, in the Wissenschaftslehre (1794), starts from the absolute I that posits itself and posits the not-I, and deduces from this activity consciousness, the world and morality; freedom is the first principle, and the world is only the material of duty. Accused of atheism in 1799 for having identified God with the moral order of the world, he loses his chair at Jena, in what became the atheism controversy, and goes on to develop a doctrine of absolute being and the Addresses to the German Nation.

Schelling and Hegel

Schelling, a prodigy at twenty, adds to Fichte's I a philosophy of nature, in which nature is visible spirit and spirit invisible nature, then a System of Transcendental Idealism (1800), which makes art the organ of philosophy, and a philosophy of identity, in which the absolute is the indifference of subject and object, which Hegel will mock as the night in which all cows are black. After the Philosophical Investigations into the Essence of Human Freedom (1809), which think evil and a dark ground in God, Schelling publishes almost nothing for thirty years, teaches at Erlangen and then at Munich and, in 1841, in Berlin, where Kierkegaard, Engels, Bakunin and Burckhardt hear him, a positive philosophy, a philosophy of mythology and a philosophy of revelation, which starts from existence and not from the concept, against the dead Hegel. Hegel, the last to arrive, builds the completed system in which the absolute is spirit and knows itself through history, and in which religion is the representation of what philosophy thinks in concept.

The end of idealism and its sequels

Hegel's death opens the crisis: the Hegelian right and left dispute the inheritance, Feuerbach turns idealism into anthropology, Marx sets it on its feet, Kierkegaard opposes existence to the system, Schopenhauer despises it, neo-Kantianism and positivism turn away from it. Idealism is nonetheless reborn among the British neo-Hegelians, Bradley, in Croce and Gentile, in Kroner, and its study was renewed in the twentieth century by Heidegger, Hyppolite, Tilliette, Henrich and research on the Jena constellation.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the pantheism controversy and the rediscovery of Spinoza, with Schelling's philosophy of revelation, from which it quotes the question of why there is something, and with Hegel's system. It locates in the exit from idealism the birth of the Hegelian left and of the modern critique of religion, and reports that Schelling was called to Berlin to combat the dragon's seed of Hegelian pantheism.

In the essay: Georg Wilhelm Friedrich Hegel · Kant · Part Five

Further reading: Johann Gottlieb Fichte, Grundlage der gesamten Wissenschaftslehre (1794) ; F. W. J. Schelling, System des transzendentalen Idealismus (1800) ; Philosophie der Offenbarung (1841-1842) ; Richard Kroner, Von Kant bis Hegel (1921-1924) ; Xavier Tilliette, Schelling. Une philosophie en devenir (1970) ; Alexis Philonenko, La Liberté humaine dans la philosophie de Fichte (1966) ; Jean-François Marquet, Liberté et existence. Étude sur la formation de la philosophie de Schelling (1973)

The Young Hegelians: from the critique of religion to the critique of politicsCurrents and doctrines

The Young Hegelians, or Hegelian left, are the group of German writers who, between Hegel's death in 1831 and the revolution of 1848, drew from his philosophy a radical critique of religion and then of the State, and from whom Feuerbach, Stirner, Marx and Engels emerged. The essay devotes its Part Six to them, and it is among them that the law of refoundations is stated for the first time.

The split in the school

Hegel had held religion to be true in its content and philosophy to be its completed form; the question whether this sublation preserved or dissolved Christianity divides his pupils. David Friedrich Strauss, whose Life of Jesus (1835) causes a scandal, introduces in 1837 the distinction, borrowed from the French Assembly, between a right, which holds philosophy to be the ally of faith, Göschel, Gabler, a centre, Rosenkranz, and a left, which concludes that they are incompatible: Strauss himself, then Bruno Bauer, who moved from the right to the left, Arnold Ruge, Ludwig Feuerbach, Moses Hess, Max Stirner, Edgar Bauer, Karl Marx, Friedrich Engels. Ruge founds in 1838 the Hallische Jahrbücher, then the Deutsche Jahrbücher, the organ of the movement, banned in 1843; the Rheinische Zeitung of Cologne, which Marx edits in 1842-1843, and the Deutsch-Französische Jahrbücher, which Ruge and Marx publish in Paris in 1844, carry it on.

From religion to politics

The movement follows a logic: the critique of religion is the premise of all critique, Marx says in 1844. Strauss dissolves the Gospels into myths and transfers the predicates of Christ to humanity; Bauer makes the Gospels works of self-consciousness and attacks the Christian State; Feuerbach, in 1841, discovers that theology is anthropology and that man has projected his own essence into God; Hess transposes the critique to money, the God of bourgeois society; Stirner, in 1844, turns the critique against the critics, Feuerbach's Man being a ghost just as God is; Marx and Engels, in The Holy Family (1845) and The German Ideology (1845-1846), settle their accounts with Saint Bruno and Saint Max and move from the critique of ideas to that of the relations of production. The accession of Frederick William IV in 1840, who summons Schelling to Berlin against Hegelian pantheism and drives the Young Hegelians out of the universities, makes the critique of religion the obligatory form of political critique in a State that calls itself Christian.

Legacy

The group scatters after 1848: Bauer turns to conservatism, Ruge to national liberalism, Feuerbach to retirement in Bruckberg, Marx and Engels to socialism, Stirner to oblivion; Kierkegaard, who read Feuerbach and Strauss, and Nietzsche, who replied to Strauss and read Feuerbach in his youth, inherit their critique. Löwith wrote their history in From Hegel to Nietzsche, and recent scholarship, Breckman, Moggach, has rehabilitated Bauer and Ruge as political theorists.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the split in the Hegelian school, the sequence Strauss, Bauer, Feuerbach, Stirner, Marx, the Deutsch-Französische Jahrbücher, and the role of Frederick William IV and Schelling; the history Löwith wrote of them is not cited there. In this sequence it reads the statement of the law of refoundations, each critic accusing the previous one of having remained religious, and sees in Stirner the one who formulated it without escaping it.

In the essay: Part Six · Max Stirner · David Friedrich Strauss and Bruno Bauer

Further reading: Karl Löwith, Von Hegel zu Nietzsche (1941) ; Jacques D'Hondt, Hegel et l'hégélianisme (1982) ; Warren Breckman, Marx, the Young Hegelians, and the Origins of Radical Social Theory (1999) ; Douglas Moggach (ed.), The New Hegelians (2006) ; Jean-Pierre Osier, introduction to L'Essence du christianisme (1968) ; Henri Arvon, Aux sources de l'existentialisme. Max Stirner (1954)

Positivism and scientism: science as a substitute religion in the nineteenth centuryCurrents and doctrines

Positivism is the doctrine of Auguste Comte, which limits knowledge to the laws of phenomena and makes science the model of all knowledge; scientism, a word coined by its opponents at the end of the nineteenth century, designates the belief that science can answer every question, including moral and metaphysical ones, and replace religion. The essay meets both in Comte, Renan, Strauss, the freethinkers and, by inheritance, the new atheists.

From Comte's positivism to the positivists

In the Cours Comte had set out positive philosophy, then founded in the Système a religion of Humanity; his disciples split. Émile Littré, the lexicographer, and the scientific positivists keep the method, the law of the three stages and the classification of the sciences, reject the religion and the priesthood, and give the word positivist its ordinary meaning: a supporter of a philosophy of science, hostile to metaphysics and theology, republican and secular. Pierre Laffitte and the religious positivists perpetuate the cult, in France and in Brazil. Stuart Mill, in England, and the logical positivism of the Vienna Circle, in the twentieth century, keep the name while changing the thing. Positivism, in its various forms, trains the elites of the Third Republic, Gambetta, Ferry, and inspires secular schooling, medicine, the methodical history of Langlois and Seignobos, and the sociology of Durkheim, who both claims it and departs from it.

Scientism

Alongside strict positivism, the nineteenth century saw the flowering of a faith in science that goes beyond method. Renan, in The Future of Science, written in 1848, expects science to organize humanity and replace religion; Taine applies to history and literature the laws of race, milieu and moment, and declares that vice and virtue are products like vitriol and sugar; Berthelot, the chemist turned minister, proclaims in 1885 that the world is henceforth without mystery, and in 1897 dedicates a morality to science; Haeckel, in Germany, draws from Darwin a monism that he sets up as a religion, and founds a Monist League in 1906; Büchner, Vogt and Moleschott spread a popularized materialism. In France, free thought, organized in societies from the 1860s onwards, combines anticlericalism, a cult of reason and of science, and civil rites, secular funerals and baptisms. Brunetière, in 1895, announces the bankruptcy of science, and the word scientism, which spreads at the turn of the century, serves the spiritualists, and later Hayek and Popper, to denounce the illegitimate extension of scientific method.

Posterity and critiques

Nietzsche sees in faith in scientific truth the last form of the ascetic ideal; Weber sets science, which gives no meaning, against the prophets of the lectern; Husserl speaks of a crisis of the European sciences; the new atheists of the twenty-first century, Dawkins and Harris in particular, have been accused of scientism by their critics, and Science written with a capital letter is, for the essay, one of the essences in Stirner's sense, a comparison it presents as constructed.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the difference between Comte's positivism and Littré's, the scientism of Renan and Berthelot, the monism of Haeckel, which touches Drews, and free thought. It ranks the Reason and the Science of the new atheists among the Stirnerian essences, turns § 344 of The Gay Science against Freud's faith in science, which holds against any scientism, and reports that Durkheim proves Comte right on substance and wrong on method.

In the essay: Auguste Comte · Ernest Renan · Part Eight · Contemporary militant atheists

Further reading: Auguste Comte, Discours sur l'esprit positif (1844) ; Émile Littré, Auguste Comte et la philosophie positive (1863) ; Ernest Renan, L'Avenir de la science (1848, publ. 1890) ; Marcelin Berthelot, Science et morale (1897) ; Annie Petit, Le Système d'Auguste Comte (2016) ; Jacqueline Lalouette, La Libre Pensée en France (1997) ; Owen Chadwick, The Secularization of the European Mind in the Nineteenth Century (1975)

Existentialism: from Kierkegaard to Sartre, believers and atheistsCurrents and doctrines

Existentialism is the name given after 1945 to a family of philosophies, born in the nineteenth century with Kierkegaard and developed in Germany and then in France between the two world wars, which start from the concrete existence of the individual, from his freedom, his anguish and his death, against systems and essences. It had a believing wing and an atheist wing, and the essay meets both in its tenth and twelfth parts.

The sources

Kierkegaard, against Hegel, holds that existence does not let itself be thought within the system, that subjectivity is truth and that faith is a leap; Dostoevsky stages the underground man, Ivan and Kirillov; Nietzsche announces the death of God and asks how to live after it; Pascal, Augustine and Job are reread as ancestors. In the twentieth century the word existence takes on a technical sense: the mode of being proper to man, who is not a thing but a relation to himself, to others and to death.

The philosophy of existence in Germany

Karl Jaspers, in his Philosophie (1932), calls philosophy of existence a thinking that illuminates existence without knowing it, through limit situations, communication and the ciphers of transcendence; Heidegger, in Being and Time (1927), analyses Dasein, being-towards-death, anxiety and authenticity, while refusing the label, as he would say in the Letter on Humanism (1947). The theologians of existence, Bultmann with demythologization, Tillich, Gogarten, take up these analyses; Martin Buber, in I and Thou (1923), reflects on relation; Lev Shestov, a Russian émigré in Paris, sets the faith of Jerusalem and the cry of Job against Athens and necessary reason.

French existentialism

Gabriel Marcel, who converted to Catholicism in 1929, a philosopher of mystery, incarnation and fidelity, is regarded as having introduced the word existentialism into French, applying it to Sartre before refusing it for himself. Sartre, in Being and Nothingness (1943) and the 1946 lecture, makes it a doctrine: existence precedes essence, man is freedom, condemned to choose himself without God or human nature, and responsible for everything; Simone de Beauvoir draws from it an ethics of ambiguity and The Second Sex; Merleau-Ponty a phenomenology of perception and of the body; Camus, who rejects the word, draws the absurd and revolt. In 1947 Mounier distinguishes Christian existentialisms, Marcel, Jaspers, Berdyaev, from atheist ones, Sartre, Heidegger; Pius XII condemns existentialism in 1950 in Humani generis, and the Communist Party denounces it as a bourgeois ideology. Saint-Germain-des-Prés, the cellars, the theatre, Huis clos, The Flies, Caligula, and the novels, Nausea, L'Étranger, make it the first mass philosophical movement. It declines in the 1960s before structuralism, which declares the death of the subject, but its themes, freedom, commitment, authenticity, the choice of oneself, have passed into common culture.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes for granted the genealogy Kierkegaard, Dostoevsky, Nietzsche, the difference between philosophy of existence and existentialism, the 1946 lecture, Heidegger's reply and the division between believers and atheists. It places Kierkegaard and Jaspers on the side of faith, Sartre among the atheists of the marketplace and Camus among the exceptions; Gabriel Marcel does not appear in it.

In the essay: Sartre · Søren Kierkegaard · Jaspers

Further reading: Jean Wahl, Les Philosophies de l'existence (1954) ; Emmanuel Mounier, Introduction aux existentialismes (1947) ; Walter Kaufmann (ed.), Existentialism from Dostoevsky to Sartre (1956) ; Sarah Bakewell, Au café existentialiste (2016) ; Jean-Paul Sartre, L'existentialisme est un humanisme (1946) ; Jacques Colette, L'Existentialisme (1994)

Marxism and religion: from Lenin to liberation theologyCurrents and doctrines

Marx had regarded the critique of religion as completed and had turned to the critique of the economy; his heirs had to decide what the workers' movement, and then the socialist States, would do with religion. From combative materialism to State atheism, and from dialogue with Christianity to liberation theology, Marxism has been at once the secular religion par excellence and an interlocutor of Christianity.

Social democracy and Lenin

Engels, in his studies of early Christianity, saw in the first Christians a movement of the oppressed comparable to the workers' movement; Karl Kautsky, theorist of the Second International, makes of it in 1908 a materialist history, Foundations of Christianity, a communism of consumption among slaves and the poor. The Erfurt programme (1891) declares religion a private matter, a position Lenin combats from 1905: religion is a private matter with regard to the State, not with regard to the party, which must carry on atheist propaganda without making it a precondition of membership; militant materialism, he says in 1922, must ally itself with the bourgeois atheists of the eighteenth century. After 1917, atheism becomes State doctrine, the Orthodox Church is persecuted, the League of the Godless organizes propaganda, and Marxism-Leninism, with its classics, its dogmas, its trials and its personality cult, takes on, in the eyes of Berdyaev, Voegelin and Aron, the form of an inverted religion.

The heterodox

Antonio Gramsci, in his Prison Notebooks, analyses the Church as an apparatus of hegemony and religious common sense as a terrain of struggle, and compares Marxism to an intellectual and moral Reformation; Walter Benjamin blends Jewish messianism and historical materialism in his theses On the Concept of History (1940); Ernst Bloch, in The Principle of Hope (1954-1959) and Atheism in Christianity (1968), reads the Bible as a book of revolt, from Exodus to the Apocalypse, and holds that only an atheist can be a good Christian and only a Christian a good atheist; Lucien Goldmann links Pascal and Marxism through the wager; Roger Garaudy, for the French Communist Party, opens in the 1960s a dialogue with Christians, welcomed by the Second Vatican Council and the journal Esprit, before converting to Islam.

Liberation theology

In Latin America, after Medellín (1968), Gustavo Gutiérrez, Leonardo Boff, Jon Sobrino and Juan Luis Segundo construct a liberation theology that starts from the praxis of the poor, borrows from Marxism the analysis of structures of dependency and reads the Gospel as a historical liberation; the base communities and Archbishop Romero, assassinated in 1980, are its figures. Rome, through the 1984 instruction signed by Ratzinger, condemns its Marxist borrowings, and Michael Löwy has spoken of this current as a liberation Christianity. In Europe, the political theology of Metz and Moltmann and left-wing Christians are its parallels.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with Engels's texts, Lenin's position, State atheism, the reading of Marxism as a secular religion by Aron and Voegelin, and State Marxism among the strict refoundations. It retains the view that Marx dismissed the sacred without dismissing salvation, and that the Protestation he named remains the question the wisdoms have not settled; the article extends the essay towards a Christian Marxism and a liberation theology that it does not treat.

In the essay: The sigh without a heaven · Part Eleven

Further reading: Friedrich Engels, Zur Geschichte des Urchristentums (1894-1895) ; Karl Kautsky, Der Ursprung des Christentums (1908) ; Lenin, Socialisme et religion (1905) ; Ernst Bloch, Das Prinzip Hoffnung (1954-1959) ; Atheismus im Christentum (1968) ; Gustavo Gutiérrez, Teología de la liberación (1971) ; Michael Löwy, La Guerre des dieux. Religion et politique en Amérique latine (1998) ; Roland Boer, Criticism of Heaven (2007)

Twentieth-century Christian theology: dialectical, existential, Catholic, death of GodCurrents and doctrines

Twentieth-century Christian theology responded to the critique of religion in two ways: by separating faith from religion, Barth, Bonhoeffer, or by translating faith into the terms of modernity, Bultmann, Tillich, Rahner. The essay reads its protagonists in its tenth part and draws up, at the end of its section on Marion, an assessment: a victory and a capitulation.

Protestantism: dialectical, existential, political

The liberal theology of the nineteenth century, Ritschl, Harnack, Troeltsch, is shattered by the war of 1914 and by Barth's Römerbrief (1919, recast in 1922): dialectical theology, Barth, Gogarten, Brunner, Bultmann, reaffirms the transcendence of God and the judgement of revelation on every religion. The group splits in the 1930s. Barth builds the Church Dogmatics; Bultmann, an exegete, proposes in 1941 to demythologize the New Testament, that is, to interpret its mythical language, the three-storeyed world, miracles, the end of time, as the expression of an understanding of existence, using Heidegger's concepts, so that the kerygma may reach modern man; Jaspers objects that myth cannot be translated without loss. Paul Tillich, who emigrated to the United States, develops a theology of correlation, in which revelation answers the questions of culture, and defines God as what concerns us ultimately, the ground of being beyond the God of theism; Bonhoeffer, in his prison letters, sketches a non-religious Christianity. After 1960, death of God theology, Altizer, Hamilton, Van Buren, radicalizes Bonhoeffer and Nietzsche; Moltmann's theology of hope (1964), nourished by Bloch, and Metz's political theology turn eschatology back towards history; process theology, inspired by Whitehead, conceives of a God in becoming.

Catholicism: from modernism to Vatican II

The condemnation of modernism in 1907 closes Catholicism to critical exegesis for half a century; Neo-Thomism, Maritain, Gilson, is the official philosophy. The nouvelle théologie of the 1940s, Henri de Lubac, Daniélou, Chenu, Congar, returns to the Fathers and to Scripture, rethinks the relation of nature and the supernatural and prepares the council, after being sanctioned by Humani generis in 1950. Karl Rahner, a German Jesuit, builds a transcendental theology of man as hearer of the Word, open to the absolute, and coins the notion of anonymous Christians; Hans Urs von Balthasar sets against him a theological aesthetics; Joseph Ratzinger, an expert at the council, then prefect of the Congregation for the Doctrine of the Faith and Pope Benedict XVI, defends the rationality of faith against relativism in Introduction to Christianity (1968) and in his dialogue with Habermas (2004). Vatican II (1962-1965) consecrates these currents and opens the post-conciliar crisis, between Concilium, a progressive journal, and Communio, founded by Balthasar, de Lubac and Ratzinger.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with dialectical theology, demythologization and the attested debate with Jaspers, the positivism of revelation for which Bonhoeffer reproaches Barth, death of God theology, the nouvelle théologie and the dialogue between Habermas and Ratzinger, which it has not read. It draws from this century the conclusion that purified faith has given up knowing, consoling, founding and instituting, and that what remains of it is a spirituality that atheists also claim.

In the essay: Bonhoeffer · Part Ten · Assessment of Part Ten

Further reading: Rosino Gibellini, Panorama de la théologie au XXe siècle (1992) ; Bernard Sesboüé, La Théologie au XXe siècle et l'avenir de la foi (2007) ; Rudolf Bultmann, Neues Testament und Mythologie (1941) ; Paul Tillich, Systematic Theology (1951-1963) ; Karl Rahner, Grundkurs des Glaubens (1976) ; Jürgen Moltmann, Theologie der Hoffnung (1964) ; Joseph Ratzinger, Einführung in das Christentum (1968)

Buddhism: landmarks for a religion without a creator GodCurrents and doctrines

Buddhism was born in northern India in the 5th century BCE from the teaching of Siddhārtha Gautama, the Buddha, the Awakened One, and today counts some five hundred million followers in Asia and beyond. A religion without a creator God, but not without gods, nor without rites or institutions, it served the European philosophers of the nineteenth century as a mirror and a projection, and the essay makes it the test of any definition of religion by God.

The teaching

According to tradition, Prince Gautama, born at Lumbinī c. 560 BCE according to the traditional chronology, c. 480 according to the revised chronology, leaves the palace on discovering old age, sickness and death, practises asceticism, abandons it, and attains awakening under the tree at Bodhgayā; he preaches for forty-five years and dies at Kuśinagar. His first sermon, at Sārnāth, sets out the Four Noble Truths: everything is suffering, duḥkha, unsatisfactoriness; suffering has an origin, craving, desire, hatred and ignorance; it has a cessation, nirvāṇa, the extinction of craving; there is a path to this cessation, the Noble Eightfold Path, right view, thought, speech, action, livelihood, effort, mindfulness and concentration. The teaching rests on dependent origination, every phenomenon arising from conditions, on impermanence, and on non-self, anātman: there is no substantial soul, only aggregates, and what transmigrates according to karma, the law of deeds, is not a person but a stream. The Buddha refuses to answer metaphysical questions, the eternity of the world, existence after death, as useless for liberation; the gods exist, but are subject to the cycle like human beings, and no one saves anyone.

The schools and history

The community, the saṅgha, monks and nuns supported by lay people, forms with the Buddha and the Dharma, the doctrine, the triple refuge that every Buddhist pronounces. After the master's death, councils fix the canon and the schools divide: the Theravāda, school of the Elders, preserved in Pali in Ceylon, Burma, Thailand, Cambodia and Laos, where the ideal is the arhat; the Mahāyāna, the Great Vehicle, born around the beginning of the Common Era, which exalts the bodhisattva who renounces nirvāṇa to save all beings, develops Nāgārjuna's philosophy of emptiness and the mind-only school, and spreads to China, Korea and Japan, in the forms of Chan and Zen, Pure Land and Tiantai; the tantric Vajrayāna, in Tibet and Mongolia, with its lamas and its Dalai Lama. The emperor Aśoka, in the 3rd century BCE, makes it a state religion and a missionary religion; Buddhism almost disappears from India after the 12th century. Monastic institutions, cults of relics and images, funeral rites, ties with the powers that be, wars and persecutions, including recent ones, make it a complete religion.

The Buddhism of the Europeans

Known in fragments before the nineteenth century, Buddhism is constituted as an object by philology, Burnouf in 1844, and read by Schopenhauer, Renan and Nietzsche as a nihilism, a cult of nothingness, a European image that Droit has studied, or, in Nietzsche, as a positivist religion without God; Droit and Lopez have shown what this image owed to Europe. Twentieth-century Western Buddhism, Suzuki's Zen, vipassanā meditation, Kabat-Zinn's medicalized mindfulness, is a selective reinvention of it.

What the essay assumes

The essay takes as known the Four Noble Truths, nirvāṇa, karma, the saṅgha and the triple refuge, the division into vehicles, and the European construction studied by Droit. It draws from them that religion is not defined by God, that a doctrine without sacrifice can have sacrificial institutions, and that Buddhism is, for its thesis, neither an exception nor an ally but a test; corporate meditation seems to it a form of palliative care.

In the essay: The Buddhism of the philosophers · Arthur Schopenhauer · Part Twelve
