Les philosophes et le Bouddha (1997) ; Bernard Faure, Bouddhisme, philosophie et religion (1998) ; Donald S. Lopez, Prisoners of Shangri-La (1998)

The new atheists: Dawkins, Harris, Dennett, HitchensCurrents and doctrines

The name new atheists, or New Atheism, is given to a group of English-speaking authors whose books, published between 2004 and 2007 after the attacks of 11 September 2001, attacked religion with a virulence and a commercial success unseen since the Enlightenment: Sam Harris, Richard Dawkins, Daniel Dennett and Christopher Hitchens, nicknamed the Four Horsemen. The essay treats them together with Onfray, of whom they are the other side, and places them in the poorest version of the regime of error.

The books

Sam Harris, trained in philosophy and then a neuroscientist, opens the series with The End of Faith (2004): faith, belief without evidence, is intrinsically dangerous, Islam especially, and religious moderation shields fanaticism; he will later defend a spirituality without religion based on meditation. Richard Dawkins, an Oxford biologist, author of The Selfish Gene and inventor of the word meme, publishes The God Delusion in 2006: the God hypothesis is a scientific hypothesis that is almost certainly false, the argument from the improbability of the designer, religion is a by-product of evolved dispositions, a virus of the mind, the religious education of children a form of abuse, and the atheist can be happy and moral. Daniel Dennett, a philosopher of mind, asks in Breaking the Spell (2006) that religion be studied as a natural phenomenon, by the cognitive sciences and evolution, and defends, with Dawkins, the word brights proposed in 2003 for naturalists. Christopher Hitchens, a journalist and polemicist, draws up in God Is Not Great (2007) the historical and moral indictment: religion poisons everything. Victor Stenger, A. C. Grayling, Michel Onfray in France, with the Traité d'athéologie (2005), and Ayaan Hirsi Ali are associated with them, and public debates, associations and advertising campaigns on buses make it a movement.

The common theses

New Atheism holds religion to be a set of false empirical propositions that science refutes; it judges faith harmful in itself, and not only in its abuses; it rejects the respect owed to beliefs and the non-overlapping magisteria proposed by Stephen Jay Gould; it grounds morality in evolution and reason; and it claims the legacy of the Enlightenment, of Hume, of Russell and of Clifford. It is indebted to evolutionary biology and the cognitive sciences, Boyer, Atran, whose results it simplifies.

The criticisms

They have come from every side: from believers, Alister McGrath, John Lennox, who point to a caricature theology; from philosophers, Michael Ruse, Thomas Nagel, who judge the arguments threadbare; from the left, Terry Eagleton, for whom Dawkins does not know what he is talking about and serves Islamophobia; from sociologists, who see in it a scientism and one more secular religion, with its prophets and its heretics; and from within, as controversies over sexism and politics divided the movement after 2011. New Atheism ebbed in the 2010s, leaving an organized atheist culture, mostly American.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the four authors and their books, which it does not quote, the thesis of religion as scientific error, the legacy of Clifford and Russell, and the link with the cognitive sciences. It sees in them the poorest version of the regime of error, Reason and Science set up as essences in Stirner's sense, the men of the marketplace of § 125, and against their Clifford-style crusades it sets the combination of James and Clifford: the problem is not belief, it is the Inquisitor.

In the essay: Contemporary militant atheists · Do we have the right to believe? Clifford, James and analytic philosophy · Boyer

Further reading: Sam Harris, The End of Faith (2004) ; Richard Dawkins, The God Delusion (2006), trans. Pour en finir avec Dieu ; Daniel Dennett, Breaking the Spell (2006) ; Christopher Hitchens, God Is Not Great (2007), trans. Dieu n'est pas grand ; Terry Eagleton, Reason, Faith, and Revolution (2009) ; Stephen LeDrew, The Evolution of Atheism (2016)

Contemporary spiritualities: New Age, meditation, spiritual but not religiousCurrents and doctrines

Since the 1960s, the decline of the Churches in the West has not resulted in the disappearance of the religious but in the growth of spiritualities outside institutions, individual, composite, centred on experience and well-being, which sociologists call the mystical-esoteric nebula, religion à la carte or the spiritual revolution. The essay encounters them in its twelfth part, around Western Buddhism and spiritualities without God.

The New Age and the nebula

The New Age, born in California and England in the 1960s and 1970s, announces the entry into the Age of Aquarius, an age of expanded consciousness, and brings together heterogeneous sources: nineteenth-century theosophy, Western esotericism, humanistic and transpersonal psychology, Maslow, Jung, Eastern spiritualities imported by Indian gurus and Zen masters, shamanism, ecology, alternative medicine, channelling and crystals. Its implicit doctrine comes down to a few features: the divine is within oneself, personal experience takes precedence over dogma, everything is energy and interdependence, the transformation of the self will transform the world, and one chooses freely from all traditions. In France Françoise Champion described a mystical-esoteric nebula, Paul Heelas a spirituality of the self, Hervieu-Léger the figure of the pilgrim. The term New Age grew dated in the 1990s, but its contents spread into personal development, coaching, wellness and bookshops.

Meditation, mindfulness, yoga

Yoga, practised in the West since Vivekananda in 1893 and popularized by Indian teachers of the twentieth century, has become a mass form of exercise, largely detached from its Hindu metaphysics. Buddhist meditation, Zen, vipassanā, Tibetan, has followed the same path: in 1979 Jon Kabat-Zinn created a stress reduction programme based on mindfulness, which entered hospitals, then schools, prisons, Silicon Valley companies and apps, backed by clinical studies; Matthieu Ricard and the Dalai Lama are the media faces of this Western Buddhism, and some Buddhists criticize a McMindfulness that has stripped meditation of ethics and liberation to make it a performance technique. Corporate meditation is, for the essay, the end point of this transfer.

Spiritual but not religious

In the United States, the phrase spiritual but not religious has designated since the 1990s a growing share of the population, about a quarter at the turn of the 2020s, which rejects institutions without giving up a quest for meaning; in Europe, the nones, disaffiliated rather than atheist, likewise improvise their own arrangements. Frédéric Lenoir, in France, has described and accompanied this metamorphosis of God towards a secular spirituality of wisdom and happiness, from Spinoza to Buddhism; pocket Stoics, books of wisdom and retreats are its market. Sociologists debate whether this spirituality is a disguised secularization, a privatization of religion or a new religion, without a Church but with its merchants; the Churches see in it sometimes a competitor, sometimes a field of action, and the French state, through the Miviludes, monitors its sectarian excesses.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the New Age and the nebula, Hervieu-Léger's pilgrim, mindfulness and corporate meditation, the phrase spiritual but not religious, and Lenoir, heir to Renan. It judges Western Buddhism a reinvention and corporate meditation a palliative treatment, sees in these spiritualities the position of the atheists of the marketplace when they believe themselves to be without history, and recalls that a spirituality without a lineage escapes refoundation but is not handed down.

In the essay: Part Twelve · The Buddhism of the philosophers · The spiritualities of the Universe · Hervieu-Léger · André Comte-Sponville

Further reading: Paul Heelas, The New Age Movement (1996) ; Paul Heelas and Linda Woodhead, The Spiritual Revolution (2005) ; Françoise Champion and Danièle Hervieu-Léger (eds.), De l'émotion en religion (1990) ; Frédéric Lenoir, Les Métamorphoses de Dieu (2003) ; Jon Kabat-Zinn, Full Catastrophe Living (1990) ; Robert Fuller, Spiritual, but not Religious (2001) ; Nathalie Luca, Individus et pouvoirs face aux sectes (2008)

Received revelations: spiritism, channeling, the Christ LettersCurrents and doctrines

Received revelations, or channeling in the vocabulary of the New Age, are texts presented as dictated to a human intermediary by an outside entity: a dead person, a spirit, an ascended master, an angel, Christ or God himself. The intermediary claims to be a transcriber, not an author. The genre is old, but it took its modern form in the nineteenth century and has flourished since the 1970s in the nebula of contemporary spiritualities.

From table-turning to spiritism

In the eighteenth century Swedenborg published the account of his visions of the world of spirits. In 1848 the rappings heard in the house of the Fox sisters, in New York State, launched American spiritualism; the fashion for table-turning reached Europe in 1853. Allan Kardec, a teacher from Lyon, founded spiritism in 1857 with The Spirits' Book, a doctrine built from the answers obtained from spirits through mediums: God, the immortality of the soul, reincarnation and moral progress through successive lives. Spiritism presents itself as an experimental science and a philosophy rather than a Church; it remains very much alive in Brazil. Victor Hugo, on Jersey, made the tables speak from 1853 to 1855. Scientists were divided: Faraday and Chevreul explained the movements by unconscious muscular effort, while others, such as William Crookes or Charles Richet, studied mediums, and the Society for Psychical Research, founded in London in 1882, counted William James among its members.

Modern channeling

In the twentieth century the genre detached itself from spiritism and from the dead. The Seth messages, received by Jane Roberts from 1963 onwards, teach that each person creates their own reality. A Course in Miracles, dictated from 1965 to 1972 to the New York psychologist Helen Schucman by a voice presenting itself as Jesus, proposes a rereading of Christianity in which the world is an illusion and salvation a forgiveness that undoes guilt. Neale Donald Walsch's Conversations with God, from 1995 onwards, are written dialogues with God that became worldwide bestsellers.

The Christ Letters belong to this family. They are nine letters, accompanied by articles, presented as dictated by Christ to an anonymous South African woman, his Recorder, who says she prepared for the task over decades; circulated online in English, they appeared in French from Interkeltia in 2010, from Atlantes in 2012 and then in paperback. In them Christ corrects the interpretations of the Churches: no sin against God, no Trinity, no salvation through the blood of the cross; in their place, a universal Consciousness, an ego that is the source of suffering, a law of cause and effect described in terms of electromagnetism, and a practice of meditation and purification of thoughts.

Common features and scholarly readings

These texts share a source declared to lie outside the intermediary, a correction of the instituted religions in the name of an older or purer teaching, a cosmology of consciousness or energy, and an ethics of self-transformation. Historians of esotericism such as Wouter Hanegraaff and Olav Hammer study the genre's strategies of authority: the appeal to experience, to a lost tradition and to scientific vocabulary. Psychologists invoke dissociation, automatic writing and unconscious creativity; as early as 1900 Théodore Flournoy had shown in From India to the Planet Mars how a medium recomposed her forgotten reading. The Churches generally see in these texts a private revelation without guarantee, and the bodies that monitor sectarian abuses keep watch on the groups that form around certain messengers.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with spiritism, the channeling genre and the existence of the Christ Letters, which it examines in Part Twelve together with quantum mysticism. It reads in them a replacement revelation that contests the authority of the Churches in the name of a higher authority, a new Jesus made to the measure of his age, and a law of retribution that refuses the sacrifice of the cross but makes the sick responsible for their sickness.

In the essay: The spiritualities of the Universe · Part Twelve · William James

Further reading: Allan Kardec, Le Livre des Esprits (1857) ; Jane Roberts, Seth Speaks (1972) ; Helen Schucman, A Course in Miracles (1976) ; Neale Donald Walsch, Conversations with God (1995) ; Olav Hammer, Claiming Knowledge. Strategies of Epistemology from Theosophy to the New Age (2001) ; Wouter J. Hanegraaff, New Age Religion and Western Culture (1996) ; Guillaume Cuchet, Les Voix d'outre-tombe. Tables tournantes, spiritisme et société au XIXe siècle (2012)

Quantum mysticism, synchronicity and the law of attractionCurrents and doctrines

Quantum mysticism is the name given to the contemporary spiritualities that invoke quantum physics to assert that consciousness creates or influences reality, that everything is connected and that the mind can act directly on matter. It is often associated with Jung's synchronicity and with the law of attraction, and it inherits a long tradition that, since the eighteenth century, has borrowed its vocabulary from the physics of the moment.

The ancestors: fluid, magnetism, thought

Franz Anton Mesmer, a Viennese physician who settled in Paris in 1778, treated patients with animal magnetism, a universal fluid he claimed to capture and transmit; a royal commission on which Lavoisier and Franklin sat concluded in 1784 that the effects observed were due to imagination. Magnetism survived into the nineteenth century, with Puységur and his disciples, and then in hypnosis. In the United States Phineas Quimby, a clockmaker turned healer, taught around 1850 that illness arises from false beliefs; from him descend Mary Baker Eddy's Christian Science (1875) and the New Thought movement, which William James described in 1902 under the name of mind-cure, and whose heirs are positive thinking and personal development.

Synchronicity

Jung called synchronicity the coincidence of a psychic state and an external event linked not by a cause but by a meaning, and he made it a principle of acausal connection. He set it out in 1952 in a volume shared with Wolfgang Pauli, Nobel laureate in physics, who published a study of Kepler in it; the best-known example is that of the golden scarab that strikes the window while a patient is recounting a dream about a scarab. The notion has had an immense posterity in popular spiritualities. Cognitive psychology explains the impression of meaningful coincidence by the tendency to perceive patterns and intentions in chance, which the psychiatrist Klaus Conrad named apophenia in 1958, and by selective memory, which retains coincidences and forgets non-coincidences.

From The Tao of Physics to The Secret

In 1975 Fritjof Capra, who holds a doctorate in physics, published The Tao of Physics, which brings quantum mechanics close to Hindu, Buddhist and Taoist mysticism. David Bohm, a recognized physicist, proposed in 1980 an implicate order, an undivided totality of which things would be the unfoldings, and held dialogues with Krishnamurti. Deepak Chopra, a physician, launched quantum healing in 1989. The film What the Bleep Do We Know!? (2004) popularized the idea that the observer creates reality, and Rhonda Byrne's The Secret (2006), book and film, presented the law of attraction, according to which thoughts attract the events that resemble them, as a secret known to the great men of history.

The physicists' answer

Physicists object that the observer of quantum mechanics designates an interaction with a measuring apparatus and not a consciousness, that entanglement transmits no information and does not connect minds, and that quantum effects vanish at the scale of the brain and the body. Murray Gell-Mann spoke of quantum flapdoodle, Victor Stenger devoted a book to refuting the thesis of a quantum consciousness, and the Sokal affair, in 1996, opened a wider debate on the use of the exact sciences by the humanities. The law of attraction is also criticized for its consequences: making the sick and the poor responsible for their lot, and sometimes turning people away from medical care.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with Jung's synchronicity, quantum mysticism, the law of attraction and their ancestor, the mind-cure described by William James. It examines them in Part Twelve together with the Christ Letters, as a demand that science testify for the sacred, a Providence renamed the Universe and a theodicy without God that turns complaint into a negative thought.

In the essay: The spiritualities of the Universe · Jung · Boyer · William James

Further reading: C. G. Jung and W. Greek myths: sources, gods and divine generationsGreek myths

Greek mythology is not a sacred book but an open set of narratives about the gods, the heroes and the origin of the world, handed down by poets, vase painters, festivals and sanctuaries, and constantly reinvented for more than a millennium. It has no single author, no canonical version, no clergy charged with guarding its meaning, and that is what allows the philosophers, from the sixth century BCE onwards, to discuss it, correct it or interpret it.

The sources

The oldest texts are the Homeric poems, the Iliad and the Odyssey, fixed around the eighth century BCE, and the poems of Hesiod, the Theogony and the Works and Days, slightly later. Then come the Homeric hymns, the lyric poetry of Pindar, then the Athenian tragedians of the fifth century, Aeschylus, Sophocles and Euripides, who take up the heroic legends to stage them before the city. In the Hellenistic and Roman period, mythographers compile the versions: the Library attributed to Apollodorus, Ovid's Metamorphoses, Pausanias's notes on local cults. A large part of what is told today comes from these late compilations, and the mythology Europe received is often Ovid's.

The divine generations

Hesiod's Theogony recounts the birth of the world and of the gods as a succession of generations and acts of violence. In the beginning is Chaos, then come Gaia, the Earth, and Eros. Gaia gives birth to Ouranos, the Sky, who holds their children back in her womb; their son Cronos mutilates him and takes power. Cronos in turn devours his children, until Rhea gives him a stone in place of Zeus and saves him; Zeus overthrows his father and the Titans at the end of a long war. The reign of Zeus closes the series: he shares out the world with his brothers, the sky for himself, the sea for Poseidon, the underworld for Hades, and founds a lasting order based on justice and the distribution of honours.

The Olympians and the heroes

The twelve Olympians form the divine family that dwells on Olympus: Zeus and Hera, Poseidon, Demeter, Athena, Apollo, Artemis, Ares, Aphrodite, Hephaestus, Hermes, and Hestia or Dionysus depending on the lists. These gods have human form, passions, rivalries and loves, but they are immortal and far more powerful than men; they do not create the world, they are a part of it. Below them, the heroes, often born of a god and a mortal woman, perform exploits, found cities and receive a cult at their tomb: Heracles, Theseus, Perseus, Achilles, Oedipus.

Myth and logos

The Greek word muthos first means speech, narrative, before gradually being opposed to logos, argued discourse. Historians have shown that this opposition is itself a construction of the Greek philosophers and historians, who classed under the name of myth what they wanted to hold at a distance. Paul Veyne raised the question of belief: the Greeks believed in their myths in various modes, ranging from literal adherence to learned interpretation, without these ways of believing excluding one another.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the gods of Homer and Hesiod, whom Plato judges unworthy and whom Aristotle says were handed down to the ancients in the form of myth, as well as the reign of Zeus that several literary boxes evoke. It also assumes the distinction between myth and argued discourse, on which the first part and Blumenberg's work on myth rest.

(1983) ; Marcel Detienne, L'Invention de la mythologie (1981) ; Pierre Grimal, Dictionnaire de la mythologie grecque et romaine (1951) ; Timothy Gantz, Early Greek Myth (1993)

The religion of the Greek city: sacrifices, festivals, oracles, impiety trialsGreek myths

Greek religion is first a matter of collective practices rather than of beliefs: honouring the gods of the city with sacrifices, festivals, processions and contests, according to the customs of the ancestors. It has no revealed book, no dogma, no Church, no clergy distinct from the citizens, and it is the city itself, through its magistrates and its assemblies, that organizes the cults and punishes those who threaten them.

Sacrifice and festivals

The central rite is blood sacrifice. A domestic animal, an ox, a sheep or a goat, is led to the altar in procession, consecrated, slaughtered; the bones wrapped in fat burn for the gods, and the meat is shared among the participants in a banquet. Sacrifice thus fixes the place of each: the gods receive the smoke, men eat, and the sharing of the meat outlines the civic community. Alongside sacrifices, libations, offerings and prayers accompany daily life. The Athenian calendar counted dozens of festivals, among them the Panathenaea in honour of Athena and the Dionysia, during which the tragedies were performed.

Sanctuaries and oracles

The great Panhellenic sanctuaries, Olympia, Delphi, Delos, brought the Greeks together beyond their cities. At Delphi, the Pythia, seated on the tripod, delivered the oracles of Apollo, which priests put into shape; cities and individuals came to consult her there about the founding of a colony, a war, a purification. On the temple were inscribed maxims, the most famous of which invited one to know oneself, and which Socrates took up as the programme of philosophy.

Impiety and its trials

In the absence of dogma, impiety, asébeia, does not consist in believing wrongly but in offending the gods of the city: profaning a sanctuary, parodying mysteries, mutilating statues, teaching doctrines that turn people away from worship. In Athens, several trials targeted it in the fifth century: the philosopher Anaxagoras, who held the sun to be a red-hot stone, had to leave the city; the sophist Protagoras is said to have been prosecuted for his agnosticism; in 415, the mutilated Herms and the parody of the Eleusinian mysteries set off a wave of trials. In 399, Socrates was accused of not recognizing the gods of the city, of introducing new divinities and of corrupting the young; condemned to death, he drank the hemlock.

From Greece to Rome

Roman religion shares this civic character: a public cult regulated by colleges of priests who are also magistrates, a piety defined as ritual exactness. The Christians, who refused to sacrifice to the gods and to the emperor, were accused of atheism, like Socrates before them, and the apologists drew an argument from this parallel.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the trial of Socrates and the notion of impiety, which Plato's Laws turn into an offence punished by prison and death, as well as with the accusation of atheism brought against the Christians, which Justin compares to the one brought against Socrates. It also assumes the idea of a religion of rites rather than of beliefs, that of Cicero's relegere, and the begging priests of Cybele to whom Celsus compares the Christians.

In the Gallery:The Death of Socrates (1787, New York, Metropolitan Museum of Art): condemned in 399 BCE for impiety (ἀσέβεια, asebeia) and corrupting the young, Socrates drinks the hemlock; David places at the foot of the bed an aged Plato, whom the Phaedo (59b) says was absent that day

Further reading: Louise Bruit Zaidman and Pauline Schmitt Pantel, La Religion grecque (1989) ; Walter Burkert, Greek Religion (1977, trans. 1985) ; Jean-Pierre Vernant, Mythe et religion en Grèce ancienne (1990) ; Marcel Detienne and Jean-Pierre Vernant (eds.), La Cuisine du sacrifice en pays grec (1979) ; Robert Parker, Athenian Religion (1996) ; John Scheid, La Religion des Romains (1998)

The philosophers against the myths: from Xenophanes to allegoryGreek myths

The critique of religion begins in Greece as a critique of myths. From the sixth century BCE, thinkers reproach the poets for having lent the gods unworthy actions, and seek either to purify the idea of the divine or to reinterpret the narratives so as to save their meaning. These two gestures, correction and allegory, run through the whole later history of religious critique.

Xenophanes and Heraclitus

Xenophanes of Colophon reproaches Homer and Hesiod for having attributed to the gods everything that is shameful among men, theft, adultery, deceit. He observes that the Ethiopians depict their gods as black and snub-nosed, the Thracians as red-haired and blue-eyed, and that if oxen could paint, they would paint ox gods. Against this projection he sets a single god, the greatest, who resembles mortals neither in body nor in thought. Heraclitus of Ephesus likewise mocks rites of purification by blood and prayers addressed to statues, and makes fire and the logos the law of the world.

Plato against the poets

In the Republic, Plato excludes from the ideal city the narratives of Homer and Hesiod that show quarrelsome, lying or changeable gods, because they shape the souls of the guardians badly. Two rules must guide the poets: the god is good and is the cause only of good; he is simple and does not deceive. Plato does not for all that give up myth: he invents some himself to say what argument cannot reach.

The interpretations

To save Homer, readers read his narratives as allegories: as early as the sixth century, Theagenes of Rhegium sees in the battles of the gods struggles between natural elements, and the Stoics, from Zeno to Cornutus, make Zeus fire or ether, Hera air, Athena thought. Another reading, euhemerism, comes from Euhemerus of Messene, who relates around 300 BCE that he found on a distant island an inscription proving that Zeus was a king deified after his death: the gods would be illustrious men who became objects of worship. The Epicureans, finally, keep blessed and indifferent gods, but reject the whole mythology of fear.

Aristotle and useful myths

Aristotle, in the Metaphysics, distinguishes in the tradition a true core, the divinity of the first substances, and mythical additions, the human or animal form of the gods, introduced to persuade the multitude and to serve the laws. This political reading of myth will be taken up from Antiquity to the Enlightenment.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with Xenophanes, to whom it compares a formula of Montaigne, with the Platonic rejection of fables, Aristotle's thesis on the myths added to persuade the multitude, and the allegorical method that Philo of Alexandria applies to the Bible after the Stoics applied it to Homer. It does not name Euhemerus, but the explanation of the gods by the deification of men is the distant ancestor of the theories of projection it studies in d'Holbach and Feuerbach.

In the Gallery:The School of Athens (1509-1511, Vatican, Stanza della Segnatura): in the centre, Plato pointing to the sky and Aristotle stretching his hand towards the earth; on the left, the turbaned figure traditionally identified as Averroes

In the essay: Plato · Aristotle · Epicurus and Lucretius · Montaigne

Further reading: Jean Pépin, Mythe et allégorie (1958) ; Luc Brisson, Introduction à la philosophie du mythe, vol. I (1996) ; Werner Jaeger, The Theology of the Early Greek Philosophers (1947) ; Jean-Pierre Vernant, Mythe et pensée chez les Grecs (1965) ; Geoffrey Kirk, John Raven and Malcolm Schofield, Les Philosophes présocratiques (1983, trans. 1995)

Plato's myths: Er, Gyges, the noble lie, the androgyneGreek myths

Plato, who drives the poets out of his ideal city, is also the greatest inventor of myths in philosophy. His dialogues contain some twenty narratives, some taken from tradition, others forged for the occasion, which come in where argument stops: the origin of the world, the fate of the soul after death, the foundation of the city. Their status has been debated since Antiquity: pedagogical ornaments, likely truths, or political instruments.

The myths of the soul

At the end of the Republic, the Pamphylian soldier Er, left for dead on the battlefield, comes back to life and recounts what he saw: the judgement of souls, the rewards and punishments of a thousand years, then the choice each soul makes of its next life, under the gaze of the Moirai, before drinking the water of Lethe. The narrative makes each person the author of his fate: the god is not responsible, responsibility belongs to the one who chooses. In the Phaedrus, the soul is compared to a winged team, a charioteer driving two horses, one docile, the other unruly, which rises to contemplate the intelligible realities or falls back towards the body. The Gorgias and the Phaedo also have their myths of the judgement of the dead.

The myths of the city

The Protagoras tells that Prometheus, having given fire and the arts to men, could not give them the political art; Zeus sent Hermes to distribute to all of them shame and justice, without which cities cannot last. In the Republic, Gyges, a shepherd who finds a ring that makes him invisible, seduces the queen and kills the king: the narrative raises the question of whether anyone would remain just without fear of being seen. The same dialogue proposes to the founders of the city a noble lie, the myth of the metals: all the citizens would be born of the earth, brothers, but the god would have mixed gold into the soul of the rulers, silver into that of the guardians, iron and bronze into that of the producers. The Timaeus and the Critias, finally, tell of Atlantis, a maritime power swallowed up after being defeated by primitive Athens.

The myths of love and of the world

In the Symposium, Aristophanes tells that the first humans were double, round, with four arms and four legs; Zeus cut them in two to weaken them, and each has been looking for his other half ever since. The Timaeus describes a demiurge who shapes the world by looking at an eternal model, a narrative that Philo and the Fathers will read as a philosophical version of creation.

The readings

The Neoplatonists read these myths as allegories of a higher truth. Karl Popper saw in the noble lie the proof of an authoritarian project, which others dispute. Leo Strauss read in it an illustration of writing on two levels, one for the multitude, the other for the philosophers.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with Plato's ambivalence, the legislator of the Laws who punishes impiety and the thinker of conversion who tells of the cave, and with the idea that useful narratives can be imposed on those who do not philosophize, an idea it finds again in Averroes, in Leo Strauss and in the Grand Inquisitor.

In the Gallery:Jan Saenredam after Cornelis van Haarlem, Plato's Cave (engraving, 1604, Washington, National Gallery of Art): the prisoners of the cave (Republic, Book VII); Murdoch makes this myth the pattern of the moral life, a conversion of attention that turns away from the self towards the Good

In the essay: Plato · Leo Strauss · Averroes

Further reading: Luc Brisson, Platon, les mots et les mythes (1982) ; Jean-François Mattéi, Platon et le miroir du mythe (1996) ; Monique Dixsaut, Platon et la question de la pensée (2000) ; Karl Popper, La Société ouverte et ses ennemis, vol. I (1945, trans. 1979) ; Catherine Collobert, Pierre Destrée and Francisco Gonzalez (eds.), Plato and Myth (2012)

The mysteries and Orphism: Eleusis, Orpheus, Cybele, Isis, MithrasGreek myths

Alongside the public cults of the city, the Greek and Roman world knew mystery cults: secret initiation rites, open to the individuals who chose them, promising a personal relationship with a deity and often a better fate after death. They complement civic religion without replacing it, and they have held a large place in the debates on the originality of Christianity.

Eleusis

The mysteries of Eleusis, near Athens, celebrated Demeter and her daughter Persephone, also called Kore. The myth, told by the Homeric Hymn to Demeter, relates the abduction of Persephone by Hades, the grief of her mother, who makes the harvests perish, then the compromise of Zeus: the girl will spend part of the year under the earth and the other part with her mother, which has been read as the cycle of the seeds. The initiation, open to men and women, to free people and to slaves who spoke Greek, included a procession from Athens and nocturnal revelations in the hall of the Telesterion. The secret was so well kept that almost nothing is known of what was shown there; the initiates went away with the hope of a better life in the hereafter.

Orphism

Under the name of Orpheus, the Thracian singer who went down to the underworld to fetch Eurydice, poems and rules of life circulated. Their central narrative tells that the child Dionysus, sometimes called Zagreus, was torn apart and devoured by the Titans, whom Zeus struck with lightning; men would have been born from their ashes, mixing a guilty Titanic part and a divine part. Hence a doctrine of the soul exiled in the body as in a prison or a tomb, purifications, abstinence from meat, and the promise of escaping the cycle of births. Gold tablets found in tombs give the dead instructions for finding their way in the hereafter. Plato and the Neoplatonists owe much to these ideas.

The Oriental cults

In the Hellenistic and Roman period, cults coming from the East spread: Cybele, the Phrygian Great Mother, and her consort Attis, served by eunuch and begging priests; Isis, the Egyptian goddess who finds and revives Osiris, honoured by initiations that Apuleius's Golden Ass describes; Mithras, a god of Iranian origin worshipped in underground sanctuaries by soldiers and merchants.

The debate over Christian parallels

The history of religions school, at the turn of the twentieth century, explained part of Pauline Christianity by these cults: baptism and the meal as initiation rites, the Lord invoked as a saviour god, death and resurrection as a myth of a dying god. Frazer grouped Attis, Adonis and Osiris into a category of gods who die and are reborn with vegetation. Later work has strongly qualified these comparisons: the sources for the mysteries are often late, Frazer's category is disputed, and Judaism is enough to explain many Christian features.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the mysteries to which Plotinus compares the ineffable union with the One, the mystery cults with which Bousset compares the invocation of the Lord, the god who dies and is reborn whom the mythicists make the model of Christ, and the priests of Cybele to whom Celsus compares the Christians.

In the Gallery:The Return of Persephone (1891, Leeds Art Gallery): Hermes brings Persephone back from the underworld to her mother Demeter

In the essay: Plotinus · Celsus and Origen · Tylor and Frazer

Further reading: Walter Burkert, Les Cultes à mystères dans l'Antiquité (1987, trans. 1992) ; Franz Cumont, Les Religions orientales dans le paganisme romain (1906) ; Marcel Detienne, Dionysos mis à mort (1977) ; Alberto Bernabé and Ana Isabel Jiménez San Cristóbal, Instructions for the Netherworld (2008) ; Jonathan Z. Smith, Drudgery Divine (1990) ; Tryggve Mettinger, The Riddle of Resurrection (2001)

Dionysus and the Bacchae: the foreign god, madness, dismembermentGreek myths

Dionysus is the Greek god of wine, of intoxication, of the theatre and of metamorphoses. Son of Zeus and of the mortal Semele, born a second time from his father's thigh, he appears in the myths as a god who arrives from elsewhere, always a stranger, whom cities refuse before being forced to welcome him. His cult mingles collective festival, trance and a violence that tragedy has put on stage.

The god and his retinue

Dionysus is accompanied by satyrs and by maenads, or bacchants, women seized by the god who leave the city for the mountain, dressed in fawnskins, armed with the thyrsus, a stalk crowned with ivy. The narratives lend them extraordinary acts: making milk and wine spring from the ground, tearing animals apart with their bare hands, the ritual dismemberment called sparagmos, and eating their raw flesh. In Athens, the god was honoured by civic festivals, the Dionysia, during which tragedies and comedies were performed: Greek theatre was born in his cult.

Euripides' Bacchae

Euripides' last tragedy, performed after his death in 405 BCE, tells of the return of Dionysus to Thebes, his native city, where his divinity is denied. The young king Pentheus, his cousin, has the stranger arrested and wants to forbid the rites. The god drives him to disguise himself as a woman to spy on the bacchants on Cithaeron; discovered, Pentheus is torn to pieces by the women of Thebes, and first of all by his own mother, Agave, who comes back to the city carrying his head, which she takes for a lion's. The god then drives out the royal family. The play has been read as an apology for piety, as a critique of religion, and as a study of the crowd.

Nietzsche: the Apollonian and the Dionysian

In The Birth of Tragedy (1872), Nietzsche contrasts two drives of Greek art: the Apollonian, dream, form, measure, and the Dionysian, intoxication, fusion, the dissolution of the individual. Tragedy is born of their union, and dies when Socrates and Euripides impose reason on it. In his last writings, Dionysus becomes the name of his affirmation of life, which he sets against the Crucified, symbol of life judged and condemned; Ecce Homo ends on this opposition.

Girard: the lynching seen from within

René Girard reads The Bacchae as an almost transparent description of the scapegoat mechanism: a crisis in which differences fade, a unanimous crowd, a victim torn to pieces, then an order restored that the myth attributes to the god. Taking up Nietzsche's formula, he maintains that the difference between Dionysus and Christ lies in the victim: the Dionysian myth proves the crowd right, the Gospels the victim.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with Dionysus, the bacchants and the death of Pentheus, which the box in the Girard section evokes, as well as with the closing formula of Ecce Homo, Dionysus against the Crucified, which Girard takes up to say that Nietzsche was right in his diagnosis and wrong in his choice.

In the Gallery:Dionysus in His Ship, Attic black-figure cup (c. 530 BCE, Munich, Staatliche Antikensammlungen): the pirates who had captured the god are turned into dolphins

In the essay: Girard

Further reading: Euripides, Les Bacchantes (405 BCE) ; Marcel Detienne, Dionysos à ciel ouvert (1986) ; Walter F. Otto, Dionysos (1933) ; Jean-Pierre Vernant and Pierre Vidal-Naquet, Mythe et tragédie en Grèce ancienne (1972-1986) ; Albert Henrichs, “Loss of Self, Suffering, Violence” (1984) ; René Girard, La Violence et le sacré (1972)

Oedipus: the oracle, the Sphinx, the plague of ThebesGreek myths

Oedipus is the Theban hero who, without knowing it, kills his father and marries his mother. His legend, already known to Homer, received its classical form in the tragedies of Sophocles, and in the twentieth century it became one of the most commented narratives of European culture, by psychoanalysis, structural anthropology and mimetic theory.

The legend

An oracle of Apollo foretold to Laius, king of Thebes, that his son would kill him. The child, his feet pierced, hence his name of swollen foot, is exposed on Mount Cithaeron, taken in by shepherds and raised in Corinth as the king's son. Grown up and warned in his turn by the oracle that he will kill his father and marry his mother, he flees Corinth, kills at a crossroads an old man who was blocking his way, Laius, then delivers Thebes from the Sphinx by solving its riddle: what being walks on four legs in the morning, on two at noon, on three in the evening. He becomes king and marries the widowed queen, Jocasta, with whom he has four children, Eteocles, Polynices, Antigone and Ismene.

Oedipus Rex

Sophocles' tragedy begins years later: plague ravages Thebes, and the oracle demands that the murderer of Laius be driven out. Oedipus leads the inquiry relentlessly; the blind seer Tiresias accuses him, he suspects Creon of a plot, and the successive witnesses gradually reveal the truth. Jocasta hangs herself, Oedipus puts out his eyes and asks for exile. In Oedipus at Colonus, Sophocles' last play, the old wandering Oedipus, guided by Antigone, dies mysteriously near Athens and becomes a protecting hero for the city that welcomes him.

Modern readings

Freud sees in the legend the expression of a universal desire of the child for the parent of the other sex and of its rivalry with the other parent, the Oedipus complex, which in Totem and Taboo he extends to the origin of religion through the murder of the primal father. Claude Lévi-Strauss analyses the myth as a system of oppositions, between overrated kinship and underrated kinship, autochthonous origin and sexual origin. Jean-Pierre Vernant insists on the reversal: the saviour king becomes the defilement to be expelled, like the pharmakos, the scapegoat victim that certain cities drove out during festivals of purification.

Girard's reading

René Girard reads Oedipus Rex as the narrative of a sacrificial crisis: the plague figures the contagion of violence in which differences fade, the symmetrical accusations between Oedipus, Tiresias and Creon show doubles, and the expulsion of Oedipus restores order. Parricide and incest would be the typical crimes that a crowd attributes to its victim; the tragedy would half show the mechanism, the myth would hide it entirely.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the oracle, the plague of Thebes and Oedipus's inquiry, which the Girard section summarizes, with Freud's Oedipus complex and the murder of the father in Totem and Taboo, as well as with Becker's rereading, which, after Otto Rank, makes the Oedipus complex a causa sui project, the child's desire to become his own father.

In the Gallery:Oedipus Explains the Riddle of the Sphinx (1808, Paris, Musée du Louvre)

In the essay: Girard · Freud · Becker

Further reading: Sophocles, Œdipe roi (c. 430 BCE) and Œdipe à Colone (401 BCE) ; Jean-Pierre Vernant, “Ambiguïté et renversement” (1970), in Mythe et tragédie en Grèce ancienne ; Claude Lévi-Strauss, “La structure des mythes”, in Anthropologie structurale (1958) ; René Girard, La Violence et le sacré (1972) ; Didier Anzieu, L'Auto-analyse de Freud (1959) ; Lowell Edmunds, Oedipus (2006)

Antigone: the law of the city and the unwritten lawsGreek myths

Antigone, daughter of Oedipus and Jocasta, is the heroine of the tragedy that Sophocles had performed around 442 BCE. She buries her brother against the king's prohibition and dies of this act. Since Hegel, the play has become the reference text for the conflict between the law of the State and a higher law, and each age has read its own question in it.

The myth and the play

After the exile of Oedipus, his two sons, Eteocles and Polynices, were to reign over Thebes in turn. Eteocles refuses to give up the throne; Polynices returns with a foreign army, and the two brothers kill each other before the gates of the city. Creon, their uncle, now king, has Eteocles buried with honour and orders that the body of Polynices, a traitor to his country, remain without burial, left to the dogs and the birds, on pain of death for whoever buries it. Antigone, despite the pleas of her sister Ismene, scatters a handful of dust over the body. Arrested, she answers Creon that she did not believe his decrees could prevail over the unwritten and unchanging laws of the gods, which are neither of today nor of yesterday. Creon has her shut up alive in a tomb. The seer Tiresias warns him that the gods are offended; when he gives way, it is too late: Antigone has hanged herself, his son Haemon, who loved her, kills himself over her body, and his wife Eurydice kills herself in turn.

What is at stake

Burying the dead was in Greece a religious and family duty; to deprive a dead person of burial was to prevent him from reaching the underworld. The conflict therefore sets against each other the city and the family, man and woman, written law and divine law, but also two forms of excess: the rigidity of the king, who confuses public order with his own will, and the obstinacy of a young woman who chooses death.

The readings

Hegel, in the Phenomenology of Spirit, sees in the play the collision of two equally legitimate powers, the human law of the State and the divine law of the family, each of which is both right and wrong; tragedy is the reconciliation born of their mutual destruction. Kierkegaard, Hölderlin, who translated it, and then Heidegger commented on it. Simone Weil, in 1936, wrote for the workers of a factory at Rosières a presentation of it that makes Antigone the figure of love that refuses to take sides in hatred. Jean Anouilh, in 1944, under the Occupation, gave Creon the arguments of a realist ruler, and the play was read sometimes as a work of resistance, sometimes as an accommodating one. Judith Butler has examined what it says about kinship and mourning.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with Antigone's answer to Creon, which it quotes from Sophocles in the box in the Weil section, and with the idea of a self-offering that refounds no religion: Antigone dies for a duty she imposes on no one, and the essay places her on the side of the refusal of the scapegoat.

In the Gallery:Antigone before the Body of Polynices (1865, Athens, National Gallery)

In the essay: Simone Weil

Further reading: Sophocles, Antigone (c. 442 BCE) ; Hegel, Phénoménologie de l'esprit (1807) ; George Steiner, Les Antigones (1984, trans. 1986) ; Simone Weil, “Antigone” (1936), in La Source grecque (1953) ; Jean Anouilh, Antigone (1944) ; Judith Butler, Antigone's Claim (2000)

Prometheus and Pandora: the sharing of the sacrifice, fire, evilGreek myths

Prometheus is the Titan who deceives Zeus for the benefit of men, gives them fire and pays for this gift with eternal torment. His myth tells at once of the origin of sacrifice, of technology and of human misfortune, and from Antiquity to Romanticism it became the figure of the man who challenges the gods.

Hesiod: Mecone and Pandora

In the Theogony, during a division at Mecone between gods and men, Prometheus carves up an ox and presents two portions to Zeus: on one side the meat and the entrails hidden under the hide and the stomach, on the other the white bones wrapped in glistening fat. Zeus chooses the appetizing portion and uncovers the bones; since then, men burn the bones for the gods and eat the meat. The myth thus founds the division of the sacrifice and the separation of gods and men. To take revenge, Zeus hides fire; Prometheus steals it and brings it back to mortals in a fennel stalk. Zeus then has the first woman fashioned, Pandora, adorned with all the gifts of the gods, whom Epimetheus, the improvident brother, accepts despite the warnings; she lifts the lid of the jar, and all evils spread over the earth, only hope remaining inside. Prometheus, chained to the Caucasus, sees an eagle devour his liver each day, which grows back at night, until Heracles delivers him.

Aeschylus and Plato

The Prometheus Bound attributed to Aeschylus makes the Titan the benefactor of humanity, to whom he gave fire, but also numbers, writing, medicine and the art of foresight, and the rebel who refuses to bend before a tyrannical Zeus whose secret he knows. In the Protagoras, Plato has him give men the arts, but not the political art, which Zeus alone grants.

The Prometheus of the moderns

From the Renaissance onwards, Prometheus becomes the symbol of man the creator. Goethe, in a poem of 1774, has him defy Zeus and declare that he shapes men in his own image, who will have no need of gods. Shelley writes a Prometheus Unbound, Mary Shelley subtitles Frankenstein the Modern Prometheus, and Marx, in the preface to his thesis of 1841, makes Prometheus the noblest saint and martyr in the philosophical calendar. Camus, in The Rebel, makes him one of the first figures of metaphysical rebellion, and Blumenberg devotes the last part of Arbeit am Mythos to the reworkings of the myth, from Goethe's poem to its exhaustion.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with Goethe's poem, Mary Shelley's Frankenstein and Kafka's legends of Prometheus, which the box in the Blumenberg section relates to the self-assertion of the modern age, as well as with the idea, present in the poem, of gods who live off the sacrifices of men, the essay's criterion read in reverse.

In the Gallery:Peter Paul Rubens and Frans Snyders, Prometheus Bound (1611-1618, Philadelphia, Philadelphia Museum of Art)

In the essay: Hans Blumenberg · Camus

Further reading: Hesiod, Théogonie and Les Travaux et les Jours (7th century BCE) ; Aeschylus, Prométhée enchaîné (5th century BCE) ; Jean-Pierre Vernant, “À la table des hommes”, in La Cuisine du sacrifice en pays grec (1979) ; Hans Blumenberg, Arbeit am Mythos (1979) ; Raymond Trousson, Le Thème de Prométhée dans la littérature européenne (1964) ; Olivier Got, Le Mythe de Prométhée (2006)

Sisyphus and the underworld: Hades, Tantalus, the DanaidsGreek myths

The Greeks pictured the abode of the dead as an underground world ruled by Hades, where most souls lead an existence of shades without strength or memory. A few great culprits undergo eternal punishments there, of whom Sisyphus has become, through Camus, the most famous figure.

The kingdom of Hades

Hades, brother of Zeus, received the underworld as his share, and he rules it with Persephone. To reach it, the dead cross the Acheron or the Styx in the boat of Charon, who was paid with an obol placed in the mouth of the deceased; the three-headed dog Cerberus guards its entrance. In the Odyssey, in Book XI, Odysseus calls up the dead on the shore of Ocean: they are shades who must drink the blood of victims to recover speech for a moment, and the shade of Achilles tells him that he would rather be the servant of a poor man on earth than reign over all the dead. Below lies Tartarus, prison of the defeated Titans. Later there develops the idea of a judgement of the dead by Minos, Aeacus and Rhadamanthus, and of a happy abode, the Elysian Fields or the Isles of the Blessed, reserved for heroes and the just, then for initiates of the mysteries.

The great punished

Sisyphus, the cunning king of Corinth, deceived the gods and, according to some versions, chained up Death himself, or obtained leave to go back up to earth and refused to return; he is condemned to push a rock eternally to the top of a slope from which it endlessly rolls back down. Tantalus, who had served the gods the flesh of his son to test their perspicacity, stands in water up to his chin beneath branches laden with fruit, without ever being able to drink or eat. The Danaids, who had killed their husbands on their wedding night, endlessly fill leaking jars. Ixion, who had tried to seduce Hera, turns bound to a flaming wheel, and Tityos has his liver devoured by vultures.

The philosophical readings

Lucretius, in Book III of his poem, maintains that these torments do not exist after death but in this life: Tantalus is the man who dreads the gods, Sisyphus the ambitious man who endlessly seeks power, the Danaids those whom nothing satisfies. The myth becomes the description of the passions from which philosophy delivers. Camus, in 1942, makes Sisyphus the hero of the absurd: the human condition is a task without end and without hope, but the man who looks it in the face and takes it up again, at the moment when he goes back down towards his rock, is superior to his fate, and one must imagine him happy.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the punishment of Sisyphus and with the conclusion of The Myth of Sisyphus, which it quotes, and in which it reads a spirituality without consolation, as well as with the fear of punishment after death, which the Letter to Menoeceus and Lucretius's poem seek to dispel at the same time as the fear of the gods.

In the Gallery:Sisyphus (1548-1549, Madrid, Prado): the figure whom Camus makes the hero of the absurd

In the essay: Camus · Jaspers · Epicurus and Lucretius

Further reading: Homer, Odyssée, Book XI (8th century BCE) ; Lucretius, De la nature, Book III (1st century BCE) ; Albert Camus, Le Mythe de Sisyphe (1942) ; Christiane Sourvinou-Inwood, Reading Greek Death (1995) ; Jan Bremmer, The Rise and Fall of the Afterlife (2002) ; Robert Garland, The Greek Way of Death (1985)

The Trojan War: Iphigenia at Aulis, the wrath of Achilles, PriamGreek myths

The Trojan War is the great legendary cycle of the Greeks: ten years of siege of a city of Asia Minor by a coalition of Achaean kings, to take back Helen, the wife of Menelaus abducted by the Trojan prince Paris. The Iliad recounts only a few weeks of it, but the poems of the cycle, the tragedians and the mythographers fixed all its episodes, from the quarrel of the goddesses to the fall of the city.

The origins

At the wedding of Peleus and Thetis, Discord throws down a golden apple meant for the most beautiful. Hera, Athena and Aphrodite contend for it; the Trojan shepherd Paris, chosen as judge, awards it to Aphrodite, who promises him the love of the most beautiful of women. He carries off Helen from Sparta, and the Greek princes, bound by an oath, gather under the command of Agamemnon, brother of Menelaus.

The sacrifice of Iphigenia

At Aulis, the Greek fleet is held back by contrary winds. The seer Calchas reveals that Artemis, offended by Agamemnon, demands the sacrifice of his daughter Iphigenia. Agamemnon sends for the girl on the pretext of marrying her to Achilles, and leads her to the altar. Depending on the versions, her throat is cut, or the goddess substitutes a hind for her at the last moment and carries her off to Tauris. The sacrifice of Iphigenia is the first cause of Clytemnestra's hatred and of the murder of Agamemnon on his return. Euripides drew from it Iphigenia at Aulis, and the scene has often been compared to the sacrifice of Isaac.

The Iliad

The poem begins with the wrath of Achilles, the best of the Achaeans, from whom Agamemnon has taken his captive Briseis. Achilles withdraws from the fighting, and the Greeks are routed by Hector, son of Priam. When Hector kills Patroclus, Achilles' friend, Achilles returns, kills Hector and drags his corpse around Patroclus's tomb. In the last book, the old king Priam, guided at night by Hermes, comes to Achilles' tent to beg that the body of his son be given back to him; the two enemies weep together, each for his own dead, and Achilles gives back the body. The gods are present everywhere, but partial, and guarantee no justice.

The fall of Troy

After the death of Hector and then that of Achilles, killed by an arrow of Paris guided by Apollo, the Greeks take the city by the ruse of the wooden horse, sack it and burn it. The Odyssey and the tragedies then tell of the difficult and bloody returns of the victors.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the sacrifice of Iphigenia, which Lucretius recounts as the example of the crimes engendered by religion, and with the scene of Priam and Achilles in the last book of the Iliad, which the box in the Weil section quotes after L'Iliade ou le poème de la force, and in which it sees the refusal to give a meaning to misfortune.

In the Gallery:The Sacrifice of Iphigenia, fresco from the House of the Tragic Poet in Pompeii (1st century CE, Naples, Museo Archeologico Nazionale): Agamemnon turns his head away, Artemis sends the hind that will take the girl's placeJupiter and Thetis (1811, Aix-en-Provence, Musée Granet): Thetis entreats Zeus on behalf of her son Achilles, in the first book of the Iliad

In the essay: Epicurus and Lucretius · Simone Weil

Further reading: Homer, Iliade (8th century BCE) ; Euripides, Iphigénie à Aulis (405 BCE) ; Simone Weil, “L'Iliade ou le poème de la force” (1940-1941) ; Pierre Vidal-Naquet, Le Monde d'Homère (2000) ; Gregory Nagy, Le Meilleur des Achéens (1979, trans. 1994) ; Jean-Pierre Vernant, L'Individu, la mort, l'amour (1989)

The Atreids: Agamemnon, Clytemnestra, Orestes and the ErinyesGreek myths

The house of the Atreids, descendants of Tantalus and Atreus, is in Greek legend the family in which crime calls for crime over several generations, until a court puts an end to it. Aeschylus drew from it the Oresteia, the only surviving tragic trilogy, which recounts the passage from vengeance to the justice of the city.

The curse

Atreus, king of Mycenae, to take revenge on his brother Thyestes, who had seduced his wife, had him served at a banquet the flesh of his own children. From this horror a curse is born. The son of Atreus, Agamemnon, leader of the Greeks before Troy, sacrifices his daughter Iphigenia at Aulis to obtain favourable winds. During the ten years of the war, his wife Clytemnestra takes as her lover Aegisthus, the surviving son of Thyestes. On Agamemnon's return, she kills him in his bath, together with the Trojan captive Cassandra, and reigns with Aegisthus.

The Oresteia

Aeschylus's trilogy, performed in 458 BCE, comprises three plays. In Agamemnon, the king returns victorious and dies murdered. In The Libation Bearers, his son Orestes, an exile, returns at the command of Apollo, finds his sister Electra at their father's tomb, and kills Aegisthus and then his mother. At once the Erinyes, archaic divinities of blood vengeance, rise up to pursue him. In The Eumenides, Orestes takes refuge at Delphi and then in Athens; Athena institutes a court of citizens to judge him, the Areopagus. The votes are equal, and the goddess tips the balance towards acquittal. The Erinyes, appeased, accept a cult in the city and become the Eumenides, the Kindly Ones. The cycle of private vengeance is closed by an institution.

The other versions

Sophocles and Euripides each wrote an Electra, centred on the sister who awaits her brother's return, and Euripides an Orestes in which the hero, pursued by madness, is condemned by the assembly of Argos. In the twentieth century, Giraudoux wrote an Électre, Eugene O'Neill transposed the trilogy to the America of the Civil War, and Sartre, in 1943, staged The Flies, in which Orestes refuses the remorse that Jupiter and Aegisthus impose on the city of Argos.

The readings

The Oresteia has been read as the story of the birth of law, public justice replacing blood vengeance; as that of the passage from a matriarchal order to a patriarchal order, according to Bachofen and then Engels; and, in René Girard, as the description of a reciprocal violence that stops only through institution, sacrificial or judicial.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with Orestes, Clytemnestra and the Erinyes, whom the box in the Sartre section encounters in The Flies: Orestes there leaves God through an act and carries off the flies like a scapegoat, thus keeping from religion the form of sacrifice.

In the Gallery:The Remorse of Orestes (1862, Norfolk, Chrysler Museum of Art)

In the essay: Sartre

Further reading: Aeschylus, Orestie (458 BCE) ; Sophocles, Électre ; Euripides, Électre and Oreste ; Jean-Paul Sartre, Les Mouches (1943) ; Pierre Vidal-Naquet, “Chasse et sacrifice dans l'Orestie”, in Mythe et tragédie en Grèce ancienne (1972) ; Christian Meier, De la tragédie grecque comme art politique (1988, trans. 1991)

Heracles: the labours, the choice, the apotheosisGreek myths

Heracles, whom the Romans call Hercules, is the most popular of the Greek heroes: son of Zeus and of the mortal Alcmene, pursued by the hatred of Hera, he performs twelve labours, dies in the flames and rises among the gods. A hero of strength, he has also become, among the philosophers, a moral figure of effort and virtue.

The birth and the labours

Zeus, having taken the appearance of Amphitryon, lies with his wife Alcmene; Hera, jealous, sends two serpents against the newborn, who strangles them in his cradle. Later, driven mad by the goddess, Heracles kills his wife and his children; to atone for this crime, the oracle of Delphi orders him to serve King Eurystheus, who imposes twelve labours on him. He strangles the Nemean lion, whose skin he then wears, kills the Lernaean hydra with its regrowing heads, captures the Ceryneian hind and the Erymanthian boar, cleans the Augean stables in a single day by diverting two rivers, drives off the birds of Lake Stymphalus, captures the Cretan bull and the mares of Diomedes, takes the girdle of the queen of the Amazons, the cattle of Geryon, the golden apples of the Hesperides, and finally brings back from the underworld the dog Cerberus. Other exploits are added, among them the deliverance of Prometheus.

The death and the apotheosis

His last wife, Deianira, believing she will win back his love, sends him a tunic soaked in the blood of the centaur Nessus, which burns him beyond all remedy. Heracles has a pyre built on Mount Oeta and has himself burned alive on it; he is then carried up to Olympus, reconciled with Hera, and marries Hebe, youth. A hero become a god, he received a double cult, as a mortal and as an immortal.

The choice of Heracles

The sophist Prodicus, whose story Xenophon reports, imagines the young Heracles at the parting of two ways: two women approach him, Vice, who promises an easy life full of pleasures, and Virtue, who promises efforts and lasting glory; he chooses the second. The Cynics, then the Stoics, made Heracles the model of the sage who faces trials and makes himself free through effort; Seneca devotes two tragedies to him. The Fathers of the Church discussed the resemblances between the apotheosis of the hero, son of a god and a mortal woman, and the story of Christ, and the Renaissance often depicted the choice of Heracles as a moral allegory.

What the essay assumes

The essay mentions Hercules in the box in the Malinowski section, in connection with La Fontaine's fable in which the carter stuck in the mud calls on him and is told in reply that he must act first. The Stoic hero of effort also sheds light on the spiritual exercises that Pierre Hadot rediscovers in ancient philosophy.

In the Gallery:Hercules and the Nemean Lion (1634, Madrid, Prado), from the series of the labours of Hercules painted for the Buen Retiro palace

In the essay: Malinowski · Pierre Hadot

Further reading: Prodicus, in Xenophon, Mémorables, II, 1 ; Sophocles, Les Trachiniennes ; Euripides, Héraclès furieux ; Seneca, Hercule furieux and Hercule sur l'Œta ; Marcel Simon, Hercule et le christianisme (1955) ; G. Karl Galinsky, The Herakles Theme (1972) ; Emma Stafford, Herakles (2012)

Narcissus and Echo: the reflection, the voice, narcissismGreek myths

Narcissus is the young man of perfect beauty who, refusing all loves, falls in love with his own reflection and dies of it. The myth, known above all through Ovid's Metamorphoses, was read by the philosophers as an image of the soul that mistakes itself for appearance, and in the twentieth century it gave its name to one of the concepts of psychoanalysis.

Ovid's narrative

Narcissus is the son of the river Cephissus and of the nymph Liriope. Consulted at his birth, the seer Tiresias foretells that he will live long if he does not know himself. Grown into an adolescent desired by boys and girls alike, he rejects them all. The nymph Echo, condemned by Hera to be able only to repeat the last words she hears, loves him without being able to speak to him first; rejected, she wastes away with grief until she is nothing but a voice. One of the scorned asks the gods that Narcissus in his turn should love without being able to possess what he loves. Leaning over a spring to drink, Narcissus sees his image, falls in love with it, and understands too late that he loves his own reflection; he pines away at the water's edge, and in his place only a flower is found, the narcissus. Pausanias reports a variant in which he mourns a twin sister whose features his own face recalls to him.

The ancient readings

Plotinus, in the treatise on beauty, compares the one who runs after sensible beauties to someone who would try to seize a beautiful image reflected in the water and would drown in it; Pierre Hadot has shown how the Neoplatonists and the Fathers made Narcissus a figure of the soul that loses its way in the world of reflections instead of turning towards its source. In the Middle Ages and the Renaissance, the myth becomes a warning against pride and self-love.

Narcissism

The psychiatrist Paul Näcke and the sexologist Havelock Ellis used the word at the end of the nineteenth century to designate a perversion. Freud, in 1914, makes it a stage of development: primary narcissism, in which the child invests his libido in himself before turning it towards objects, and a secondary narcissism, a return of the libido towards the ego. In Civilization and Its Discontents, he traces the oceanic feeling that Romain Rolland describes to him back to this primary narcissism, a state in which the infant does not yet distinguish his ego from the world. Beyond psychoanalysis, the word has become a diagnosis of society, notably in Christopher Lasch.

What the essay assumes

The essay assumes familiarity with the notion of primary narcissism, by which Freud explains Rolland's oceanic feeling, in the Freud section and in the constructed debate on the reasons for believing: a remnant of childhood in which ego and world are not yet separated.

In the Gallery:Narcissus (c. 1597-1599, Rome, Galleria Nazionale d'Arte Antica)

In the essay: Freud · Why do people believe? A constructed debate

Further reading: Ovid, Métamorphoses, III (c. 8 CE) ; Pausanias, Description de la Grèce, IX, 31 ; Sigmund Freud, Pour introduire le narcissisme (1914) ; Pierre Hadot, “Le mythe de Narcisse et son interprétation par Plotin”, Nouvelle Revue de psychanalyse (1976) ; Louise Vinge, The Narcissus Theme in Western European Literature (1967) ; Christopher Lasch, La Culture du narcissisme (1979, trans. 1981)

Eros and Psyche: the primordial god, the ladder of the Symposium, Apuleius's taleGreek myths

Eros, the Greek god of love, has had two very different histories: a cosmic power born at the origins of the world, and the young winged god, son of Aphrodite, who wounds with his arrows. The philosophers made him the name of the desire that raises the soul towards the beautiful and the good, and the tale of Eros and Psyche, handed down by Apuleius, became an allegory of the soul.

The primordial god and the winged god

In Hesiod's Theogony, Eros is one of the first beings, appearing with Chaos and Gaia, the most beautiful of the immortals, who loosens the limbs and overpowers the reason of gods and men; he is the force that makes beings unite and engender the world. The Orphic cosmogonies have him born from a primordial egg. In lyric poetry and then in the Hellenistic period, he becomes a winged child, son of Aphrodite and often of Ares, armed with a bow, whom the Romans call Cupid or Amor.

Plato's Symposium

In the Symposium, each guest delivers a eulogy of Eros. Socrates reports the teaching supposedly given to him by a priestess of Mantinea, Diotima: Eros is not a god but a daimon, an intermediary between gods and men, son of Penia, poverty, and of Poros, resource; he is desire for what he does not have, and therefore a philosopher. Diotima describes an ascent: from the love of one beautiful body to that of all beautiful bodies, then to the beauty of souls, of institutions and of the sciences, up to the sudden contemplation of beauty itself, eternal and unmixed. The Phaedrus takes up this ascent in the form of the winged soul that remembers the realities seen before birth.

Apuleius's tale

In the second century, Apuleius inserts into his Metamorphoses the tale of Cupid and Psyche. Psyche, a princess of a beauty that makes Venus jealous, is exposed on a rock, then carried off to a palace where an invisible husband joins her each night on condition that she does not try to see his face. Urged on by her sisters, she lights him with a lamp, discovers Cupid asleep, lets fall a drop of oil that wakes him, and he flees. After a series of trials imposed by Venus, among them a descent to the underworld, she is restored to her husband, receives immortality and gives birth to Pleasure. The Neoplatonists and the Christians read in it the journey of the soul, in Greek psychè, towards the divine.

Eros and Agape

In the twentieth century, the Lutheran theologian Anders Nygren contrasts Greek eros, the ascending desire of the soul seeking its own good, with Christian agape, the descending and gratuitous love of God for men, and sees the history of Christianity as their mixture. Freud names Eros the life drives, which he sets against the death drives.

What the essay assumes

The essay mentions the Symposium among the works that had marked Iris Murdoch most, whose sovereignty of the Good takes up the Platonic ascent: a desire that purifies itself into attention, and that tends towards a Good it does not possess.

In the Gallery:Psyche Revived by Cupid's Kiss (1787-1793, Paris, Musée du Louvre)

In the essay: Iris Murdoch

Further reading: Hesiod, Théogonie (7th century BCE) ; Plato, Le Banquet and Phèdre ; Apuleius, Les Métamorphoses or L'Âne d'or, IV-VI (2nd century) ; Anders Nygren, Éros et Agapè (1930-1936, trans. 1944-1952) ; Denis de Rougemont, L'Amour et l'Occident (1939) ; Iris Murdoch, The Fire and the Sun (1977)
